Principia Humanitatis

To the Strait-Jacketed, the Other Always Seems Baggy

A Vatic-Scholastic Disputation upon Dave Snowden’s “Neither Baggy Nor Boxed” (1 November 2026) and Chris Mowles’s “Staying with the Trouble” (26 October 2026)

Two Books · Twenty-Eight Parts · Twenty-Five Questions · Fifty Theses · Glossary · Sources

Before we begin

Two essays, one week, and a quarrel as old as gathering itself

In the last week of October 2026, a professor of complexity and management sat down to write about a conference he loved and a workshop he hated. At the conference, he said, almost nothing was fixed except when it started and when it ended — and it was often the best weekend of the year. At the workshop, every minute was timed, every output was read aloud and applauded, and votes were cast with sticky dots; he went along with it, he admitted, like a drone.

A week later, one of the best-known practitioners in the field answered him. The conference, he suggested, was not as free as it looked: without structure, the confident and the senior quietly run the room. The workshop was indeed dreadful. But the answer to both was neither looseness nor control. It was a third way — his own method — which he described as neither baggy nor boxed.

You may already have a side. Most people who have sat through a bad meeting do. Perhaps you have suffered the sticky dots and long for the open conversation. Perhaps you have sat in an “open” conversation where the same three people did all the talking, and long for some structure. Hold that instinct. This treatise is about where it comes from.

Because here is the strange thing you will discover as you read. Each of these two serious, thoughtful men describes the other’s kind of room with great precision — and his own with something close to blindness. The man who loves the open conference sees the workshop’s cage clearly and does not see the walls of his own beloved room. The man who designs methods sees the conference’s hidden power clearly and does not see the hand of the designer in his own. And when the treatise turns to a third tradition, then to a whole field of thought, then to two thousand years of argument in China and in the Church, and finally to its own author, the same pattern appears every time.

That pattern has a name in what follows: to the strait-jacketed, the other always seems baggy. Its twin is just as true: to the baggy, the other always seems boxed. The rest of this work is an attempt to understand why that is, what it costs, and what a room might look like in which people could see their own walls for once.

You do not need to read it all at once. The page that follows maps the whole argument and offers three ways through it. Every Part opens by telling you where you are and what it is about to ask, and ends by pointing you onward. Think of it as a long conversation, conducted in the old scholastic form of question, objection and answer, but conducted with you.

At a Glance

This book examines two short essays from autumn 2026: Dave Snowden’s “Neither Baggy Nor Boxed” and Chris Mowles’s “Staying with the Trouble”, which it answered. Both concern how gatherings of people should be structured. This book tests both essays against a single idea, follows that idea’s roots through philosophy, classical Chinese thought and democratic theory, and ends with a practical discipline.

  1. The central idea. To the strait-jacketed, the other always seems baggy, and to the baggy, the other always seems boxed. We see other people’s constraints and are blind to our own.
  2. Snowden rightly shifts the question from how much structure to what kind, but wrongly places his own method outside the axis he describes (Part One).
  3. His animal roles borrow sacred symbols, and an appointed trickster protects the frame it pretends to challenge (Part Two).
  4. His case against open rooms tells as much against his own method as against the club (Part Three).
  5. Mowles rightly defends the human encounter, but his best examples, therapy and his own conference, depend on rigorous form (Part Four).
  6. Three traditions, three jackets: design, tradition, method-choice. None can be taken off (Part Five).
  7. Why we cannot see our own form: tacit knowing, the tool that withdraws from view, habit, thought collectives (Part Six). Classical China named the problem and located the answer in cultivation (Part Seven).
  8. Politics: who is in the room matters most; the frame must be contestable and power reversible (Part Eight).
  9. The proposal: a discipline of declared form, with three registers, six questions and three moments (Part Nine).

Book Two widens the field.

  1. The Connoisseur interrogated. The imprint’s earlier tribunal on Jackson is read through this book’s idea. Jackson’s grid is the curator’s jacket, and the most declared of the three. The tribunal itself left a chair empty and chose its own witnesses (Parts Ten to Twelve).
  2. The body of work revisited. Snowden’s own instruments would have revealed the club’s jacket (Part Thirteen). The imprint’s works on the Stacey Matrix, Ryōan-ji, Schismatrix and King Lear all say there is no view from nowhere (Part Fourteen).
  3. The full complexity corpus. Across eight complexities runs one fault line, with two symmetrical charges (Part Fifteen). In May 2026 Snowden named a trialectic that forbids midpoints and so convicts his own “neither” (Part Sixteen). The matrix’s deleted political axis shows that agreement is never free (Part Seventeen). The living map treats each framework as an offer (Part Eighteen). There are many ways of convening (Part Nineteen), every room has a weather (Part Twenty), and the established do not know they are established (Part Twenty-One).
  4. Agency and its two strokes. Facilitation and anti-facilitation are one respiration; each alone is its own shadow (Part Twenty-Two). Play, carnival and the dérive follow (Part Twenty-Three), then the Church’s long argument about order and Spirit (Part Twenty-Four), and stability through movement (Part Twenty-Five).
  5. The mirror and the objections. The imprint audits its own jackets (Part Twenty-Six) and answers seven objections to this book (Part Twenty-Seven). It closes with fifty theses and the Grand Ruling (Part Twenty-Eight).

How to read this

Twenty minutes: read this page, the In brief box at the head of each Part, the three tables, and the Final Ruling.
One sitting: add the Overture, the Rulings and Parts Five and Nine.
In full: Book One takes roughly two and a half hours; Book Two another five.

The questions follow an old pattern from medieval debate, but in plain English. Each one opens with the best case for the view being questioned (Someone might say), then the strongest point against it (On the other hand), then my own answer (My answer is), and finally a word back to the original objection (Back to that first objection). Short of time? Read the answer.

Key terms are defined in the Glossary at the end.

Book One
The Disputation

Snowden’s “Neither Baggy Nor Boxed” and Mowles’s “Staying with the Trouble”, weighed in nine Parts

OvertureThe central idea and its lineage

Where we areYou are at the very beginning. Two short essays, published a week apart in the autumn of 2026, have reopened one of the oldest arguments about how people should gather.
What this asksBefore we touch either essay, this opening asks a deceptively simple question: when someone calls a room “baggy”, what exactly are they describing — the room, or themselves?
In brief 3 min read
  • The central idea: those who have made their own constraints explicit tend to perceive other people’s tacit constraints as formlessness.
  • Henry James’s “loose baggy monsters” is the classic case: Tolstoy had a form that James could not see.
  • The twin idea: to the baggy, the other always seems boxed. Snowden and Mowles each see clearly only the other’s garment.

Takeaway“Baggy” and “boxed” describe the observer’s position at least as much as they describe the room.

Here is the idea this whole book turns on, and it fits on a postcard: to the strait-jacketed, the other always seems baggy. Once you have written your own rules down — timed them, named them, printed them on laminated cards — you start to look at any group that keeps its rules in its habits, its history or its bones, and you see nothing there at all. You see mess. But the mess is usually in your eye, not in the room. When someone says “there’s no structure here”, they very often mean “there’s no structure here that I recognise”.

The word “baggy” has a famous history that makes the point for us. In 1908, in the preface to his novel The Tragic Muse, Henry James looked at three great sprawling books — Thackeray’s The Newcomes, Dumas’ The Three Musketeers and Tolstoy’s War and Peace — and asked what such “large loose baggy monsters, with their queer elements of the accidental and the arbitrary” could possibly mean as art. James was the great perfectionist of the English novel. He controlled every point of view as tightly as a watchmaker. Seen from inside that discipline, Tolstoy looked shapeless. But Tolstoy had already answered, back in 1868: his book wasn’t a novel or an epic or a chronicle; it was exactly what he wanted to say, in the only form he could say it. A century of readers has mostly sided with Tolstoy. War and Peace has a shape — the swing between the drawing room and the battlefield, the essays that break in, the refusal to have one hero at the centre. It just isn’t James’s shape. James’s “baggy” is the classic case of our idea: a tight jacket naming somebody else’s clothes.

The essay this book answers uses that very word as its hinge. Dave Snowden, replying to Chris Mowles and, through him, to Michael Jackson, offers a third way between the “baggy” gathering and the “boxed” workshop. A lot of what he says is right, and I’ll say so as we go. But I’ll also argue that the backbone of his essay repeats James’s mistake, cleverly. It calls baggy what it can’t see as structured. And it calls itself “neither” from a spot that is, unavoidably, a jacket of its own.

No room has ever had no rules. The rules you wear are the ones you can’t see. So the real question is never whether a room is constrained, but by whom, for what, and whether the people inside can tell.

And the idea has a twin, which I won’t let either side forget: to the baggy, the other always seems boxed. Snowden looks at Mowles’s gathering and sees a room that has come loose. Mowles looks at the facilitated workshop and sees a cage. Each describes the other’s outfit down to the buttons and says nothing about his own. So this isn’t really a fight between structure and freedom. It’s two people, each dressed to the nines, accusing each other of being naked and of being tied up — at the same time.

First Interlude · A Declaration on Jackets

There’s never been a room without walls. There are only rooms whose walls you put up yourself — so you can see them — and rooms whose walls were up long before you arrived, so you walk past them every day without noticing. The facilitator writes his walls on the flip chart, and people resent them because they can see them. A tradition writes its walls into its members — into when they speak, when they wait, how they disagree, what they leave unsaid — and nobody resents them, because there’s nothing visible to resent.

We’ve got into the habit of calling the first kind of wall “structure” and the second kind “freedom”. Both names come from where you’re standing, not from the walls. Bagginess, really, is what form looks like when your nose is pressed to the wrong side of the window.

Hold on to that predicate — and its twin. Everything that follows tests them. First, though, you need the essay itself on the table.

The Essay in BriefWhat Snowden actually argued, fairly stated

Where we areYou now hold the central idea. Before it can be tested, you need to know what Snowden actually said.
What this asksThis section lays out his essay as fairly as possible, so that every criticism that follows can be checked against what he wrote rather than what he might be imagined to have meant.
In brief 2 min read
  • Mowles contrasted his loose research conference with a tightly facilitated workshop; Snowden replies to him, and through him to Jackson.
  • Snowden’s key move: the issue is not how much structure but what kind. Governing constraints fix outcomes; enabling constraints shape conditions.
  • His case against open rooms rests on three grounds: hidden power, entrainment on the first confident voice, and scale.
  • His alternative is the triopticon, with its Eagles, Ravens, Beavers, Coyotes and Magpies. In his postscript he concedes that he is no neutral judge of it.

Snowden published his essay on The Cynefin Co website on 1 November 2026, under a line from Goethe about mastery showing itself through limits. It joins a three-way conversation. A few days earlier, Mowles had written on his Substack about two events. One was his research group’s annual gathering: loose, conversational, with almost nothing fixed except when it starts and ends. The other was a one-day facilitated workshop: every minute timed, flip charts read aloud, sticky notes and dot voting. Mowles leaned on Sherry Turkle’s work on artificial intimacy, and he took on Michael Jackson’s criticism of the complex responsive processes tradition. Snowden thinks Mowles is a bit unfair to Jackson in lining up Jackson’s liking for structure with that workshop — but he runs with the contrast anyway.

He agrees with Mowles about the workshop. Timed exercises, decisions rushed to a close, what Doug Griffin called the “doubling of process” — none of it is good. He remembers how IBM trained its staff in a house style for controlling groups, and he didn’t like that either. He shares Mowles’s worry, borrowed from Turkle, that we’re outsourcing our thinking and growing passive. But then he changes the question. The issue, he says, isn’t how much structure you have; it’s what kind. Some constraints — he calls them governing constraints — fix the outcome. Others — enabling constraints — shape the conditions and make some things more likely without deciding what will happen. Jazz, cooking and martial arts are his examples: years of drilled form are what make improvisation possible.

Then he turns on the loose gathering. Without structure, he says, the existing pecking order takes over: seniority, confidence, familiarity. Groups quickly fall into familiar patterns, and the first confident voice sets the tune. A research group that has met for years can afford to let things drift, but it’s really a club — people who already share a tradition. Mixed groups — hospitals, ministries, communities in conflict — can’t afford that, and the people with least power pay the price. Taking the facilitator away, he says, doesn’t remove power; it “simply leaves it unexamined”.

His alternative is a method called the triopticon. Experts from different fields — “Eagles” — speak in turn. Everyone else listens in silence as “Ravens”, then makes sense of what they heard in groups of three. The threes regroup as “Beavers” to build ideas and methods. The whole room clusters the results, and people take ownership of them. Optional “Coyotes” challenge assumptions; “Magpies” carry ideas between groups. The convener stays out of the content. The roles cut across hierarchy, disagreement is turned into ritual, odd numbers are meant to stop the room splitting in two, and early agreement is designed out. He presents it as one combination of building blocks from a wider toolkit — distribute thinking, break up habitual patterns, ritualise conflict, cut out middlemen, work at the right level of detail, don’t settle too soon — and offers a British hospital’s ritualised team decision-making as a supporting example, though without naming it.

He ends by giving Mowles the point that frustration and slippage are where new thinking happens, and giving Jackson the point that open talk doesn’t give the weakest a fair hearing. His own tradition, he says, can hold both. He suggests a joint test, perhaps in Hull, with Eagles from all three camps. And in a postscript he concedes a good deal: he may be reading a stark either-or into Mowles that Mowles didn’t intend; he is no neutral judge of his own method; it takes time and can disorient people; a loose gathering among people who trust each other can do things a structured one can’t; and choosing no structure is still a choice.

That is Snowden’s case, set out as fairly as we can manage. It is a good case. The question is whether it is the case he thinks it is.

Part OneThe case against Snowden: seven questions

Where we areYou know the central idea and you know the essay. Now the two meet.
What this asksPart One asks, seven times over and from seven angles, whether Snowden’s picture of the baggy room, and of his own method as the remedy, survives the suspicion that he is seeing his own jacket as the world.

Part One puts Snowden’s essay to the test in seven questions. Each follows the same four-step scholastic form, explained in the reading guide at the start: the case for his position, the case against it, the author’s answer, and replies to the original objections. Its verdict is set out in the Ruling that closes the Part.

In brief 18 min read
  • Question I — Mowles’s “baggy” conference is in fact densely constrained, by tradition and shared habit; a club is among the most structured of institutions.
  • Question II — Snowden’s two kinds of constraint (governing and enabling) leave out a third, the constitutive constraint that makes a community what it is. On that view the triopticon is a prosthetic for communities that lack such constitution.
  • Question III — Open rooms examine power reflexively, during and after the event; designed rooms do not remove power but move it upstream into the designer.
  • Question IV — Snowden’s jazz, kitchen and dojo analogies argue for long-incorporated tradition, not for a one-day role-script.
  • Question V — “Neither baggy nor boxed” claims a view from nowhere; trios, animal roles and ritualised conflict carry constraints of their own.
  • Question VI — Goethe’s sonnet, read whole, reconciles nature and art rather than mediating between them, and praises self-limitation.
  • Question VII — Testing the triopticon by running a triopticon is to judge one’s own case.

TakeawaySnowden is right that the kind of structure matters more than its amount, and that “making none is still a choice”. He is wrong to place his own method outside the axis he describes.

Question IIs the “baggy” room really loose — or is its structure just invisible from outside?

Is Mowles’s conference really unstructured — or is its structure just invisible to an outsider?

Someone might say the room really is loose. Mowles himself says almost nothing is fixed except the start and finish; Snowden is just taking him at his word. And surely you can measure structure by the rules on paper: no agenda, no timings, no roles — that’s less structure, by definition. Besides, Snowden is arguing about kinds of structure, so you can hardly accuse him of missing structure.

On the other hand there’s Snowden’s own remark that the gathering is really a club — people who already share a tradition and its assumptions. A club is one of the most tightly bound institutions there is. Its rules aren’t printed in the programme because they’re carried in the members: a shared vocabulary (Elias, Mead, figuration, gesture and response), a set of core texts, unspoken rules about who may interrupt and how to disagree, and years of past conversation that make each new remark make sense. Calling a club “baggy” mistakes missing paperwork for missing rules.

My answer is that “baggy” sounds like a description of the room but is really a description of a relationship — between the rules the observer is used to writing down and a practice that keeps its rules somewhere else. Pierre Bourdieu gives us the mechanism. He called it habitus: lasting dispositions that shape what we do without our ever consciously obeying them. And he called the most taken-for-granted layer doxa: what goes without saying because it comes without saying. When the rules live there, outsiders can’t see them and insiders barely can. You notice them mainly at the edges — the newcomer who doesn’t know when to speak, the visitor unsettled by the long silences, the guest whose comment somehow lands wrong. Mowles, to his credit, reports exactly this kind of inclusion and exclusion. And that report proves the gathering has rules: the rules are what include and exclude.

Snowden half sees this. Calling it a club admits it has a shape. But he treats the club’s shape as a licence for openness — they can afford to be loose — rather than as the thing that actually does the work. That difference matters. If the shared tradition is what makes the open conversation productive, then this isn’t a case of minimal structure getting lucky among friends. It’s a case of maximum structure — deep, settled, absorbed — which is precisely what his own jazz and martial-arts examples celebrate (see Question IV). On its own terms, the baggy room is the most structured room in the essay.

Wittgenstein makes the point sharper. A rule isn’t an object sitting in the room; it shows itself in what people take to be carrying on in the same way. At bedrock, he says, your spade is turned, and all you can say is: this is what we do. If you insist that every rule be stated in advance, a practice that runs on that bedrock agreement will look as though it has no rules. It has plenty. You just can’t read them off a flip chart.

Back to that first objection — that Mowles calls his gathering loose doesn’t settle it. Insiders are poor witnesses to their own taken-for-granted rules, and calling it loose is part of how the tradition understands itself; complex responsive processes prizes the unplanned. And the second — measuring structure by written rules decides the question in favour of the person with the clipboard; it’s James judging Tolstoy. And the third — yes, Snowden distinguishes kinds of structure, but as the next question shows, his list has no comfortable slot for the kind a club has, so the club ends up looking like a case of too little.

Question IIDo two kinds of constraint cover it, or is a third kind missing?

Snowden names two kinds of constraint. Is he missing a third — the kind that makes a community what it is?

Someone might say two kinds are plenty. The distinction is clean, practical, and borrowed from serious philosophy — Alicia Juarrero’s work on constraints in complex systems. It moves the debate from how much structure to what kind, which is a real step forward from Mowles’s simple contrast.

On the other hand Juarrero herself doesn’t stop at two. In Dynamics in Action (1999) and, more fully, Context Changes Everything (2023), the constraints that matter most for anything coherent are the ones that constitute it — the ties through which separate parts come to form a whole with its own identity and its own ability to act. A tradition, a community of practice, a club, a school of thought: these are held together by that kind of constraint.

My answer is that Snowden’s pair — governing and enabling — are both designer’s categories. They answer the question a facilitator asks before the event: shall I fix the outcome, or shape the conditions? Neither answers the question a sociologist asks afterwards: what made this group the group it was? The constitutive kind is missing, and leaving it out isn’t neutral. The baggy gathering’s strength lies almost entirely in that constitutive kind; the triopticon’s strength lies almost entirely in enabling constraints brought in from outside. If your list has only governing and enabling, the baggy room shows up as nothing — neither governed nor enabled — and the triopticon shows up as the ideal. The conclusion is built into the categories.

Put the third kind back and the picture changes. Now we can say: the workshop imposes governing constraints on a group with little constitutive glue (strangers, or colleagues thrown together); the club conference relies on strong constitutive glue and adds almost nothing; the triopticon brings in enabling constraints designed to do, for a mixed group in a day, part of what a tradition does for its members over decades. Read that way, the triopticon isn’t a midpoint between baggy and boxed. It’s a prosthetic for missing glue — valuable where the glue is weak (the hospital, the ministry, the community in conflict), and possibly pointless or even disruptive where it’s strong.

That’s a more modest claim than the essay makes, and a much easier one to defend. Snowden’s own point about scale almost gets him there. But getting there means admitting what his title denies: the “baggy” room isn’t under-structured; it’s structured differently.

Back to that first objection — the distinction is a real advance, and I’m not disputing that. The problem is that, as the essay uses it, it’s incomplete in a way that always tilts toward the author’s method. It gets halfway from quantity to quality, and never gets from designed quality to the constitutive kind.

Question IIIDoes an open room leave power unexamined — and does a designed room really examine it?

Does an open room leave power unexamined, as Snowden says? Or does a designed room simply hide power somewhere else?

Someone might say yes, the open room leaves power unexamined. That’s the classic finding of Jo Freeman’s “The Tyranny of Structurelessness” (written 1970, published 1972): groups that refuse formal structure don’t escape structure; they grow informal elites who are unaccountable precisely because nobody admits they exist. Snowden is simply applying Freeman to facilitation.

On the other hand the tradition Snowden is criticising is the one most explicitly built around examining power. Complex responsive processes comes from Norbert Elias, for whom power is part of every human relationship, and the work of Stacey, Griffin, Shaw and Mowles has made inclusion, exclusion, status and figuration its central subject. It would be odd if this tradition, of all of them, left power unexamined.

My answer is that the two sides examine power at different moments and in different ways, and the essay mistakes a difference in timing for a difference in attention. Snowden’s tradition examines power ex ante (beforehand), by design: roles are handed out to cut across hierarchy, speaking turns are allotted, Ravens are made to stay silent so nobody falls in line too early. Mowles’s tradition examines power in medias res (in the middle of things) and ex post (afterwards), by reflection: the group notices who spoke, who went quiet, whose comment got picked up, and makes that noticing the stuff of the next conversation. That isn’t a lack of examination; it’s examination as the whole point of the event. On its own terms, the baggy room is a laboratory where power is the specimen.

You might decide that reflecting afterwards isn’t enough — Snowden’s point that the least powerful pay the price is serious, and noticing later is cold comfort to someone who was silenced at the time. But the claim that power goes unexamined is simply untrue of this tradition, and the essay’s one quotation from Mowles — about inclusion and exclusion playing out all weekend — refutes it in Mowles’s own words.

And the designed room doesn’t get rid of unexamined power either. It moves it. Freeman’s cure for structurelessness wasn’t choreography; it was accountable structure: tasks delegated by the group, authority shared and rotated, information spread around, structures the group itself can see, argue about and change. The triopticon fails that test in an instructive way. Its roles, order, group sizes and allowed moves are written somewhere else — in a field guide, by the method’s designer — and arrive on the day as given. The convener stays out of the content, but the content of a gathering isn’t just what’s said. It’s also what may be said, by whom, in what order, in groups of what size. Marshall McLuhan’s line that the medium is the message applies in full: the form is content, and its author is fully present in the room even when he’s off the floor. Michel Foucault would call this a dispositif: an arrangement of positions and visibilities that produces behaviour without seeming to give orders. The power hasn’t been examined. It’s been moved upstream, into the design, where nobody can challenge it without stepping out of the game.

Snowden’s own IBM story sharpens the irony. He remembers, with distaste, staff being trained in a corporate method for controlling groups. A branded method with named roles, published components, field guides and a wiki isn’t immune to the same fate. Max Weber described exactly how a founder’s personal craft becomes an institution’s routine. The question isn’t whether the triopticon is the IBM way — it’s plainly much smarter than that — but whether it builds in the means for the people inside it to push back against it.

Back to that first objection — Freeman is the right person to quote, but she proves more than the essay wants. She shows every group is structured. She doesn’t show that designed structure is accountable structure. Read in full, Freeman is as much a warning to the method designer as to the unconference.

Question IVDo jazz, cooking and martial arts really back up the triopticon?

Snowden’s examples — jazz, cooking, martial arts — are meant to support his method. Do they actually support his opponent?

Someone might say they do. In each case, drilled form comes first and makes freedom possible: the jazz player’s years of repertoire, the cook who only leaves the recipe after long practice, the kata in the dojo that makes improvisation in a real fight possible. In the same way, the triopticon’s constraints make fresh thinking possible.

On the other hand in every one of those examples the constraint has been absorbed by the person over years, until it no longer feels like a constraint at all. None of them describes a stranger handed a rule-sheet on the morning of a one-off event.

My answer is that the examples do testify — but for the other side. Think about how a jam session looks to a musician trained only to read orchestral parts: no score, no conductor, players coming in and dropping out on a glance — baggy. Yet the session runs on a huge unwritten grammar of chord changes, standards, trading fours and the nod that brings everyone back to the tune. The freedom is the visible face of an invisible jacket, worn so long it has become skin. The same goes for the cook leaving the recipe and the fighter improvising. Each looks loose to the person who only sees the moment, and each rests on form laid down in the body by repetition.

That describes the club conference, not the triopticon. Mowles’s community, with its shared texts and years of conversation, is the jazz band; its apparent looseness is the freedom that a settled tradition makes possible. The triopticon, by contrast, hands participants a new role-script for one day. Its Ravens haven’t practised silent listening for a decade; its Beavers haven’t rehearsed recombining ideas until it’s second nature. By the logic of Snowden’s own examples, the triopticon is closer to a beginner’s recipe card than to the master leaving the recipe behind — closer, awkwardly, to the very workshop it opposes, however much better designed.

You could rescue the analogy another way: the designer is the master, the event is the performance, and the participants are the instruments. But that rescue concedes Question III’s point — the freedom is the designer’s, and the behaviour is the participants’.

The hospital example doesn’t help much either. Ritualised, role-based team decision-making in clinical settings is a well-established and valuable pattern. But there the roles are practised again and again, built into professional identity, and reinforced by real consequences. They are kata. The fact that this scaffolding works for a surgical team that rehearses it every day tells us little about whether a one-off gathering of strangers in animal roles will think better than a long-standing community talking freely. And the essay names no hospital, no study and no results.

Back to that first objection — getting from “drilled form enables freedom” to “this designed form enables this group’s freedom” needs a missing step: that the form has actually been drilled. Without it, the analogy argues that tradition is the condition for improvisation. That’s Mowles’s case, not Snowden’s.

Question VIs the triopticon truly “neither baggy nor boxed”?

Can any method really stand “between” baggy and boxed — or is “neither” just another position, seen from inside?

Someone might say it is. Like the baggy gathering, it refuses to fix outcomes; like the boxed workshop, it refuses to go without designed constraint. It sits, as the essay says, deliberately between conference and unconference.

On the other hand remember the twin of our idea: to the baggy, the other always seems boxed. Show someone steeped in open, reflective conversation a room where experts are labelled Eagles, listeners are told to keep silent, people are put into threes by rule, regrouped into Beavers by rule, challenged by appointed Coyotes and cross-pollinated by appointed Magpies — and it won’t look halfway. It will look heavily boxed, perhaps more elaborately boxed than the sticky-note workshop, whose rules are at least few and familiar.

My answer is that “neither” is a claim to a view from nowhere, and in this argument there isn’t one to be had. The line running from baggy to boxed isn’t a ruler laid on the world; each observer projects it from inside their own jacket. Aristotle knew this about the “golden mean”: in the Nicomachean Ethics (II.6) the mean is relative to us, not to the thing itself — the right helping for Milo the wrestler is too much for a beginner. A midpoint between baggy and boxed is set by whoever draws the line. Snowden draws it with the workshop at one end, the club at the other and his method in the middle. Mowles would draw it with the club’s reflective conversation as the healthy middle between authoritarian facilitation and chaos. Neither drawing is neutral. The title performs the oldest move in argument — set up two extremes, then stand in the middle — and the postscript half admits it, conceding the either-or may be more Snowden’s than Mowles’s.

Three features of the method make the point sharper.

First, the threes. The essay says odd numbers prevent polarisation. Georg Simmel’s classic analysis of the three-person group in his Soziologie (1908) says something quite different. Going from two to three creates the possibility of coalitions: the tertius gaudens (the third party who profits) who benefits from the other two quarrelling, and divide et impera (divide and rule). Threes don’t dissolve polarisation; they turn two-sided opposition into the isolation of a minority of one. Whether that’s better or worse for the least powerful — the very people the essay is worried about — is a question the essay never asks. It treats the claim as a design rule; Simmel suggests it’s at least debatable.

Second, the animals. Eagles, Ravens, Beavers, Coyotes, Magpies: the cast is charming and easy to remember. But Claude Lévi-Strauss noticed that animals are chosen for totems less because they’re good to eat than because they’re good to think with — they encode a social order in natural kinds. A role system mapped onto species makes a design choice look natural. It presents an authored division of labour as if it were a taxonomy of temperaments. That’s exactly how jackets become invisible to the people wearing them.

Third, ritualised conflict. The anthropologist Victor Turner distinguished structure from “anti-structure”: the in-between phase where everyday roles are suspended and communitas (fellowship beyond roles) springs up. Ritual can host anti-structure — but only by fencing it in. Ritualised opposition is tamed opposition: the Coyote challenges because it’s the Coyote’s turn. Here the borrowed title of Mowles’s post comes into focus. “Staying with the trouble” is Donna Haraway’s phrase, from her 2016 book, and Haraway’s trouble isn’t a scheduled role. It’s the uninvited mess of tangled lives that can’t be designed in or out. A method that decides where trouble may happen isn’t staying with the trouble; it’s staging it. The essay grants that slippage and frustration are where new thinking happens — and then proposes to manufacture slippage on cue.

Finally there’s the Griffin irony. Snowden borrows Doug Griffin’s complaint about the “doubling of process” to attack the workshop. But a method of named roles and phased regroupings is itself a second layer laid on top of conversation — a process about the process, drawing attention to its own machinery. Seen from Mowles’s side, the triopticon is doubling of process in a more refined form.

Back to that first objection — the triopticon really does refuse to fix outcomes, and that’s a real and important difference from the workshop. But refusing one kind of box doesn’t take a method off the line. It just moves it to a different spot on a line whose middle depends on who drew it.

Question VIDoes Goethe’s sonnet say what it is quoted to say?

Snowden quotes Goethe on freedom through limitation. What does the rest of the poem say?

Someone might say it fits perfectly. The final lines are the classic German statement that mastery shows itself in limits and that only law can give freedom:

Wer Großes will, muß sich zusammenraffen;
In der Beschränkung zeigt sich erst der Meister,
Und das Gesetz nur kann uns Freiheit geben.Who would do great things must gather himself; in limitation the master first shows himself, and only law can give us freedom.

On the other hand the sonnet begins with a movement the epigraph leaves out —

Natur und Kunst, sie scheinen sich zu fliehen
Und haben sich, eh man es denkt, gefunden;Nature and art seem to flee each other, and have found each other before one knows it.

My answer is that the poem is about reconciliation, not ranking. Its argument is that nature (the free and unbidden — what James would call the accidental and arbitrary) and art (form, law, limits) only seem to be opposed, and turn out to have found each other already. Read in full, the sonnet undoes the very either-or a “third way” needs: there’s no need for a method to mediate between baggy nature and boxed art, because the opposition was only apparent.

What’s more, the limitation Goethe praises is self-limitation: sich zusammenraffen, to pull oneself together. It’s the discipline the master takes on himself in reaching for something great, not a role-script handed to a participant by a convener. So the poem sides again with the absorbed, constitutive form of the practitioner and the tradition (Questions II and IV), not with the enabling constraints of the designer.

And there’s a joke in the form that Goethe would have enjoyed. The poem is a sonnet — the strait-jacket of European lyric — and Goethe was openly ambivalent about sonnets, which the Romantics around him were reviving with enthusiasm. So the poem praising law was written by a poet who elsewhere chafed against this particular law. Mastery, for Goethe, wasn’t choosing the right box. It was living in a box so freely that its walls disappeared: the jacket become skin.

Back to that first objection — the closing lines do support the idea that constraint and freedom aren’t opposites. They don’t support the idea that a designed in-between method beats a tradition’s absorbed form — and that’s the idea the essay needs.

Question VIICould a joint trial “perhaps in Hull” settle the argument?

Could a test of the triopticon, run by the triopticon, ever settle the argument fairly?

Someone might say it could. The offer is generous, practical, and in the spirit of the small safe-to-fail experiments that Snowden’s tradition recommends. Inviting Eagles from all three camps is real intellectual hospitality.

On the other hand there’s an old legal principle: nemo iudex in causa sua (no one should judge his own case). The proposed trial would test the triopticon by running a triopticon. Its rules, roles and order would all belong to one side.

My answer is that the gesture is admirable and the design is circular. Asking whether open conversation or a triopticon serves thinking better, by holding a triopticon, settles the argument inside one side’s jacket. Picture a practitioner from Mowles’s tradition invited as an Eagle. She’d have to speak in her allotted slot, then fall silent while Ravens made sense of her in threes, then watch Beavers build concepts out of her words — every one of which is a constraint her tradition would dispute. Someone else would get the Coyote role. She wouldn’t be able to do the thing her tradition actually does: let the conversation find its own shape and then reflect on the shape it found. At best the trial would show that the triopticon is good at being a triopticon.

A fair test would need symmetry: a triopticon, and an open reflective gathering, and perhaps a design of the mix-and-match kind Jackson’s critical systems thinking would propose — since his commitment to coherent pluralism suggests he’d see the triopticon as one tool among several, right for some situations, rather than as a reconciliation of traditions. Each would need its own measure of success agreed in advance. And here the real difficulty shows. The traditions don’t agree on what a good gathering produces. Snowden’s values distributed thinking, broken habits and actionable outputs that participants own. Mowles’s values people being changed through conversation and noticing how power works — outcomes you can’t cluster on a wall. Jackson’s values emancipation and the right method for the job. A trial with no agreed measure decides nothing; a trial with one side’s measure decides in that side’s favour.

The essay’s handling of evidence makes this worse. Its claims — that silent listening reduces early bias and widens what people take in, that odd numbers prevent polarisation, that premature agreement is designed out — are stated as design truths. None is backed by data in the essay. The field guides it cites describe how the method is used, not what it achieves, and the trial is still in the future. For a tradition that rightly prefers evidence to consultant folklore, and small experiments to grand design, that’s a striking imbalance: the open room is held to account for what it might do to the weakest, while the triopticon’s benefits are simply asserted. The postscript’s honesty — that Snowden is no neutral judge of his own method — is real and to his credit. It should have been the starting point of the essay, not its afterthought.

One last small thing. Hull is the historic home of Jackson’s school of critical systems thinking. Suggesting the trial there is courteous, a bit like offering to play the away match. It’s also a reminder that every venue is a jacket too.

Back to that first objection — the spirit of the offer deserves full credit, and I’m not discouraging it. What I dispute is the idea that a trial set up this way could settle anything beyond whether the method hangs together on its own terms.

The RulingWhat stands, what falls, and the audit of jackets

Where we areSeven questions have been put to Snowden. It is time to add up the score.
What this asksThe Ruling asks what survives the cross-examination and what does not — and proposes, for the first time, something better than either room.
In brief 4 min read
  • Stands: kind over quantity; the critique of sticky-dot workshops; power in open rooms is real; design earns its keep where trust is low.
  • Falls: the architecture of “neither”. If choosing no structure is a choice, then the “baggy” room is a design, not a void.
  • Proposed: an audit of jackets, in which every gathering declares its governing, enabling and constitutive constraints.

What stands

A lot of Snowden’s essay is right, and right in ways that matter. Shifting the question from how much structure to what kind is a genuine step forward from the conference-versus-workshop contrast. His criticism of the sticky-note workshop — rushed agreement, outcomes fixed in advance while pretending to be participation — is sound, and plenty of people share it. His warning that taking the facilitator away doesn’t abolish power is Freeman’s lasting insight, correctly applied. His insistence that mixed, high-stakes, low-trust settings can’t rely on the habits of a club is practical wisdom: hospitals, ministries and divided communities are exactly where design earns its keep. The postscript is unusually honest for someone who owns a method, and its last concession — that choosing no constraints is still a choice — is the most important sentence in the essay. Follow it through, though, and it undoes the title.

What falls

What falls is the whole architecture of “neither”. If choosing no structure is a choice, then the baggy room is a design, not a void; its rules are real even though they aren’t written down; and calling it baggy tells you about the observer’s jacket, not about the room. The essay’s categories leave out the constitutive kind of constraint where the club’s form actually lives (Question II). Its account of power mistakes reflecting-in-the-moment for not paying attention, and moves the designer’s power upstream instead of examining it (Question III). Its examples argue for long-settled tradition, not a one-day role-script (Question IV). Its claim to the middle is a claim to a view from nowhere, open to the twin charge that to the baggy the other looks boxed (Question V). Its epigraph, read whole, dissolves the either-or it was recruited to mediate (Question VI). And its proposed trial would be judged inside one side’s form (Question VII).

The central idea, restated

To the strait-jacketed, the other always seems baggy — and Snowden’s essay, for all its cleverness, turns out to be an elegant example of the rule rather than an exception to it. His jacket is beautifully cut: it doesn’t fix outcomes, it gives disagreement a ritual of its own, it shares the talking more fairly than a sticky-dot workshop ever could. But it’s still a jacket, made to a designer’s pattern, and from inside it the club’s old skin of tradition, worn soft by years of use, doesn’t look like clothing at all. The right conclusion isn’t that the triopticon is bad (it may be excellent where a group has little glue of its own), nor that the baggy room is innocent (Freeman still stands). It’s that you can’t frame this dispute on a single line from less to more structure without one side’s rules becoming the measure of everything.

The jacket audit

So what would a fairer practice look like? It would ask every gathering — baggy, boxed or in between — to declare its rules in three registers. Governing: what outcomes are fixed? Enabling: what conditions have been shaped, and by whom? Constitutive: what shared tradition, vocabulary, texts or habits does this room rely on — and who doesn’t have them? The open conference would have to name its unspoken canon, the thing that makes a newcomer’s remark land wrong. The triopticon would have to name its author — the designer present in every role and turn. And both would have to give participants the means, in Freeman’s sense, to see, argue about and change the jacket while wearing it. That’s harder than either loose freedom or designed scaffolding, and it belongs to neither tradition alone.

A closing word in three registers: 道 · 理 · 勢

道 Dào, the Way. No gathering is without a way. The baggy room walks a path so worn it no longer looks like a path; the triopticon cuts a new one and puts up signs at every fork. Both are ways. The mistake is calling the unsigned path wilderness.

理 Lǐ, pattern. The old Chinese dictionary, the Shuowen, explains the character as the working of jade: the carver follows the veins already in the stone. Lǐ is constraint you discover in the grain, not one you stamp on with a template. To a craftsman with a template, the carver who follows the veins seems to have no plan at all. That’s our whole idea in one character. A method worthy of the word “complexity” would read the grain of a group before laying a pattern on it — and would sometimes find that the grain already holds the shape.

勢 Shì, propensity. François Jullien’s reading of shì as effectiveness that comes from the configuration of a situation, not from an imposed plan, is closer to Snowden’s enabling constraints than anything in the Western books he cites. But shì belongs to the situation, not the method. The general who masters it doesn’t bring a formation with him; he sees which way things are already leaning. The baggy room and the boxed room each have their lean. The art is reading it — and that art can’t be bottled in a field guide.

Bagginess is just the name a jacket gives to someone else’s skin. Whoever calls a room shapeless is mostly telling you the shape of his own form. And the master, in Goethe’s sense, isn’t the one who has escaped limits, but the one who has worn them so long and so freely that they’ve become the body that wears them.

Snowden has had his hearing on structure. But a method is not only a structure; it also tells a story through the names it gives its parts. That story comes next.

Part TwoThe borrowed bestiary: (post-)shamanic and (post-)Jungian symbology in the search for relevance

Where we arePart One weighed the triopticon as a structure. But it also has a cast of characters with names: Eagles, Ravens, Beavers, Coyotes, Magpies.
What this asksPart Two asks what those names are doing. Are they a harmless memory aid, or are they borrowing something — and from whom?
In brief 8 min read
  • Animal role-names are not neutral memory aids: they make an authored division of labour feel like a natural taxonomy (Lévi-Strauss).
  • Raven and Coyote come from specific Indigenous North American traditions, and reach management through Eliade, Harner and Jung.
  • An appointed trickster is a licensed jester: a “ritual of rebellion” that protects the frame it seems to challenge (Gluckman).
  • Either the symbols are decoration, which means borrowed aura, or they are meaningful, which means an unexamined metaphysics. Either way, symbolic power is power.

TakeawayA method that indicts unexamined power must examine the power of its own symbols.

Part One looked at the triopticon’s roles as constraints. This Part looks at them as symbols. The method doesn’t number its roles, or give them plain job titles like speaker, listener, builder, challenger and carrier. It names them after animals — Eagle, Raven, Beaver, Coyote, Magpie — and two of those, Raven and Coyote, are among the most loaded figures in the world’s stories: the tricksters of Indigenous North American tradition and, thanks to Paul Radin and Carl Jung, the trickster “archetype” of Western psychology. Intended or not, that choice puts the method in a familiar corner of modern organisational practice: the post-shamanic, post-Jungian language of circles, councils, talking pieces, totems, archetypal roles and “ancient wisdom” that facilitation uses to give technique a bit of enchantment. So what does that clothing claim, what does it borrow, and what does it cost?

First, in fairness: Snowden has often been a sharp and funny critic of New Age management — of colour-coded development schemes, guru-led “presencing”, and facilitation that swaps atmosphere for thinking — and his methods claim roots in cognitive science and anthropology, not mysticism. That’s exactly why the animal names deserve a closer look. They’re the point where a self-declared scientific method borrows the aura of the very style it usually mocks.

A symbol nobody questions runs a room as firmly as a senior voice nobody interrupts — with the added advantage that nobody thinks to call it power.

Question VIIIAre the animal names just a memory aid — or something more?

Are the animal names just a handy memory aid, or do they carry meaning — and borrow it from someone?

Someone might say they’re just a memory aid. Animal names are vivid, easy to remember and playful, and they take the starch out of a room full of experts. Nobody is asked to believe in Raven just because they’ve been told to sit quietly and listen like one.

On the other hand if the names were only there to help memory, any lively set would do — colours, chess pieces, planets, kitchen utensils. The set actually chosen isn’t random. It brings together a sky-seer (Eagle), a trickster who knows things (Raven), a maker (Beaver), a trickster who breaks things (Coyote) and a thieving messenger (Magpie): a little pantheon of functions taken from the symbolic toolbox that Western neo-shamanism and Jungian psychology have spent a century labelling “universal”.

My answer is that a name is never only a label. It carries a field of meaning, and that field does work. Lévi-Strauss’s argument in Totemism (1962) settles it: animals are chosen for totems not because they’re useful but because they’re good to think with. The differences between species are used to encode, and make natural, the differences between social groups. A role system mapped onto animals does exactly that. It turns an authored division of labour — who speaks, who must keep quiet, who builds, who disrupts — into something that feels like a classification of natures. The Raven isn’t told “you’re not allowed to talk now”; she’s told she’s a Raven, and Ravens listen. The rule disappears into the totem.

Second, these symbols aren’t ownerless. Raven is a culture-hero and trickster in the stories of the Pacific Northwest Coast — Tlingit, Haida, Tsimshian, Kwakwaka’wakw — woven into clans, crests, and rules about who may tell which story and show which image. Coyote is the trickster of many Plains, Great Basin, Plateau and Californian traditions, again within particular ceremonial and seasonal rules (in some, Coyote stories are only told in winter). In those traditions the figures aren’t free-floating archetypes; they’re kin, owned stories and law. Their route into management method runs through a particular Western path: Mircea Eliade’s invention of “shamanism” as a universal ancient technique (1951), Michael Harner’s “core shamanism” (1980), which deliberately stripped practice from culture to make it portable, and Jung’s reading of tricksters as expressions of a collective unconscious shared by everyone. Ronald Hutton’s Shamans (2001) and Alice Kehoe’s Shamans and Religion (2000) show how much of that universal category is a Western creation. Philip Deloria’s Playing Indian (1998) traces the long Euro-American habit of borrowing Indigenous figures to soothe settler worries about authenticity and belonging.

So the animal names aren’t neutral decoration. They are “post-shamanic” in the strict sense: they depend on the earlier Western extraction of shamanic figures from their peoples, and they inherit that extraction’s convenience — the figure without the obligations. A method that warns so carefully against unexamined power in the room hasn’t examined where its own symbols came from.

Back to that first objection — nobody being asked to believe in Raven doesn’t make the name innocent. Symbols shape behaviour without needing belief; that’s what Bourdieu meant by symbolic power — power that works because people don’t recognise it as power. If the names were really empty, renaming the roles “Speaker, Listener, Builder, Challenger, Carrier” would cost nothing. I suspect the method’s practitioners would resist that renaming. The resistance tells you what the names are worth.

Question IXCan you appoint a trickster?

Can you appoint someone to be the trickster, or does the appointment destroy the very thing you wanted?

Someone might say of course you can. Institutions have licensed dissent for centuries: the devil’s advocate (the advocatus diaboli of the old process for making saints), the red team, the court jester. A Coyote role simply gives the job of challenging a name and a permission slip.

On the other hand in every tradition the figure comes from — and in Jung’s own account — the trickster is defined by not being licensed. He turns up uninvited, breaks the boundary, mocks the order, and is as likely to undo the convener as the participants.

My answer is that an appointed trickster is a contradiction, and an instructive one. Jung’s essay “On the Psychology of the Trickster Figure” (1954, published with Radin’s study of the Winnebago trickster stories) reads the figure as the eruption of what consciousness has pushed away — the shadow coming back, out of control. Lewis Hyde’s Trickster Makes This World (1998) reads him as the boundary-crosser whose disruptions create culture precisely because no existing order allows them. A trickster scheduled to challenge assumptions in phase three, in a group of a set size, isn’t Jung’s or Hyde’s or Coyote’s. He’s the jester: licensed, fenced in, and therefore safe.

Anthropology has a precise name for this. Max Gluckman’s Rituals of Rebellion in South-East Africa (1954) showed that permitted inversions — moments when subjects may insult the king — tend to strengthen the established order by giving dissent a bounded outlet. Some of Bakhtin’s sterner critics read his carnival the same way: permitted misrule as a safety valve. The triopticon’s ritualised opposition and its appointed Coyote belong to this family. They make challenge a role — which relieves everyone else of the duty to challenge, and removes from anyone the possibility of a challenge that would overturn the frame itself. The one assumption the Coyote can never question is that there should be a Coyote.

The post-Jungian tradition sees this clearly. James Hillman’s archetypal psychology insisted that images have a life of their own — you don’t use an archetype; it uses you — and that trying to harness one for the ego’s purposes is the typical modern arrogance. Andrew Samuels, in The Plural Psyche (1989), warned against archetypal language hardening into a fixed typology that assigns people to roles. Either way, handing out archetypal roles to make a workshop productive isn’t post-Jungian depth. It flattens the very thing depth psychology tried to protect.

Back to that first objection — the devil’s advocate and the red team are honest about what they are: functional jobs, named as such. The problem isn’t licensed challenge, which is often valuable. It’s dressing licensed challenge in the costume of the unlicensed — borrowing the trickster’s aura of real disruption for a job whose whole design is to keep disruption in its place.

Question XDo sacred symbols give a method relevance, or only borrow it?

Do sacred and archetypal symbols give a method real depth — or only the appearance of it?

Someone might say they give real depth. Archetypal symbols speak to deep layers of the mind; Jung argued they turn up across cultures because they express the structure of the psyche. A method that uses them is more resonant, more memorable and more human than one that talks in dry functional terms.

On the other hand the universality of archetypes is the most disputed claim in Jung’s whole system. In its strong form you can’t test it. In its weak form — certain images recur — it explains nothing that shared history, shared environments and the way stories travel can’t explain without a collective unconscious.

My answer is that in the market for methods, archetypal and shamanic symbols work less as insight than as branding. Eric Abrahamson’s work on management fashion (1996) shows how techniques compete for attention by promising rationality and progress. There’s a second strategy too, after Max Weber’s diagnosis of a “disenchanted” modern world: promise re-enchantment — meaning, depth and ancient resonance in organisations that have been drained of them. Eric Hobsbawm and Terence Ranger’s idea of “invented tradition” names the move: new practices gain authority by claiming continuity with a deep past. A facilitation method invented in the last couple of decades gains, through its animals, the borrowed patina of the totem pole and the medicine story.

For Snowden’s tradition in particular this creates a tension the essay doesn’t admit. It presents itself as scientific: it appeals to cognitive science, to the evolutionary roots of storytelling, to measurable distributed thinking, and it has been harsh on approaches it considers pseudo-science. So either the animals are decoration — in which case the method is borrowing aura it isn’t entitled to, and should drop it, as the tradition would demand of anyone else — or they mean something, in which case the method is importing, unexamined, a whole symbolic metaphysics (totemic, archetypal, shamanic) that its own philosophy would reject. There’s no third option in which the symbols do work without making a claim.

And here the argument of Part One comes back hard. The essay’s main charge against the baggy room is that it leaves power unexamined. Symbolic power is power. A room whose roles are named after tricksters and sky-seers is shaped by those names — by the expectations they carry about who sees from above (Eagle, the expert), who must watch in silence (Raven), who disrupts and who builds. To leave that unexamined while condemning the club for its unexamined seniority is to commit, with symbols, the very fault charged against people.

Finally, relevance. Reaching for enchanted symbols suggests an anxiety: that the technique, stated plainly, wouldn’t hold attention. But the strongest claim the triopticon can make — that a sequence of expert input, quiet processing and small-group regrouping can widen what a mixed group considers — is a plain claim, testable in plain language. It doesn’t need a Raven. Stated plainly it would be more honest, and for the sceptical audiences who matter most — clinicians, civil servants, communities in conflict, the very people the essay invokes — probably more persuasive. Relevance borrowed from someone else’s sacred stories isn’t relevance earned.

Back to that first objection — resonance is real, and engaging language has value. But resonance drawn from someone else’s sacred repertoire, stripped of its roots, comes with debts — to the peoples whose figures they are, and to the truth about what the method actually is. A complexity method, of all things, should be first to notice that symbols, like constraints, have a history, and that the history is part of the meaning.

The bestiary turns out to carry more than it says. And it prepares the ground for Snowden’s main charge against the open room, which we can now examine line by line.

Part ThreeThe refutation: the passage on power, entrainment and scale, taken clause by clause

Where we areWe have examined Snowden’s method and its symbols. Now we turn to his attack.
What this asksPart Three takes the passage in which he indicts the open room — on power, entrainment and scale — and asks, claim by claim, whether it hits its target or rebounds on the method he defends.
In brief 7 min read
  • Six claims are tested. Two of them (B and D) are true of every room and so discriminate nothing.
  • Two (A and C) tell against the triopticon: putting experts first formalises recognised power and engineers an anchor.
  • Claim E rests on an output metric and a reversible “club” charge; the Macy Conferences and Bohm’s dialogue complicate it.
  • Claim F, once its slip is corrected, names the out-group homogeneity effect: the outsider’s bias, which undermines the club charge itself.

This is the passage where Snowden rolls up his sleeves and goes after the open room, in three short moves: power, entrainment and scale. I’ve set out each claim below in my own words, lettered A to F, and answered each in turn. I’m not denying that power, entrainment and scale are real. I’m denying that they separate the open room from the designed one in the way the passage needs — and at several points, by the passage’s own standards, the designed room comes off worse.

Claim A — In the absence of structure, recognised power structures the room: seniority, confidence, familiarity and the regulars’ habits decide who speaks, who is heard, and which ideas survive.

My reply. First, “nothing is structured” describes no real room. As Part One argued, the gathering in question is structured by a shared tradition, so the “if” of the claim is never met, and the claim proves nothing about real gatherings.

Second, design doesn’t avoid the “then”. Status characteristics theory (Joseph Berger, Bernard Cohen and Morris Zelditch, from the 1970s on) shows that background status cues — seniority, profession, gender, accent, perceived expertise — shape whose contributions get asked for, credited and adopted whatever formal roles people have, unless the structure actively works against them. Formal roles don’t dissolve those expectations; people read the roles through them.

Third, and this is decisive: the triopticon doesn’t just fail to neutralise recognised power; it formalises it. Its opening phase gives the floor to designated experts from established disciplines while everyone else listens in enforced silence. That’s the lecture — the oldest structure of recognised authority in the academy. Seniority and expertise, which the passage complains decide who speaks informally, here decide it by rule. The method doesn’t examine recognised power. It appoints it and calls it Eagle.

Claim B — A facilitator’s absence no more removes power than a facilitator’s claim to neutrality does; it leaves power unexamined.

My reply. The symmetry is right, and it cuts against the method. If a facilitator’s neutrality is a fiction, so is the claim that the convener “stays out of the content”. The convener — and behind the convener, the designer — is in the form, and the form is content (Question III). So the passage states the very principle that convicts its own method: a designer’s self-effacement is a claim to neutrality, and like any such claim it leaves the designer’s power unexamined. As for the open room, Part One showed that its tradition examines power by reflecting during and after the event. “Unexamined” isn’t true of it, and the essay’s own report of Mowles’s attention to inclusion and exclusion confirms that.

Claim C — Groups entrain quickly on familiar patterns, and the first confident voice shapes what follows.

My reply. True in general; useless for telling the two rooms apart. The best-known evidence — Amos Tversky and Daniel Kahneman on anchoring (1974), Solomon Asch on conformity, and the brainstorming research summarised by Michael Diehl and Wolfgang Stroebe (1987) on “production blocking” — points to a specific remedy: think independently before hearing anyone else (the logic behind the nominal group technique and the Delphi method). The triopticon does the reverse. It exposes everyone first to a string of confident, credentialed voices, then imposes silence while the group absorbs them, then asks threes to make sense of what they’ve just heard. That isn’t breaking up entrainment; it’s engineering an anchor. The first confident voices are chosen in advance and given the floor by rule. Having a variety of Eagles widens the range of anchors, which is a real benefit, but it doesn’t escape the anchoring. It multiplies it.

Claim D — A conversation free of structure is not free of pattern; it is governed by the patterns people brought with them.

My reply. True of every conversation, so it proves nothing against any particular one. People carry their patterns into threes and Beaver groups just as surely as into an open plenary; roles don’t strip away habits at the door. In fact this sentence is the most important concession in the passage, because it admits that the patterns people bring — tradition, habit, constitutive constraint — govern rooms. That’s this book’s thesis: the “unstructured” room is structured by what its members carry in. The real question is whether those patterns are generative or deadening. For a long-standing community with a shared, reflective tradition, the essay’s own jazz example (Question IV) says they may be exactly what makes improvisation possible.

Claim E — A research group’s weekend, among people who know and trust each other, can afford to let things go nowhere; it is closer to a club; open conversation among the like-minded differs from bringing together disciplines that disagree.

My reply. There are three things to say back. The first is that “going nowhere” assumes an output measure — clusters, owners, actions — which is one tradition’s idea of value, not a neutral one (Question VII). For a tradition that locates the value of conversation in how it changes participants’ thinking and identity, “nowhere” isn’t where such a weekend goes.

Second, the “club” charge is a tu quoque (“you too”: the charge turned back on its maker) waiting to happen. Every school of practice is a club in this sense, Snowden’s included, with its own language (Cynefin domains, enabling constraints, safe-to-fail probes), its own texts and its own regulars. Sharing a tradition isn’t a vice; it’s the condition of any inquiry that builds over time.

Third, it simply isn’t true that open conversation between disagreeing disciplines must fail. The Macy Conferences on cybernetics (1946–53) brought mathematicians, neurophysiologists, anthropologists and psychiatrists together in loosely structured discussion and helped found a whole field — while also, as their historians note, showing exactly the status dynamics the passage describes. David Bohm’s dialogue practice was built for sustained disagreement with very little procedure, relying instead on people suspending their assumptions. Both cases point to a mixed verdict: open conversation across difference can be creative and riddled with status. That’s precisely the verdict the passage’s either-or stops it from reaching.

Claim F — Groups that, from outside, seem homogeneous in their assumptions are perceived within the group as significantly homogeneous.

My reply. As written in the passage you quoted, this sentence says that groups that look uniform from outside look uniform from inside. There’s no contrast there, so nothing “curious” about it. The context strongly suggests the intended word was heterogeneous: groups that look uniform to outsiders look varied to their own members. If so, the sentence names a well-documented effect — the out-group homogeneity effect (George Quattrone and Edward Jones, 1980; Patricia Linville and colleagues, 1989). We see other groups as more alike than our own, because we know our own group’s internal differences and don’t know theirs.

And there the passage refutes itself. The out-group homogeneity effect is a bias of the outside observer. It predicts that Snowden, looking at Mowles’s tradition from outside, will see a uniform club, while its members see real internal disagreement. The effect doesn’t show the club is uniform; it shows outsiders will think so whether it is or not. Properly understood, the passage’s “curious” observation is the evidence-based version of this book’s central idea: to the strait-jacketed, the other always seems baggy — and, we can now add, to the outsider, the other always seems of one mind. By citing the effect, the essay hands us the very reason to distrust its description of the club.

The six claims at a glance
ClaimIn shortVerdict
AWithout structure, recognised power decides who speaksPremise never met; status persists under roles; the triopticon formalises expertise
BNo facilitator, like a “neutral” one, leaves power unexaminedTrue, and it indicts the designer’s own claim to neutrality
CGroups entrain on the first confident voiceTrue in general; the triopticon puts confident experts first, which engineers the anchor
DUnstructured talk follows the patterns people bringTrue of every room, so it discriminates nothing; it concedes the role of constitutive form
EA club of the like-minded can afford to go nowherePresupposes an output metric; the charge applies to every school; history offers counter-cases
FOutsiders see a group as homogeneousOnce its slip is corrected, this is the out-group homogeneity effect: the observer’s bias

What the refutation concludes

Put together, the six claims don’t show that the open gathering is especially exposed to power, entrainment and narrowness. Claims B and D are true of every room, so they don’t separate anything. Claims A and C, checked against the triopticon’s own design, count more against the method than against the club: it formalises recognised expertise and engineers an anchor. Claim E rests on a disputed measure and a charge that cuts both ways, and history offers counter-examples. Claim F, once its slip is fixed, names the very bias that undermines the outsider’s picture of the club. What survives is a modest, true proposition: mixed, low-trust, high-stakes groups benefit from carefully designed enabling constraints that are visible, open to challenge, and accountable to the people living under them. That’s the jacket audit proposed in the Ruling — and it belongs to neither side alone.

What the outsider calls a club, the member knows as an argument that has run for thirty years. What the designer calls scaffolding may feel like bars to the person inside it. And since every room wears some kind of jacket, the most honest thing a room can do is say who stitched it.

Snowden’s charge rebounds more often than it lands. But we have still heard Mowles only through Snowden’s mouth. Time to let him speak.

Part FourThe trouble with staying: an interrogation of Chris Mowles’s “Staying with the Trouble”

Where we areSo far Mowles has appeared only as Snowden described him. That is not fair to either man.
What this asksPart Four reads Mowles’s own essay closely and asks the same question it asked of Snowden: does he see the form of the room he loves, or only the form of the room he dislikes?
In brief 21 min read
  • Mowles’s chain from chatbot to workshop to Jackson convicts by family likeness. The real danger, frictionlessness, points at the like-minded club.
  • His conference is a liturgy, resting on the rigorous frame of group analysis, which the essay never declares.
  • Therapy, his best example, is the most tightly framed encounter there is: a crack needs a vessel.
  • Rosa, Han and Jaeggi, read in full, support the idea of enabling conditions rather than a rejection of design.
  • Jackson’s question about who gets heard goes unanswered, and “going nowhere” is a privilege, Bourdieu’s skholè (leisure from necessity).
  • He felt the trouble in the workshop and complied. His title is Haraway’s, and Haraway rejects both the jeremiad and the romance of a world without machines.

TakeawayMowles’s enemy is not structure but alien structure: a form that has not yet become his skin.

Snowden’s essay was a reply, and after three Parts spent weighing the reply, it’s only fair to hear the other side. Chris Mowles — Professor of Complexity and Management at Hertfordshire, director of its Doctor of Management programme, associate member of the Institute for Group Analysis — published “Staying with the trouble”, subtitled On working with uncertainty, on his Substack On complexity on 26 October 2026. It’s a reflective essay, not a study: personal observation woven with other people’s ideas, written in a humane, slightly melancholy key, and there’s nothing wrong with that. But an essay that blames structure for killing the living encounter has to be read for its own structure. And an essay that champions uncertainty can fairly be asked what it’s certain of.

The essay, fairly stated

Mowles starts with Sherry Turkle’s Artificial Intimacy (2026). AI, Turkle argues, doesn’t just learn from us; it trains us, making us more passive, more dependent and more averse to real experience. A machine that never argues back is easier than a flawed human being, and that ease shuts out surprise. Against this Mowles sets Hartmut Rosa’s idea of resonance — the mutual ability to affect and be affected. Turkle describes a shift from an attention economy to an attachment economy, in which people confide their inner lives to bots. Mowles calls the bot a thin respondent with no hinterland, with nothing at stake in its advice, serving up a probabilistic remix of scraped text.

Turkle tells of Eric Schmidt’s view that AI will put therapists out of business, since psychological knowledge is now on tap. Mowles replies that this confuses knowledge with wisdom and practical judgement, and misunderstands therapy, which depends on an alliance with a feeling human being who wants the client’s good — an alliance whose messiness is where, in a famous phrase he borrows from Leonard Cohen, the light gets in. He admits some therapists are poor or badly motivated.

He then sets AI inside a bigger picture: individualism, competition and treating everything as a means to an end (Rosa, and Byung-Chul Han’s Burnout Society); the speeding-up of the collapse Robert Putnam described in Bowling Alone; a steep rise in loneliness, isolation and anxiety, especially among the young (citing a House of Lords Library briefing); and, through Hannah Arendt, the link between mass loneliness and the conditions for totalitarianism. We are, he concludes, destroying the space for shared experience. His own university is the local example: colleagues and students avoiding face-to-face contact, humanities departments closing, the particular playing out the general. Where, he asks, can the spontaneous, the new and the eccentric still live?

Then come the two events. His research group’s annual conference always has the same shape — supper on Friday, a keynote on Saturday, then open conversation, fixed start and finish, presentations kept to a minimum. It’s baggy, sometimes full of conflict, marked by inclusion and exclusion, and yet often the highlight of people’s year, not least because the group can talk about how it’s talking. He recalls his late colleague Doug Griffin’s phrase, the “doubling of process” — feeling you need a process in order to have a process. The facilitated strategy workshop, by contrast, was tightly timed and constantly rearranged; flip-chart outputs were read aloud and applauded; votes were cast with sticky labels. He felt pushed from pillar to post, met nobody properly, reflected on nothing, and went along with it like a drone. He insists the lesson isn’t “conference good, workshop bad” — each has its uses. What he objects to is the assumption that meaningful discussion needs total structure, which squeezes out uncertainty and the ability to affect and be affected.

Last comes Jackson. In Critical Systems Thinking and the Management of Complexity (2019), Jackson compares Stacey’s complex responsive processes unfavourably with soft systems thinking, frustrated that Stacey puts conversation ahead of structured method, and asks whether it serves critical systems thinking’s commitment to democratic participation and protecting disadvantaged stakeholders. As Mowles reads him, Jackson prefers more structure because badly chaired meetings go nowhere, and treats open, shared sense-making as one of those meetings. Mowles replies in a footnote that badly chaired meetings are actually the ones that race through the agenda with no time to think. In the main text he argues that going nowhere can be the point: it gives you material to notice.
