If our problems are “second-order” — political and social rather than technical, as Rahel Jaeggi argues — then how a group handles its dilemmas is itself the lesson. What’s needed is a change in how we approach things, not more methods that pre-shape conversation. He ends by asking whether our tools for avoiding uncertainty, from AI to frameworks, are shrinking our ability to become ourselves, as they try to “cabin, crib and confine” what matters most in experience.

Second Interlude · On the Mourning of Rooms

Every lament for the open room is written in a closed form. Mowles mourns the loss of spontaneity in tidy paragraphs, with numbered footnotes, in an order he chose — and he could hardly do otherwise, because a lament with no form wouldn’t get read, and an unread lament comforts nobody.

That isn’t hypocrisy, and I don’t mean it as a dig. It’s the condition under which any of us talks about freedom at all: we can only describe the unbounded in a form that holds still long enough to be understood. In miniature, that’s the whole argument of this book.

Question XIDoes the chain from chatbot to workshop to Jackson hold?

Mowles links chatbots, workshops and Jackson in one chain. Does each link actually hold?

Someone might say it holds. The essay finds one tendency — the flight from uncertainty — at three scales: the person confiding in a machine, the group processed by a facilitator, the discipline that prefers structured method. Seeing one pattern across scales is exactly what complexity thinking is good at; the essay itself says the particular plays out in the general.

On the other hand a pattern across scales is only as strong as the resemblance at each link, and here the links between chatbot and workshop, and between workshop and Jackson, are held together by nothing stronger than analogy — the kind of argument that gets a boy kept in after school because his older brother was trouble.

My answer is that the argument works like a chain of contagion. The first link is strong: Turkle’s chatbot really is a respondent with no body, no stake and no hinterland. The second link passes that charge to a room full of real people with stakes and histories, held back by a bad design. The third link passes it on again, to a scholar who argued — on behalf of the disadvantaged — for some structure in deliberation. At each step the moral colour of the first link, the hollow flattering machine, leaks forward. By the end, structure itself is faintly accused of artificial intimacy, and Jackson, whose concern is the voice of the weak, finds himself lined up with Eric Schmidt.

Snowden saw this and said so: framed like this, anyone who likes structure ends up on the drone’s side. Mowles disclaims the either-or and then enacts it, because the essay doesn’t keep both columns of the ledger. The conference is described from inside, with affection, as the highlight of the year; the workshop is described from inside, with distaste, as an ordeal. One example of each — the best of the open kind against nearly the worst of the structured kind. A sticky-dot workshop is to structure what a pub argument is to conversation. A fair comparison would put the best open gathering beside the best designed one — a well-run citizens’ assembly, a Quaker business meeting, a hospital’s morbidity and mortality review, a well-held group-analytic session. The essay doesn’t, and its win depends on not doing so.

There’s a deeper mix-up at the first link. Turkle’s complaint about the bot isn’t that it’s structured; it’s that it has no friction. It never pushes back, never wants anything, never refuses. By that standard the danger isn’t structure but the absence of resistance — and the room most at risk of no friction isn’t the workshop, whose rules chafe, as Mowles testifies. It’s the gathering of the like-minded, where tradition supplies agreement before anyone opens their mouth. The club is the human version of the flattering machine. It too can become an attachment economy: you go back every year for the warmth of being understood. Held firmly, the essay’s own first link points at its own beloved room.

Back to that first objection — seeing patterns across scales is complexity thinking’s gift; mistaking resemblance for identity is its besetting vice. The particular only plays out in the general where the mechanism is the same. Here it differs at every link — a machine with no friction, a manager’s over-design, a methodological argument about fairness — and what’s really shared is the author’s mood.

Question XIIIs the conference baggy, even by Mowles’s own description?

Read Mowles’s own description slowly. Is his “baggy” conference baggy at all?

Someone might say it is: almost nothing is fixed except start and finish, presentations are minimal, and the conversation goes where it goes.

On the other hand read his own description slowly — supper on Friday, keynote on Saturday, open conversation after, fixed times at either end, and the same order faithfully repeated year after year. Whatever that is, it isn’t the absence of form. It’s a liturgy — as regular as morning assembly, and every bit as binding.

My answer is that the conference, as Mowles describes it, is a ritual calendar of remarkable stability: a shared meal that opens the rite, a single authorised voice that sets the theme (the keynote is the sermon), then a long stretch of open exchange within fixed bounds of time and membership, repeated every year. Anthropologists would recognise the shape at once: eating together, proclamation, assembly. Its rules are few but heavy, and they’re constitutive (Question II) — they make the community what it is.

Behind the liturgy stands a lineage the essay never names but relies on everywhere. Mowles is an associate member of the Institute for Group Analysis, and the gathering’s prized ability — to talk about how it’s talking — is group analysis’s signature skill. Yet S. H. Foulkes’s group analysis is one of the most carefully structured kinds of human gathering there is: a conductor, fixed membership, fixed time and place, a circle, norms about free-floating discussion, and what Foulkes called dynamic administration — the conductor’s constant tending of the boundaries. The famous freedom of the group-analytic “matrix” is bought with a rigorous frame. The conference inherits the freedom and quietly inherits the frame too, but only tells itself the story of the freedom.

Here the twin idea bites. Mowles, shaped by decades of group-analytic and complex-responsive practice, sees his own clothes as bagginess and the facilitator’s as a straitjacket. But talking about how you talk isn’t the absence of process. It’s a process about the process, governed by unspoken norms about what counts as noticing, who may interpret whom, and when an interpretation lands. Griffin’s “doubling of process” — needing a process in order to have a process — comes back on the conference that quotes it. A group that has to talk about how it talks in order to talk well has also built a process on top of its process. The doubling hasn’t gone away; it’s been absorbed and renamed reflexivity.

The very lineage the essay quietly relies on also supplies the strongest support for Snowden’s point about entrainment — support the essay never faces. W. R. Bion’s Experiences in Groups (1961) distinguished the work group, focused on its task, from the basic-assumption group, which slides into shared unconscious postures: depending on a leader, fight-or-flight, or “pairing” in hope of a coming saviour. Bion found that relatively unstructured groups slide into basic-assumption mode readily; in his terms, the first confident voice is the one the group appoints to carry its dependency. Isabel Menzies Lyth’s study of nursing (1960) adds the other side: organisational structures often work as social defences against anxiety, protecting people from the raw demands of their work at the cost of its meaning. Menzies Lyth arms Mowles against the workshop — its timetable and applause may be a defence against the anxiety of not knowing. But she arms Snowden against the conference too: a cherished annual ritual among people who already understand each other may be the subtlest defence of all — a defence against the anxiety of the stranger. It’s striking that an essay subtitled On working with uncertainty, from a tradition steeped in group analysis, mentions anxiety only as a statistic about the population and never as something at work in its own rooms.

Back to that first objection — a minimal programme isn’t minimal structure. The fewer the rules on paper, the more has to be carried in the members, and the higher the price of entry for anyone who doesn’t carry them yet. That’s why, by Mowles’s own account, the conference produces inclusion and exclusion. Those dynamics are what the invisible jacket feels like to someone not yet fitted for it.

Question XIIIDoes therapy really count against structure?

Mowles uses therapy as his emblem of the unscripted encounter. But isn’t therapy the most structured encounter there is?

Someone might say it does. Therapy is the model of the irreducibly human encounter: two embodied people in an alliance, spontaneous and messy, where wisdom, not information, heals. AI and manualised methods alike threaten it.

On the other hand no human encounter is more tightly framed than the therapy hour.

My answer is that the essay has picked, as its emblem of the unscripted, the one practice where structure is most explicit, most theorised and most fiercely defended. Freud’s papers on technique fixed the hour, the fee, the regular schedule, the couch, the rule of free association and the analyst’s restraint. Winnicott called the setting a holding environment and insisted that its reliability is what lets the patient risk chaos inside it. Robert Langs built a whole clinical theory on the integrity of the frame, arguing that breaches of it are among the most powerful events in treatment. Group analysis, as we saw, adds dynamic administration. The spontaneity Mowles rightly treasures isn’t the opposite of this structure; it’s the fruit of it. The mess is safe to enter because the walls are reliable.

And here the Cohen line turns round. A crack is a feature of a vessel. Without a vessel there’s no crack — just spillage. The light gets in through the flaw in the form, which means it needs the form. Without meaning to, Mowles has arrived at Goethe’s reconciliation (Question VI): nature and art have found each other before you know it. His example is the strongest argument in the essay, and it’s an argument for the right structure, not against structure.

That matters for his quarrel with Schmidt too. What Schmidt misses isn’t only the difference between knowledge and wisdom, real as that is. It’s the frame. A chatbot is available at any hour, free, endlessly, with no restraint and no fifty-minute discipline. It has no frame. The danger Turkle describes isn’t too much structure in the encounter but too little: unlimited availability, infinite patience, no ending. By Mowles’s own best example, the bot isn’t a straitjacket. It’s a room with no walls — and it’s the room with no walls that makes us passive.

Back to that first objection — therapy is indeed the model of the human encounter, and for that very reason it undoes the essay’s drift. It teaches that the deepest spontaneity flourishes inside the most reliable form, and that the enemy of resonance is the formless and frictionless, not the framed.

Question XIVCan resonance happen without the right conditions?

Can resonance — the moment a room comes alive — happen without any conditions at all?

Someone might say resonance, in Rosa’s sense, is exactly what can’t be engineered. It’s a two-way responsiveness that comes unbidden; trying to produce it destroys it. So methods that try to secure good conversation kill what they’re after.

On the other hand Rosa never says resonance comes out of a clear blue sky. What he says is that it can’t be commanded — a different claim — and that it depends on conditions that can be nurtured or wrecked.

My answer is that the essay uses only half of Rosa. In The Uncontrollability of the World, Rosa argues that resonance is uncontrollable in the sense that you can’t produce it at will or stockpile it — but it isn’t random either. It needs what he elsewhere calls axes of resonance: settings, institutional and material, that make responsiveness possible without guaranteeing it. You might say resonance is half-available: beyond command, but not beyond cultivation. A concert hall doesn’t produce the moment the music takes the audience, but a hall with dead acoustics rules it out. So the question for any gathering isn’t whether to have conditions; it’s which conditions leave the most room for what can’t be commanded.

That is almost word for word Snowden’s distinction between governing constraints, which fix outcomes, and enabling constraints, which shape conditions without deciding what happens. Read in full, Mowles’s own authority stands nearer to Snowden than to the essay that quotes him. Through Rosa, the quarrel between the two men shrinks from a dispute about structure to a dispute about acoustics.

Byung-Chul Han points the same way. The Burnout Society argues that people today are exhausted not by prohibition but by unlimited permission: the achievement-driven self exploits itself because nothing stops it. Han’s diagnosis is too few walls, not too many. Quoting Han against structure is quoting the doctor of formlessness against form.

Back to that first objection — what can’t be engineered can still be hosted. A host can’t force a guest to be delighted; she sets the table. Refusing to set the table for fear of forcing delight isn’t reverence for the uncontrollable. It’s giving up, dressed as reverence.

Question XVHas Jackson actually been answered?

Jackson asked who is protected in an open room. Did Mowles actually answer that question?

Someone might say he has. Jackson thinks open conversation is a badly chaired meeting; Mowles shows that the badly chaired meeting is actually the over-driven one, racing through the agenda with no time to think. Charge returned to sender.

On the other hand by Mowles’s own account, Jackson’s question wasn’t about chairing. It was about who gets heard — democratic participation and the protection of disadvantaged stakeholders.

My answer is that the essay turns an ethical and political objection into a question about method and timing, and then answers that instead. Jackson asks: when making sense of things is left to open conversation, who is protected? Mowles answers: a rushed meeting leaves no time to think. Both may be true, and they don’t touch. The disadvantaged stakeholder isn’t protected by slowness; a slow room can silence people as surely as a fast one, and more comfortably. The footnote’s quip about all activity and no thinking is witty and beside the point.

Worse still, the essay quietly concedes the premise of Jackson’s question without noticing. When it owns up to the inclusion and exclusion at the beloved conference, it’s describing exactly what Jackson was asking about. There is a reply available from Mowles’s tradition — that power relations are always present, that the work is to notice and negotiate them rather than design them away, that design merely hides the designer’s power (as this book argued against Snowden in Question III). But the essay doesn’t make it. It leaves Jackson’s concern for the weak standing unanswered at the edge of the conference, like the newcomer who doesn’t yet know when to speak.

There’s also a question of fidelity. Jackson’s view reaches the reader through Mowles’s paraphrase and a single page reference, and reaches Snowden’s readers through Mowles’s paraphrase again. Snowden says his own reading of Jackson is different. So Jackson appears in this exchange as a third party spoken for by others — someone two traditions disagree about, voiced by neither in his own words. A discussion of whose voice gets heard in a room has, in its own textual room, a participant who hasn’t spoken yet.

Back to that first objection — the charge wasn’t returned to sender; it was redirected to a different address. Jackson’s letter is still unopened.

Question XVIIs “going nowhere” a virtue or a privilege?

Is “going nowhere” a virtue — or a luxury only some people can afford?

Someone might say it’s a virtue. If our problems are second-order — crises in our ways of life, not technical puzzles — then how we handle dilemmas together is itself what we learn from. Going nowhere gives us data to notice. The gaps and frustrations are where new thoughts become thinkable.

On the other hand being able to go nowhere means being free of any need to arrive anywhere — and that freedom, like most freedoms, is handed out very unevenly.

My answer is that the essay describes, honestly and tenderly, a luxury good and calls it a method. Bourdieu, in Pascalian Meditations, named the condition: skholè (leisure from necessity), the leisure from necessity that underlies the scholar’s point of view, and the “scholastic fallacy” of mistaking the view that leisure affords for the human view in general. A weekend where nothing has to be decided is available to tenured academics, funded doctoral students and consultants between jobs. It isn’t available to the ward sister who has to agree a rota by Monday, the civil servant whose minister needs a recommendation, or the community whose dispute will turn violent if nobody settles it. For them, going nowhere isn’t a lesson. It’s abandonment.

Snowden’s point about scale — which I’ve otherwise disputed — is unanswerable here on Mowles’s own terms. The essay’s examples of where spontaneity should live — the university, the research group — are places of relative leisure. Its example of where structure oppresses — the strategy workshop — is a place where decisions must be made. The comparison smuggles a difference of situation in as a difference of method.

And Jaeggi, whom the essay invokes, doesn’t license the virtue it claims. Her account of progress and regress treats ways of life as attempts to solve problems and judges them by whether they enable or block learning. In that language, going nowhere is neither good nor bad in itself. It’s progress when it unblocks a stuck learning process and regress when it merely repeats. A tradition that meets every year to talk about how it talks, among people who already share its language, runs a real risk of the second. Second-order problems need second-order learning — and second-order learning can get as stuck as any agenda.

Back to that first objection — it’s perfectly true that frustration can give birth to a new thought. But whether it reliably does, for whom, and at whose expense, are questions the essay never stops to ask. A virtue only the secure can afford is a virtue of the secure, and it should be practised with the humility of knowing so.

Question XVIIDid the drone stay with the trouble?

Mowles felt the trouble in the workshop and went along with it. Did he stay with the trouble, or step around it?

Someone might say the drone confession is the essay’s most honest moment: it shows how total structure produces passivity even in a seasoned critic. The workshop turned Mowles into a drone; that’s the indictment.

On the other hand the trouble was right there in the room with him. He felt it plainly — the frustration, the alienation, the ache of having no space to reflect — and by the essay’s own doctrine, that frustration is exactly where new thinking becomes possible. And still, like the rest of the room, he went along with it.

My answer is that the confession convicts the confessor at least as much as the workshop. Staying with the trouble, in that room, would have meant voicing the discomfort there: saying out loud, in the plenary or to the facilitator or to a neighbour, that the process was stopping people thinking; refusing a sticky dot; asking the group to notice how it was being herded. Those are exactly the moves of someone trained to talk about how we’re talking. They were available. The structure didn’t abolish them; it just made them costlier. Mowles chose — as most of us would — to pay the smaller price of going along in the room and to recover his agency later, in prose, in front of his readers.

That’s entirely human. But it shows that the essay’s commitment to staying with trouble is selective. It stays with the trouble of the beloved conference, where frustration is honoured as fertile. It flees the trouble of the alien workshop, where frustration is treated as proof of oppression. The difference between fertile trouble and oppressive trouble turns out to be the difference between your own tradition and someone else’s. That’s our idea again, in its emotional form: to the baggy, the other’s frustration feels like a cage; one’s own feels like growth.

Shakespeare offers the deepest comment, and the essay supplies the cue. Its closing three words — cabin, crib, confine — echo Macbeth on hearing that Fleance has escaped: “But now I am cabin’d, cribb’d, confin’d, bound in / To saucy doubts and fears.” Think about who’s speaking. Macbeth is the man who tried to abolish uncertainty by violence — to make the prophecy safe by murdering the future. He isn’t confined by any structure imposed on him, but by his own fear, the moment his control slips. Literature’s great image of being cabined and cribbed is an image of inner imprisonment: a jacket stitched from your own anxiety. Read through its source, the essay’s final image says something its author may not have meant: we’re confined less by method than by the dread with which we meet what we can’t control — and no tradition, open or structured, is exempt from that.

Back to that first objection — the confession is honest, and honesty deserves credit. But honesty about passivity isn’t evidence that structure caused it. Group analysis itself knows that a group that goes along is enacting something, and that the enactment belongs to all its members — the drone included.

Question XVIIIDoes the essay keep faith with the title it borrowed?

Mowles’s title is Haraway’s phrase. Does his essay keep faith with what she meant by it?

Someone might say it does. Staying with the trouble means staying present to uncertainty instead of escaping into technique, and the essay recommends exactly that, against AI and frameworks alike.

On the other hand the phrase is Donna Haraway’s, the title of her 2016 book, and the essay never mentions her name — which matters more than a missing footnote usually would, because Haraway’s trouble turns out to be a very different kind of trouble from Mowles’s.

My answer is that the title borrows a glow it doesn’t pay back. Haraway’s Staying with the Trouble: Making Kin in the Chthulucene is about living entangled with other species on a damaged planet. Its central advice is to refuse two temptations equally: faith in a technological fix, and the bitter cynicism that says the game is already over. Between them lies the work of sympoiesis — making-with — putting together odd kinships across species, technologies and histories. Haraway is also, famously, the author of “A Cyborg Manifesto” (1985), which refused the romance of an organic wholeness lost to machines and insisted that we’re already hybrids of flesh and technology, with all the political responsibility that brings.

Measured against its source, the essay keeps half the advice and breaks the other half. It rightly refuses the tech fix — Schmidt’s fantasy of therapy on tap. But it leans — through Putnam, Han, an unquantified surge in loneliness, the shuttered humanities, and Arendt on loneliness as the soil of totalitarianism — toward exactly the lament Haraway warns against: the story of decline, of humanity being lost, of shared space being destroyed. Moving from a parcel-delivery survey to the conditions of totalitarianism in a few paragraphs is the rhetoric of the elegy, not of someone making kin. Haraway asks us to stay in the mess and make something with what’s there, machines included. The essay asks us to mourn what the machines are taking.

The irony deepens when it comes to structure. Haraway’s “tentacular thinking” is deeply relational but nowhere anti-structure. Her central image of thinking together is the string figure — cat’s cradle — a pattern made by passing form from hand to hand according to rules. It’s neither baggy nor boxed. It’s a game with constraints that two players hold together, each able to change the figure only by honouring what the other made. Had the essay taken its title seriously, it would have found in Haraway not an ally against structure but a model of structure made with people rather than imposed on them — exactly what the jacket audit asks for.

Back to that first objection — the essay does recommend staying present to uncertainty, and that’s partly Haraway’s advice. But staying with the trouble isn’t staying away from the machine, or staying among your own. It’s staying with the other — the alien, the strange kin, the method you dislike, the drone in the next chair — and making something with them that neither of you could make alone.

The Ruling on MowlesWhat stands, what falls, and what the essay teaches against itself

Where we areEight questions have been put to Mowles. Time again to add up.
What this asksThis Ruling asks what stands in his essay, what falls, and what his own best examples quietly teach against his argument.
In brief 2 min read
  • Stands: the critique of frictionless machine intimacy; knowledge is not wisdom; the portrait of over-facilitation; the second-order nature of problems.
  • Falls: the implicit metaphysics that treats structure as the enemy of the human encounter.
  • Lesson: each of his strongest examples depends on form.

What stands

A lot stands, and stands high. The critique of frictionless machine intimacy is serious, humane and timely. The distinction between knowledge and practical judgement is the right weapon against the Schmidt fantasy, and Mowles uses it with care. His portrait of over-facilitated process — cued applause, outputs read aloud, dots on the wall, the illusion of participation — is accurate and widely recognised, and Snowden agrees with it. The insistence that second-order problems can’t be solved by first-order technique is a real insight. And the essay’s honesty — about inclusion and exclusion at his beloved conference, and about his own drone-like compliance — is rarer than it should be and deserves respect.

What falls

What falls is the essay’s unspoken philosophy of structure. The chain from chatbot to workshop to Jackson convicts by family resemblance (Question XI). The conference it loves is a liturgy, not a void, and inherits a tightly framed lineage it never declares (Question XII). Its best example, therapy, is the most framed encounter in human practice and argues for reliable form as the condition of spontaneity (Question XIII). Its authorities — Rosa, Han, Jaeggi — read in full, sit closer to a theory of enabling conditions than to a rejection of design (Questions XIV and XVI). Jackson’s question about the weak is redirected, not answered (Question XV). Going nowhere is offered as a method when it’s also a privilege (Question XVI). The trouble it tells us to stay with is honoured selectively — in its own room, not in someone else’s (Question XVII). And its title is borrowed from a thinker who’d reject both its lament and its suspicion of machines (Question XVIII).

What the essay teaches against itself

Here’s the twist. Every one of Mowles’s strongest moments proves that the human encounter he’s defending depends on form: the therapy frame, the conference liturgy, the group-analytic habit of noticing, Rosa’s axes of resonance, Haraway’s string figures. His real enemy isn’t structure but someone else’s structure — the kind he didn’t grow up inside, the kind he can still see because it hasn’t yet worn down into skin. That’s our idea in perfect form. Snowden calls Mowles’s jacket baggy. Mowles calls the facilitator’s jacket a cage. Each is right about the other’s clothes and silent about his own.

Two men, two jackets, each seeing the other’s clearly. There is a third tradition in the room, though, and it has barely spoken.

Part FiveThe three jackets: a closing triangulation

Where we areBoth essays have now been weighed, and a third tradition — Jackson’s — has been hovering at the edge of the room throughout.
What this asksPart Five asks what happens if we put all three in the room together and describe each one’s garment honestly.
In brief 3 min read
  • Three jackets: design (anthro-complexity), tradition (complex responsive processes), and method-choice (critical systems thinking).
  • None of them can be taken off. The aim is to make each one legible, contestable and changeable by those who wear it.
  • Proposed opening rite for Hull: each tradition first describes itself as the other two see it.

Picture the room Snowden imagines at Hull. Three traditions file in, each wearing a jacket, each seeing the others’ jackets far more clearly than its own. It’s worth naming them carefully.

The jacket of design belongs to Snowden’s anthro-complexity. Its buttons are all on the outside where you can count them: roles, sequences, group sizes, timings, principles. Its great virtue is that it’s visible — anyone can read the method and argue with it. Its weakness is authorship hidden behind modesty: the convener stays out of the content, while the designer is present in every turn of it. From inside this jacket, the tradition next door looks baggy, because its rules aren’t written down.

The jacket of tradition belongs to Mowles’s complex responsive processes. Its buttons are all on the inside: a set of core texts, a vocabulary, a group-analytic discipline of noticing, a little liturgy of supper, keynote and open talk. Its great virtue is depth — constraint practised until it becomes the freedom to improvise, just like the jazz Snowden himself mentions. Its weakness is that it can’t see itself, and so charges newcomers a high entry price that nobody mentions. From inside this jacket, the designed workshop looks like a cage, because its rules are foreign.

The jacket of method-choice belongs to Jackson’s critical systems thinking. Its buttons are of a higher order: a commitment to choosing between methods according to the situation and to the goal of emancipation, and to protecting those with the least voice. Its virtue is genuine pluralism and political seriousness. Its weakness — the one this imprint has elsewhere called the connoisseurship of partial truths — is to stand above the methods like a curator, as if the curator’s own position weren’t also a jacket. From up there, both the others look partial, because this jacket is tailored to spot partiality everywhere except in the mirror.

The three jackets compared
DesignTraditionMethod-choice
TraditionAnthro-complexity (Snowden)Complex responsive processes (Mowles)Critical systems thinking (Jackson)
Where the constraints liveOn the outside: roles, sequences, timingsOn the inside: canon, vocabulary, habitAbove: rules for choosing among methods
VirtueVisible, so it can be argued withDepth; freedom to improvisePluralism; political seriousness
Blind spotThe designer’s authorshipIts own form, and the newcomer’s price of entryThe curator’s own standpoint
Sees the others asBaggyBoxedPartial

None of these jackets comes off, and that’s the point. The dream of a room with no jacket at all — the perfectly open conversation, the perfectly neutral design, the perfectly impartial view from above all methods — is the one fantasy all three share. The grown-up question isn’t which jacket is the true one. It’s how jackets can be made readable, open to argument and changeable by the people wearing them, while they’re being worn. That’s the jacket audit from the Ruling, now applied to all three houses: let each declare the rules that govern it, enable it and make it what it is; let each name who designed it; and let each give the newcomer some way of seeing where the walls are — and moving them if need be.

If the meeting in Hull ever happens, I’d propose one small ritual to open it, before any Eagle clears its throat. Each tradition describes not the other two, but itself as the other two see it. Snowden’s tradition describes its own method as a cage. Mowles’s describes its own conference as a club. Jackson’s describes its own pluralism as a throne. Only then — once everyone has looked through someone else’s glasses for a while — would the conversation be ready to start, in whatever form the three could make up together. That would be staying with the trouble. That would be neither baggy nor boxed. That would be the string figure passed from hand to hand.

Interlude at the Threshold

You’re wearing a jacket, and the reason you can’t see it is the reason you can’t see your own back: you’re inside it. The people across the room can see it perfectly well, because they’re not — and you can see theirs. The designer calls his walls scaffolding, the traditionalist calls his walls freedom, the pluralist calls his walls perspective, and every one of those names is true from inside and a disguise from outside.

So there’s no point asking a room to have no walls; it can only answer with a pretence. The real request is different: show me where your walls are, tell me who put them up, and let me know whether I’m allowed to move them. Bagginess is what a jacket calls someone else’s skin; confinement is what skin calls someone else’s jacket.

The master, when you finally meet one, isn’t the person who has done away with form, but the one in whom form has become a body — and who still has the humility to remember that it started as a garment someone handed them. 道 the way is worn in by walking it; 理 the grain is read before the blade is lifted; 勢 the moment is met when it comes.

Three jackets, none removable. But we have described the pattern without explaining it. Why can no one see their own garment?

Part SixThe ontology of the jacket: tacit form, the invisible tool, and the thought collective

Where we areBy now the pattern is unmistakable: every party sees the others’ form and misses its own. But why?
What this asksPart Six goes underneath the dispute and asks what it is about human knowing — the tacit, the habitual, the communal — that makes our own form so hard to see.
In brief 16 min read
  • Polanyi: we attend from our own constraints, so they feel like freedom; other people’s press upon us as obstacles.
  • Heidegger: form becomes visible only in breakdown, so people a structure fails will always describe it most vividly.
  • Merleau-Ponty: habit incorporates form over years, so comparing a tradition with a one-off method compares a body with a garment.
  • Fleck: each thought collective sees its own style as reality and other styles as arbitrary.
  • Gadamer and MacIntyre: tradition is an argument that stays alive only while it is put at risk.
  • Kant and Hegel: freedom exists where form is both owned and articulable.

TakeawayThe asymmetry is not a vice of the two authors. It is built into how every embodied, socialised knower relates to the forms that make knowing possible.

By now one idea has been tested against two essays, and it holds against both: each author sees the other’s rules clearly and his own barely at all. Which raises the obvious question — why? If the same thing keeps happening, it needs explaining. And it would be both unkind and shallow to explain it by vanity or partisanship. Snowden and Mowles are serious, thoughtful people; neither is a fool and neither lacks self-awareness. If they both show the same blind spot, the cause probably isn’t their characters. It’s more likely to lie in how perception itself works — in how any person, with a body and a social history, relates to the forms that make their knowing possible. This Part tries to give that deeper account. It draws on five bodies of thought that rarely meet but agree remarkably on this: Michael Polanyi on tacit knowledge, Martin Heidegger on tools, Maurice Merleau-Ponty on habit, Ludwik Fleck on “thought collectives”, and Hans-Georg Gadamer and Alasdair MacIntyre on tradition. It ends with the older question underneath all of them: how law and freedom relate, as Kant and Hegel saw it.

§ 1. We know more than we can tell

Polanyi built his account of tacit knowing — first given as the Terry Lectures at Yale in 1962 and published in 1966 as The Tacit Dimension — on a fact he put in words that have become proverbial: “we can know more than we can tell.” His examples are deliberately ordinary. We recognise a face among thousands without being able to say how. We ride a bike through constant corrections we couldn’t describe. A skilled doctor sees a pattern in symptoms before she can name the features that make it up. Polanyi distinguished two kinds of awareness. Focal awareness is aimed at what we’re attending to. Subsidiary awareness is what we attend from — the particulars we rely on to grasp the whole. When I hammer a nail, I’m focally aware of the nail and subsidiarily aware of the hammer in my palm. If I switch my attention to my palm, the hammering falters. Subsidiary things work precisely by not being attended to. Make them focal and they stop working.

The link to our idea is immediate. The rules of a mature practice are held almost entirely in subsidiary awareness. Members of Mowles’s research community attend from their shared vocabulary, their unspoken norms of turn-taking and interpretation, their sense of what counts as a good contribution, to the conversation those norms make possible. They aren’t focally aware of the norms — and couldn’t be, mid-conversation, without the conversation stalling, just as the hammering stalls when you look at your palm. Hence the feeling of freedom: what’s held subsidiarily isn’t felt as constraint at all, but as the medium you act through. The newcomer hasn’t absorbed the norms yet, so they press on her focally, as obstacles: she notices she spoke at the wrong moment, or that her comment landed coldly, without knowing why. The same norms are freedom to the member and constraint to the newcomer. That isn’t a difference in the norms. It’s a difference in how they’re held.

The designer’s constraints are the mirror image. Roles, sequences and timings announced at the start of a triopticon are focal by nature: written down, explained, displayed. Participants have to attend to them before they can attend from them, and in a one-off event there’s rarely time to make that switch. So the design is felt as constraint — as objects in the room — rather than as a medium. To the designer, who has run the method many times, the same roles have long since sunk into subsidiary awareness; he attends from them to the dynamics they reveal, and experiences them as freeing. The participant meeting them for the first time experiences them as a box. Polanyi’s distinction explains both halves of the twin idea at once. Each side experiences its own rules subsidiarily, as freedom, and the other side’s focally — as formlessness (because the other’s rules aren’t on display) or as confinement (because they are on display and not yet absorbed).

One consequence matters for the proposal this book ends with. If subsidiary particulars only work when they’re not attended to, then asking a tradition to declare its tacit rules isn’t innocent. Making the tacit focal disturbs it. A community asked to name its unspoken assumptions may find, for a while, that it can’t talk as fluently as before. Polanyi’s answer wasn’t to give up on reflection. It was to recognise that tacit knowing, once made explicit, can be re-absorbed at a higher level: the pianist who analyses her fingering has to forget the analysis to play again — but she plays better for having done it. So the jacket audit isn’t a permanent exposure of every rule. It’s a periodic swing from subsidiary to focal and back — a swing a mature practice can survive and learn from.

§ 2. The tool that disappears

Heidegger’s analysis of tools in Being and Time (1927, sections 15–16) comes at the same thing from the object’s side. In everyday use, things are what he calls ready-to-hand. The hammer in use isn’t something we contemplate; it disappears into the work, becomes transparent, so that what’s present to the worker is the job, not the tool. The hammer only becomes noticeable when it fails. Heidegger names three ways that happens: when the tool is broken (it becomes conspicuous), when a needed tool is missing (obtrusive), and when something gets in the way of the work (obstinate). In each case the tool, previously invisible, suddenly lights up as an object — and with it, briefly, the whole workshop becomes visible.

A gathering’s form is a tool in exactly this sense. When things go smoothly it disappears; people are absorbed in the conversation, not in the structure holding it. The form only becomes visible when it breaks down. Read through Heidegger, Mowles’s account of the facilitated workshop is a description of obstinacy: the structure got in the way of what he thought the work was, so it lit up, became an object, became oppressive. His account of his own conference describes readiness-to-hand: its structure disappeared into the conversation it supported, so he could only experience it as freedom. The newcomer’s experience of exclusion, which Mowles honestly reports, is the moment the conference’s tool becomes conspicuous for someone else — the moment its tacit structure fails to work for a person who hasn’t been fitted to it yet.

Heidegger adds something Polanyi doesn’t: an explanation of why seeing a form tends to go with resenting it. A form becomes visible exactly when it’s not working for the person who sees it. So structure is often first met as an obstacle, and its enabling side is noticed, if at all, only in hindsight. That builds a systematic bias into testimony. People for whom a structure works will describe the task; people for whom it fails will describe the structure. Accounts of facilitated workshops written by people who found them obstinate will always be more vivid, more detailed and more about structure than accounts by people for whom the same workshops worked. The same goes for open gatherings: the excluded newcomer will describe the hidden rules, while the happy regular describes only the conversation. Any comparison of the two kinds of room that relies on people’s testimony needs to correct for this. Neither essay does — and Mowles’s, which rests entirely on his own experience of one event of each kind, is especially exposed.

§ 3. Habit: when form becomes body

Merleau-Ponty’s Phenomenology of Perception (1945) carries the analysis into the body. His famous examples — the woman who keeps the feather in her hat clear of a doorway without calculating it, the blind man for whom his cane has stopped being an object and become an extension of touch, the organist who, after an hour on an unfamiliar instrument, inhabits its stops and pedals as if they were his own limbs — all show what he called the incorporation of instruments into the body. For Merleau-Ponty, habit is neither a mechanical reflex nor an intellectual rule. It’s the body gaining a new power — a reorganisation of what things mean to it, so that the world now invites it differently. The feather, the cane and the organ are no longer things the body uses; they’re part of how the body is in the world.

Two things follow for our question. First, absorbed form isn’t just invisible; it’s generative. The organist who has absorbed the instrument doesn’t just play without noticing the stops; he can now improvise, because the instrument has become a range of possible movement rather than a set of obstacles. That’s exactly what Question IV claimed about the jazz player, the cook and the martial artist. It’s also what Goethe’s closing lines mean. Limitation, once absorbed, stops being limitation and becomes capacity.

The second consequence is less comfortable for both sides. Absorption takes time, and the body sets the pace, not the design. Merleau-Ponty’s organist needed an hour; a group-analytic practitioner needs years; a method met once needs at least as long as the method takes to run, and probably much longer. That’s the deepest reason Snowden’s triopticon can’t simply be offered as an upgrade on a tradition’s open conversation. The tradition’s form has been absorbed; the method’s hasn’t. Comparing them at a single event compares a body with a garment. It’s also the deepest reason Mowles’s dismissal of the facilitated workshop is premature. A method experienced once hasn’t had a chance to become a body, and what feels like a box may become, with practice, a range of motion. Neither essay asks what either form would feel like after a decade of use by people who didn’t grow up inside it. That’s the question that would actually settle things.

§ 4. Thought collectives, and why other people seem arbitrary

If Polanyi, Heidegger and Merleau-Ponty explain the blind spot in individual perception, Ludwik Fleck explains it in communities. His Genesis and Development of a Scientific Fact (1935), a study of how the Wassermann test came to be accepted as a test for syphilis, introduced two ideas that anticipated a lot of later sociology of knowledge. A thought collective is a community of people exchanging ideas and staying in intellectual contact. A thought style is the readiness to perceive things in a certain directed way that such a community cultivates in its members. A thought style decides not just what its members think but what they see: what counts as a fact, a problem, an anomaly, a relevant observation.

Fleck’s sharpest observation is about how collectives see each other. Inside a thought collective, the style isn’t experienced as a style but as reality: its assumptions seem obvious, its methods natural, its perceptions simply what’s there. Other collectives’ styles, by contrast, seem arbitrary, mystical or confused. So communication between collectives is systematically distorted; ideas passing from one to another get transformed, often beyond recognition, because the receiving collective fits them to its own style. Fleck also distinguished the inner, “esoteric” circle of specialists from the wider, “exoteric” circle of the educated public, and noticed that a style is most rigid — and least visible to itself — at the inner core.

It’s hard to imagine a more exact description of this exchange. Snowden’s, Mowles’s and Jackson’s traditions are thought collectives in Fleck’s sense, each with a distinct style: a readiness to see groups as fields of constraint and entrainment; a readiness to see them as figurations of power and identity under constant negotiation; a readiness to see them as places to select methods in the service of emancipation. Each sees its own style as just paying attention to what’s there, and each sees the others’ as arbitrary. Snowden sees Mowles’s conference as baggy because his style only perceives constraint where it’s been designed. Mowles sees the workshop as a cage because his style perceives design as the suppression of emerging relationships. Jackson, on both men’s accounts, sees Mowles’s tradition as failing to protect the weak because his style sees deliberation through the lens of emancipatory method. Fleck would add that the changes Jackson’s position has gone through in passing via Mowles and then Snowden — the ventriloquism noted in Question XV — are exactly what you’d expect when an idea travels between collectives.

Fleck offers one more resource. He thought the most productive moments in the history of thought happen where collectives overlap — where one person belongs to several and has to hold their styles in tension. Such people, for Fleck, carry stylistic change. That suggests a humbler and more useful aim for the proposed meeting in Hull than the verdict Snowden imagines. Its value wouldn’t lie in deciding which style is correct — a question Fleck would think badly formed — but in creating conditions in which some participants might come to inhabit more than one style at once, and so see their own as a style for the first time.

§ 5. Tradition as argument, prejudice as a starting point

The charge that Mowles’s conference is a club rests on a hidden theory of tradition: that a tradition is a set of shared assumptions, and that people who share assumptions think less well than people who don’t. Two of the most important twentieth-century accounts of tradition reject that theory, and their rejection bears directly on the dispute.

Gadamer’s Truth and Method (1960) rehabilitated the idea of prejudice — literally a pre-judgement — against the Enlightenment’s blanket suspicion of it. Understanding, Gadamer argued, never starts from nothing. It proceeds from the expectations tradition supplies, and the attempt to purge oneself of all prejudice is itself a prejudice: the Enlightenment’s prejudice against prejudice. What separates productive prejudice from distorting prejudice isn’t whether you have it but whether you’ll put it at risk when you meet something different. Gadamer’s model of understanding is the fusion of horizons: the interpreter’s horizon, shaped by tradition, meets the horizon of the text or the other person, and both are changed. The interpreter doesn’t leave her horizon behind; she brings it into play. On this view, a community sharing a tradition isn’t thereby disabled from thinking. It’s equipped for it — provided the tradition is held open to being questioned.

MacIntyre’s After Virtue (1981) and Whose Justice? Which Rationality? (1988) go further. A living tradition, MacIntyre wrote, is “an historically extended, socially embodied argument” — an argument partly about the very goods that make up the tradition. Traditions aren’t bodies of agreement; they’re continuities of conflict. When a tradition stops arguing about its own goods, it’s dying. MacIntyre also described how traditions win out over each other rationally: a tradition shows it’s better when it can explain, in its own terms, why a rival ran into the difficulties it did — in a way the rival’s own members can come to accept. He called the crisis that sparks such encounters an epistemological crisis, and held that traditions grow by going through them.

Applied here, these accounts both defend the club and convict it. They defend it because a long-standing community of practice is, on MacIntyre’s account, likely to be a place of sustained argument rather than mere agreement — as the out-group homogeneity effect from Part Three would predict, its members experience it as far more internally contested than outsiders imagine. They convict it because both Gadamer and MacIntyre make a tradition’s vitality depend on its willingness to be put at risk. A tradition that only talks to itself, however vigorously, hasn’t yet met its epistemological crisis. Read this way, Mowles’s essay shows a tradition confident in its own goods and not much inclined to imagine that a rival — Jackson’s pluralism or Snowden’s design — might explain its difficulties better than it can itself. The right response to the club charge isn’t denial but invitation: to seek out exactly the encounters in which the tradition’s horizon might fuse with another’s, at the risk of being changed. That’s the very encounter Snowden proposes. The only problem, as Question VII argued, is that he proposes to stage it inside his own horizon.

§ 6. The law that gives freedom

Underneath all this lies a question as old as moral philosophy, which Goethe’s epigraph gestures at without pursuing: in what sense can law give freedom? Two German answers throw a sharp light on our dispute.

Kant’s answer, in the Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals (1785), is autonomy. Freedom isn’t the absence of law but giving law to yourself; a will that obeys only its own rational law is free, while a will moved by outside causes, even pleasant ones, is “heteronomous” — ruled from outside. On this view, what matters about any constraint isn’t how tight it is but whose law it is. A constraint you’ve given yourself — or could give yourself, reflecting on what you’d want as a universal rule — expresses freedom; a constraint simply imposed denies it. For our case: the open conference’s rules are, for its members, largely autonomous in Kant’s sense — taken up and endorsed over years. The triopticon’s rules are, for a first-timer, heteronomous — handed down by a designer. But the newcomer to the conference meets its rules heteronomously too, and a seasoned triopticon practitioner may well have made its form her own. Autonomy isn’t a property of forms; it’s a relationship between a form and the people living under it. So Kant supplies the test the jacket audit needs: not “how much structure?”, nor even “what kind?”, but “could the people living under this form recognise it as their own law?”

Hegel’s answer, in the Philosophy of Right (1820), corrects Kant in a way that bears just as much on the dispute. Hegel thought Kant’s autonomy too abstract and too individual: a self that gives law only to itself is empty, with no content but the bare form of universality. Real freedom, Hegel argued, only happens in ethical life (Sittlichkeit): in institutions — family, civil society, state — whose customs people recognise as expressing their own will, and where they feel at home. Freedom is “being at home with oneself in another”. On this view, the open conference at its best is a small example of ethical life: a set of customs in which members feel at home and through which they’re free. But Hegel also insisted that ethical life must be rational — its customs must be capable of being stated, criticised and justified — or it collapses into mere habit, and freedom into conformity. The danger of a tradition that doesn’t declare its rules is exactly that its ethical life decays into what Hegel called mere “positivity”: custom without reason, homely only for those born into it.

Together, Kant and Hegel give the philosophical footing for the position this book has been heading towards. Against Snowden, they say freedom isn’t secured by better design, because a design not recognised as one’s own law is imposed from outside, however enabling. Against Mowles, they say freedom isn’t secured by custom alone, because custom that isn’t stated and justified decays into conformity and shuts out those who didn’t grow up in it. Freedom lives where form is both owned and explainable: where the people living under it can recognise it as their own, and can also explain it to those who don’t share it yet. That double condition is what Part Nine is about.

Third Interlude · On the Visibility of Forms

The forms that hold us become visible in only three situations: when they fail us, when they’re new to us, and when someone outside them describes them to us. In the first we resent them, in the second we resist them, and in the third we tend to deny they exist. It’s a rare, hard discipline to see your own form while it’s working, as an old form, in your own words. Yet without that discipline every argument about structure ends up like this one: a quarrel between people who can each see only the other’s clothes.

The roots of the blindness are deep and very human. They are also very old. Long before business schools, Chinese philosophers fought the same battle.

Part SevenThe carpenter’s square: the classical Chinese debate on ritual, nature and form

Where we areYou might suspect this is a modern problem, born in business schools. It is not.
What this asksPart Seven travels back more than two thousand years, to a Chinese argument about ritual and nature that rehearsed this whole dispute — and reached conclusions the modern essays never did.
In brief 14 min read
  • Confucius at seventy follows his heart “without overstepping the square”: freedom as fully incorporated form.
  • Rustic and clerk (Analects 6.18) are the ancient names for baggy and boxed; the remedy lies in the person, not in a method.
  • Mencius against Xunzi loosely maps Mowles against Snowden, and both modern authors omit the time and the tending that their classical counterparts required.
  • The farmer of Song names both errors at once: neglecting the field, and pulling at the sprouts. The answer is weeding.
  • Cook Ding finds the form in the material, and slows down at the knot.
  • Laozi 11: emptiness is useful only when walls bound it.

TakeawayThe classical tradition located the answer in the formation of those who gather, not in the design of events.

It would be parochial to think the quarrel between baggy and boxed was invented in the business schools of Hertfordshire and Hull, or in the consultancies that grew up around them. The longest and subtlest argument any civilisation has had about imposed form versus spontaneous conduct happened in China between roughly the sixth and third centuries BCE, among thinkers later grouped as Confucian, Mohist, Daoist and Legalist. They argued about ritual (禮 lǐ), human nature (性 xìng), deliberate effort or artifice (偽 wěi), spontaneity (自然 zìrán) and the pattern inherent in things (理 lǐ). They argued with a seriousness and a literary brilliance that today’s exchange, for all its merits, doesn’t come near — and they reached conclusions that bear on our question with uncanny directness. This Part follows five moments in that argument. The translations are my own.

§ 1. At seventy, the square became desire

The most famous piece of autobiography in the Analects (2.4) traces a life in stages:

吾十有五而志于學，三十而立，四十而不惑，五十而知天命，六十而耳順，七十而從心所欲，不踰矩。At fifteen I set my heart on learning; at thirty I took my stand; at forty I was free of doubts; at fifty I knew the decree of Heaven; at sixty my ear was attuned; at seventy I could follow what my heart desired without overstepping the square.

Everything hangs on the last word. 矩 jǔ is the carpenter’s square — the tool for drawing right angles and testing straight lines — and by extension a norm or measure. What Confucius describes at seventy isn’t freedom from the square but its complete absorption: desire and measure have become one. He can follow his heart because, over fifty-five years, his heart has been shaped to the square. It’s the most compressed statement in any literature of what this book calls the jacket become body.

Three things in the passage matter for our dispute. First, absorbing the square takes a lifetime — not a weekend conference or a one-day workshop. Its stages are measured in decades. Any comparison of gathering styles that ignores the time it takes to form people, as Part Six argued, compares things that can’t be compared. Second, the passage puts freedom at the end of a long submission to form, not at the beginning. The young Confucius set his heart on learning — the study of ritual, music and the classics — and took his stand inside them; only at the end does constraint become indistinguishable from spontaneity. That’s a direct rebuke to any romance of the unplanned. What looks like unconstrained behaviour in a master is the fruit of the most thorough discipline. Third — and this cuts against the designers — the square Confucius absorbs isn’t a method invented for an occasion but a tradition received and made his own. The Analects is explicit that he passed things on rather than inventing them (7.1: 述而不作, “I transmit; I do not create”). The seventy-year-old’s freedom is the freedom of a tradition lived in, not of a technique applied.

§ 2. The rustic and the clerk: ancient names for baggy and boxed

If this book’s idea has an ancient version, it’s Analects 6.18:

質勝文則野，文勝質則史。文質彬彬，然後君子。When substance prevails over refinement, one is rustic; when refinement prevails over substance, one is a clerk. Only when refinement and substance are in due proportion is one a person of quality.

The two failures Confucius names are almost exactly the two in Snowden’s title. 野 yě — literally “the wilds”, the uncultivated land outside the city walls — is the person whose natural substance has had no shaping: sincere, perhaps, but rough, unpredictable, driven by impulse. That’s the baggy. 史 shǐ — the scribe, the archivist, the clerk of ceremonies — is the person whose form has outrun their substance: correct in every ritual, and hollow. That’s the boxed. And Confucius’s remedy isn’t a third thing placed between them but a balance within a person: 彬彬 bīnbīn, an even blend in which form and substance are fused.

The passage helps in two ways. First, it confirms that the opposition is ancient and that its terms are relative, not absolute. Rustic and clerk are judged from the standpoint of a cultivated person, and what counts as rustic depends on the refinement it’s measured against; a village elder may seem rustic to a court official, and the court official a clerk to a sage. That’s our idea in Confucian dress. Second, it shows that the classical answer wasn’t to design a method sitting between rusticity and clerkliness, but to form people — and communities of people — in whom the two are reconciled. The place where baggy and boxed are overcome isn’t the event; it’s the character.

Two more passages sharpen this. Asked about the root of ritual (3.4), Confucius says that in ceremonies it’s better to be sparing than extravagant, and in mourning it’s better to grieve deeply than to get every observance right. Form serves substance, and where they clash, substance wins. And at 3.3 he asks what someone without humaneness (仁 rén) could possibly have to do with ritual or music. Ritual without the inner quality it’s meant to express isn’t just useless; it’s a kind of fraud. Both passages warn the designers: a form that doesn’t express and nurture something its participants already have, or are coming to have, is clerkish however clever. Both warn the traditionalists too, because they assume ritual is indispensable — the question is never whether to have it, but whether it’s alive.

§ 3. Sprouts and the press-frame: Mencius against Xunzi

Within two centuries of Confucius, his tradition split over the question beneath our dispute: does human goodness grow by itself if nothing blocks it, or does it have to be imposed on a stubborn nature by deliberate effort?

Mencius (fourth century BCE) took the first view. Human beings, he argued (2A6), have four “sprouts” (四端 sì duān) — of compassion, shame, deference and discernment — as surely as they have four limbs. Anyone who suddenly saw a child about to fall into a well would feel alarm and concern, not from calculation but from the sprout of compassion. Moral growth is the nurturing of these sprouts until they become the full virtues. Goodness is native; it needs tending, not manufacturing.

Xunzi (third century BCE) took the second. His chapter titled “Human Nature Is Bad” (性惡, chapter 23) opens with a thesis designed to scandalise Mencius’s followers: human nature is bad, and its goodness comes from deliberate effort (偽 wěi). Left alone, people are born loving profit, envious and full of sensual desires; follow those and conflict and disorder follow. Goodness is the work of the ancient sage-kings, who devised ritual and right conduct to order those desires. Xunzi’s guiding image comes from carpentry:

故枸木必將待檃栝烝矯然後直，鈍金必將待礱厲然後利。Thus warped wood must await the press-frame and steaming before it becomes straight; blunt metal must await the whetstone before it becomes sharp.

The press-frame (檃栝 yǐnkuò) is a device for straightening timber by steady pressure. For Xunzi, ritual is the press-frame of human nature: an external structure, deliberately designed, that, applied over time, produces a straightness nature alone never would. And crucially, in his chapter on ritual (禮論, chapter 19), Xunzi argued that the rites came about because the ancient kings, hating disorder, set them up to apportion and nurture desires — to give each person a measure. Ritual is designed; it’s the product of a sage’s intelligence responding to a problem.

The match with our dispute is close and instructive, though not exact. Mowles stands nearer Mencius: his confidence that something valuable emerges when conversation isn’t blocked, that the new and spontaneous will sprout if you leave space, is a Mencian confidence in the native goods of human relating. Snowden stands nearer Xunzi: his conviction that unstructured groups fall prey to entrainment and the dominance of recognised power, and that carefully designed constraints are needed for fair and creative deliberation, is a Xunzian diagnosis with a Xunzian cure. The triopticon is a press-frame. Its roles and sequences are wěi — artifice devised by a practitioner-sage in response to disorder.

But the ancient debate also shows what both modern sides leave out. Xunzi’s press-frame only works through sustained pressure: the warped wood has to be held and steamed over time. A press-frame applied for an afternoon straightens nothing. And Xunzi insisted that the point of ritual is to transform the person until the artifice becomes second nature — until, as he put it elsewhere, learning enters through the ear, settles in the heart, spreads through the limbs and shows in conduct. Snowden’s method is Xunzian in its diagnosis but not in its timescale: it offers the press-frame without the years of steaming. And Mencius never said sprouts flourish untended. His sprouts need deliberate cultivation, carried out within the ritual forms he took for granted as much as Xunzi did. Mowles’s Mencianism is selective: it trusts the sprouts and forgets the tending.

§ 4. The farmer of Song: neither neglect nor forcing

Mencius gives us, in one short story, the ancient rebuke to both sides. In a long discussion of how the vital energy, qì, should be nurtured (2A2), he tells of a man from the state of Song who, worried that his sprouts weren’t growing, went out and pulled at them to help them along. He came home exhausted and told his family he’d been helping the sprouts grow. His son ran out to look and found them withered. Mencius draws the moral precisely. Few people in the world don’t help their sprouts grow like this. Those who think tending is useless and abandon the field are the ones who don’t weed. Those who try to hurry growth along are the ones who pull at sprouts. And that doesn’t just fail to help — it does harm.

Two mistakes, and they match our twin idea almost exactly. The farmer who abandons the field and doesn’t weed makes the baggy mistake: the belief that growth will happen by itself if only you stay out of the way. The farmer who pulls at sprouts makes the boxed mistake: the belief that growth can be produced by acting directly on the thing that grows. For Mencius, neither is cultivation. Cultivation is weeding — clearing obstacles, tending conditions, the patient attention that neither abandons nor forces.

The bearing on our dispute couldn’t be stronger. Snowden’s best insight — that enabling constraints shape conditions without deciding outcomes — is a theory of weeding. His method’s risk, as Questions IV and V argued, is that its elaborate choreography of roles and phases turns into pulling at sprouts: trying to produce emergence by specifying in advance the steps it must go through. Mowles’s best insight — that new things arise in gaps and frustrations nobody can plan — is the recognition that sprouts grow by themselves. His essay’s risk, as Questions XV and XVI argued, is abandoning the field: refusing to pull, it also refuses to weed, leaving the weaker plants to be shaded out by the strong. The farmer of Song warns the designer; the farmer who won’t weed warns the traditionalist. The figure Mencius praises — the patient cultivator who tends conditions without forcing outcomes — is neither of them. It’s perhaps what both are trying to be.

§ 5. Cook Ding and the hollows of the ox

The Daoist tradition came into this debate as a critic of Confucian ritual as such, and its critique deserves a hearing, because it’s the strongest version of Mowles’s case. The Zhuangzi, in the chapter called “Webbed Toes” (駢拇, chapter 8), attacks the imposing of measures on nature with an image as memorable as Xunzi’s press-frame:

鳧脛雖短，續之則憂；鶴脛雖長，斷之則悲。Though the duck’s legs are short, to lengthen them would grieve it; though the crane’s legs are long, to cut them would bring it sorrow.

The same chapter says that anything that needs the hook, the line, the compass and the square to be made correct has had its nature pared away. Here the carpenter’s square from Analects 2.4 comes back as an instrument of violence. Every imposed measure, on this view, deforms what it measures; the Confucians’ rites and virtues are so many attempts to lengthen ducks and shorten cranes. The facilitated workshop Mowles describes is exactly such a procedure: participants of every temperament and gift put through one uniform sequence, their contributions trimmed to fit the flip chart and the dot. On its own ground, Zhuangzi’s critique of the boxed is unanswerable.

But the Zhuangzi doesn’t stop at criticising imposed form, and here it parts company with any romance of formlessness. In the chapter on nourishing life (養生主, chapter 3) it gives us Cook Ding, who carves an ox for Lord Wenhui with such grace that every movement of his hand and shoulder, every push of his knee, is in rhythm, as if he were performing an ancient dance. The lord marvels at his skill. Ding replies that what he cares about is the Way, which goes beyond skill. When he began, he saw nothing but the whole ox; after three years he no longer saw the whole ox; now he meets it with his spirit rather than looking with his eyes. He follows the natural pattern (依乎天理), striking into the big hollows and guiding the blade through the big openings, going along with how things inherently are. A good cook changes his knife once a year, because he cuts; an ordinary cook changes it once a month, because he hacks. Ding has had his knife for nineteen years, and its edge is as fresh as if it had just come off the whetstone, because there are spaces between the joints and the blade has no thickness; put what has no thickness into what has space, and there’s plenty of room for the blade to move.

This isn’t formlessness. Cook Ding’s freedom is the freedom of someone who has learned, over nineteen years, to perceive the lǐ — the built-in pattern — of the ox and to move with it. His form isn’t imposed on the ox, as the Confucian rites are imposed on nature in “Webbed Toes”; nor is it absent, as in the ordinary cook’s hacking. It’s a form discovered in the material and absorbed into the body until carving becomes dance. That’s the third possibility neither modern essay spells out: not design imposed on a group, and not openness left to whatever forces happen to win, but a trained perception of the pattern already latent in a particular group at a particular moment, and a responsiveness to it. It’s what Part One’s closing word called reading the grain before lifting the blade.

And the story has one more detail that might have been written for Mowles’s title. Even so, Ding says, whenever he comes to a knot — a tangled place — he sees its difficulty, becomes wary and alert, his gaze settles, his movements slow, and he moves the blade very delicately, until with a soft thud the parts come apart like a clod of earth crumbling to the ground. That’s staying with the trouble in its most exact sense: not abandoning skill when things get difficult, and not applying it mechanically, but the master’s heightened attention exactly where the pattern resists. The knot is where the master slows down. It isn’t where he throws the knife away.

§ 6. The usefulness of the empty room

The Daodejing supplies the image to close this Part, because it reconciles both halves of our idea in a single figure. Chapter 11 reads:

三十輻共一轂，當其無，有車之用。埏埴以為器，當其無，有器之用。鑿戶牖以為室，當其無，有室之用。故有之以為利，無之以為用。Thirty spokes share one hub; it is in the emptiness at its centre that the cart has its use. Clay is shaped into a vessel; it is in the emptiness within that the vessel has its use. Doors and windows are cut to make a room; it is in the emptiness within that the room has its use. Thus what is there gives the benefit; what is not there gives the use.

Mowles is right that a gathering’s usefulness lies in its emptiness — in the space left open for what hasn’t been thought yet. A room crammed with procedure, like a pot filled with clay, is useless. But the Daodejing is just as clear that emptiness is only useful because it’s bounded. The hub’s emptiness works because thirty spokes meet there; the pot’s because clay surrounds it; the room’s because walls enclose it and doors and windows have been cut. Take away the walls and there’s no room, just the open field, whose emptiness is no use to anyone looking for shelter. Snowden is right that the walls matter; Mowles is right that the emptiness matters; and Laozi’s point is that you can’t have either without the other, because each is made by the other. 有 yǒu and 無 wú — presence and absence, form and openness — aren’t opposites to be balanced by some third term. They’re two sides of one usefulness.

So the ancient argument reaches, by several roads, a conclusion the modern essays approach and miss. The opposition of baggy and boxed — rustic and clerk, untended field and pulled sprout, hacked ox and pared nature — is old and relative. It’s overcome not by a method placed between its poles, but by a trained perception of the pattern in things (lǐ), by forms absorbed over years until they become desire (the square at seventy), by tending rather than forcing (the farmer who weeds), by heightened attention at the knot (Cook Ding), and by recognising that bounded emptiness is the only useful kind (the room of chapter 11). None of these are techniques. They’re dispositions, formed in people and communities over time. That’s the deepest reproach the classical tradition has for both modern sides: each, in his own way, has looked for the answer in the design — or the absence of design — of events, while the classical tradition looked for it in the formation of the people who gather.

The ancients located the answer in people rather than procedures. That leaves a modern question unanswered: who gets to speak in the room, and who decides?

Part EightThe politics of the room: ideal speech, three faces of power, and the agonistic alternative

Where we areWe have the psychology and we have the ancient philosophy. What we still lack is the politics.
What this asksPart Eight asks who actually holds power in each kind of room, whose speech counts there, and what the evidence from real experiments in deliberation says.
In brief 13 min read
  • Neither room meets Habermas’s ideal speech situation. Foucault’s aim is the more realistic one: games of power played with minimal domination, and kept reversible.
  • Lukes: the designer holds agenda power, and both rooms shape what their participants want (the third face of power).
  • Young: each room privileges one style of speech, expert exposition in one and reflexive interpretation in the other.
  • Evidence: like-minded groups polarise (Sunstein), while designed, balanced deliberation works (Fishkin, Ireland). Above all, who is in the room matters most.
  • Mouffe: real agonism needs a frame that can itself be contested, and real adversaries.

TakeawayFive conditions for a just gathering: composition, a declared and contestable form, plural modes of speech, agonistic conflict, reversible power.

Strip away the talk of facilitation and method, and both essays turn out to be about politics. Neither says so, but they are. Snowden’s case against the open room is about domination: if nobody designs the constraints, he says, recognised power decides who gets to speak and which ideas survive. Mowles’s case against the facilitated workshop is about alienation: over-designed process turns people into drones and takes away their ability to affect each other and be affected. And Jackson’s question, which both men pass along and neither answers, is about justice: are the disadvantaged protected? These are the oldest questions in democratic theory, shrunk down to the size of a meeting room. The last fifty years of democratic theory have worked on them hard, and neither essay draws on any of it. So that’s what this Part does. We’ll take, in turn, Habermas’s ideal of undistorted communication and Foucault’s reply; Steven Lukes’s three dimensions of power; Iris Marion Young’s critique of deliberation; what the deliberative experiments actually found; and Chantal Mouffe’s agonistic alternative.

§ 1. The dream of a room without power

Jürgen Habermas built his theory of communicative action through the 1970s and set it out in full in The Theory of Communicative Action (1981). Its core claim is that whenever we communicate seriously, we quietly assume it’s possible to reach agreement through nothing but the force of the better argument. He called the idealised conditions for that the ideal speech situation. Anyone competent can take part. Anyone can question any claim, make any claim, and say what they feel, want and need. And nobody can be stopped from doing those things by pressure from inside or outside. Habermas didn’t think such a situation ever actually exists. His point was that we have to assume it in order to argue at all — so it gives us a yardstick for measuring real conversations and spotting where they’re bent out of shape.

You can read Snowden’s triopticon as an engineer’s attempt to get close to that ideal. Spreading roles across the hierarchy, holding back the first confident voice, building challenge into the ritual, keeping the convener out of the content — each of these is a way of cutting down the distortion that recognised power brings into a conversation. It’s an honourable aim with a distinguished philosophical pedigree. But measure it against Habermas’s own conditions and the limits show. In the ideal speech situation anyone can question anything at any point. The triopticon keeps Ravens quiet in its opening phase, hands challenge to the Coyote as a job, and puts the shape of the whole conversation beyond question, because that was decided beforehand. So the method reduces some distortions by adding others. It’s a better approximation than the sticky-dot workshop. It isn’t the real thing. And the designer’s claim to be neutral, as Part Three argued, is exactly the kind of claim Habermas’s critique was built to expose.

More surprisingly, you can read Mowles’s open conference as another attempt at the same ideal. With no designed procedure, everyone is formally free to speak, question and raise any topic, and the group’s habit of talking about how it’s talking is a way of catching distortion as it happens. But Habermas’s analysis bites here too. Being formally free to speak isn’t the same as being able to. The inner pressures that stop someone using that freedom — shyness, not knowing the group’s language, a sense of one’s own lower status — work hardest in exactly the room that offers no designed protection. The conference comes close to the ideal speech situation for its established members and falls well short of it for newcomers. Neither room, held up to Habermas, is undistorted. Each bends things in its own way.

§ 2. Playing power games with as little domination as possible
