Michel Foucault’s reply to Habermas, scattered through interviews from the last years of his life, was that the dream of power-free communication isn’t just out of reach — it doesn’t make sense. For Foucault, power isn’t a substance some people have and others lack, and it isn’t a distortion of an otherwise neutral medium. It’s a relationship, one that produces things as well as holding them down, and it’s there in every interaction. There’s no place outside power from which you could purify a conversation. In a 1984 interview on the ethics of care for the self, Foucault accepted that power relations are unavoidable but drew a sharp line between them and states of domination — where power relations have set hard and lopsided, leaving one side no room to move. The job, he suggested, isn’t to dissolve power in some utopia of transparent talk. It’s to give ourselves the rules, techniques, ethics and practices of the self that let power games be played with as little domination as possible.

That sentence is, in effect, the philosophical charter for what this book has been arguing. It rules out Snowden’s quiet Habermasianism — the idea that good design can get you close to a room where only the better argument wins — because it denies any room is free of power. It equally rules out the quiet romanticism in Mowles’s essay — the idea that leaving the conversation open lets something more authentic emerge — because openness is a field of power relations too, and it can harden into domination for anyone who lacks the resources to play. What it asks for instead is attention to whether power in a given setting is reversible: whether the people who are losing out at one moment have the means to push back, resist and change the rules at the next. That’s the test both rooms should face. The question isn’t whether power is present. It always is. The question is whether the game it shapes can still be replayed on different terms.

Elias, whose sociology sits underneath complex responsive processes, would have recognised this; his account of power as a shifting balance within webs of interdependence is a close cousin of Foucault’s. That makes the silence of Mowles’s essay on domination all the more striking. His own tradition has, in Elias, exactly what’s needed to answer Jackson’s question in Foucault’s terms — to say that power in the open room is relational and negotiable rather than fixed, and to spell out what practices keep it that way. The essay doesn’t use any of it.

§ 3. The three faces of power

Steven Lukes’s Power: A Radical View (1974; second edition 2005) gives us a sharper tool. Lukes split power into three dimensions. The first, linked with Robert Dahl, is the one you can see in decisions: A has power when A beats B in an open conflict. The second, linked with Peter Bachrach and Morton Baratz, works through the agenda: A can win by making sure certain issues never reach the table at all, using the built-in bias of institutions to keep grievances quiet. The third, which Lukes added, is the sneakiest: A can have power over B by shaping what B wants, sees and prefers, so that B goes along with an arrangement that’s against B’s real interests because B can’t imagine anything else.

Snowden’s critique of the open room works in the first two dimensions. Without structure, he says, the senior and the confident win the conversation (first dimension), and the habits of the regulars decide which ideas get picked up and which drop away (second dimension). The triopticon is built to counter both. But it does that by concentrating second-dimensional power in the designer. The shape of the event — what gets discussed, in what order, by whom, in what groups, aiming at what kind of output — is an agenda in Bachrach and Baratz’s sense, fixed before anyone walks in. Participants can’t raise the question of whether the event should take this shape at all; the method itself keeps that question off the table. The designed room doesn’t get rid of the second face of power. It moves it upstream.

It’s the third dimension, though, where both rooms are most exposed and least aware of it. The open conference shapes what its members want to say, what they think counts as a good contribution, which questions seem worth asking — through the tradition’s vocabulary, its key texts, and the skill it prizes most, talking about how you talk. Members feel all this as their own genuine interest. The triopticon shapes what counts as a contribution too: something an Eagle can present, Ravens in trios can make sense of, Beavers can build on, and that can be clustered on a wall and owned by a named person. A contribution that doesn’t fit that shape — an unease nobody can quite put into words, a grief, a refusal — has nowhere to go. Each room exercises third-dimensional power by defining what its participants recognise as worth wanting. Lukes’s third face is the political name for what Parts One and Six called the jacket: the form that shapes desire so completely that it feels like freedom.

§ 4. Whose way of talking counts?

Iris Marion Young, in Inclusion and Democracy (2000), pushed a further objection against deliberative democracy, and it hits both rooms equally. Theories of deliberation, she argued, tend to favour one style of speech — articulate, calm, orderly, argumentative. That style isn’t evenly spread across society; it tracks education, class, gender and culture. People who talk differently — with passion, through stories, through rhetoric or gesture — get heard as less reasonable, and what they say gets marked down. Young argued that genuinely inclusive deliberation has to recognise other ways of communicating: greeting, publicly acknowledging others as participants; rhetoric, the emotional and figurative side of speech that makes it land with a particular audience; and narrative, telling the story of where you stand, which makes visible what abstract argument can’t.

Hold the triopticon up to Young and you see that it favours expert presentation in its opening phase, sense-making in the middle and concept-building later on. Those are the registers of the educated professional. Imagine a participant whose most important contribution is a story about what it was like to be on the receiving end of the system under discussion. In this sequence she has no natural place. She isn’t an Eagle, and her story doesn’t turn easily into a cluster. The open conference, for its part, favours the reflective, psychoanalytically tinged style of group analysis: noticing and naming group dynamics, interpreting, sitting with ambiguity. That’s a learned style too, and unevenly spread. Someone who speaks bluntly, or tells stories instead of reflecting on process, or gets angry, may find what she says treated as a symptom rather than an argument. Neither essay asks which ways of speaking its favoured room actually makes room for. Young’s question is the one that would test both men’s claims to serve the least powerful.

§ 5. What the experiments actually show

The argument between baggy and boxed isn’t beyond testing, and there’s a large research literature on deliberation that bears on it. Neither essay cites any of it. Three findings matter most.

The first is Cass Sunstein’s “law of group polarization” (2002), drawn from a large body of social-psychology research. When like-minded people deliberate together, they tend to end up at a more extreme version of where they started. Two things drive it. The pool of arguments is limited, so members mostly hear arguments for what they already leaned towards. And there’s social comparison: people shift to look good against what they take the group norm to be. This backs up Snowden’s worry about the club in a specific way. A community that shares a tradition and mostly deliberates inside it isn’t just at risk of standing still; it’s at risk of drifting towards a purer version of its own assumptions. Mowles’s essay, which presents the conference as a place of productive frustration, doesn’t consider this.

The second is the record of deliberative polling, which James Fishkin developed from the late 1980s and summed up in When the People Speak (2009). You poll a random sample of citizens, give them balanced briefing papers, bring them together for a weekend of moderated small-group discussion and plenary sessions with competing experts, and then poll them again. Fishkin’s results, repeated in many countries, are that participants know more afterwards, their opinions often change a lot, and the polarisation Sunstein’s law predicts generally doesn’t happen. What makes the difference is the design: balanced information, trained moderators, mixed groups. That evidence supports Snowden’s central claim — that well-designed structure can beat both the club and the unmoderated crowd — more strongly than anything Snowden himself offers. It’s odd that his essay rests the case for structured deliberation on practitioner say-so when a solid body of evidence was right there.

The third is the experience of citizens’ assemblies, the best known being the Irish Citizens’ Assembly of 2016–18. Ninety-nine randomly chosen citizens, with a chair, heard expert presentations, deliberated in facilitated small groups and made recommendations on contested questions, including abortion; their recommendations fed into the 2018 referendum. In structure, the assembly looks like the triopticon: expert input, then small-group sense-making, then collective formulation. But it differs in one way that dwarfs every question of internal design. Its members were chosen by lot. What these experiments suggest is that the most important thing about the politics of a room isn’t how the people in it are arranged but who they are. Random selection tackles the club problem at the root, by making sure the room contains people who don’t share a tradition, and goes at least part of the way on Young’s problem, by making sure it contains people who talk in many different ways. Neither Snowden nor Mowles discusses who is in the room or how they got there. Both take the make-up of the gathering as given and argue about its form. The deliberative research suggests they’ve been arguing about the second most important question.

§ 6. Adversaries, and the frame they fight within

Chantal Mouffe’s agonistic theory of democracy, set out in The Democratic Paradox (2000) and On the Political (2005), gives us the last angle. Against Habermas, Mouffe argued that a consensus without exclusion is impossible. Every consensus is a temporary dominance that rests on shutting out some alternative, and pretending that rational deliberation can reach agreement beyond power just disguises that dominance. You can’t remove conflict from political life. What democratic institutions can do is turn antagonism — the relationship between enemies who want to destroy each other — into agonism — the relationship between adversaries who share a commitment to the institutions they fight within, while disagreeing deeply about what should fill them.

Mouffe gives Snowden some support. The triopticon’s ritualised opposition — the odd numbers, the appointed Coyote, the design against early consensus — is an agonistic device. It builds disagreement into the structure so that it can be voiced without becoming destructive. That’s a real strength, and one the consensus-hunting workshop Mowles describes clearly lacks. But Mouffe’s theory makes a demand the triopticon can’t meet. For agonism to be real rather than managed, the frame itself has to stay open to challenge; whatever dominance a set of institutions embodies has to be contestable by the people it disadvantages. A Coyote who can challenge any assumption except the assumption that there should be Coyotes isn’t an adversary in Mouffe’s sense. He’s a licensed critic inside an order nobody gets to question — Gluckman’s ritual of rebellion again, now in the language of political theory.

Mouffe gives Mowles some support too. His insistence that frustration and conflict are where new thinking happens, and his reluctance to design them away, is agonistic in spirit. But agonism needs adversaries — people who genuinely disagree about fundamentals. A conference of people who share a tradition may host lively arguments, but they’re arguments among friends, inside a shared consensus. That isn’t the clash of adversaries Mouffe sees as the lifeblood of democratic politics. Measured against Mouffe, the open conference isn’t too full of conflict. It’s not full enough: its disagreements are the ones a shared tradition allows.

§ 7. What the politics of the room actually needs

Put these threads together and democratic theory suggests that a gathering worthy of the concerns all three traditions claim to share would need to meet at least five conditions. Neither essay tackles any of them directly. First, its composition: it would have to include people who don’t share the organisers’ tradition, ideally picked in a way that doesn’t just reproduce existing networks. Second, its form: this would have to be declared, so its power over the agenda is visible, and open to challenge, so participants can question the form itself and not just what’s said inside it. Third, its ways of speaking: these would have to be plural, with room for greeting, story and passion as well as presentation and reflection. Fourth, its conflicts: these would have to be agonistic, reaching the frame as well as the content. And fifth, its power relations: these would have to stay reversible, in Foucault’s sense, so that whoever loses out at one moment still has the means to change the rules at the next.

Five conditions, two rooms
ConditionTriopticonOpen conference
Composition beyond the organisers’ traditionSilentFails by design
Form declared and contestableDeclared, but not contestableContestable for members, undeclared to newcomers
Plural modes of speechWeak: favours expertise and sense-makingPartial: favours reflexive interpretation
Conflict reaching the framePartial: ritualised, frame protectedPartial: argument among friends
Reversible powerSilentFor members only

The triopticon meets the third condition poorly and the second only partly; it partly meets the fourth; and it says nothing on the first and fifth. The open conference meets the fifth for its members and fails it for newcomers; it fails the first by design; and it meets the second and third only within its own tradition’s idiom. Neither room is a small-scale democracy. Each is one particular settlement of the politics of the room, and each would get better by taking seriously what the other’s critique shows about it.

Five conditions for a just room, and neither room meets them all. You might reasonably ask: so what should a convener actually do?

Part NineToward a discipline of declared form

Where we areEight Parts of diagnosis is a lot. You deserve to know what to do on Monday morning.
What this asksPart Nine turns from critique to proposal: a practical discipline any convener could carry into any room.
In brief 10 min read
  • A discipline, not a method: a quality of attention that can be carried into any gathering.
  • Constraints are declared in three registers: governing, enabling and constitutive.
  • Six questions are put to any gathering: what is fixed; what has been shaped and by whom; what is presupposed; who is present and who is absent; which modes of speech count; how the form can be changed.
  • Its rhythm runs at three moments: threshold, breakdown and close, never continuously.
  • Specific demands are made of each tradition, the discipline’s own failure modes are named, and it ends in cultivation over design.

A critique that ends in more critique leaves you with nothing to do on Monday morning. So let’s take stock. The Parts so far have argued four things. Neither the open room nor the designed room escapes this book’s idea. The lopsided way each side sees the other has deep roots — in how tacit knowing works, in the way tools disappear while we use them, in how habits sink into the body, and in the styles of thought that groups share. The classical Chinese tradition found the reconciliation of form and spontaneity not in designing events but in forming people. And democratic theory asks every gathering to attend to who is in it, whether its form can be challenged, how many ways of speaking it welcomes, how far its conflicts reach, and whether its power relations can be reversed. The question now is what practice follows. The answer here is deliberately modest. It isn’t a new method to set beside the triopticon or the open conference. The world has enough methods, and a new one would just become a new jacket. It’s a discipline — in the older sense of a practice through which a person or community shapes itself — that you can carry into any gathering, whatever its form. It has one aim: that the people who gather should be able to see, name and change the forms that bind them.

§ 1. Why a discipline, not a method

The difference matters, and it comes straight out of Foucault’s line in Part Eight. A method tells you what to do: a sequence of steps, a share-out of roles, a set of outputs. A discipline tells you what kind of attention to bring to whatever you do. Methods can be licensed, packaged and handed on. Disciplines can only be practised, and you learn them slowly — the way Merleau-Ponty described, by absorbing them into the body. A method is a garment; a discipline is a way of wearing garments. This proposal has to be the second kind because the argument of this book rules out the first. If every method is a jacket, and every jacket tends to vanish from its wearer’s sight, then what we need isn’t a better jacket. It’s a trained ability to see jackets as jackets — above all, your own.

§ 2. Declaring three kinds of constraint

The first part of the discipline is saying out loud what constraints are in play, using the three kinds we separated in Question II, now filled out by everything since.

Governing constraints fix outcomes, or fix the shape outcomes must take, before anyone starts: a decision that has to be reached by a certain time, a deliverable that has to be produced, a format contributions have to fit. They’re usually the easiest to declare and the ones most often hidden, because a gathering that admits up front that its conclusions must take a certain shape looks less participatory than one that doesn’t. The discipline asks for them to be stated at the start, along with whose authority they come from.

Enabling constraints shape how people interact without deciding what comes out: roles, sequences, group sizes, rules about whose turn it is, periods of silence. These are the designer’s proper territory, and the triopticon is an elaborate system of them. The discipline asks for them to be declared not just as procedures but as choices — for the convener to say not only what the form is but why this one was picked over others, and who picked it. It also asks the declaration to name what the form is meant to prevent, and what it might squash in the process.

Constitutive constraints make the gathering what it is: the shared tradition, vocabulary, key texts, habits and history members bring with them. They’re the hardest to declare, because people hold them in the background and because naming them threatens members’ sense of being free. They’re also the most important to declare, because they’re the constraints newcomers walk into like glass walls. The discipline asks a gathering with a strong constitutive form to make some of it explicit to people who don’t share it yet — its key terms, its unspoken rules about interpreting and taking turns, its usual ways of disagreeing — not as rules to obey but as an honest account of what the newcomer is walking into.

§ 3. Six questions

The second part of the discipline is a set of questions anyone can put to a gathering — the convener, the participants, or both. They come out of the earlier Parts and line up with the conditions listed at the end of Part Eight.

One: what’s fixed here, and on whose authority? This brings governing constraints into view, and where their legitimacy comes from. It’s answered honestly when the convener can name the outcomes the gathering has to deliver and the person or body requiring them.

Two: what conditions have been shaped, by whom, and to prevent what? This brings enabling constraints into view, along with the designer’s power over the agenda. It’s answered honestly when the designer is named and the form is justified by pointing to specific dangers — entrainment, domination, rushing to consensus — rather than being presented as neutral.

Three: what does this gathering take for granted that some people here might not have? This brings constitutive constraints into view, and the price of getting in. It’s answered honestly when established members can name the vocabulary, skills and norms their own participation depends on, and can see that not everyone shares them.

Four: who is here, how did they get here, and who isn’t here? This brings composition into view — which Part Eight showed is the most important and most neglected factor. It’s answered honestly when the gathering can say how its membership was put together, who that process consistently leaves out, and whether any of the members were chosen by a method — lot, nomination by outsiders, deliberately inviting adversaries — designed to bring in people who don’t share the organisers’ tradition.

Five: which ways of speaking does this form welcome, and which does it mark down? This brings third-dimensional power into view, and Young’s concern for greeting, rhetoric and story. It’s answered honestly when you can show that the form makes room for contributions that aren’t argument or reflection — stories, emotion, refusal — and when established participants can see that the styles they’re most comfortable in are particular, not universal.

Six: how can participants challenge and change the form itself — during the gathering, not just afterwards? This brings into view whether power can be reversed and whether the frame can be contested. It’s answered honestly when there’s a recognised moment or channel within the gathering where people can propose changing the form, and when such proposals have a real chance of being taken up.

§ 4. When to ask

Polanyi’s warning from Part Six has to govern how these questions get used. Pull tacit constraints into the spotlight and you disturb how they work. A gathering that questioned its own form non-stop would never get round to anything else, and would be the most exhausting version of what Griffin called the doubling of process. So the discipline works by moving back and forth, not by constant exposure. It suggests three moments in a gathering’s life to ask the questions. At the threshold, before the real work starts, the convener declares the governing and enabling constraints and gives newcomers an account of the constitutive ones. At a breakdown — when, in Heidegger’s sense, the form suddenly becomes visible because it’s failing someone — the gathering stops to ask whether the form should change. And at the close, the gathering looks back at what its form made possible and what it shut down, so the form can be revised before next time. In between, the form is allowed to fade back into use, as tools have to if any work is going to get done.

§ 5. What the discipline asks of each side

The discipline makes specific demands of each tradition in this dispute, and it’s worth stating them plainly.

Of the open conference, it asks that the liturgy be called a liturgy: that supper, keynote and open conversation be recognised as a form someone chose, which could have been different. It asks that newcomers get, at the threshold, some account of the tradition’s vocabulary and its unspoken norms — not to tame them, but to lower the price of entry. It asks that the gathering deliberately include some people who don’t share the tradition, and that their presence be treated not as a problem to be absorbed but as the kind of crisis through which, on MacIntyre’s account, traditions grow. It asks that the habit of talking about how we’re talking be stretched to talking about who isn’t talking, and why. And it asks that the tradition’s faith in emergent relating be tempered by the evidence on group polarisation, and by Bion’s account of how unstructured groups slide into basic-assumption thinking.

Of the triopticon and methods like it, it asks that the designer be named and the form justified, not just described. It asks that the method include a recognised moment when participants can challenge and change the roles and sequences themselves — that the Coyote’s licence stretch, at least once, to the frame. It asks that the method make room for story and for styles other than expert presentation and professional sense-making, so that people whose knowledge comes from where they stand rather than from a discipline have somewhere to put it. It asks that the method be practised over time with the same people, so its forms get the chance to be absorbed rather than just imposed — that it become, in Xunzi’s terms, a press-frame applied with the patience the image calls for. And it asks that the method’s symbolic kit be checked for where it comes from and what it does, and renamed if the names can’t survive the check.

Of critical systems thinking, it asks the curator of methods to declare his own position as a position — to admit that the vantage point from which he picks methods is itself a jacket, with its own unspoken commitments about what counts as emancipation and who counts as disadvantaged. And it asks that, where possible, the choice of method be put to the participants it will govern, rather than made for them by an expert in methods.

§ 6. How the discipline can go wrong

Honesty means admitting that this discipline falls under the same idea as everything else, and can fail in ways we can name. It can turn into a method: its six questions become a checklist, run by consultants, licensed and branded, until it’s one more jacket its wearers can’t see. It can spiral into infinite regress: declaring constraints calls for declaring the constraints on declaring, and so on, until the gathering vanishes into its own self-reflection. It can become a performance: constraints declared with no intention of ever changing them, so the declaration ends up legitimising the form instead of opening it to challenge. And it can damage the tacit knowledge that mature practice relies on, by dragging into the spotlight what only works in the background.

There’s no guarantee against these failures — only the safeguards the analysis so far suggests. Keep the discipline a quality of attention, not a procedure. Use it in rhythm — at threshold, breakdown and close — not all the time. Test declarations by whether they ever actually lead to change. And practise it in communities over time, so its own demands can in turn be absorbed and forgotten, then rediscovered when they’re needed. The discipline, in short, has to be held the way Cook Ding holds his knife: not thrown away, not hacked with, but moved lightly through the spaces, and slowed right down at the knot.

§ 7. Forming people, not designing events

The deepest conclusion of this book isn’t about gatherings at all. It’s about the people who gather. Both essays look for the answer to the problem of structure in the shape of events — Snowden in their design, Mowles in their openness. The classical Chinese tradition, the phenomenology of habit and the hermeneutics of tradition all point somewhere else. Form and spontaneity are reconciled, when they are, in people and communities shaped over long periods to see the pattern in things and move with it. Think of the seventy-year-old who follows his heart without stepping outside the square; the cook who, after nineteen years, carves along the hollows of the ox; the organist who has made the instrument part of his body; the tradition that argues with itself about what’s good and goes looking for the rival who might explain its failures. No event, however carefully designed or however open, can stand in for that kind of formation. What events can do is help it or get in its way.

In the end, the discipline of declared form is a discipline of formation. Its goal isn’t better meetings but better practitioners: people who can see their own jackets, step into other people’s without contempt, name the walls of any room they’re in, and suggest moving them when they hem in the people least able to move them alone. Such people will make better use of the triopticon and of the open conference alike, because they’ll hold both as forms rather than as truths. That’s a slower ambition than either essay offers. It’s also, I’d argue, the only one that matches the size of the problem both essays have spotted.

A discipline, not a method. With that, Book One’s argument is complete, and only the verdict remains.

The Final RulingUpon the central idea, the two essays, and the room in Hull

Where we areBook One’s argument is complete. All that remains is the verdict.
What this asksThe Final Ruling gathers every finding and asks what a room worthy of all three traditions would actually require.
In brief 5 min read
  • The central idea and its twin hold against both essays.
  • Snowden is right about kind, power and choice, and wrong about “neither”.
  • Mowles is right about machines, wisdom and uncertainty, and wrong about structure.
  • Jackson is owed a hearing in his own voice.
  • Conditions are laid down for the room in Hull.

We started with one idea: to the strait-jacketed, the other always seems baggy. We tested it on Snowden’s essay and found that his title, his categories, his examples and his proposed trial all show it. He sees the constitutive form of Mowles’s tradition as bagginess because it isn’t designed, and he sees his own method as the middle ground because he’s standing inside it. We tested the twin — to the baggy, the other always seems boxed — on Mowles’s essay and found it holds just as well. He sees the facilitated workshop as a cage because its form is foreign, and his own conference as open because its form has become his body. We looked at the triopticon’s animal names and found that a borrowed bestiary shapes the room as surely as any senior voice, while pretending to be play. And we took apart, claim by claim, the passage in which Snowden indicts the open room, finding that its strongest claims count more against the designed room than against the club — and that its last observation, properly read, names the very bias that undermines its picture of the club.

Then we went looking for the roots of all this. From the philosophy of tacit knowledge, tools and habit, we learned that the rules we live inside are held in a kind of awareness that makes them invisible as rules, and that form shows up mainly when it fails or when it’s foreign. From the sociology of “thought collectives”, we learned that communities see their own style as reality and other styles as arbitrary. From the philosophy of tradition, we learned that shared assumptions make thinking possible as long as they stay open to risk. From the philosophy of law and freedom, we learned that freedom lives where form is both owned and explainable. From classical China, we learned that the opposition of baggy and boxed was named long ago — the rustic and the clerk — and that its resolution lies in cultivated people and in the bounded emptiness of a room. And from democratic theory, we learned that the most important questions about any gathering — who’s there, whose speech counts, whether the frame can be challenged, whether power stays reversible — are questions neither essay asks.

So here’s the verdict. Snowden is right that structure is unavoidable, that its kind matters more than its amount, that open rooms don’t abolish power, and that choosing no constraints is still a choice. He’s wrong to think his method stands outside the line he draws, wrong to treat a tradition’s constitutive form as no form at all, and wrong to propose a trial run inside his own form. Mowles is right that over-designed process can reduce people to compliance, right that knowledge isn’t wisdom, right that frustration and uncertainty can be fertile, and right that machine intimacy threatens the human encounter. He’s wrong to treat structure as such as the enemy of that encounter, wrong to overlook the rigorous form of the practices he admires most, and wrong to leave unanswered the question of who is protected in the room he loves. And Jackson, who hasn’t spoken in his own voice in either essay, is owed a hearing in it.

As for the room in Hull, if it ever meets: let it include, at least in part, people from none of the three traditions. Let each tradition begin by describing itself as the others see it. Let the gathering’s form be declared, justified and open to challenge from the first hour. Let it make room for stories as well as arguments. Let its disagreements reach the frame itself. And let everyone who comes understand that the point isn’t to decide which tradition is right, but to help some of those present see their own jacket for the first time. That would be neither baggy nor boxed. It would be a room whose walls everyone could see — and whose emptiness, bounded, declared and shared, might finally be of use.

Every gathering is clothed. The question worth asking of any room isn’t whether it has walls, but whether the people in it can see them, name them, and — when they hem in the weakest — move them together.

Book One ends here. But the argument does not. A larger reckoning is waiting — with an earlier work, with a whole body of thought, and finally with the author himself.

Book Two
The Wider Reckoning

The Connoisseur put to the test, the wider body of work read again, and the mirror turned on the questioner

Book One looked at two essays published in the same week. Book Two widens the lens in three directions. First, it goes back to an earlier and much bigger work from this imprint, The Connoisseur of Partial Truths — a scholastic tribunal on Michael C. Jackson’s critical systems thinking that has grown, pass by pass, to around three hundred thousand words — and puts it through the same test Book One applied to Snowden and Mowles. Second, it revisits the imprint’s other treatises — on Cynefin and the Stacey Matrix, on Boyd and the Malayan Emergency, on Schismatrix and King Lear, on facilitation, play, the Situationists, the discernment of spirits and the strategy of movement — and asks what each one, read again, has to say about baggy and boxed. Finally, it turns the mirror on the imprint itself: it puts seven objections to this book, boils its findings down to fifty theses, and gives a final ruling. The voice stays the one Book One settled into — careful in method, plain in its English, and just as ready to find against its own author as it was to find against Snowden and Mowles.

Book Two’s main sources are the imprint’s own works. They include The Connoisseur of Partial Truths, the roughly three-hundred-thousand-word tribunal on Jackson, and the imprint’s edition of Kevin Flinn’s A Web of Relations, its complexity corpus of around eight hundred thousand words. They include The Stacey Matrix & the Zen Garden, and The Living Map with its companion Chaos Puzzles. They include The Hand That Enters the River, seventeen questions on human agency finished in October 2026. And they include the wider set of treatises on Cynefin, Boyd, the Malayan Emergency, Schismatrix, King Lear, facilitation, play, spirituality and strategy. Snowden’s and Mowles’s 2026 writings are read as those works record and quote them. Where an imprint work quotes another author, the quotation is credited to that author, not to the imprint.

Part TenThe grid as jacket: the Connoisseur’s three theses read through this book’s idea

Where we areBook One ended with a debt: Jackson was given only a paragraph. And this imprint has already written a far longer tribunal on him.
What this asksPart Ten returns to that tribunal, The Connoisseur of Partial Truths, and asks how Jackson’s great grid looks once you know that every grid is also a garment.
In brief 18 min read
  • The Connoisseur of Partial Truths indicted Jackson’s System of Systems Methodologies as a “generous prison”, his pragmatist turn as the settlement of a “converted judge”, and his whole edifice for a “missing quality”: the texture of local interaction in the living present.
  • Read through this book’s idea, the grid is the most self-conscious jacket in the management sciences: a garment designed to describe every other garment, which therefore cannot see its own seams.
  • The Connoisseur’s most penetrating line — “every grid is a confession” — is the central idea in its earliest form within this imprint.
  • Jackson’s 2025 avowal that his truths are “adequate partial truths” is the nearest any of the three traditions has come to declaring its own jacket, and it deserves more credit than the Connoisseur, or Book One, has yet given it.

TakeawayOf the three jackets compared in Part Five, Jackson’s is the one whose wearer has most often tried to describe it from the inside. That does not free him from the central idea, but it changes the verdict.

Part Five named three jackets and gave the third — the jacket of choosing methods — just a paragraph. That was a debt, and Jackson is owed it. Book One pointed out more than once that Jackson turns up in the exchange between Snowden and Mowles only as a third party spoken for by others. His position is relayed through two traditions that each read him differently. So an argument about whose voice gets heard in a room had, in its own pages, someone who hadn’t yet spoken. The fullest attempt this imprint has made to let Jackson speak, and to answer him, is The Connoisseur of Partial Truths. It would be an odd failure to check everyone else’s jacket and leave that book unexamined. So this Part sets out the Connoisseur’s case against Jackson as fairly as it can, and then asks what happens to that case when this book’s idea is pressed against it.

§ 1. The tribunal and its three claims

The Connoisseur opens with the picture of a mapmaker who arrives after the explorers and draws the map that will from then on decide what counts as land. Jackson — operational researcher, social theorist, dean of the business school at Hull, and for four decades the most systematic mind in applied systems thinking — is cast as that mapmaker. His two culminating books, Critical Systems Thinking and the Management of Complexity (2019) and Critical Systems Thinking: A Practitioner’s Guide (2024), are read as an imperial survey. Philosophy is annexed in one part, the sciences in another, ten methodologies are catalogued and given their territories, and the whole thing is run from a single head office: the System of Systems Methodologies, Jackson’s famous grid matching kinds of problem situation to kinds of method. The treatise questions that survey using the three ideas that run through all the imprint’s work — 道 the moving whole, 理 the pattern in the jade, 勢 the pull of the situation — and it states three claims up front, on the grounds that Jackson is a man who declares his tools before he uses them.

The first claim is the generous prison. The grid is the most transparent, self-aware and useful-to-teach classification the management sciences have come up with — and it’s still a classification, which means it captures things. It pays every methodology the compliment of its own cell. Werner Ulrich, the Connoisseur notes, felt the bars first; Gerald Midgley rattled them; Ralph Stacey simply declined to go into the building. The second claim is the converted judge. Jackson’s late move from a Habermasian master-framework to what he calls systemic pragmatism is presented in his own work as growing up. The Connoisseur argues you can read it as a deal struck with his fiercest critics: he gives up the philosophy and keeps the machinery. The corridor of pragmatism is announced, but it’s still his hotel, and the concierge still tells you which room to take. The third claim is the missing quality. What the whole structure can’t digest — because its basic unit is the methodology and its stage is the intervention — is the texture of ordinary local interaction in the living present, where identity, power and new things are made all the time. Jackson knows this, the Connoisseur says. He admits that Stacey gives a more revealing account of this level than anything in the soft systems tradition — and then files that insight away as one more perspective to draw on. The filing is the failure. Some truths don’t survive being turned into perspectives.

The treatise pursues these claims across some ninety Books — through tribunals on the founders of systems thinking, on Morin and on Wuli-Shili-Renli, on Pepper’s root metaphors and Peirce’s theory of signs, on Indigenous relational thought and Gramsci’s hegemony — and it reaches a two-sided verdict it never lets go of. The grid is convicted as a survey that claims to see from nowhere, and vindicated as something useful when held lightly: an adequate partial truth. Its last big finding, stated in the Book of verdicts, is that critical systems thinking is a magnificent education for practitioners and a consistent misdescription of what they actually do. It forms their judgement while officially giving the credit to frameworks — like a martial-arts school that credits the manual for what only the training passes on. Part Twelve takes that finding up. Here a prior question comes first: what is the grid, seen through this book’s idea?

§ 2. A coat cut for describing coats

Every jacket Book One looked at was cut to be worn in a room: Snowden’s for the designed gathering, Mowles’s for the reflective conversation. Jackson’s is a different sort of thing. It’s a garment cut for describing garments — a method about methods, whose job is to sort the forms other traditions wear, judge how well they suit different situations, and recommend which to wear when. That gives it an unusual relationship to this book’s idea. The designer sees the traditionalist as baggy. The traditionalist sees the designer as boxed. The curator of methods sees both as partial, because his whole kit is built for spotting partiality. Part Five said this in one sentence. It deserves more, because the curator’s jacket is the subtlest of the three, and the easiest to mistake for no jacket at all.

The subtlety is that the grid knows it’s about methods. A designer can forget his method is a method; a tradition can forget its customs are customs. The grid can’t forget that methods are methods, because noticing that is its whole job. What it can forget is that the noticing is done from somewhere. To sort methods by the problem situations they suit, you need a theory of problem situations already — a way of carving up the world into simple and complex, unitary, pluralist and coercive — and that carving doesn’t fall from the sky. It comes out of a particular intellectual upbringing, which the Connoisseur traces to Hull, to operational research, to the Habermas of Knowledge and Human Interests, and to life in a British business school in the 1980s. The grid sees the jacket on every method it sorts. It doesn’t see the loom its own categories were woven on.

That’s why the Connoisseur’s sharpest line — every grid is a confession; it tells you what its maker feared to leave unsorted — stands as the first time this imprint put into words the idea this book is built on. A classification gives its author away: through what it thinks needs classifying, which axes it picks, which cells it leaves empty and what it rules out of scope. To the classifier, what hasn’t been classified looks like disorder — that is, like bagginess. Stacey, who wouldn’t go into the building, looks from inside the grid not like a different kind of order but like a refusal of order, and ends up being given the status of one more perspective, a voice for the neglected level of local interaction. The grid sees the tradition that won’t be gridded exactly the way Snowden saw Mowles’s conference: as a room with nothing fixed except when it starts and when it ends.

§ 3. The prison and the club

The first claim gets a new edge when you put it next to Book One’s analysis of the club. The Connoisseur accused the grid of capture: of giving each methodology the compliment of a cell, and so locking it up. Book One accused the club of another kind of capture: of absorbing its members so completely into its unspoken form that they feel the form as freedom, and of charging newcomers an entry price nobody mentions. These are two styles of confinement, and this book’s idea explains why each one is invisible to the people practising it. The grid confines openly and generously; its bars are drawn on the page, and it invites every inmate to inspect them. The club confines quietly and warmly; it has no bars, just a fireside, and the people sitting round it can’t imagine anyone outside feels shut out.

Which is worse? The Connoisseur, written from a position sympathetic to Stacey’s tradition, preferred the fireside to the bars, and saw in Stacey’s refusal to go in a freedom the grid couldn’t grasp. Book One complicates that preference. A generous prison with its bars drawn on the page is at least a prison you can argue with; its walls are declared. A warm fireside whose exclusions are unspoken is harder to challenge, because it doesn’t admit to excluding anyone. By the standard of the jacket audit — that forms should be visible, open to challenge, and changeable by the people wearing them — Jackson’s grid does better on the first test than the Connoisseur allowed, and Stacey’s tradition does worse. That doesn’t reverse the earlier verdict, which found the grid guilty of claiming to see from nowhere. It just notes that a view which declares its frame, even while claiming too much for it, has met one condition of honesty that a view declaring nothing hasn’t.

To its credit, the Connoisseur saw much of this. Its Book of verdicts honours Jackson under 理 as the carver who shows you the grain and shows you his chisel. It names a scholarly habit rare in the management sciences: every serious objection the treatise pressed had already been printed, in some form, by Jackson himself, in the critique sections of his own books. He’s the only combatant in those tribunals who reliably arms his opponents. That’s a portrait of a man trying to see his own jacket. This book would add only that this effort, which the Connoisseur praised as a personal virtue, is also something the curator’s position makes possible — and something the designer and the traditionalist haven’t matched. Snowden’s postscript admits he’s no neutral judge of his own method. Mowles’s essay admits the inclusion and exclusion at work in his own conference. Jackson builds criticism of his own position into the structure of his books. Of the three, his jacket is the most openly declared. That doesn’t make it no jacket.

§ 4. The converted judge and the trick of the middle way

The second claim — that Jackson’s pragmatist turn gives up the philosophy but keeps the machinery — has an exact parallel in Book One’s analysis of Snowden’s claim to be “neither” baggy nor boxed. Question V argued that Snowden makes the oldest move in polemics: set up two extremes, then stand in the middle. And, as Aristotle knew, where the middle lies depends on who draws the line. The Connoisseur’s reading of Jackson finds a similar move in the history of systems thinking. The paradigm wars of the 1980s set hard systems against soft, functionalist against interpretive, emancipatory against both. Jackson’s complementarism, and later his pragmatism, offered to end the wars by standing above them, from where each paradigm could be given its territory. The peace was real; the Connoisseur grants that the pluralist settlement genuinely stopped the shooting. But the position above the paradigms was itself a paradigm, and its authority to hand out territory was the authority of one combatant who’d found a way to describe himself as the referee.

The parallel is close enough to teach us something. Snowden puts the triopticon between the conference and the workshop; Jackson puts critical systems practice above the methodologies. Each claims a place that isn’t one of the positions in the fight, and each, looked at closely, is one of the positions in the fight, described from the inside. The difference is that Jackson has spent forty years answering that objection and Snowden has spent a postscript. The Connoisseur’s picture of the hotel — the pragmatist corridor announced, the rooms still numbered by his grid, the concierge still recommending which room to take — fits the triopticon with hardly any adjustment. The convener stays out of the content, but the rooms, the corridors and the order you visit them in have been decided by the architect. The converted judge and the designer of the middle way are cousins. Both have worked out that the most powerful position in an argument is the one that looks as if it stands outside it.

§ 5. The missing quality, looked at again

The third claim is the one the Connoisseur bet most on, and it’s the one Book One’s reading of Mowles puts under most pressure. The missing quality was the texture of ordinary local interaction in the living present: the level Stacey’s tradition pays attention to and Jackson’s grid files as a perspective. The Connoisseur held that this level can’t be classified without being lost, so a tradition that attends to it directly — complex responsive processes — has something the grid can’t have. Book One accepted that this level is real and important; Mowles’s essay is a moving defence of it. But Book One also argued, in Questions XII and XIII, that the tradition attends to it through a rigorous form of its own — the group-analytic frame, the conference liturgy, the discipline of noticing — and that the essay’s unspoken assumptions about structure hide that form.

That matters a lot for the third claim. If attending to the living present is itself a trained practice, carried by a tradition with key texts, a vocabulary and a frame, then the missing quality isn’t missing from the grid because the grid has form and the living present doesn’t. It’s missing because the grid’s form and the tradition’s form pay attention to different things. The grid attends to methods and how they fit situations; the tradition attends to relating and its patterns. Each is a jacket cut for a particular way of seeing. The Connoisseur’s sentence — some truths don’t survive being turned into perspectives — is still true. But it now needs a companion: some perspectives don’t recognise themselves as perspectives. People whose tradition has trained them to see the living present see it as a truth beyond any perspective; people whose tradition has trained them to curate perspectives see it as one among many. Neither view comes from nowhere.

The Connoisseur saw this coming in one of its most striking lines, set in capitals in its opening Book: Jackson’s deepest belief is that pluralism can be organised; Stacey’s deepest belief is that organisation is what pluralism looks like before it escapes. It’s a beautiful line, and fair to both men. Through this book’s idea it picks up a third clause the Connoisseur didn’t write but its own logic allows: and each man’s belief is the jacket the other can’t help seeing.

§ 6. Adequate partial truths

The Connoisseur takes its title from a phrase Jackson himself uses in his 2025 paper on the future of systems thinking through action research and critical systems practice: “adequate partial truths”. He borrowed it from Stephen Pepper’s World Hypotheses (1942), where each of the great root metaphors — formism, mechanism, contextualism, organicism — is said to give a partial but adequate account of the world. The Connoisseur reads Jackson’s use of the phrase as a last testament, in which the connoisseur calls his truths partial and calls them adequate, and treats this as the vindicating half of its two-sided verdict: the grid convicted as a survey claiming to see from nowhere, vindicated as something useful when held lightly.

This book would push the point further than the Connoisseur did. To call your own truths partial is to do, for an entire intellectual system, exactly what the jacket audit asks every gathering to do: declare the form you wear as a form, and admit it shows some things and hides others. Jackson’s 2025 paper also takes from Checkland and Holwell the framework of ideas, methodology and area of concern (the FMA framework), along with the test of recoverability: the researcher should declare the framework and methodology in advance, so that any interested outsider can retrace and check the whole process. The Connoisseur gave a whole Book to this test and linked it to the cognitive scientist John Challoner’s account of how frameworks sink below awareness and start to feel self-evident. Challoner names the illness; Checkland names the cure. Declare the framework.

That’s the discipline of declared form, reached by Jackson’s own route. It would be a failure of the fairness this book claims not to say so plainly. Of the three traditions in the proposed room at Hull, the one whose leading figure has committed most explicitly, in print, to declaring his framework in advance and calling his truths partial is the one Book One spent least time on. Snowden’s method declares its procedures but not who wrote them. Mowles’s tradition declares its reflectiveness but not its frame. Jackson declares both, at least in principle. The Connoisseur’s verdict stands — the grid still overreaches when it presents its map of situations as neutral — but the sentence is lighter. Of the three, Jackson has come closest to seeing his own jacket. That he hasn’t seen all of it is just being human, not a special failing.

§ 7. What the grid sees that the others don’t

One more thing needs saying in Jackson’s favour, because this book’s idea demands it. If each jacket sees the others clearly, then the grid sees something about the designer and the traditionalist that they can’t see about themselves, and a fair audit has to record it. What the grid sees is that both are methods, whatever they say. The triopticon presents itself as a set of enabling constraints suited to complexity. The grid sees a methodology with assumptions about the problem situation and about how participants relate, and with a range of proper use outside which it will fail. The open conference presents itself as the refusal of method, a space for relating in the living present. The grid sees a methodology there too — one that assumes participants who share a tradition, have time to spare and can cope with ambiguity, and that will fail where those assumptions don’t hold. The grid’s insistence that every approach has a proper range is, in the end, this book’s idea in the language of operational research: every jacket fits some bodies and not others.

That’s a real insight, and both Snowden and Mowles would gain from it. Snowden argues as though the triopticon were better than the club across the board, when by the grid’s logic it’s better in some situations — mixed groups, low trust, high stakes — and worse in others. Mowles argues as though over-structured process were bad across the board, when by the grid’s logic it’s bad in some situations and essential in others: nobody wants the open-conference format in an operating theatre. The grid’s pluralism, which the Connoisseur rightly found guilty of claiming a view from above, also rightly insists that no form fits everywhere. That insistence is half the jacket audit. The other half — seeing that the classifier’s own form is a form too, fitting some bodies and not others — is the half the grid finds hardest. The Connoisseur supplied it. This book supplies it again, and adds that it applies to everyone, the Connoisseur included.

§ 8. What Jackson might say back

This book has said more than once that Jackson is owed a hearing in his own voice, and that neither the November 2026 essays nor this treatise has given him one. A full hearing would need his own words, at length, which this book has chosen not to reproduce. But fairness allows a reconstruction, offered as just that — a sketch of the reply his published positions suggest he might make to the baggy-and-boxed argument, and to this book’s idea. It’s set down here so that the third tradition in the proposed room at Hull isn’t represented only by its critics.

Jackson might start by pointing out that the argument between Snowden and Mowles is exactly the kind of paradigm quarrel his whole career has tried to get past. Each of them, he might say, has mistaken a partial perspective for a full account of what gatherings are. Snowden sees gatherings mainly through what Jackson’s own work would call the lens of interrelationships and complex adaptation — patterns of entrainment and constraint to be adjusted by design. Mowles sees them mainly through the lens of purposes and power — the meanings people make together and the patterns of inclusion and exclusion they reproduce. Each lens shows something real. Neither shows everything. Critical systems thinking, Jackson might say, exists precisely to help practitioners use several such lenses — his late work names five systemic perspectives, looking in turn at the machine, at interrelationships, at the organism, at purposes and at the social and environmental — and to choose and combine methods according to what the situation, seen through all of them, needs.

Then he might turn this book’s idea back on it. If every tradition sees others’ jackets but not its own, he might say, the cure isn’t a discipline of attention practised inside each tradition. It’s a framework that makes practitioners look at a situation from perspectives other than the one they’re comfortable with — which is exactly what critical systems practice, with its cycle of exploring, producing, intervening and checking, is built to do. On this reply, the jacket audit is critical awareness rediscovered under a new name, and the treatise’s reluctance to call it a framework is just the processual tradition’s dislike of frameworks talking through it. The first chaos puzzle applies to the treatise too.

And on the question both essays left unanswered, he might add that the disadvantaged aren’t protected by reflective conversation alone, or by the designer’s good intentions, but by deliberately including the perspectives of the people affected and asking of every intervention who benefits and who pays. He took those questions, through Werner Ulrich’s critical systems heuristics, from a tradition of boundary critique that asks who ought to be the client, the decision-maker and the expert — and who is affected but not involved. Composition, he might say, isn’t a new discovery of democratic theory. It’s the oldest question in critical systems thinking: who is inside the boundary of the system, and who has been left outside?

That reconstructed reply has real force, and this book records it here rather than rebutting it. Much of the jacket audit does line up with critical awareness — as § 6 above already admitted, in noting that Jackson’s adoption of recoverability reaches the discipline of declared form by another road. Where this book would still differ is on the question of standpoint the Connoisseur kept pressing: can a practitioner who looks at a situation through five perspectives also look at the vantage point from which the five were chosen? Ulrich’s boundary critique, which Jackson rightly calls on, asks who is inside the boundary of the system being studied. This book’s idea asks who is inside the boundary of the framework doing the studying. That second boundary is the curator’s jacket, and his reply, like every reply from inside a tradition, doesn’t see it. But the reply deserves a hearing before that difference is pushed — and the right place for that hearing is the room at Hull, in Jackson’s own words.

Fourth Interlude · On the Mapmaker’s Coat

The mapmaker wears the most complete coat of all. It has a pocket for every country, a button for every border, a seam along every river. He can tell you, with real learning and real generosity, what every other traveller is wearing and why it suits the country they came from. What he can’t tell you is that his own coat was cut in a particular town by a particular tailor, and that its pockets fit some countries better than others. The traveller who has spent his whole life walking one valley can’t see his own coat either. But the mapmaker is the one who has talked himself into believing he has none, because he’s spent his life describing coats. Seeing your own clothes is hardest for the person whose job is looking at clothes.

Jackson’s jacket, it turns out, is the most declared of the three. But the tribunal that tried him wore a jacket too — and that is where we go next.

Part ElevenThe interrogator’s jacket: what the Connoisseur could not see of itself

Where we areIf the central idea is true, it must be true of the Connoisseur too. Here the book turns its own weapon on the imprint’s earlier work.
What this asksPart Eleven asks the uncomfortable question: what could the Connoisseur not see about itself?
In brief 13 min read
  • The Connoisseur kept an undeclared constraint: by a strict protocol, it never names Snowden, his framework, or his co-author of 2003. Its tribunal on pluralism was therefore a two-tradition room with a third tradition absent.
  • Its sympathies lay with Stacey’s tradition; Book One turned the blade on that tradition’s leading voice. This is not inconsistency but the movement between thought collectives that Fleck identified as the source of new seeing — provided it is declared.
  • Its strongest argument, the “hundred rivers” of independent traditions converging on one verdict, is weaker than it claims: the rivers were chosen by one cartographer, and the traditions that might have vindicated the grid were not summoned.
  • The Additive Covenant and the 道理勢 triad are constitutive constraints of the imprint: real disciplines, and also jackets that outside readers may see as baggy or boxed.

TakeawayThe Connoisseur’s doubled verdict on Jackson stands, but it needs a third clause: the tribunal itself was a room, with a composition, a form and a style of speech, and the audit of jackets applies to it too.

If you find two authors guilty of seeing every jacket but their own, and then find a third guilty too, you invite an obvious comeback: what about you? This imprint’s earlier tribunal on Jackson was itself a gathering, in the sense Book One has been using the word. It was a room. Some voices were invited in and others weren’t. It was ordered by a form its author chose, run in a style of speech its author preferred, and judged by standards its author brought along. If this book’s idea holds generally, it holds for the Connoisseur. This Part applies it. That isn’t an exercise in self-flagellation; the Connoisseur is a serious work, and most of its verdicts on Jackson stand up. It’s an exercise in the discipline Book One proposed: bringing unspoken constraints into the light for a while, looking at them, and then letting them fade back into use, better for having been seen.

§ 1. The empty chair

The Connoisseur works under a rule it never tells its readers about. It never names Dave Snowden, never names his Cynefin framework, and never names Cynthia Kurtz, his co-author on the celebrated 2003 paper in the IBM Systems Journal. Across roughly three hundred thousand words on managing complexity, the most widely used sense-making framework in the field, and the practitioner who has done most to bring complexity thinking to managers, are left out on purpose. There may be good reasons. Perhaps the aim was to keep the tribunal focused on Jackson versus Stacey; perhaps to avoid getting tangled in a quarrel with its own literature; perhaps a judgement that the framework had been dealt with elsewhere in the imprint, which has indeed given it a treatise of similar size. But the rule isn’t declared in the work. So from the reader’s side it works exactly as Book One said a constitutive constraint works: invisibly, shaping what can be said without seeming to shape anything.

Through this book’s idea, that matters in a particular way. The Connoisseur is a tribunal on pluralism: on whether Jackson’s grid can organise the many systems traditions, and on what the grid misses. Its Index of Interlocutors is long and generous. It calls Ulrich and Midgley, Checkland and Beer, Churchman and Ackoff, Morin and Zhu Zhichang, Stacey, Shaw, Griffin and Mowles, Wenger and Rodgers, Peirce, Husserl, Gramsci, Habermas, Rovelli and Yunkaporta. One chair is empty — the one that would have been taken by the third tradition in the room Snowden now proposes to convene at Hull. By design, the Connoisseur’s room was one where the grid met the living present and the designer of enabling constraints wasn’t invited. Part Eight argued that the most important thing about the politics of any room is who’s in it: who’s there, how they got there, and who isn’t. Applied to the Connoisseur, the jacket audit asks that question of the Connoisseur. The answer is that the tribunal on pluralism left out one of the three pluralisms.

The way things have unfolded sharpens the irony. The tradition missing from the Connoisseur is the one whose leading figure has now, in “Neither Baggy Nor Boxed”, proposed a three-way conversation with the two traditions the Connoisseur did let in. Whatever his proposal is worth — and Book One found it mixed — he has in one sense done what the Connoisseur didn’t: he’s asked for all three to be in the room. This book names him on every page, and in doing so it lifts, for this work at least, the rule the earlier work was written under. Not to disown the earlier choice, which had its reasons, but because you can’t audit jackets with someone missing. A jacket audit with an empty chair would be one more room whose walls are invisible to the people inside.

§ 2. Changing sides?

The Connoisseur is admirably open about its sources. On one side of the bench sit Jackson’s two major books, read in full. On the other sits Stacey’s work in depth — Complex Responsive Processes in Organizations (2001), Complexity and Group Processes (2003), the volumes edited with Griffin, and the sardonic Tools and Techniques of Leadership and Management (2012), which the Connoisseur calls Stacey’s pre-emptive review of everything Jackson stands for — and alongside them his heirs: Shaw, Griffin, Rodgers, Flinn and Mowles. Two of its Books are about Mowles. One, titled “The Critic Who Turned the Blade on Himself”, honours his recognition that management scholars have a stake in the game they describe and an interest in talking it up, and calls this the deepest sociological reading of the grid the treatise found. Another honours his line that organisational life is predictably unpredictable, and uses it to argue that a general category can never reach a particular situation.

Book One turned the blade on Mowles. It argued that his essay condemns structure by guilt by association, overlooks the rigorous form of the practices he most admires, leaves Jackson’s question about protecting the weak unanswered, and treats the privilege of having time as if it were a method. A reader of both works could fairly ask whether the imprint has changed sides, contradicted itself, or just admires whoever it isn’t criticising at the moment.

This book’s idea gives a more interesting answer than any of those. The Connoisseur used the seeing equipment of Stacey’s tradition — its attention to the living present, its suspicion of the observer standing outside the system, its insistence that power is a relationship — to see the jacket of Jackson’s grid. That equipment is excellent for seeing the jackets of designed and classifying systems, because it was cut in opposition to them. This book uses other equipment too — the grid’s insistence that every method has a proper range, the designer’s insistence that open rooms don’t abolish power, democratic theory’s insistence on who’s in the room — to see the jacket of Stacey’s tradition. That second set is excellent for seeing the jackets of open and traditional practices, because it was cut in opposition to them. Neither set sees everything. Moving between them isn’t inconsistency. It’s the movement Fleck identified, back in Part Six, as the source of new seeing in the history of thought: the position of someone who belongs to more than one thought collective and has to hold their styles in tension.

But Fleck’s position comes with a condition that this book’s idea makes explicit: the movement has to be declared. A thinker who moves between groups without saying so will look like a deserter to each of them, and will be tempted to present each new loyalty as the discovery of the truth the old one hid. The Connoisseur presented the living present as what the grid couldn’t see. This book presents the frame around the living present as what Stacey’s tradition can’t see. Both claims are true, and neither is the whole truth. The honest way to put it is the way this book’s idea suggests: each tradition sees the other’s garment, and the imprint has worn both pairs of glasses in turn. That’s now declared.

§ 3. The hundred rivers, looked at again

By its own account, the Connoisseur’s strongest argument isn’t any one critique but a convergence. Its Book on “the hundred rivers to the sea” gathers witnesses from relational quantum mechanics (Rovelli), complex responsive processes (Stacey), Aboriginal Australian relational thought (Yunkaporta), cognitive science (Challoner), Deleuzian assemblage theory (Buchanan), phenomenology (Heidegger), Confucian, Daoist and Buddhist philosophy, critical theory (Gramsci, Habermas), cybernetics (Beer, Hoverstadt), knowledge theory (Nonaka), the ecology of mind (Bateson), physics and wholeness (Bohm), social learning theory (Wenger) and soft systems methodology (Checkland). These traditions, it argues, share no school, method, evidence, century or continent, and yet reach the same small set of conclusions about the grid by separate routes. When witnesses that independent agree that completely, the agreement isn’t coincidence but confirmation — the mark not of a shared bias but of a truth found.

Arguing from convergence is respectable. William Whewell called it the consilience of inductions, and a lot of what we believe in science rests on it. But it only works under one condition, which this book’s idea forces us to press: the witnesses have to be independent not just of each other but of whoever calls them. The Connoisseur’s rivers didn’t rise by themselves and flow to the sea. One author with one thesis picked them out of the vast landscape of human inquiry, and picked them because they bore on that thesis. That doesn’t make their testimony worthless. It means their agreement is evidence that a family of process-minded, relational sensibilities, spread widely through human thought, shares a suspicion of grids — a real and interesting finding — and not independent confirmation that the suspicion is right.

The test is to ask which rivers weren’t called. Operational research has a long record of successful interventions where explicit modelling — exactly the kind the grid organises — improved real outcomes, in logistics, in queuing, in sharing out scarce clinical resources. Evidence-based management has documented where intuition fails and where structured judgement pays off. Decision science, from Paul Meehl’s clinical-versus-statistical studies onward, has shown again and again that simple explicit procedures can beat expert judgement in well-defined areas. The deliberation research in Part Eight shows that designed structure can improve how groups reason. None of these traditions backs the grid wholesale, and several would have their own arguments with it. But their evidence would have complicated the convergence, and they weren’t called. A tribunal that only summons witnesses for one side, however independent of each other, hasn’t established consilience. It has shown that the case for that side can be made from many directions.

Part Eight cited Cass Sunstein’s law of group polarisation, and especially its mechanism of the limited argument pool: groups that mostly hear arguments for what they already lean towards end up at a more extreme version of it. A treatise is a group made of one author and many summoned voices, and the same mechanism can work inside it. On the evidence of its own text, the Connoisseur’s verdict on the grid gets harsher in its later Books than in its early ones; the convergence grew as the rivers piled up. That may be because the truth emerged. It may also be because the argument pool was curated. This book’s idea can’t decide between the two. What it can do is insist that the second possibility be declared — which this Part now does.

§ 4. The Additive Covenant as a constraint

The imprint’s works follow a rule it calls the Additive Covenant: never delete, only add. Each pass over a treatise can add, deepen, weave in and annotate, but it can’t cut what earlier passes made. Identifiers are permanent, Books aren’t renumbered, and you can still read the history of the work’s growth in its structure. The Connoisseur describes itself, in its closing Book, as the product of many additive passes, each checked, each keeping what came before, each building higher. This book, at its author’s instruction, has been extended under the same rule.

The Covenant is a constraint, and through this book’s idea it has to be examined like any other. It has real strengths. It’s a form of what Checkland and Holwell call recoverability: because nothing is removed, an interested reader can recover the whole story of how the work was made, and see how its arguments built up and where its emphasis shifted. It guards against a typical vice of revision — the after-the-fact tidying by which an author hides the path he took and presents his conclusions as if they’d always been obvious. It respects the labour of earlier passes. And it practises, in the act of writing, a principle the imprint champions everywhere: that process matters, that a work is a history rather than a finished object, and that the living present of composition deserves keeping rather than overwriting.

It also has costs, and this book’s idea predicts exactly how they’ll be seen. Goethe’s three lines, which Book One looked at, put mastery in limitation — in der Beschränkung zeigt sich erst der Meister — and the limitation he mainly has in mind is choosing: pulling yourself together, refusing what doesn’t serve. Pascal, apologising for the length of his sixteenth Provincial Letter, said he’d only made it longer because he hadn’t had time to make it shorter. The Covenant rules out the shortening. A reader trained in the tradition of the essay — compression, selection, one line of argument — will meet a three-hundred-thousand-word treatise built up layer by layer and see what James saw in Tolstoy: a large loose baggy monster, with odd bits of the accidental and the arbitrary. Its author, who knows the architecture of Books and Parts, the permanent identifiers, the reference apparatus, the weaving of earlier and later passes, will see a rigorously ordered form, and he’ll be right. Neither reader is wrong. Each is wearing a jacket. Through this book’s idea, the Covenant produces the purest example of a form invisible from outside: rigorously structured to the person who built it, baggy to the person who meets it cold.

That has a practical consequence, which this book has tried to honour in its own layout. A work built under the Covenant owes its reader the means to see its form. The reading aids added to this treatise — the At a Glance page, the In brief boxes, the tables, the glossary — are attempts to make the form visible to someone who didn’t build it. They’re the discipline of declared form applied to the treatise itself. A Covenant that adds without declaring would be a jacket that gets heavier without getting any easier to see.

§ 5. A lens that always finds what it’s looking for

The imprint reads its subjects through the triad 道理勢. The Connoisseur defends this with a sentence worth repeating: it reads a British operational researcher through Song-dynasty categories not for decoration but because the categories cut. And they do cut. The difference between 理 as pattern built into the flow and 理 as principle laid on top of it — Wang Fuzhi’s insistence that no principle stands outside the 氣 it patterns — is a genuinely precise statement of the objection Stacey, Mowles and Zhu make against frameworks that hover above practice, and it’s older and neater than their versions.

Still, this book’s idea demands a further question. A lens that cuts into every subject you point it at may be a lens of extraordinary power. Or it may be a lens that finds in every subject what it was ground to find. Under 理 Jackson is honoured as the carver who shows his chisel; under 道 he’s found guilty of stopping the flow; under 勢 he’s found to have no reading of the pull of situations. Apply the same triad, in the imprint’s other treatises, to Snowden’s framework, to Boyd, to the Malayan Emergency, to Shakespeare, and each time you get an analysis in which the flowing whole goes beyond the explicit pattern and the decisive knowledge is reading the pull of the situation. That consistency may be the mark of a deep truth about human affairs. It may also be the mark of a constitutive constraint: a jacket the imprint wears into every room, shaping what it sees there.

There’s also the question Part Two raised about the triopticon’s menagerie: do symbols borrowed from another civilisation’s sacred or philosophical store give real insight, or just borrowed glamour? The imprint’s use of Chinese categories differs in important ways from the triopticon’s use of Raven and Coyote. The categories are studied in their source texts, translated, placed in their original debates, and used to think with rather than handed to people as roles. But scholarship doesn’t remove the risk of borrowed glamour, and a reader who meets the characters set in gold on a deep blue page might fairly wonder whether some of their force is decorative. The honest response isn’t to drop the triad, which does real analytical work. It’s to declare it as a lens — to say, as the Connoisseur half-said, that these categories were chosen because they cut, and to accept that a chosen lens is a jacket like any other. Part Twenty-One comes back to this as a formal objection to this book.

§ 6. The third clause

After all this, the Connoisseur’s two-sided verdict on Jackson — guilty as a survey claiming to see from nowhere, vindicated as something useful when held lightly — still stands. Nothing in this book’s idea requires withdrawing it. What the idea adds is a third clause the Connoisseur couldn’t have written from inside its own form: and the tribunal that gave this verdict was itself a room — one that left out one of the three traditions, whose witnesses were picked by one hand, whose form was governed by rules and lenses its readers weren’t told about — and so its verdict is an adequate partial truth in exactly Jackson’s sense.

That clause doesn’t shrink the Connoisseur.
