It puts it where this book has put everyone else: inside a jacket, seeing clearly what others wear, and now — for a moment, in the back-and-forth the discipline asks for — seeing something of its own. The connoisseur of partial truths, the Connoisseur concluded, called his truths partial and called them adequate. Whoever puts the connoisseur on trial can do no less.

The court that tries the mapmaker for claiming to see from nowhere has to sit somewhere too — and the most honest verdict it can give is one that names the bench it’s sitting on.

Even the interrogator could not see his own back. Yet the Connoisseur’s deepest finding still stands, and it reaches further than anyone noticed.

Part TwelveThe manual and the training: recoverability, declared form, and the creamed and the parked

Where we arePart Eleven found a jacket on the interrogator. Part Twelve picks up the Connoisseur’s deepest finding and follows it further than the Connoisseur did.
What this asksDoes every tradition, not just Jackson’s, credit its manual for what really comes from its masters?
In brief 12 min read
  • The Connoisseur’s deepest finding — that critical systems thinking forms judgement while crediting frameworks, like a martial lineage that ascribes to the manual what only the training transmits — applies to all three traditions in the room.
  • Each tradition presents its form to the world because forms can be written, taught and sold; each depends in practice on the formation of its practitioners, which cannot.
  • Checkland’s recoverability and the discipline of declared form converge but differ: one declares the framework to outsiders after the fact; the other declares the form to participants during it. A just gathering needs both.
  • Every room creams and parks: the designed room favours experts, the club favours the fluent, the grid favours methods with articulate theory. The Two-Row Wampum offers a different model of how jackets might travel together.

TakeawayThe real dispute is not between forms but between accounts of where competence lives. All three traditions locate it publicly in their method and privately in their people.

The Connoisseur’s Book of verdicts closes its reading of Jackson under 勢 with a finding the whole treatise had been heading towards: critical systems thinking is a magnificent education for practitioners and a consistent misdescription of what they actually do. It forms their judgement while officially giving the credit to frameworks — like a martial-arts school that credits the manual for what only the training passes on. The Connoisseur got there through its chapter on Jackson’s years as dean at Hull, which it found full of the reading of propensity: the dean who knows which department to bring in first, when the university’s own crisis makes his school’s case, how an accreditation deadline can be made to do the work of a strategy — none of it taken from the grid, all of it delivered by the man. This Part takes that finding and pushes it beyond Jackson, because this book’s idea suggests it isn’t a quirk of one tradition but true of all three.

§ 1. The manual and the master

The gap between what a manual contains and what training passes on is ancient, and there’s a modern literature on it. Hubert and Stuart Dreyfus, in Mind over Machine (1986), described five stages in learning a skill — novice, advanced beginner, competent, proficient, expert — and argued that moving through them means moving away from rules. The novice follows rules that ignore context. The advanced beginner starts to notice features of situations the rules don’t mention. The competent performer picks a plan and feels responsible for it. The proficient performer sees the situation as a whole and knows what matters without having to think it through. The expert just acts, intuitively, drawing on a huge store of situations already told apart. Patricia Benner’s From Novice to Expert (1984) traced the same path in clinical nursing and showed that expert nurses often couldn’t say what rules they were following — because they weren’t following rules any more. Polanyi, whose account of tacit knowing we looked at in Part Six, had already argued that skill is mostly passed on through apprenticeship: the learner accepts a master’s authority and, by copying and being corrected, picks up what the master can’t put into words.

A manual helps at the start of that path and matters less and less towards the end. Yet manuals are what traditions publish. The martial-arts school writes down its forms; the kata are drawn in sequence; a student who learns them from the page learns their outline and nothing of their life, which passes only from teacher to student over years of practice. The Connoisseur’s charge against critical systems thinking is that it mistakes the outline for the life. It presents its frameworks — the grid, the five perspectives, the EPIC cycle of critical systems practice — as the source of good intervention, when good intervention comes from a judgement those frameworks may help train but can’t contain.

§ 2. One charge, three times over

Through this book’s idea, the charge can’t be limited to Jackson. Take the triopticon. Snowden presents it as a method: a sequence of roles and phases, published in field guides, assembled from parts on a wiki, usable by other conveners. But anyone who has watched a skilled facilitator run a structured method knows how much its success depends on the convener reading the room — when to stretch a phase, when to cut it short, which Eagles to invite, what to do with a Coyote who’s just being disruptive, how to sense that a trio has agreed too quickly. Snowden is, by all accounts, a convener of formidable skill. The method he’s written down carries some of that skill in its design. The rest travels with him, and you won’t find it in the field guide. The triopticon, like the grid, credits the manual for what the training passes on.

Now take the open conference. Mowles presents it as the refusal of method: a space with little fixed except start and finish, where the conversation goes wherever it goes. But Questions XII and XIII argued that whether it’s productive depends on the trained judgement of its seasoned members — their group-analytic habit of noticing, their comfort with ambiguity, their shared vocabulary, their years of practice in talking about how they talk. The conference works because its regulars have been shaped, through doctoral programmes, annual meetings and professional supervision, into people who can make an open conversation productive. Its public story credits the openness. Its private reality depends on the formation. The open conference too credits its form — here, the form of having no form — for what only training passes on.

So the finding is general. All three traditions in the proposed room at Hull show the world a form: a grid, a method, an openness. All three depend, for whatever success they have, on forming practitioners: the dean who reads propensity, the convener who reads the room, the regular who reads the dynamics. And all three, when they describe themselves in public, credit the form. This is the deepest sense in which each sees the other’s jacket and not its own. Each sees the other’s form, which is public. None sees its own formation, which is private, unspoken, carried in people, and invisible precisely because it has become those people’s bodies.

§ 3. Why the manual gets the credit

Mowles supplies the explanation, in the passage the Connoisseur called the deepest sociological reading of the grid it found. Management scholars and business schools, he notes, have a big stake in the game they describe and an interest in talking it up; there’s a strong professional and financial incentive to keep making the case for method while pitching your own method as better than all the rest. Forms can be written down, taught in modules, licensed, published, cited and sold. Formation can’t. A tradition that wants to survive in the knowledge economy has to present itself as a form, because only forms circulate. The grid becomes a textbook; the triopticon becomes a field guide and a training course; the open conference becomes a doctoral programme with a curriculum and a reading list. Each tradition’s public jacket is the part of it that can go to market.

The Connoisseur turned this point against Jackson. This book’s idea turns it against all three, including the tradition it came from. Mowles’s doctoral programme is a form through which the tradition’s formation is passed on, and its public description — a programme that takes experience seriously, refuses the objective observer, attends to the living present — describes its form, not the years of supervised reflective practice that make its graduates who they are. That isn’t a criticism of the programme; formation always needs a form to travel in. It’s just to notice that the critique of method-selling applies to every tradition that has to sell something to keep going — which is every tradition there is.

That clarifies the argument between Snowden and Mowles. Their quarrel looks like it’s about forms: designed constraint versus open conversation. But if what really makes both traditions competent is how they form practitioners, then the quarrel about forms is partly a quarrel between two marketable surfaces, each hiding a depth of trained judgement the other can’t see. The designer sees the club’s openness and thinks it baggy, because he can’t see the formation that makes it work. The traditionalist sees the designer’s method and thinks it boxed, because he can’t see the formation that makes it work. Both are looking at manuals. Neither is looking at masters.

§ 4. Recoverable afterwards, challengeable at the time

The Connoisseur gave a whole Book to Checkland and Holwell’s test of recoverability, which Jackson adopted in his 2025 paper, and linked it to Challoner’s account of how frameworks sink below awareness and start to feel self-evident. Part Ten noted that this test lines up with the discipline of declared form proposed in Part Nine. The overlap is real, but the two aren’t the same, and the difference matters.

Recoverability, as Checkland and Holwell put it, is a standard of rigour for action research. Social situations are one-offs and can’t be reproduced, so research in them can’t claim repeatability. But it can claim recoverability if the researcher declares in advance the ideas and the methodology she’ll use to engage the situation, and records the process so that any interested outsider can retrace the whole story and examine it. It’s addressed to the outsider — the reviewer, the critic, the later scholar — and it looks backwards: the story is recovered after the event.

The discipline of declared form is addressed to someone else, at a different time. It’s addressed to the participant — the person inside the gathering, whose behaviour the form is shaping — and it happens at the threshold, the breakdown and the close: during the event, while the form can still be changed. Its aim isn’t scrutiny but the chance to challenge: people living under a form should be able to see it, question it and change it while it’s still binding them.

A fair gathering needs both. Recoverability without the chance to challenge gives you forms that are clear to scholars and murky to participants: the researcher declares her framework in the methods section of a paper the participants will never read. The chance to challenge without recoverability gives you forms that are open to question in the room and lost afterwards: the gathering changes its form on the spot, and nobody can later find out what changed or why. Put together — declared to participants during the event, recoverable by outsiders afterwards — they make a fuller discipline than either the action-research tradition or this book has stated alone. Jackson’s late work got to the first; Part Nine got to the second; the Connoisseur’s link between Checkland and Challoner pointed towards joining them. So here it is, as a refinement of the discipline: declare the form to the people it binds while it binds them, and record it for the people who’ll judge it afterwards.

§ 5. Creamed and parked

One of the Connoisseur’s most moving Books reads a case Jackson gives in his 2024 practitioner’s guide. People at the margins of a public service — a single mother, an Indigenous carer, the dying — are, in the Connoisseur’s phrase, creamed and parked by a system doing exactly what it was built to do. The terms come from studies of payment-by-results contracts in public services. Providers paid on outcomes learn to focus their effort on the clients most likely to produce a payable result (creaming) and give as little attention as possible to the least likely (parking). It’s well documented in evaluations of employment programmes, and it illustrates Stafford Beer’s maxim that the purpose of a system is what it does: a system that parks the hardest cases has, whatever its mission statement says, the purpose of parking them.

This book’s idea suggests that every room creams and parks. The triopticon creams expertise. Its opening phase gives the floor to Eagles, chosen because they represent disciplines, so it focuses attention on the contributions most easily recognised as knowledge. It parks story, grief and unease nobody can quite put into words — exactly the contributions Iris Marion Young said deliberative forms tend to mark down. The open conference creams fluency in its own idiom: the regular who can name a group dynamic, offer an interpretation, sit with ambiguity without discomfort. It parks the newcomer who hasn’t learned the idiom yet, whose contributions land wrong without her knowing why. The grid, as the Connoisseur noted in its Book on the vestibule, creams methodologies that can explain themselves and parks practices that can’t: in Jackson’s 2024 guide, Indigenous approaches get a respectful chapter among the “related approaches” — in a vestibule outside the main hall where the ten methodologies live. Each room does exactly what it was designed to do, so each one parks somebody.

The discipline of declared form can’t abolish creaming and parking. Every form has a shape, and every shape suits some contributions better than others. What the discipline can do is make the parking visible. The fifth of Part Nine’s six questions — which ways of speaking does this form welcome, and which does it mark down? — is a question about creaming and parking. A gathering that asks it honestly will find out whom it parks, and can then choose to open a second door for them: a phase for stories in the triopticon, an induction for newcomers at the conference, a place in the main hall rather than the vestibule for practices that can’t explain themselves in theory. The purpose of a system is what it does. The point of declaring a form is to let a gathering see what its form does, so it can choose to do something else.

§ 6. Two rows

The Connoisseur’s Book on the vestibule also looks at Jackson’s respectful treatment of the Two-Row Wampum — the treaty belt associated with the early seventeenth-century agreement between the Haudenosaunee and Dutch traders — and of what the Mi’kmaw Elder Albert Marshall has called two-eyed seeing (Etuaptmumk). The belt is usually read as showing two parallel rows: two vessels, a canoe and a ship, travelling side by side down the river of life, each carrying its own people, laws and customs, neither trying to steer the other. Two-eyed seeing means looking with the strengths of Indigenous knowledge through one eye and the strengths of Western knowledge through the other, and using both eyes together. The Connoisseur argued that critical systems practice can host two-eyed seeing but can’t become it, because the grid’s habit is to put every kind of knowledge in a cell of its own classification.

These images need the care Part Two applied to the triopticon’s menagerie. They belong to living peoples, they aren’t decorations, and the imprint has no right to adopt them as names for its own devices. But it can learn from the relationship they describe, as Jackson and the Connoisseur both tried to. And what they describe is exactly the alternative to the fantasy that has run through the baggy-and-boxed argument: the fantasy that one form has to win, or that some third form must be found in which the others are reconciled. The Two-Row Wampum shows neither victory nor synthesis. It shows two forms travelling together, each in charge of its own vessel, each visible to the other, bound by an agreement about how they’ll relate rather than by a shared garment.

Applied — carefully — to the room Snowden proposes at Hull, that relationship suggests something different from the triopticon Snowden offers and different from the rite Part Five proposed. It suggests the three traditions don’t need to meet inside any one tradition’s form at all. They could travel in parallel. Each could run part of the gathering its own way — a phase designed by the anthro-complexity tradition, a phase of open reflective conversation run by complex responsive processes, a phase of explicit reflection on method run by critical systems thinking — with agreed points where the vessels exchange, and each tradition free to watch how the others work without having to work the same way. The gathering’s overall form would then be not a single jacket but an agreement between jackets: a treaty rather than a uniform. Whether that could work is a question for experience. That none of the three has proposed it is, through this book’s idea, no surprise. Each has spent its life arguing that its own form is the one that can best hold the others.

Every tradition sells its manual and lives by its masters. The quarrel between the manuals is loud; the kinship between the masters is silent; and a room that wanted to learn from all three would invite the masters and leave the manuals at the door.

Every tradition sells its manual and lives by its masters. Let us go and look, now, at the instruments those masters actually own — beginning with Snowden’s.

Part ThirteenThe sense-maker’s own instruments: Cynefin, Boyd, Putney and the Malayan jungle

Where we areWe leave the Connoisseur and widen the lens to the whole of the imprint’s work, beginning with Snowden’s own territory.
What this asksPart Thirteen asks a pointed question: did Snowden already own the instruments that would have shown him the club’s hidden form — and if so, why didn’t he use them?
In brief 16 min read
  • The imprint has written at length on Snowden’s framework, on Boyd’s OODA loop through Cromwell and Hồ Chí Minh, and on the Malayan Emergency. Read again, each supplies instruments for seeing jackets.
  • By Snowden’s own epistemology, Cynefin is a sense-making framework in which data precede categories. His essay categorises Mowles’s conference before attending to its data — a lapse from his own method.
  • In Cynefin’s later mapping of constraint types, the club’s tacit constraints are enabling and constitutive, which places its conference in the complex domain — the domain where his own framework recommends exactly what the club already has.
  • Boyd’s “Orientation”, with its cultural traditions and previous experience and its implicit guidance of action, is the jacket in diagrammatic form; the Putney Debates of 1647 are the historical type of a room in which the rank and file contested the frame; and the Malayan Emergency shows each side reading the other’s order as disorder.

TakeawaySnowden possesses, in his own body of work, the instruments that would have revealed the club’s jacket. This book’s idea predicts that the maker of an instrument is the last to turn it on a rival’s room.

Book One treated Snowden’s essay as a short text to be judged on its own terms. But Snowden has written a great deal, and this imprint has engaged with it at length — most fully in Cynefin Dynamics and the Choreography of Organisational Change, a treatise of around two hundred and seventy thousand words built over six passes, and also in a study of Boyd’s OODA loop through the campaigns of Cromwell and Hồ Chí Minh, and in a study of the Malayan Emergency read through complexity theory and the imprint’s triad. Those works have their own commitments, including a rule about credit the imprint keeps throughout: Snowden created the Cynefin framework, and Cynthia Kurtz, who co-wrote the celebrated 2003 paper with him, is properly described as its co-author, not as a co-creator of the framework. This Part goes back over that ground and asks something Book One didn’t: what would Snowden’s essay have looked like if he’d turned the tools of his own wider work on Mowles’s conference?

§ 1. Making sense versus sorting

The 2003 paper, “The new dynamics of strategy: sense-making in a complex and complicated world”, drew a distinction that has stayed at the centre of Snowden’s thinking. In a categorisation model, the framework comes before the data: you have a set of boxes, and the job is to put each thing in its proper box. The consultant’s favourite two-by-two matrix is the classic example. In a sense-making model, the data come before the framework: you pay attention to the situation, gather its texture, and let the distinctions that matter emerge from it. Cynefin, Snowden has insisted ever since, is a sense-making framework, not a categorisation one. Its domains aren’t boxes you sort situations into at a glance. They emerge from the data of a particular situation, often through the shared work of the people in it, and their boundaries move.

By that standard, the essay Book One examined is odd. Its organising contrast — baggy versus boxed, with the triopticon in between — is a categorisation. It has two poles and a middle, fixed before any particular gathering is looked at. And it handles Mowles’s conference by sorting rather than sense-making. On the strength of Mowles’s own short description, the conference is put at the too-little-structure pole, and the arguments against that pole — hidden power, entrainment, the club — are then applied to it. At no point does the essay ask what people who go to that conference actually experience, what unspoken constraints they’d report, how newcomers have got on, or what patterns of inclusion and exclusion have actually been seen. Snowden, among other things, has developed methods for gathering exactly that kind of data — distributed ethnography in which many participants record fragments of their own experience and say what those fragments mean, so that patterns come out of the material rather than being imposed on it. His essay doesn’t use them. It sorts.

This book’s idea predicts the slip. A sense-making tool is a way of seeing, and like every way of seeing, its user is most likely to turn it on situations he thinks of as problems to be understood, and least likely to turn it on situations he thinks he already understands. Snowden thinks he already understands the open conference — it’s the kind of thing his whole practice was built to improve on — so he doesn’t make sense of it; he sorts it. A rival’s room is the hardest room to enter with the humility of sense-making, because you arrive already knowing what it lacks.

§ 2. Putting the club on the constraint map

As Cynefin has developed, it’s been framed more and more in terms of constraints, and Snowden has linked each of its domains to a typical kind of constraint. In the clear domain, constraints are rigid or fixed: there’s a right way, and best practice applies. In the complicated domain, constraints are governing: expertise and analysis can work out good practice within known limits. In the complex domain, constraints are enabling: they shape the conditions in which patterns emerge without deciding which patterns will emerge, and the right response is to probe, sense and respond through safe-to-fail experiments. In the chaotic domain, effective constraints are missing, and the immediate job is to act so as to put some in place. Around and between these are the in-between zones of transition, and at the centre a domain of confusion, or aporia, where you don’t yet know what kind of situation you’re in.

Where does the open conference go on this map? Snowden’s essay, by stressing its lack of structure — nothing fixed except start and finish — quietly puts it near the chaotic edge: a situation without effective constraints, where whatever patterns are already in people’s heads take over. But Book One’s analysis suggests a very different place. The conference isn’t unconstrained. Its constraints are unspoken, built up over time, and part of what makes it what it is: a shared tradition, key texts, a vocabulary, a liturgy of supper, keynote and open talk, and a group-analytic habit of noticing. These constraints don’t fix outcomes, so they aren’t rigid or governing. They shape the conditions of the conversation without deciding where it goes — which is exactly Snowden’s definition of an enabling constraint. Mapped properly, the conference doesn’t sit near chaos. It sits squarely in the complex domain.

And in the complex domain, by Snowden’s own account, the right way to organise is with enabling constraints: conditions that make some things more likely without deciding what emerges. That’s exactly what the club already has. Used carefully, his own framework doesn’t find the conference guilty of bagginess. It describes the conference as a complex-domain practice with an existing set of enabling and constitutive constraints, whose effects — inclusion, exclusion, entrainment — could be probed and adjusted, but whose basic form suits its domain. The triopticon would then look not like a cure for missing constraints but like an alternative set of enabling constraints — better suited to some complex situations (mixed groups with no shared tradition) and less suited to others (communities with a mature tradition of their own). That’s a more modest claim than the essay makes, and it’s the claim Snowden’s own framework supports.

§ 3. Estuarine mapping and the weight of habit

In recent years Snowden and his colleagues have developed what they call estuarine mapping. You identify the constraints acting on a situation and plot them by how much energy, and how much time, it would take to change them. The picture is an estuary: some features — the bedrock, the deep channels — shift only over centuries, while others — sandbanks, eddies — move with every tide. Strategy, on this view, works with the constraints that can be shifted and around the ones that can’t. Whatever the detailed steps, the approach rests on a principle that’s very useful here: you can survey a situation’s constraints, including the ones nobody has written down, and how hard they are to change is itself a piece of evidence.

Imagine turning that method on the open conference. Its unspoken constraints — the shared vocabulary, the habits of turn-taking, the norms of interpretation, the key texts — would show up as constraints of very high energy and long time. They’re habitus, in Bourdieu’s sense, laid down in people over years, and you can’t change them by announcement. The explicit constraints — the fixed start and finish, the Friday supper, the keynote — would show up as low-energy constraints the convener could change at will. The map would show a gathering whose most powerful constraints are the least visible, and whose visible constraints are the least powerful. That’s this book’s idea, rendered precisely. And by contrast it would show that the triopticon’s constraints are the other way round: highly visible, low in energy (the designer can change the roles tomorrow), and so powerful only while the method is running, after which people go back to whatever habits they came with.

A map like that would have shown what the essay missed: that the club is densely constrained, and constrained in exactly the way that’s hardest to see and hardest to change. In other words, Snowden’s own tool is a close relative of the jacket audit. Of the three traditions in the room, the anthro-complexity tradition has the most developed tool for surveying unspoken constraints. That its leading figure didn’t turn it on the room he criticised is this book’s idea in its most exact form: the maker of a tool for seeing jackets is the last to point it at a rival’s wardrobe, because he arrives already sure what he’ll find.

§ 4. Why the “anthro” matters

Snowden calls his tradition anthro-complexity to set it apart from the computational complexity of agent-based models and from naïve transfers of complexity science to human affairs. People, he has argued again and again, aren’t ants or termites or simulated agents following simple rules. They have several identities and move between them. They act with intention. They use symbols, tell stories and remember. They can see things coming and change the rules of the game they’re in. A science of human complexity has to take all this into account, and mustn’t assume that what holds for an ant colony holds for an organisation.

That is, in its own language, a statement of the foundations of this book’s idea. People carry identities into every room. They arrive already shaped by the traditions, stories and symbols of the groups they belong to, and they see the room through that shaping. The multiple identities Snowden stresses are, in this book’s terms, multiple jackets — the clinician’s, the manager’s, the parent’s, the member of a school of thought — and the ability to move between them is the ability Fleck saw as the source of new seeing. An anthro-complexity that took its own starting points fully seriously would conclude that you can’t understand the open conference without paying attention to the identities its members bring, and that those identities are exactly the constraints the essay couldn’t see. Read carefully, the “anthro” in anthro-complexity is an argument for this book’s idea.

§ 5. Orientation: a jacket in a diagram

The imprint’s study of Cromwell and Hồ Chí Minh set out faithful reconstructions of John Boyd’s OODA loop in the full form Boyd drew in his late briefing “The Essence of Winning and Losing” (1996), rather than the simplified four-box cycle that circulates in management books. In the full diagram, the second element, Orientation, is by far the biggest. It’s drawn as a field where several factors interact: cultural traditions, genetic heritage, previous experiences, new information, and the processes of analysis and synthesis. Arrows run forward from Orientation to Decision and Action — and, crucially, one runs straight to Action, labelled implicit guidance and control, skipping explicit decision altogether. Boyd called Orientation the “big O” and saw it as the centre of gravity of the whole loop: it shapes how we observe, how we decide and how we act.

Orientation is the jacket, drawn by a fighter pilot. It’s the built-up form — tradition, heritage, experience — through which every situation is seen, and its most powerful effect is guiding action implicitly, without passing through conscious decision, so that the person acting can’t see it. Boyd’s whole doctrine of manoeuvre warfare rests on the insight that you can beat an opponent by getting inside his orientation: acting in ways his orientation doesn’t expect, so that his perceptions mislead him and his actions, steered implicitly by an orientation that no longer fits reality, defeat themselves. This book’s idea is the knowledge-side counterpart: each party in an argument sees through its orientation, and the parts of its orientation that steer it implicitly are the parts it can’t see.

Boyd’s earlier essay “Destruction and Creation” (1976) adds a further lesson. Drawing on Gödel’s incompleteness theorems, Heisenberg’s uncertainty principle and the second law of thermodynamics, Boyd argued that any closed set of concepts, used to understand a changing world, will drift further and further out of step with it and decay. The cure is a back-and-forth between destructive deduction, where existing concepts are broken into their pieces, and creative induction, where the pieces are recombined into new concepts that fit what you now see. Orientation has to be broken and rebuilt from time to time. That’s the back-and-forth Part Nine prescribed for the discipline of declared form — from background to spotlight and back — put in the language of military theory. A tradition that never breaks its own orientation will in the end be beaten by a world that has moved on.

§ 6. Putney: a room where the frame itself was argued over

The Cromwell strand of the imprint’s work gives us something rarer than a diagram: a real historical gathering where the rank and file challenged the frame itself. In the autumn of 1647, at St Mary’s Church in Putney, the General Council of the New Model Army met to debate how England should be settled after the first civil war. The army’s senior officers were there — Cromwell and his son-in-law Henry Ireton among them — along with elected representatives of the soldiers, the Agitators, and civilian Levellers. The text on the table was the Agreement of the People, a proposed constitution that would have widened the vote far beyond property-owners. William Clarke took the debates down in shorthand, and they preserve one of the most remarkable exchanges in the history of political speech. Colonel Thomas Rainsborough, arguing for votes for all men against Ireton’s defence of a property qualification, said: “I think that the poorest he that is in England hath a life to live, as the greatest he.”

Putney was a structured gathering — a council with members, a chair, procedures, a text under debate — and it was also a gathering where the structure itself became the thing being argued about. Soldiers formed by the strictest military discipline in Europe, the drill and order that made the New Model Army so formidable, used the opening that discipline gave them to question the constitutional frame they and everyone else would live under. It’s the historical model for Part Nine’s sixth question: how can participants challenge and change the form itself? At Putney, for a few weeks, they could. Then the senior officers shut the debates down, the army was brought back under its officers’ authority, a mutiny at Corkbush Field was put down, and the frame was settled by the people in command.

There are two lessons here for the present argument. First, a highly disciplined form doesn’t rule out challenging the frame. The New Model Army’s discipline was what let its soldiers gather, organise and speak with collective force. The boxed can host the most radical questioning. Second, the ability to challenge the frame is fragile, and depends on the people in power allowing it; it can be taken away the moment it threatens them. A Coyote whose licence stretches to the frame, as Part Nine asked of the triopticon, is a Putney in miniature — and Putney’s history warns that such licences get revoked once they become inconvenient.

§ 7. Hồ Chí Minh and designed self-criticism

The other figure in that study offers a more troubling case. The Vietnamese revolutionary movement Hồ Chí Minh led developed practices of collective criticism and self-criticism, in which cadres and members examined their own conduct and each other’s in structured sessions. Seen one way, these practices built reflection into the organisation in a remarkable way: a designed form through which a movement tried to make its own conduct visible to itself and fixable, at every level, as a matter of routine. Seen another way, they were a tool of discipline, in which the party set the form of self-examination and the outcome — falling in line — was effectively decided in advance. Historians disagree about how the balance fell in different periods, and this book is in no position to settle that history.

What the case shows is that reflection can be designed, and that designed reflection can be either an enabling constraint or a governing one, depending on whether the frame it happens within can itself be reflected on. That’s the hinge both of Book One’s essays turn on. Mowles’s tradition prizes reflection — talking about how we’re talking — and presents it as the opposite of design. Snowden’s method designs the conditions for a kind of reflection — the Coyote, the ritualised challenge — and presents it as the opposite of mere openness. The Vietnamese case shows that reflection and design aren’t opposites, and that what matters isn’t whether reflection is designed but whether its design can be questioned. Self-criticism inside a frame nobody may question is a governing constraint dressed up as an enabling one.

§ 8. The jungle and the estate

The imprint’s study of the Malayan Emergency, The Emergency That Would Not Speak Its Name, takes its title from a well-known feature of the conflict. The colonial government declared a state of emergency in 1948 and kept calling the long counter-insurgency an emergency rather than a war — a choice often put down partly to the fact that commercial insurance for rubber estates and tin mines covered losses from riot and civil commotion under an emergency but not in a war. In other words, the name given to a form was itself shaped by a constraint most of the people using the name never saw: the wording of insurance contracts drawn up in London.

The conflict is a study in each side misreading the other’s order. To the colonial administration, the insurgents of the Malayan Communist Party’s army were first “bandits” and later “communist terrorists” — names that cast them as shapeless criminality operating in a jungle that was itself the very picture of disorder. Yet the insurgents were organised in a fairly rigid hierarchical party structure, with cells, committees, supply networks running through the squatter communities at the jungle’s edge, and a doctrine of protracted war. To the insurgents, the colonial order of plantation estates — straight rows of rubber trees, labour lines, managers’ bungalows, the regimented export economy — was the very picture of an imposed, alien box. Each side saw the other’s order as either chaos or a cage. This book’s idea was at work on a national scale.

The counter-insurgency’s most far-reaching measure was the resettlement of rural squatter communities under the Briggs Plan from 1950. Several hundred thousand people, most of them ethnic Chinese, were moved into fenced “New Villages” under guard. That was the boxed at its most coercive: an imposed form designed to cut the insurgents off from their supplies, which achieved that aim at enormous human cost and reshaped the peninsula’s society for generations. Later accounts of the campaign often stress instead the language of “hearts and minds” associated with General Sir Gerald Templer — winning consent rather than imposing control. Historians have argued over how far the words matched what happened. For this argument, what matters is that the campaign’s description of itself moved over time from the language of the box to the language of the open hand, while much of what it actually did was still fences and curfews. A form can describe itself as enabling while working as governing. That’s the danger Part Eight found in every designed room, written across a whole country.

§ 9. What the sense-maker’s tools teach

Put together, the tools of the imprint’s Cynefin works all point the same way. Snowden’s distinction between making sense and sorting, his matching of constraint types to domains, his estuarine survey of constraints by energy and time, and his insistence on the “anthro” in anthro-complexity add up to a toolkit for seeing exactly what this book’s idea says is hardest to see: a gathering’s unspoken constraints, how hard they are to change, and the identities people bring with them. Boyd’s Orientation supplies the diagram of the jacket and its implicit steering of action, and his destruction-and-creation cycle supplies the rhythm by which a jacket is seen and rebuilt from time to time. Putney supplies the historical model of a frame being challenged, and its ending supplies the warning. The Vietnamese practice of self-criticism supplies the hinge between enabling and governing reflection. The Malayan Emergency supplies this book’s idea at the scale of a war.

So what this Part adds to Book One isn’t a reversal but a sharpening. Snowden’s essay on baggy and boxed falls short not of some outside standard but of his own. If he’d approached Mowles’s conference as a sense-maker rather than a sorter, mapped its constraints by their energy rather than how visible they are, and paid attention to the identities its members bring, as his anthro-complexity demands, he’d have found a densely constrained complex-domain practice with its own enabling and constitutive form — and the argument would have become, as Part Five suggested, an argument about acoustics rather than structure. That he didn’t isn’t a special failing. It’s what this book’s idea predicts of anyone who walks into a rival’s room already knowing what it lacks.

Fifth Interlude · On the Instrument Maker

The man who makes a fine instrument for measuring depth will sound every harbour he doesn’t know, and chart its hidden shoals with care. He won’t sound his rival’s harbour, because he thinks he already knows it’s shallow. The instrument sits in his hand, calibrated and true, and he walks the shore of the one place where it would have surprised him, sure there’s nothing there to measure. It isn’t ignorance that stops him lowering the line. It’s knowledge — the kind that turns up before the data and leaves no room for them.

Snowden had the instruments and did not turn them on the room he criticised. Before we check Mowles for the same lapse, we need the imprint’s oldest conviction in full.

Part FourteenNo view from nowhere: the Stacey Matrix, Ryōan-ji, the joiner, the clades and the unaccommodated king

Where we areOne conviction runs through almost everything this imprint has written: there is no view from nowhere.
What this asksPart Fourteen gathers that conviction from a Kyoto garden, a Hockney collage, a science-fiction war and a mad king on a heath, and asks what each adds.
In brief 16 min read
  • Stacey drew a famous matrix and later turned away from it — the rare case of a maker taking off his own jacket. The matrix lived on in consulting without him: the manual outlived the master’s renunciation.
  • Ryōan-ji’s garden is a rigorous form designed so that no single viewpoint sees all fifteen stones; Hockney’s joiners compose many partial views without pretending to one. Both model a gathering that is neither baggy nor boxed.
  • Bourdieu shows that calling a rival “baggy” or “boxed” is a move in a classification struggle; Haraway names the claim to see from nowhere “the god trick”.
  • Sterling’s Shapers and Mechanists are body and prosthesis, tradition and design; the sundog who moves between them is Fleck’s multi-collective thinker.
  • King Lear demands explicit display and reads Cordelia’s tacit bond as “nothing”; on the heath he imagines a man beneath all clothes, and meets Edgar — whose nakedness is a costume.

TakeawayThere is no unaccommodated room. The fantasy of the formless gathering is Poor Tom: a disguise that looks like nakedness.

If one idea runs through all of this imprint’s work, it’s that there’s no view from nowhere. The most direct statement came in an earlier, shorter treatise that brought together Ralph Stacey’s famous matrix, David Hockney’s photographic joiners and the dry landscape garden of Ryōan-ji in Kyoto, alongside Bourdieu on struggles over classification and Donna Haraway on situated knowledge. The idea was dramatised in the imprint’s long reading of Bruce Sterling’s Schismatrix through complex responsive processes. And it took tragic form in the treatise on King Lear and complexity, whose title comes from the stage direction where the mad king enters fantastically dressed with wild flowers. This book’s idea is one particular case of that larger one — the case where the lack of a view from nowhere shows up as rival traditions seeing each other lopsidedly. This Part goes back to those three works and asks what each one adds.

§ 1. The matrix its maker gave up

In the 1990s Stacey published a diagram that became one of the most copied images in management education. It plotted decisions on two axes — how much the people involved agreed, and how certain they were about cause and effect. Near the origin, where agreement and certainty were high, rational planning was appropriate. Further out came zones where politics, judgement and finally anarchy took over, with a broad band of complexity between order and disorder. The matrix was intuitive, memorable and easy to sketch on a whiteboard, and it made its way into countless courses and consulting decks as a way of telling managers which approach suited which kind of decision.

Later, Stacey turned his back on it. As his thinking grew into the theory of complex responsive processes, he came to see the matrix as built on exactly the assumption his mature work rejected: that a manager could stand outside a situation, find it on a chart and pick an approach to match — that there was, in short, a view from nowhere from which decisions could be sorted. In later work he wouldn’t use it and said he was uneasy about how it was being applied. But the matrix didn’t retire with its maker. It went on circulating in textbooks, slide decks and training programmes, credited to him and taught as his, long after he’d disowned the assumptions it rested on.

That’s the rarest event in the history of jackets: a maker who saw his own garment clearly enough to take it off. Stacey did for his matrix what the Connoisseur asked Jackson to do for his grid, and what Book One asked Snowden and Mowles to do for their forms. And what happened next confirms Part Twelve’s point about manuals and masters. The matrix was a manual; it could be copied, drawn, sold and taught. Giving it up was an act of formation — a change in how one thinker saw the world — and that can’t be copied the same way. The manual travelled; the master’s change of mind didn’t. Every tradition that publishes a form should expect the same fate. Its forms will outlive its reasons for them, and they’ll be worn by people who’ve never heard that the tailor changed his mind.

§ 2. A garden where nobody sees everything

The dry landscape garden of Ryōan-ji, laid out in its present form in the late fifteenth or early sixteenth century and credited by tradition to various hands, is made of fifteen stones set in five groups on a rectangle of raked white gravel. A low earthen wall encloses two sides, and you view it from the veranda of the abbot’s quarters. People often point out that from no single spot on the veranda can you see all fifteen stones at once: wherever you sit, at least one is hidden behind another. Whether the designers meant this is debated, but for many visitors it’s part of what the garden means. To see the garden whole you have to move, and even then you only ever see it as a series of partial views, never as one complete picture.

The garden isn’t baggy. It’s one of the most tightly constrained forms in the history of landscape: a fixed rectangle, a fixed number of stones, gravel raked into exact patterns every day, walls that bound the view, a single platform to see it from. Nor is it boxed. Its rigour doesn’t fix what the viewer experiences; it opens it up. The hidden stone doesn’t confine you; it makes you contemplate — you realise every view is partial and the whole is more than any vantage point. Ryōan-ji is a designed form whose design makes sure nobody inside it can mistake their view for the view from nowhere. In that sense it’s the architectural version of the discipline this book has been looking for: a form that declares its own partiality by making partiality impossible to avoid.

What would a gathering designed on Ryōan-ji’s principle look like? Its form would make sure no participant, and no tradition, could see the whole — so that each had to accept that some stone was hidden from them, and that others, sitting elsewhere, could see what they couldn’t. It wouldn’t push everyone through one sequence, as the triopticon does, or leave each person with whatever perspective they walked in with, as the open conference does. It would arrange people so that the partiality of each view became part of what the gathering saw. Part Five’s proposed rite — each tradition describing itself as the others see it — is a first go at that kind of arrangement. Ryōan-ji suggests it could be built into the architecture of a gathering, rather than tacked on as an opening exercise.

§ 3. The joiner

In the early 1980s David Hockney started making what he called joiners: composite pictures put together from dozens or hundreds of photographs, each taken from a slightly different spot at a slightly different moment, fixed into one collage where the edges of the prints still show and the seams don’t quite line up. The best known, Pearblossom Highway (1986), builds a desert crossroads in California from hundreds of prints taken over several days, so that the road signs are seen face-on and close up while the road runs away into the distance and the sky is put together from many skies. Hockney argued that an ordinary photograph — one lens, one fixed point of view, frozen in an instant — gets wrong how we actually see. We look from many positions over time, and our sense of a scene is built up from many glances.

The joiner is a model of knowledge that refuses both the single viewpoint and the dissolving of viewpoints altogether. It doesn’t pretend to a view from nowhere; every print is taken from somewhere, and the seams show. But it doesn’t give up on the whole either; the prints are assembled into a scene that, Hockney argued, is truer than any single photograph. You can see that the composite is a composite. How it was made is declared in its form.

Applied to the argument between the traditions, the joiner suggests that the right relationship between the designer’s view, the traditionalist’s view and the curator’s view isn’t one winning, or a synthesis that makes their differences vanish, or a mere side-by-side where each stays on its own. It’s a composition where each view is set next to the others with its seams showing, so that the whole is built from declared partial views. This book has tried, in its own shape, to be something like a joiner: each Part looks from a different place, and it has tried to leave its seams visible rather than smoothing them into a single argument. Whether it has managed is for readers to judge. That it tried is a declaration.

§ 4. The fight over names

Pierre Bourdieu, whose ideas of habitus and doxa Book One leaned on, also described — in Distinction (1979) and later — what he called classification struggles: fights over the power to name and divide up the social world, to impose the categories everyone sees it through. The words we use for other people — vulgar and refined, serious and frivolous, rigorous and loose — aren’t neutral descriptions. They’re weapons in a contest for symbolic power, and whoever’s categories win gets to define what counts.

In that light, “baggy” and “boxed” aren’t just descriptions. They’re moves in a classification struggle. Calling a rival’s gathering baggy claims the authority to define what counts as structure, and puts you among the people who have it. Calling a rival’s method boxed claims the authority to define what counts as freedom, and puts you among the people who have that. Snowden’s title, which places his method between the two, is the boldest move of all: it claims the authority to define both structure and freedom, and to fix the right measure between them. This book’s idea explains why these moves come so easily and are believed so sincerely. Each side’s categories are the categories of its own habitus, so to that side they don’t feel like weapons. They feel like plain descriptions of what’s there.

Haraway’s essay “Situated Knowledges” (1988) called the claim to see everything from nowhere “the god trick”: the pretence of a disembodied, placeless gaze that sees without being seen and judges without being judged. Against it she proposed an objectivity of partial perspective — knowledge that’s located, embodied and answerable for where it stands. That’s the core of the jacket audit. It asks each party to say where it stands, to declare the position it sees from, and to take responsibility for what that position shows and hides. The god trick is refusing the audit: claiming to wear no jacket. Snowden’s “neither” is a modest god trick; Jackson’s grid, at its most imperial, a grand one; Mowles’s openness a subtle one, where claiming no structure stands in for claiming no position. Haraway’s alternative isn’t relativism — she was clear that situated knowledges aren’t views from everywhere and nowhere at once. It’s a demand that every view answer for where it stands.

§ 5. Shapers and Mechanists

The imprint’s long study of Sterling’s Schismatrix reads the novel and its related stories through complex responsive processes and the Prigogine-inspired science both drew on. It gives us a fictional world where this book’s idea drives history. Humanity in Sterling’s solar system has split into two great factions. The Shapers change themselves biologically — genetic engineering, psychological conditioning, cultivated talents. The Mechanists change themselves with prosthetics, cybernetic implants and machine-extended lives. Each side looks at the other with a mix of contempt and horror. Their conflict is a war between two ways of going beyond the human.

Seen through this book’s idea, the split is precise. The Shaper’s changes become body. They’re grown, absorbed, inherited, invisible as changes to the person who carries them, and indistinguishable from nature once made. They’re the jacket become skin — constitutive form, in Book One’s terms. The Mechanist’s changes stay attachments: visible, removable, replaceable, explicit additions to a body that stays separate from them. They’re the jacket still worn as a jacket — designed form. Each faction sees the other’s way of going beyond the human as a deformity. To the Shaper, the Mechanist is a clanking pile of boxes; to the Mechanist, the Shaper is an uncontrolled experiment in flesh. The split between tradition and design, between formation and method, between habit and procedure, is projected here into posthuman biology — and the result is a war.

Sterling’s hero, Abelard Lindsay, is a sundog — a drifter, exile and con man who moves between the factions, fully belonging to neither, using the language of each as the moment requires. His ability to survive and act depends on exactly that movement. He’s Fleck’s many-collective thinker in story form: someone who belongs to several thought styles, can see each as a style, and so can do what the loyal members of each faction can’t. The novel suggests, as Part Six argued, that the most productive place in a war of jackets isn’t inside either jacket but in moving between them — and that this place is uncomfortable, distrusted by both sides, and lonely.

The novel’s prophet of posthumanism, Wellspring, preaches that “life moves in clades”: humanity is splitting into daughter species, each a branch off a common stem. The imprint’s study took the line as its title, and it serves this argument well. The three traditions in the proposed room at Hull are clades in this sense. They share a common ancestor — the turn to the complexity sciences in management thinking in the late 1980s and 1990s, in which Stacey’s own early work on chaos and creativity played a founding part, and to which Snowden, Mowles and the critical systems tradition each responded — and they’ve diverged into distinct forms suited to distinct niches. Their failure to understand each other is the failure of daughter species who’ve forgotten they share a parent. Remembering wouldn’t erase their differences, but it might soften their contempt. Clades are cousins.

As the imprint’s study showed, the novel also draws on Ilya Prigogine’s theory of dissipative structures — forms that keep themselves going far from equilibrium only through a constant flow of energy, like a flame, a whirlpool or a living cell. The idea sheds light on the jacket in one more way. A tradition’s constitutive form is a dissipative structure: it lasts only as long as energy flows through it — the annual conference renewed, the doctoral programme recruited, the key texts taught again, the method run again. Stop the flow and the form decays. That’s why every tradition has to market its manual: the manual is the channel the sustaining energy arrives through. It’s also why every tradition is anxious about its rivals. A competing form is competing for the same flow of energy — students, clients, citations, attention — and losing that flow is death.

§ 6. The love test and the bond

King Lear opens with a gathering whose form proves fatal. The old king, having decided to divide his kingdom among his three daughters, stages a public ceremony in which each must declare how much she loves him, and her share will match her declaration. Goneril and Regan perform it magnificently, each outbidding the other in extravagant devotion. Cordelia, the youngest and most loved, won’t. Asked what she can say to win a third richer than her sisters’, she answers: “Nothing, my lord.” Pressed, she explains: “I love your majesty / According to my bond; no more nor less.”

The scene is this book’s idea as tragedy. Lear has designed a boxed ceremony — love made explicit, measured and traded for land — and he can only recognise love in the form his ceremony demands. Cordelia’s love is real and deeper than her sisters’, but it lives in a different form: the bond, the tie of daughter to father that makes her who she is, which isn’t performed but simply is, and which by its nature can’t be inflated into a bid. To Lear, whose jacket is the ceremony, her unspoken love looks like nothing — a gap where the form should be. He sees the bond as bagginess, as absence, and disinherits the one daughter who truly loves him. The sisters, who wore his form perfectly, betray him within two acts. The tragedy hinges on a king who couldn’t see a constitutive form because he was looking for a designed one.

The lesson for this argument is uncomfortable for everyone. Snowden, seeing the club’s unspoken constraints as absence, is in Lear’s position in that opening scene. But Mowles, seeing the facilitator’s explicit form as hollow performance — the cued applause, the outputs read aloud — risks the mirror-image mistake: assuming that anything performed explicitly must be insincere, as if every Goneril were the whole truth about explicitness. Cordelia’s bond and her sisters’ speeches aren’t the only options. Kent, who serves Lear in disguise, and the Fool, who serves him in riddles, show that explicit form can carry real loyalty and unspoken form can carry real truth. The question is never whether love is spoken or unspoken. It’s whether the speaker’s form answers to the bond.

§ 7. “Unaccommodated man” and the costume of nakedness

Out on the heath, in the storm, Lear meets Edgar disguised as Poor Tom, a mad beggar from Bedlam, nearly naked and raving. The king, whose own mind is breaking, sees in the beggar the truth of the human condition with all its clothes stripped off: “Thou art the thing itself: unaccommodated man is no more but such a poor, bare, forked animal as thou art.” And he starts tearing off his own clothes: “Off, off, you lendings! Come, unbutton here.”

This is the fantasy that has haunted the whole baggy-and-boxed argument: that underneath every jacket is the thing itself, a natural, unconstrained human being, and the job is to strip away the borrowed clothes of form so the truth can show. Mowles’s essay has a bit of this fantasy in it — the hope that, with structure removed, the spontaneous, the new and the eccentric will come out as what we really are. Snowden’s essay has the reverse — the fear that, with structure removed, all that’s left is the raw force of power and entrainment, the poor bare forked animal at its most brutal.

Shakespeare’s irony undoes both. The “thing itself” Lear admires isn’t unaccommodated at all. It’s Edgar, a nobleman in disguise, playing a Bedlam beggar with deliberate art — his nakedness a costume, his madness a script, his rags a carefully chosen garment designed to hide him from the men hunting him. The man Lear takes for nature is the most elaborately disguised figure on the stage. The unaccommodated man is a performance. And that’s the deepest reading of this book’s idea the imprint’s work offers. What looks like the absence of form is very often a form you don’t recognise as a form — and sometimes a form deliberately designed to look like none. The open conference, presented as the room without structure, isn’t Lear’s naked king. It’s Edgar’s Poor Tom: a gathering whose apparent formlessness is itself a form, cultivated with art over years, recognisable to its members as their own idiom and invisible to outsiders as a costume.

Earlier in the play, Lear himself had stated the truth his madness later forgets. When his daughters argue he doesn’t need his retinue, he answers: “O, reason not the need! Our basest beggars / Are in the poorest thing superfluous. / Allow not nature more than nature needs, / Man’s life’s as cheap as beast’s.” People need more than bare necessity, Lear insists. It’s the extra — the garment that does more than keep you warm, the form that does more than function — that separates the human from the beast. The jacket isn’t a corruption laid over some natural self. It’s the human condition. Argue away the need for form and you reduce the human to the animal. The job isn’t to take the jacket off. It’s to see it, choose it, and make it answerable.

§ 8. What these works teach together

Taken together, the works revisited in this Part say the same thing. Stacey giving up his matrix shows that a maker can see and take off his own jacket — and that the manual will outlive his change of heart. Ryōan-ji shows that a rigorous form can be designed to guarantee that every view is partial. Hockney’s joiner shows that partial views can be composed into a truthful whole without pretending to be one. Bourdieu shows that the names we give other people’s forms are moves in a fight over the power to name. Haraway names the god trick and proposes an objectivity of partial views that answer for themselves. Sterling projects the war of jackets into posthuman biology and makes the sundog, who moves between them, his hero. Shakespeare shows a king who can’t see a bond because he demands a designed ceremony, and who mistakes a costume of nakedness for the thing itself.

No view from nowhere; no human underneath the jacket; no room without form. These are the imprint’s oldest ideas, and the argument between Snowden and Mowles is one more occasion that proves them true.

There’s no such thing as an unaccommodated room. What passes for the absence of form is Poor Tom on the heath — a nobleman in rags, performing nakedness with art — and the king who admires him has mistaken a costume for the thing itself.

No view from nowhere, no human beneath the jacket. But the field of complexity is bigger than these three men. How does the pattern look across the whole family?

Part FifteenThe many complexities and the twin charges: the central idea across the whole field

Where we areUntil now the dispute has had two or three parties. The real field of complexity thinking has at least eight.
What this asksPart Fifteen asks whether the central idea holds across the whole family — and finds that the imprint’s own corpus spotted the pattern first.
In brief 15 min read
  • The imprint’s complexity corpus, gathered in its edition of Kevin Flinn’s A Web of Relations, distinguishes at least eight complexities: complex adaptive systems, complex responsive processes, complex phronetic process, complex social process, complex potential states, general complexity, process complexity and anthro-complexity.
  • The corpus had already identified, before this book, a single fault line running through all of them — the standpoint of the practitioner and the legitimacy of method — and a pair of symmetrical charges. These are the twin ideas in an earlier form, and that priority is acknowledged here.
  • Each complexity wears a jacket and sees its neighbours’ jackets: the adopter of complex adaptive systems sees the processual schools as quietist; the processual schools see the adopter as a systems thinker in new clothes; anthro-complexity sees both as missing method; the process philosophers see anthro-complexity as still enframed by adaptation.
  • Snowden’s own positioning map of the field, drawn in December 2025, contains the seed of the central idea: a recognition that where one stands decides how the map looks.

TakeawayThe dispute between Snowden and Mowles is one seam in a larger garment. Across the whole field of complexity thinking, every school sees its neighbour’s form and calls its own the nature of things.

Book One staged the baggy-and-boxed argument as a quarrel between two traditions, with a third — Jackson’s — waiting in the wings. That was true to the two essays, which talk to each other and to Jackson and nobody else. But the field they come out of is bigger and more varied than three traditions, and the imprint has mapped it more fully than any other part of its work, in the many-layered edition of Kevin Flinn’s A Web of Relations, which has grown through successive weavings into a body of work of around eight hundred thousand words. That corpus picks out not two or three but at least eight distinct ways of taking complexity seriously in the study of organisations, each with its own lineage, vocabulary, method and typical blind spot. If this book’s idea is true, it should hold across all eight. This Part tests it there.

§ 1. The field, mapped

The corpus’s map can be laid out briefly — at the risk of the very compression its whole argument pushes back against. Complex adaptive systems, as organisations have taken it up, treats organisations as systems of interacting agents adapting to each other and to their environment, and tries to make the science usable. Sharon Varney’s work on leading complex change, which the corpus examines at length, is its most careful current statement, and Neil Theise’s biology-based account of self-organisation is its closest scientific companion. Complex responsive processes, the tradition of Stacey, Shaw and Griffin, rejects the idea of the system as a thing altogether and places organising in the ongoing conversation of gestures between people in the living present. Complex phronetic process is the corpus’s name for the turn it sees in Mowles’s more recent work: from the descriptive question “what’s happening?” to the normative question “what should we do?”, answered through Aristotle’s practical wisdom and Hannah Arendt’s image of thinking without a banister. Complex social process draws on Norbert Elias’s figurational sociology, Andrew Abbott’s processual sociology and the practitioner writing of Chris Rodgers, who urges us to stop talking in terms of systems and start talking instead of systems. Complex potential states, Bonnitta Roy’s term, is a process-philosophy alternative to the adaptive paradigm. It draws on Whitehead to describe a causality of dispositions that works below the level of events. General complexity is Edgar Morin’s great project of a way of thinking that can hold together the dialogic, the recursive and the hologrammatic. Process complexity is Jean Boulton’s synthesis, built around seven characteristics — systemic, patterned, path-dependent, particular, punctuated, paradoxical and emergent — and set in conversation with the Daoist tradition. And anthro-complexity is Snowden’s programme. It insists that human systems differ from other complex systems because of what he calls the three I’s — intelligence, identity and intention — and it sees the practitioner’s contribution as designing methods through which a system can sense itself.

The corpus treats these not as a line of improvements but as a web: traditions that share a big refusal — of the organisation as machine, of the enlightened observer, of the plan that can be carried out as written — and that argue, inside that shared refusal, about nearly everything else. It’s the argument inside the refusal that interests this book, because that’s where its idea is at work.

§ 2. The fault line the corpus found first

The jacket audit demands that this be said plainly: the corpus had already spotted the structure this book calls the twin idea, and spotted it before either of the November 2026 essays was written. In its long critical reading of Snowden’s 2026 series on anthro-complexity, the corpus sets out the responses of four process schools — complex responsive processes, complex social process, complex potential states and complex phronetic process — and then states the structure of the whole disagreement. Under the local differences, it argues, one fault line runs through all four encounters: where the practitioner stands, and whether method is legitimate. The process traditions hold that you have to stay inside the flow of interaction, with no privileged outside. So any tool that claims to give the practitioner a grip on the system as an object brings back the objectifying standpoint their shared view of the world was supposed to dissolve. Anthro-complexity holds that the practitioner’s contribution is a matter of method — designing the conditions under which a system can sense itself — and that well-built method spreads sensing out rather than concentrating interpretation, so it gives a grip without bringing back authority.

From that fault line, the corpus goes on, come two mirror-image charges. Snowden charges the process schools that, by dissolving the practitioner into the participant, they can’t account for what the practitioner specifically contributes, can’t work at the scale of large, fast, spread-out systems, and leave open the alibi of emergence — the passivity in which nothing is anyone’s responsibility because everything just emerged. The process schools charge Snowden that, by stepping outside the conversation to design its conditions, he takes back the objectifying standpoint under a new name, moving the privileged position from interpreting content to designing the tool — and that his own admission that no method can finally avoid becoming a site of narrative power concedes as much. Each charge is serious, the corpus concludes; each lands; neither is decisively answered.

Those mirror-image charges are this book’s twin idea in the language of method. Snowden, from inside the designer’s jacket, sees the process schools’ refusal of method and calls it formlessness — the baggy room, the alibi of emergence. The process schools, from inside the participant’s jacket, see Snowden’s method and call it mastery in disguise — the boxed room, the observer brought back. What this book adds to the corpus’s finding is an explanation of why the charges mirror each other and why neither gets a decisive answer: each charge accurately describes the other side’s jacket, and neither can be answered from inside the jacket it describes. The finding belongs to the corpus. The explanation is this work’s contribution, and it should be read as a note on what the corpus had already seen.

§ 3. Eight jackets

Through this book’s idea, each of the eight complexities sees its neighbours with a clarity it can’t turn on itself. A quick tour makes the pattern plain.

The complex adaptive systems adopter, for whom Varney is the corpus’s example, keeps systems, requisite complexity and the adaptive paradigm as her home ground. She keeps a careful place for planning (asking only, in the corpus’s nice phrase, that we add the “-ing”), keeps the noticer, and keeps an optimism about method — a belief that the science can be made usable. From inside that jacket, the process schools look like a refusal to do the work: a hopeful pessimism that criticises every tool and offers none. The corpus records that Varney declines the turn to complex responsive processes, and insists she shouldn’t be converted; the work is strongest when its frameworks keep colliding. That’s this book’s idea honoured: the adopter is allowed her jacket, and isn’t told it’s nakedness.

The process schools spot the adopter’s jacket straight away. Systems language turns processes into things; the noticer stands outside what she notices; the optimism about method is exactly the hope the process tradition learned not to trust. Rodgers’s challenge — stop talking in terms of systems and start talking instead of systems — precisely names the adopter’s garment. Leaning on John Searle, he argues that the grammar of noun phrases hides the fact that process comes before product, and that there’s no higher-level system deciding what happens. But the process schools don’t see their own garment as clearly. Talking instead of systems is still talking in a particular way — about gestures, figurations, conversations and the living present — and that vocabulary is a lens, cut in opposition to systems thinking, that makes some things vivid and others invisible. The corpus records Snowden’s charge that the process schools can’t work at scale; whatever it’s worth, it points to something the process jacket finds hard to see from inside.

Anthro-complexity sees both of these jackets: the adopter’s naïve carrying-over of natural-science ideas to people who have intelligence, identity and intention, and the process schools’ refusal of methods that might help human systems sense themselves. It doesn’t easily see its own. The corpus’s reading of Snowden’s 2026 series finds what it calls a beholder-relativity boomerang in the way he positions the field, and a practitioner paradox in his ordering of claims. The method designer who insists that systems can’t be nouns, and that interpretation has to be spread out rather than concentrated, still occupies a position from which methods are designed and handed out. That position is the anthro-complexity jacket, and it’s the one place its own tools aren’t pointed.

Complex potential states, in Roy’s version, sees the adaptive paradigm itself as a jacket. Roy started, the corpus records, from a suspicion that something was wrong with the foundations of complex adaptive systems: it’s very hard to think about complex systems without framing agency as adaptation, and when a description shows up everywhere you look, it’s more likely a feature of a limiting paradigm than a feature of the world. That’s this book’s idea stated as a rule of knowledge — a description that turns up everywhere is probably the shape of your own lens — and Roy turns it on the whole adaptive tradition, including anthro-complexity, which shares its concern with fitness and response. Her Whiteheadian alternative reaches anthro-complexity’s own destination, a realism about dispositions, by a route that uses no natural-science enabling constraint at all. Yet Whitehead is a lens too, and a process philosophy that sees adaptation everywhere as a paradigm effect may be slower to notice that it sees prehension and concrescence everywhere for the same reason.

General complexity, Morin’s project, sees every other complexity as restricted: a local science of complex systems that hasn’t yet faced the complexity of knowledge itself — the loop in which the knower is part of what’s known. Morin’s three principles — the dialogic, which holds together what looks contradictory; the recursive, where products are also producers; and the hologrammatic, where the part contains the whole as the whole contains the part — are a sustained attempt to think without a jacket, or rather to think in full awareness that you’re always inside one. Of the eight, Morin’s tradition comes closest to this book’s idea as explicit doctrine. But the imprint’s own treatise on Morin, which strapped him to the ever-turning wheel of complexity, found that general complexity can turn into a cathedral — a total architecture of thought where every other tradition gets a side chapel and none is allowed to stay outside. The Connoisseur used the same image against Jackson’s grid. The cathedral and the grid are cousins: two ways of housing every tradition inside one building.

Process complexity, Boulton’s synthesis, sees the other complexities as partial expressions of one processual reality, and finds in the Daoist tradition a forerunner that grasped it whole. Her seven characteristics are an admirable tool, and her preference for “patterning” over “pattern” and for “systemic patterns” over “systems” is a small act of declaring with words — refusing the noun that turns process into a thing, and so keeping the tentativeness honest. But the corpus records, in its tribunal on Chinese thought, a set of charges against the very convergence claim her synthesis rests on: that the Dao may not be a process in Whitehead’s sense at all; that the correlative cosmology of the classical tradition isn’t the same as the dynamics of open systems; and that reading the Daodejing as an early complexity text may be the missionary’s error — reading another tradition through your own lens — that Roger Ames and David Hall warned against. Boulton’s jacket is the convergence claim itself, and its seams show where Chinese thought won’t fit.

Complex phronetic process, finally, sees the descriptive humility of complex responsive processes as a jacket that stops you acting, and offers practical wisdom as the cure. The corpus records the tension this creates inside Mowles’s own tradition, and asks whether the turn to “should” betrays the descriptive humility complex responsive processes was founded on. Through this book’s idea, the phronetic turn is a change of jacket inside one tradition: the participant who used to refuse to prescribe now asks what should be done — and in doing so puts on a garment closer to the designer’s than the tradition has been comfortable admitting.

§ 4. The boomerang

The corpus records that in December 2025 Snowden drew, at a client’s request and on the spot, a positioning map of the whole field of complexity and systems thinking. He called it an ostensive rather than a scholarly definition — a considered pointing at where things stood, not a history. The map refused to lump systems thinking into one bloc. It acknowledged Snowden’s own roots in soft systems and the Viable System Model. And it rested on a generous principle: science moves on, and that’s no disrespect to what came before. Beer, Snowden reckoned, would have built something other than the Viable System Model if he’d known the complexity science that came after him; Boyd, reading that science just before he died, would have changed the OODA loop.

The corpus’s reading of this map names a “beholder-relativity boomerang”, and the phrase is spot on. A positioning map is drawn from somewhere. Its axes and regions reflect where the person drawing it stands, and the traditions it puts at the edges are put there by that standpoint. Snowden’s map puts anthro-complexity at the frontier of a science that has moved on, and puts other traditions — systems thinking, complex responsive processes — in places defined by how they relate to that frontier.
