The boomerang is that the principle the map rests on, that science moves on, applies to the mapmaker too. Some later science will put anthro-complexity in a region defined by how it relates to a frontier we can’t see yet. The map that places everyone else in time is itself in time.

That’s this book’s idea in its time-bound form. Each tradition sees its predecessors as overtaken and its rivals as behind, and itself as the cutting edge. The Beer who would have built differently, the Boyd who would have redrawn the loop, are imagined from a standpoint that doesn’t imagine itself being redrawn. The corpus’s phrase “beholder-relativity” is the most compact statement of this book’s whole thesis the field has produced. Every map of the field is drawn by a beholder, and the beholder’s position is the one spot the map can’t show.

§ 5. A family quarrel

The corpus keeps calling the argument between anthro-complexity and the process schools a family quarrel, and the description is worth developing. These traditions agree about an enormous amount. Meaning is relational, emergent and a matter of process. The bounded, rational, calculating individual is a fiction. The system isn’t a noun standing apart from the inquiry. The enlightened consultant who sees from outside what participants can’t is an illusion, and often a dangerous one. Emotion, identity, story and history are part of how human systems move. And reflection can’t be escaped. Against the computational mainstream — process maps, targets, idealised future states, the organisation as machine — they stand together. Their quarrel is between people who have already rejected the same big mistake.

Family quarrels are the bitterest, and this book’s idea explains why. Traditions that share a refusal define themselves, inside it, by how they differ from each other, so those differences carry the whole weight of each tradition’s identity. A tradition that agreed with its nearest neighbour on everything but one point would stake its existence on that point. The fault line the corpus found — where the practitioner stands, and whether method is legitimate — is the one point where anthro-complexity and the process schools differ most. So it’s where each defines itself, and where each is most invested in seeing the other’s jacket and not its own. Sterling’s image of daughter species, from Part Fourteen, fits: branches from a common ancestor, competing for the same niche, looking at each other with contempt in proportion to how alike they are.

The family image also hints at what reconciliation might look like. Families don’t settle their quarrels by deciding who was right. When they settle them at all, it’s by remembering they’re related, recognising that each member’s particular form was shaped by the family’s shared history, and finding occasions when each can practise its own form with the others present, without demanding that they adopt it. That’s the Two-Row arrangement Part Twelve proposed, and it’s what the corpus itself does. A Web of Relations doesn’t convert Varney, or Snowden, or Mowles. It sets them side by side with their seams showing, like Hockney’s joiner, and lets their collision light up the field. The corpus’s own maxim — the work is strongest when its frameworks keep genuinely colliding — is the joiner’s principle applied to theory.

§ 6. What the field teaches the argument

Three things follow for the argument between Snowden and Mowles. First, their quarrel isn’t a one-off but an example: the same structure turns up wherever two complexities meet, and this book’s idea is the rule behind it. Second, neither side’s tools can settle the quarrel alone, because each side’s tools were cut to see the other’s jacket. Snowden’s method sees the club’s lack of method; Mowles’s reflectiveness sees the method’s hidden authority; neither sees its own. Third, the corpus already has what’s needed for a different kind of conversation — one where the eight complexities sit side by side, each declared, each allowed its form, each read by the others and reading them in turn. That conversation doesn’t produce a winner. It produces a field that can see itself.

The corpus’s big thesis is that organisation isn’t a structure that sometimes moves but a movement that sometimes looks like structure. Applied to the field of complexity thinking itself, the thesis has a reflexive edge the corpus didn’t push but its logic allows: the field isn’t a set of fixed schools that occasionally quarrel, but a movement of thought that occasionally hardens into schools — and the hardened bit is what each school calls its own position and sees in the others as rigidity. To see the field as a movement is to see your own school as one moment in it. That’s this book’s idea overcome, for an instant, by remembering that every jacket was once a gesture.

Sixth Interlude · On Family Likeness

At the wedding, the cousins recognise each other across the room by the set of the jaw, the turn of the hand, the laugh that belonged to a grandmother none of them really remembers. And because they recognise each other, they argue — about who has kept faith with the family and who has betrayed it, about whose way of talking is the true inheritance and whose is a corruption. A stranger at the same wedding would just see a family, alike in a hundred ways, split by differences too small for an outsider to notice. The cousins only see the differences. That’s what belonging to a family means: being unable to see the likeness that makes the quarrel possible.

The same fault line runs through the whole field. And in April 2026 one of the parties made a move that changes how we must read his November essay.

Part SixteenStacey unresolved, trouble unstayed: the trialectic and the midpoint

Where we areThe essays of November did not come out of nowhere. They continue a quarrel that began in public in April 2026.
What this asksPart Sixteen follows that quarrel, and asks whether Snowden’s finest idea of the year quietly overturns the title of his own November essay.
In brief 13 min read
  • The essays of autumn 2026 continue a quarrel opened in April 2026, when Snowden’s post “Stacey Unresolved” — which credited a dialogue with the present imprint’s author as having reframed its argument — relocated the central tension from Cynefin against complex responsive processes to Stacey against Mowles.
  • That post charged that complex responsive processes is Pelagian in its diagnosis and Augustinian in its practice; that its pragmatist grounding fails; that it applies Jaeggi at the wrong scale; and that its method cannot reach what its ontology describes.
  • In May 2026 Snowden completed the binary into a trialectic — assumed deficit, innate capacity, formative encounter — and insisted that a trialectic is not a spectrum with a midpoint.
  • By that criterion, the “neither” of his November title is a midpoint, a Cassian compromise rather than a Maximian third; and his own analysis of the assumed-deficit model explains why the designer must see the other room as baggy.

TakeawaySnowden’s finest work of 2026 supplies the instrument that convicts his November essay. The formative encounter he named in May is the third term the baggy-and-boxed quarrel was missing.

The two essays Book One examined didn’t come out of nowhere. They’re moments in an exchange the imprint’s complexity corpus has followed closely through 2026, and the corpus records an episode that bears directly on what they mean. In April that year, Snowden published a post on the Cynefin Company’s website called “Stacey Unresolved”, prompted by the announcement of a revised edition of Mowles’s book on complexity. The post stood out, the corpus notes, for two reasons. It shifted the debate from the familiar line of Cynefin versus complex responsive processes to what Snowden argued was a deeper and less settled tension inside complex responsive processes itself: Stacey versus Mowles. And it was openly collaborative about where it came from, crediting a conversation with this imprint’s author for completely reframing its central argument. The jacket audit demands that this be declared. This book isn’t written from outside the exchange it examines. Its author is a participant in it, credited by one of the parties, and that participation is part of the jacket it sees from.

§ 1. The charge made on Stacey’s behalf

As the corpus reconstructs it, “Stacey Unresolved” turns on a distinction borrowed from theological anthropology. In the Augustinian model, human beings don’t by nature have the capacity for real transformation; it has to be supplied from outside by an authoritative go-between — the priest, the institution, the method. In the Pelagian model, the capacity is already there in the person and the community, and the practitioner’s job isn’t to supply what’s missing but to create conditions in which what’s already present can start working. Snowden’s charge was that complex responsive processes is Pelagian in its diagnosis — its critique of representational management as imposing structure that crushes the very dynamics it claims to reveal — but Augustinian in practice, because the group-analytic facilitation it proposes brings back a hierarchical dependence on the conductor, the convener, the person who holds the reflective space. The tradition diagnoses dependence and then practises it.

The corpus judged that Snowden’s strongest move wasn’t the charge against Mowles but the charge on Stacey’s behalf: the argument that Mowles inherited Stacey’s most radical commitments about what reality is without following them through to what they mean for method. That makes the argument harder to brush aside, because you can’t treat it as an outsider getting things wrong. It’s an argument that the tradition isn’t yet faithful to its own founder. Three further charges back it up. The first says the tradition’s grounding in pragmatism doesn’t hold. Peirce’s picture of inquiry as a rope of many threads describes investigation that converges and corrects itself across a community aimed at what’s real — not a free-associating group held by a facilitator, where the container decides in advance what counts as a relevant detail. And the Deweyan test — what difference would believing this make, and how would we know if we were wrong? — has never been applied to the method itself. The second, discussed below, says the tradition applies Jaeggi at the wrong scale. The third says the method can’t reach the ontology: the most important things in organisations happen in the ordinary, continuous flow of interaction that no convened group recreates, so the tradition’s deepest insight has almost no practical consequence.

§ 2. Jaeggi at the wrong scale — and an essay that does it again

The second charge hits Mowles’s October essay, which Book One examined, especially hard. Snowden argued in April that Jaeggi is more promising for what the tradition actually does than Peirce or Dewey — but that her idea of change from within works at the scale of historical social formations over generations, not a facilitated group over an afternoon, and that shrinking it down to the convened group without argument is a category mistake. The corpus gave the charge a long analysis. Jaeggi’s forms of life, it notes, are big historical things — capitalism as a form of life, the nuclear family, the post-Enlightenment university — and the crises through which they learn or go backwards play out over decades and centuries, not ninety-minute reflective sessions.

Mowles’s October essay, published six months after the charge, uses Jaeggi in exactly the way the charge describes. It argues that if our problems are second-order — political and social rather than technical — then how a group handles its dilemmas together becomes the material for learning, so going nowhere at a research conference might be the point. Question XVI argued separately that Jaeggi doesn’t support the virtue the essay claims: in her terms, going nowhere counts as progress only when it unblocks a stuck learning process, and counts as going backwards when it just repeats itself. The April charge sharpens this. The essay doesn’t just draw a questionable conclusion from Jaeggi; it repeats, after the charge had been made in public, the very jump in scale the charge pointed out. You could defend that jump — the corpus explores how a small group might be seen as a miniature where the dynamics of forms of life are acted out and can be noticed — but the essay doesn’t defend it. It carries on as if the charge had never been made.

Through this book’s idea, that’s no surprise. A tradition hears charges from outside as descriptions of a jacket it doesn’t recognise, so it tends not to answer them so much as keep wearing the garment they describe. From inside the tradition, the charge that Jaeggi is used at the wrong scale is a charge against a move so natural it doesn’t look like a move at all. To the tradition, the small group simply is where forms of life are lived and noticed. To the critic, the small group is a container that has borrowed the dignity of history. Each is describing the other’s jacket.

§ 3. The binary becomes three

The corpus records a further development a month later, which is the most important event in this whole exchange for this book’s purposes. In May 2026 Snowden recognised that the Augustinian–Pelagian frame was itself a binary, and that binaries bring their own distortions. He completed it into what he called a trialectic by adding a third term that can’t be reduced to the other two, which he named after Maximus the Confessor. And he set out very precisely the test any such third term has to pass. A trialectic, he insisted, isn’t a spectrum with a midpoint. Three positions on a line between two poles give you a dialectic, not a triquetra. The third term has to be irreducible to the other two, not a compromise made out of them.

Two candidates were considered and turned down. John Cassian, the fifth-century author of what was later called the semi-Pelagian position — human will starts things, divine grace finishes them — fails because he bargains between the two terms of the binary instead of bringing in a genuinely different level of reality; he’s a midpoint on the line from Augustine to Pelagius. Nāgārjuna, whose doctrine of emptiness holds that nothing has inherent existence, fails for the opposite reason: he doesn’t bargain within the frame, he dissolves it — and, as Snowden put it, negating the frame isn’t a position inside it.

The corpus notes that the three remaining positions are first stated in plain language, before any theology. The assumed-deficit model, the Augustinian, assumes the system or person is missing something needed for change, and casts the practitioner as the one who supplies the missing method, insight or capability. That assumption is rarely spelled out and, tellingly, keeps itself going: without the deficit there’s no role for the go-between, so the deficit tends to stick around whatever actually happens. Most consulting, coaching and facilitation, Snowden observed, operate here without realising it. The innate-capacity model, the Pelagian, holds that the job is to create conditions in which what’s already there can start working — going with the grain of the system rather than against it. The formative-encounter model, the Maximian, holds that the meeting between practitioner and system produces something that couldn’t have been credited to either side beforehand. What emerges belongs to the coupling, not to either party. The practitioner’s own formation is part of the work. And the boundary between practitioner and system isn’t a line between two capacities that already exist, but the place where something new comes into being. Snowden found this in the experience of the best naturalists, clinicians and ethnographers, whose understanding wasn’t in them before the encounter and wasn’t in the field before they arrived. And he noted that a practitioner who turns up with a stable outside perspective, however humble, is still treating complexity as if it were merely complicated.

§ 4. The November midpoint

Put the November essay next to the May trialectic and a striking inconsistency shows up. “Neither Baggy Nor Boxed” presents the triopticon as a third option between the open conference and the facilitated workshop. It says, in so many words, that the method sits deliberately between conference and unconference. That’s a midpoint on a line between two poles. By the test Snowden himself set out six months earlier, it isn’t a trialectic. It’s a dialectic with a compromise in the middle — a Cassian position, bargaining between the binary’s two terms instead of bringing in an irreducible third.

Book One reached the same conclusion another way. Question V argued that “neither” claims a view from nowhere, and that, as Aristotle knew, where the middle lies depends on who draws the line. The trialectic gives a sharper and more damaging version, because it comes from Snowden’s own hand. The triopticon isn’t irreducible to the poles it sits between. It’s built out of them — some design from the workshop, some openness from the conference — and placed in the middle. Its claim to stand outside the line is exactly the claim Snowden refused Cassian.

What would a genuinely Maximian alternative to baggy and boxed look like? Not a better-designed event placed between two worse ones. It would move the question away from the shape of the event and towards the encounter between the practitioner and the gathering — an encounter whose outcome belongs to neither, where the practitioner’s own formation is part of what happens, and where the line between convener and convened is the place where something new comes into being. That’s very nearly the conclusion Part Nine reached under the heading “forming people, not designing events”, and the one the corpus reached in its essay on the discipline of return, under the heading “formation is the bridge”. The third term the quarrel was missing was named by Snowden in May. His November essay didn’t use it.

§ 5. Why the designer has to see the other room as baggy

The trialectic does more than catch the November essay sitting in the middle. It explains, from Snowden’s own analysis, why this book’s idea works the way it does. Think again about his account of the assumed-deficit model: an assumption rarely spelled out, and self-sustaining, because without the deficit there’s no role for the go-between, so the deficit tends to persist whatever actually happens.

That’s an exact account of why the designer needs to see the other room as baggy. A facilitation method exists to supply something a gathering is assumed to lack: structure, protection from entrainment, a fair share of voice. If the gathering already had these — if the club’s unspoken constraints already did the job of enabling constraints, as Part Thirteen argued — the method would have no role. So the designer is built to notice the absence of whatever his method supplies, and that tendency keeps itself going, because his practice depends on it. To see the club as densely constrained would be to see that his method is, in that room, unnecessary. The assumed deficit is the jacket of every method-maker, and this book’s idea is how that jacket shows up in perception: the strait-jacketed see the other as baggy because their trade depends on the other being baggy.

The same analysis applies, in mirror image, to the process tradition, and Snowden’s April charge already pointed at it. A tradition of group-analytic facilitation that diagnoses dependence in designed method, and then convenes groups that depend on the conductor, has its own assumed deficit: it assumes participants lack reflectiveness until the group gives them space for it. Its practitioners are inclined to see designed method as boxed — as crushing the reflectiveness only their own form can grow — because their trade depends on the deficit they diagnose in others. Each tradition’s view of the other’s jacket is underwritten by the economics of its own role. That’s Mowles’s own insight about having a stake in the game, turned by Snowden’s trialectic into a general rule: the go-between sees in others the deficit his role needs.

§ 6. Staying with the trouble — with practical wisdom

The corpus also traces a development inside Mowles’s own thinking that complicates Book One’s reading of his October essay, and fairness requires recording it. In a series of pieces through 2026 — “Starting with experience”, “Stuck in the middle with you”, “It’s all very well being critical, but what should we do?”, and an essay on the practice of courage for that year’s complexity and management conference — Mowles turned, the corpus argues, from the descriptive question of complex responsive processes to a normative one, answered through Aristotle’s practical wisdom and Arendt’s image of thinking without a banister. The corpus calls this turn complex phronetic process, and describes it as Stacey’s view of reality kept intact and made answerable in action: unpredictability accepted, then insisted on as the reason for judgement rather than an excuse to avoid it. It describes the practice that follows. Deliberate, oblique intervention in John Kay’s sense, since complex goods are reached sideways. Thick simplification, where reflective narrative becomes a tool for looking ahead. And growing the capacities of practical wisdom rather than managing systems.

Read against that background, “Staying with the trouble” is an odd document. Its advice that going nowhere may be the point, and its suspicion of structure as such, belong more to the earlier tradition’s descriptive austerity than to the practical-wisdom turn its author was making the same year. The essay that asked “what should we do?” and the essay defending the weekend at which nothing needs deciding came from the same hand within a few months. The corpus describes Mowles as moving back and forth — between complex adaptive systems, complex responsive processes and phronetic process as the occasion demands — and that may explain the difference. But through this book’s idea it also shows something more general. A thinker can change jackets within his own tradition and still, on a given occasion, put the old one back on without noticing — especially when the occasion is defending a much-loved room against an outsider’s description of it. The practically wise Mowles would ask what the open conference should do about the newcomer it shuts out. The October essay doesn’t.

§ 7. Maximus and the declared encounter

The choice of Maximus the Confessor as patron of the third term deserves a closing thought, because it brings in something the imprint’s theological work has explored at length. Maximus, writing in the seventh century, defended against the monothelite position the doctrine that Christ had two wills, divine and human, working in harmony without being mixed up or split apart. More broadly, he developed a theology of synergy, in which divine grace and human freedom aren’t competitors dividing a fixed amount of agency between them, but cooperate in a single action that belongs fully to both. The Augustinian worries that human freedom shrinks grace; the Pelagian worries that grace shrinks human freedom; Maximus denies they compete at all.

Carried over to gatherings, synergy suggests that form and spontaneity don’t have to compete either — that a gathering’s structure and its participants’ freedom can cooperate in a single action that belongs fully to both, as long as the structure is the right kind. What kind? The trialectic’s answer is the formative encounter: a form whose outcome isn’t owned in advance by the designer or the participants, in which the designer is changed along with the designed, and whose boundary is the place where something new arises. This book would add one condition, drawn from Parts Nine and Twelve: the encounter has to be declared. A formative encounter that hides the form it happens within — that presents itself as pure openness while resting on an unspoken liturgy, or as pure method while resting on the designer’s unacknowledged authority — isn’t synergy. It’s one more jacket. Synergy needs both parties to know what they’re cooperating inside. The declared encounter is the Maximian third, made answerable.

The go-between sees in others the deficit his role needs. The designer needs the room to be baggy; the conductor needs the method to be boxed; and the third term both were missing was the encounter in which neither owns in advance what the meeting will make.

The third term the quarrel lacked was named in May — and not used in November. To understand what the quarrel is really about, we need to look harder at agreement itself.

Part SeventeenThe matrix and the garden: the deleted axis, the cost of agreement, and the threshold object

Where we areYou have met the Stacey Matrix several times already. It is time to look at it properly.
What this asksPart Seventeen asks what was lost when the diagram was copied — and what its history teaches about the cost of agreement in any room.
In brief 13 min read
  • The imprint’s study The Stacey Matrix & the Zen Garden showed that the famous diagram had two axes of different kinds — certainty (epistemic) and agreement (political) — and that its most influential mutation, Ken Schwaber’s 2004 version for Scrum, deleted the political axis by renaming it “requirements”.
  • The deletion of the political is the characteristic move of the boxed jacket. The triopticon risks a subtler version of it: it treats disagreement as a problem of cognition and entrainment among experts rather than a conflict of interests among constituencies.
  • Read thermodynamically, agreement is a low-entropy order bought by continuous work. The club’s harmony and the designer’s order are both paid for, by someone. A room that produces novelty cannot be at equilibrium, so a productive “baggy” room is, by physics, a constrained one.
  • The study names the diagram a “threshold object”: true only when it points beyond itself, false when used to do the work it appears to offer. Every form in this dispute, and the discipline of declared form itself, is a threshold object.

TakeawayNo order is free. Whoever calls a room baggy is looking at order whose cost he cannot see; whoever calls a room boxed is looking at order whose cost he can.

Part Fourteen recalled that Stacey drew a famous matrix, later turned away from it, and watched it carry on without him. The imprint has given a study of around forty thousand words to that object, The Stacey Matrix & the Zen Garden. It traces the diagram’s family tree from 1986 to 2026, reads its axes through organisational politics and the thermodynamics of open systems, and reaches a verdict on what sort of thing it is. That study was about a diagram, not about the gatherings where baggy and boxed fight it out. But what it found bears on this argument with surprising force, because the diagram is the most widely copied attempt in the whole complexity literature to tell managers which kind of structure suits which kind of situation — and what happened to it is a parable of what happens to every such attempt.

§ 1. A copy of a copy

The study opens with five claims. First, the thing managers call “the Stacey Matrix” is a copy of a copy. Stacey drew a five-zone diagram to hold a paradox open, then disowned it, and the four-box version that took over the internet is a later mutation he never endorsed. Second, its two axes measure two different kinds of problem. The horizontal axis, certainty, is about knowledge — can we know what causes what? The vertical axis, agreement, is political — do the people who have to act want the same thing? And a political problem has no knowledge cure; no amount of analysis dissolves a clash of values. Third, the political axis is the bit everyone deleted. Fourth, disagreement is social entropy, and agreement is an unlikely low-entropy state that has to be paid for by continuous work. And fifth, the diagram’s far corner, labelled anarchy, is mislabelled. It isn’t chaos but thermodynamic equilibrium: the social heat-death where no work can be done.

From the earliest faithful versions — the teaching aid Brenda Zimmerman prepared for the National Collaborating Centre for Methods and Tools, and the entry in the Praxis Framework — the study recovers the complete five-zone structure and Stephen Larned’s diagonal of seven interventions, both dropped by the popular versions. It then follows the diagram through what it calls its mutation, migrations, disappearance and afterlife, across some two hundred and forty adaptations, each keeping the picture and losing a little more of the sense. The family tree itself demonstrates Part Twelve’s point about manuals and masters. The master changed his mind; the manual multiplied.

§ 2. The deleted axis

The turning point in the diagram’s history, the study argues, came in 2004, when Ken Schwaber, one of the founders of the Scrum framework for software development, reproduced the diagram with its axes renamed. Certainty became “technology”; agreement became “requirements”. It looks harmless: software teams are unsure about the technology they’ll use and the requirements they have to meet. But it swapped a political variable for a technical one. Stacey’s own labels for the low-agreement regions had talked about dominant coalitions and ideological control; Schwaber’s talked about requirements being more or less clear. At a stroke, the diagram stopped describing organisations as alliances of competing interests and started describing them as projects with specifications. The power Stacey had put at the heart of the diagram vanished from the version most practitioners came to know.

The study grounds its political reading of the axis in a solid literature: the theory of the dominant coalition in Cyert and March and in James Thompson; the informal coalitions and shadow side of organisations in Chris Rodgers’s work; James C. Scott’s public and hidden transcripts; Fleck’s thought collectives; and Douglas Griffin’s account of how systems thinking curdles into what he called cult values. On this reading, disagreement isn’t a shortage of information that better analysis could fix. It’s built into any organisation where different people want different things.

Through this book’s idea, Schwaber’s renaming is the signature move of the boxed jacket: turning a political problem into a design problem. The designer, whose trade is arranging conditions, sees conflict as a matter of conditions to arrange — requirements to clarify, processes to improve — and doesn’t see it as a clash of interests no arrangement can dissolve. The deletion isn’t malicious. It’s a matter of perception. The designer’s jacket has no pocket for politics, so politics doesn’t get seen.

Does the triopticon delete the political axis? Not as bluntly as Schwaber; Snowden’s essay is open about power, and its case against the open room is a case about domination. But the method’s design suggests a subtler version of the deletion. Its Eagles are chosen to represent disciplines, not interests. Its trios are designed to break up entrainment and early consensus, which are failures of thinking. Its Coyotes challenge assumptions, which are things of knowledge. The method is a superb tool for handling disagreement of the knowledge kind — between experts who see the same problem through different lenses. It has no obvious place for disagreement of the political kind — between groups who want different things and won’t stop wanting them however well the problem is understood. A hospital, a ministry, a community in conflict — the settings Snowden mentions — are places where the deepest disagreements are of the second kind. The study’s principle applies: a political problem has no knowledge cure. A method that treats all disagreement as a matter of thinking has, more quietly than Schwaber, renamed the axis.

The open conference deletes the political axis in a different way. Its tradition takes power very seriously, as Part Three acknowledged, but it mostly treats power as a dynamic to notice and talk about, not as a clash of interests to settle. Griffin’s account of cult values names the risk. When a group treats its shared values as an all-embracing whole every member must conform to, those values become ideals that allow no dissent, and breaking them is met not with argument but with blame and exclusion. A community held together by a shared tradition is exposed to exactly this risk. Its consensus about what counts as good practice, good conversation and good thinking can become a cult value, and the newcomer who doesn’t share it isn’t argued with, just quietly left out. The political axis hasn’t been renamed here. It’s been turned into a culture.

§ 3. Agreement takes work

The study’s boldest move is to read the agreement axis through non-equilibrium thermodynamics, drawing on Ilya Prigogine’s dissipative structures, Kenneth Bailey’s social entropy theory, and the information-theory view of entropy as uncertainty over possible states. On this reading, disagreement is social entropy: the natural, likely condition of any group of people with different histories and interests. Agreement is an unlikely, low-entropy order. Like every order far from equilibrium — a flame, a whirlpool, a living cell — it lasts only through a constant flow of energy. And the energy that keeps social agreement going is the work of power: persuading, negotiating, socialising, excluding, the endless labour by which a group keeps itself lined up.

That has a direct consequence for this book’s idea. If agreement is order and order costs something, then every room where people work together smoothly is paying for that smoothness somehow. The club’s easy conversation, where members understand each other without effort, is a low-entropy state, and its cost has been paid — over years of being socialised into the tradition, and all the time in the work of inclusion and exclusion that keeps people who don’t share the tradition at the edges or eases them out. The designer’s orderly sequence of roles is a low-entropy state too, and its cost is paid by the designer’s labour and by participants going along with a form they didn’t choose. Neither order is free. But the costs aren’t equally visible. The designer’s cost is out in the open: the method is up on the wall, and everyone can see the work being done. The club’s cost is hidden: it was paid long ago, by people who aren’t in the room any more or were never let in.

So here’s this book’s idea in thermodynamic terms. Whoever calls a room baggy is looking at an order whose cost he can’t see. Whoever calls a room boxed is looking at an order whose cost he can. Seeing bagginess means seeing order without visible work; seeing boxedness means seeing order with visible work. Both are seeing order. Neither is seeing its absence.

§ 4. The heat-death corner

The study’s correction of the diagram’s far corner sharpens the point further. In Stacey’s diagram, the region far from both certainty and agreement was labelled anarchy, and the popular versions have treated it as chaos: a turbulent zone of maximum disorder and danger. The study argues that’s backwards. A state of maximum social entropy isn’t turbulent but inert. It’s equilibrium: the condition where differences have evened out, gradients have gone, and no work can be done. Chaos, in the technical sense, happens far from equilibrium and is full of structure. Equilibrium is heat-death.

Apply this to the room. A truly unconstrained gathering — no shared tradition, no form, no history, no agreed time or place, nothing fixed at all — wouldn’t be a turbulent field of creativity. It would be a collection of people with no gradients between them for energy to flow along: a crowd at a railway station, each person heading somewhere private, nothing passing between them. That kind of gathering produces no conversation, no new ideas, no thought. That Mowles’s conference produces all three — that it’s often, by his own account, the highlight of its members’ year — therefore proves, on thermodynamic grounds, that it isn’t at equilibrium, and so isn’t unconstrained. A room that produces new things is a dissipative structure, held far from equilibrium by constraints and a constant inflow of energy. By the physics of open systems, the productive baggy room is a structured room. Its structure just isn’t where the designer looks for it.

§ 5. The edge in the wrong place

The study also looks at the edge of chaos, an idea much quoted in the complexity-and-management writing of the 1990s. Drawn from Christopher Langton’s work on cellular automata and Norman Packard’s experiments with evolving rules, it says that complex adaptive behaviour and computing power peak at a narrow boundary between order and disorder. The study records that in 1993 Melanie Mitchell, James Crutchfield and Peter Hraber, at the Santa Fe Institute itself, repeated Packard’s experiment and found that the evolved rules clustered elsewhere, for reasons that had nothing to do with computation at the critical point. They concluded the original results had been misread. The strong version of the idea didn’t survive the decade it was born in, though the looser intuition — that complexity peaks somewhere between order and disorder — lives on as a rule of thumb. And the study notes that the popular diagrams make things worse by drawing the edge of chaos as a line you mustn’t cross, when for Langton it was the zone of maximum capacity — the place to be.

The lesson for this argument is a warning. The rhetoric of “neither baggy nor boxed” — the fertile middle between too little structure and too much — has the shape of the edge-of-chaos romance: the belief that the creative zone sits at a precise point between two poles, and that a method can find it. The science that once seemed to back that belief didn’t hold up in its strong form. What survives is the softer rule of thumb that neither total order nor total disorder produces anything. That’s true, and it says nothing in particular about where any given method stands. The middle isn’t a place. It’s a direction to pay attention in.

§ 6. The leanest possible space

One of the study’s most illuminating chapters reads the diagram as a landscape of possibilities, drawing on Sewall Wright’s fitness landscapes, Stuart Kauffman’s rugged landscapes and the adjacent possible, Conrad Waddington’s canalisation, and David Raup’s morphospaces. Raup distinguished empirical morphospaces, built up from measured forms, from theoretical ones, generated from a few abstract parameters, which contain every form the parameters allow whether or not anything has ever taken that form. He generated the whole kingdom of coiled shells from a handful of parameters — and the price, the study notes, was every dimension those parameters left out. The Stacey field is a theoretical morphospace of the leanest kind: two parameters, certainty and agreement. That’s why it looks elegant and complete, and why it shows regions no organisation actually occupies. Its elegance is bought by leaving things out.

The line along which baggy and boxed are arranged is leaner still: one parameter, the amount of structure, running from none to total, with a fertile middle. It’s a one-dimensional space for gatherings, and like every lean space it’s seductive because it seems complete. Everything Books One and Two have added — who’s in the room, ways of speaking, whether the frame can be challenged, whether power can be reversed, how long formation takes, how visible constraints are, the political as opposed to the knowledge kind of disagreement — is a dimension the single line leaves out. In Raup’s terms, the jacket audit refuses the lean space. It insists that gatherings vary along many dimensions, that most of the space they could occupy is empty, and that any two-pole picture of where a gathering stands is a by-product of the parameters chosen to draw it.

§ 7. The web and the grid

The study closes its analysis with what it calls the two matrices, and the image is worth bringing into this argument. There’s the Cartesian grid of Stacey’s diagram, a space of coordinates read from outside. And there’s the Foulkesian matrix of group analysis, the web of communication among a group’s members, where every remark takes its meaning from the network of relationships it’s spoken in. The study shows that both come from a shared root in Norbert Elias’s figurational sociology, and that what Stacey kept, after he burned the grid, was the web.

Through this book’s idea, the grid and the web see each other with typical distortion. From inside the grid, the web looks baggy: an endless mesh of relationships with no axes and no coordinates, from which nothing can be read off. From inside the web, the grid looks boxed: a rigid space that pins living relationships to fixed positions and reads them from an observer’s standpoint the web denies. Snowden’s method, with its designed roles and sequences, stands closer to the grid; Mowles’s conference, with its matrix of conversation, stands closer to the web. But the study’s finding about family trees is that both are children of Elias. The grid and the web are two ways of rendering the same insight about how people are bound together in shifting networks of interdependence — one by mapping positions, the other by living inside relationships. They’re clades again: daughter species of a common parent, each seeing the other as a deformed version of the family form.

§ 8. The threshold object

The study’s final verdict on the diagram is that it’s neither a tool nor a mistake but a threshold object: true only at the moment it points beyond itself, false the instant it’s used to do the job it seems to offer. Its honest centre — the admission that in the complexity zone the rational toolkit fails — names a region the grid can’t map: the living web, in which the observer is one of the knots. The diagram teaches its lesson by pointing past itself; it betrays its lesson when it’s used as a map.

The idea applies to every form in this argument. The triopticon is a threshold object: true when it points beyond itself, to the formative encounter where convener and convened both change and nobody owns the outcome in advance; false when it’s used as a procedure that guarantees good deliberation. The open conference is a threshold object: true when it points beyond itself, to the living present where new things arise; false when it’s used as proof that structure isn’t needed. Jackson’s grid is a threshold object: true when it points beyond itself, to the recognition that every method has a proper range; false when it’s used as a neutral map for picking methods. And the discipline of declared form proposed in Part Nine is a threshold object too: true when it points beyond itself, to the trained ability to see your own jacket; false the moment it’s administered as a checklist that certifies a gathering as honest.

That last one is the one this book has to keep in sight. Every framework this imprint has examined has ended up guilty of becoming a map when it should have stayed a threshold. The jacket audit isn’t exempt. It stays true only as long as it points beyond itself — to the way of seeing it’s meant to grow — and it turns false the instant it’s mistaken for that way of seeing. “Threshold object” is the most honest name a framework can have, and the most demanding: it asks to be walked through, not stood on.

Seventh Interlude · On the Garden Rake

Every morning the monk rakes the garden’s gravel into the same long lines, the same circles round the same stones. A visitor who comes once sees an emptiness of white stones and thinks: nothing gets done here. A visitor who comes every morning sees the rake, the hour, the discipline, the lines put back after every footstep, and thinks: everything gets done here, and done again. The first visitor has seen the garden. The second has seen what the garden costs. Neither has seen the monk, who doesn’t think of the raking as work, because it’s become the shape of his morning — and that’s a kind of blindness too: the blindness of someone for whom order has become the same thing as peace.

No order is free. And if every form is a threshold object, perhaps we should treat frameworks less as maps and more as moves in a conversation.

Part EighteenThe living map: offers, status, both “ands”, and the map that precedes the room

Where we areWe have been talking about maps for a while. Now we ask what a map actually is, and what an actor on a stage knows about rooms.
What this asksPart Eighteen brings improvisation into the argument and asks what happens when every framework is treated as an offer.
In brief 14 min read
  • The imprint’s essay The Living Map holds that improvisation, reflexive cartography and immersive process are one practice wrongly divided, and its companion, Chaos Puzzles, opens with a puzzle that anticipates this book: the rejection of frameworks is itself a framework.
  • From Korzybski to Baudrillard, the map is not the territory — but maps can precede and produce the territories they claim to describe. The baggy-and-boxed axis is such a map, and both essays see rooms through it.
  • Keith Johnstone’s improvisation teaches that status cannot be abolished, only played consciously; the triopticon’s claim to cut across hierarchy and the conference’s claim to flatness are both status moves.
  • Rodgers’s distinction between set-piece and open play shows that the dispute concerns events, while organisational life happens in open play; his “both ands” — holding immersion and abstraction at once — is a paradox to be held, not a midpoint to be located.

TakeawayA framework is an offer. The quarrel between the baggy and the boxed is a scene in which each player blocks the other’s offer — and improvisation’s first lesson is that blocking kills the scene.

Among the imprint’s works on complexity is an essay about the oldest image in the theory of practice — the map and the territory — which argues that three practices the literature has treated separately are really facets of one. The Living Map: Improv, Reflexive Cartography and the Grammar of Immersive Process follows the image from Alfred Korzybski through Gregory Bateson, Jorge Luis Borges, Jean Baudrillard, Donna Haraway, Ludwig Wittgenstein, Maurice Merleau-Ponty, Gilles Deleuze and Félix Guattari, and David Bohm. Then it follows it into the practical worlds of scenario planning, theatrical improvisation and organisational change, ending with Chris Rodgers’s account of what he calls the immersion–abstraction paradox. Its central idea is the living map: a map drawn as you travel, revised by every encounter, never finished. Its companion volume, Chaos Puzzles, a set of movements on Mowles’s work, opens with a puzzle this book could have used as its epigraph: rejecting frameworks is itself a framework. This Part goes back to both works and asks what they add to the baggy-and-boxed quarrel.

§ 1. The first chaos puzzle

The opening movement of Chaos Puzzles uses a blog post of Mowles’s as what it calls a paradox device. Mowles’s tradition is wary of what The Living Map calls the cartographic reflex — the habit of reaching for a map, a framework, a two-by-two grid — and argues that such frameworks turn experience into a realist, representational form that hides more than it shows. The first puzzle points out that this rejection is itself a framework: a set of commitments about what counts as real knowledge, how practice should go, and what to distrust, held as tightly as any framework and taught to newcomers as the tradition’s orthodoxy.

That puzzle is this book’s idea in its earliest form within the imprint’s complexity corpus, and it deserves credit for coming first. Book One argued that Mowles’s conference, presented as the room without structure, is structured by a tradition its members don’t see as structure. The first chaos puzzle had already stated the general rule: refusing frameworks is a framework that doesn’t know itself. What Book One added was the twin — that the designer’s embrace of frameworks is equally a jacket that can’t see its own seams — and the explanation, through Polanyi and Heidegger, of why each jacket is invisible from inside. The puzzle came first. This book is its long-form answer.

§ 2. Maps that swallow territories

Korzybski’s saying, written down in the early 1930s, that the map is not the territory, is where The Living Map starts, and the essay follows it through its strange later life. Lewis Carroll, in Sylvie and Bruno Concluded (1893), imagined a map on the scale of a mile to the mile, which was never unfolded because the farmers complained it would cover the whole country and block out the sun — so, the narrator explains, they now use the country itself as its own map. Borges, in a one-paragraph fable of 1946, imagined an empire whose mapmakers made a map that matched the empire point for point. Later generations found it useless and left it to the weather, so that tattered scraps of it survive in the western deserts, home to animals and beggars. Bateson took Korzybski’s saying into the theory of mind, insisting we never perceive the territory directly, only differences turned into news by our own sensory and conceptual maps. And Baudrillard, in Simulacra and Simulation (1981), turned the fable inside out. In a world saturated with representations, it’s no longer the map that copies the territory but the map that comes first — what he called the precession of simulacra — so territories start being produced in the image of the maps that were meant to describe them.

Baudrillard’s reversal is what bears on this argument. The line on which baggy and boxed are arranged is a map, and both essays see rooms through it before they see the rooms. Snowden arrives at Mowles’s conference already holding a map with it marked “baggy”, and what he describes is the map. Mowles arrives at the facilitated workshop already holding a map with it marked “boxed”, and what he describes is the map. Neither essay reports a room seen fresh; each reports a room located on a chart drawn in advance. And because the chart circulates — in essays, conference talks, the training of practitioners — it starts to come before the rooms it describes. Conveners design gatherings to sit at the right point on the line; participants arrive expecting a baggy or a boxed experience and get one. The map produces the territory. This book’s idea, in Baudrillard’s terms, is that each tradition has drawn a map of its rival and now meets only the map.

§ 3. Meaning is use

The Living Map finds in Wittgenstein the turn that rescues frameworks from both their fans and their critics. The early Wittgenstein of the Tractatus held a picture theory of meaning: a statement is a picture of a possible state of affairs, and its sense lies in matching the world. The later Wittgenstein of the Philosophical Investigations swapped the picture for the tool: for a large class of cases, the meaning of a word is its use in the language (§43). On this second view, a framework isn’t true or false by matching the territory. It’s useful or useless in the practice where it’s used, and its value lies in the conversations, perceptions and actions it makes possible.

That dissolves a good deal of the quarrel. Snowden defends the triopticon as if it were a better picture of how deliberation should be structured; Mowles attacks designed methods as if they were false pictures of how people actually relate. If frameworks are tools, the question isn’t which picture is accurate but what each tool does, for whom, in which practice. A hammer isn’t a false picture of a screwdriver. The triopticon and the open conference are different instruments, and their value is in how they’re used. Wittgenstein also prized what he called a perspicuous representation — an übersichtliche Darstellung, a way of arranging what we already know so that we get a clear view of it (§122). The jacket audit aims to be one: not a new picture of gatherings but an arrangement that lets each tradition see clearly what it’s already doing.

§ 4. Status is the furniture

The essay’s turn to theatrical improvisation brings in something the argument has completely lacked. Keith Johnstone, whose Impro (1979) is still the founding text of modern improvised theatre, taught that every inflection, posture, glance and pause between people implies a status — a relative position, high or low — and that status exchanges are constant, mostly unconscious, and impossible to get rid of. You can’t step outside status; you can only play it more or less knowingly. The Living Map puts the lesson in a phrase: status dynamics are the furniture of organisational life. It recommends not trying to abolish them but introducing offers that disrupt status, and creating conditions where a low-status participant can make a high-value contribution.

The lesson cuts through both essays. Snowden says the triopticon’s roles cut across hierarchy, and Book One accepted that this is a real strength of the design. But Johnstone would point out that cutting across hierarchy is itself a status move — the designer’s status goes up as he hands out everyone else’s — and that new hierarchies form instantly inside the new roles: the Eagle who speaks first, the Raven who’s kept quiet, the Coyote licensed to challenge. Mowles’s conference presents itself as a flat space where conversation goes where it goes, and Johnstone would point out that flatness is the most effective status play of all for people who already have high status in the tradition, because it disguises their position as just taking part. Neither form gets rid of status. Each plays it. The question, on Johnstone’s account, is whether the playing is conscious — whether the gathering knows its status exchanges and deliberately arranges for the low-status participant to make a high-value offer. That’s the fifth of Part Nine’s six questions, in the language of the stage.

§ 5. Offers and blocks

Improvisation’s most famous teaching is the difference between offers and blocks. An offer is anything a performer brings into a scene — a line, a gesture, a premise. A block is turning the offer down, which stops the scene developing. The discipline linked with the phrase “yes, and” asks performers to accept each offer and build on it, so the scene grows from everyone’s contributions. The Living Map carries this into organisational life, where it suggests frameworks are best seen not as prescriptions but as offers: proposals put into a conversation, to be accepted, extended, transformed or, sometimes, gently declined.

Seen that way, the exchange between Snowden and Mowles is a scene where each player blocks the other. Mowles offers the open conference as a space for affecting and being affected; Snowden takes the offer, labels it baggy, and blocks it with the charge of hidden power. Snowden offers the triopticon as a method that protects the unexpected; Mowles’s tradition, as the October essay represents it, takes such offers as more structure squeezing out uncertainty, and blocks them. Each essay is a block dressed up as a reply. And improvisation’s first lesson is that blocking kills the scene. What would “yes, and” look like between the traditions? Not agreement — improvisers don’t agree with every premise; they build on it. Mowles might say: yes, the open room doesn’t abolish power, and here’s how our reflective practice notices it and works with it. Snowden might say: yes, the club is constrained by its tradition, and here’s how a designed phase might open it up to the people the tradition leaves out. Each would accept the other’s offer and move the scene on. This book’s idea explains why that’s hard: each sees the other’s offer through his own jacket, as either a void or a cage — and nobody says “yes, and” to a void or a cage.

§ 6. Minimal structure, maximal freedom

The essay also draws on Frank Barrett, a jazz pianist and organisational theorist whose Yes to the Mess (2012) carries the lessons of jazz improvisation into management. Barrett describes the jazz combo as working with minimal structure and maximal autonomy. A tune’s chord changes and form give just enough structure to coordinate the players, while leaving each free to improvise inside it and against it. He also talks about provocative competence — a leader’s ability to disrupt habitual patterns in ways that invite people to go beyond their routines without leaving them lost.

“Minimal structure, maximal autonomy” can sound like another way of saying “neither baggy nor boxed”, and in a sense it is. But Barrett’s jazz supplies the qualification Book One’s Question IV insisted on. The structure is only minimal to people who have absorbed it. To a player who doesn’t know the changes, the tune isn’t minimal structure but a baffling maze; to one who’s played it a thousand times, it’s the barest scaffold. The autonomy is only maximal for people whose formation has made the structure their own. “Minimal structure” describes how a form feels to the people formed by it. It isn’t a property the form has regardless of who’s playing. A designer who offers minimal structure to people who haven’t been formed has handed them a box; a tradition that offers maximal freedom to people who haven’t been formed has handed them a void.

§ 7. The gentle art of reperceiving

The Living Map gives a chapter to Pierre Wack, who led scenario planning at Royal Dutch Shell in the 1970s and whose two 1985 essays in the Harvard Business Review made scenario practice famous. Wack argued that the point of scenarios wasn’t to forecast the future but to change decision-makers’ mental models — what he called their microcosm, the inner map through which they saw the world. He called the practice the gentle art of reperceiving. Scenarios worked, when they did, by loosening the microcosm enough for managers to see what their existing model was hiding. Wack also went looking for what he called remarkable people: unusual individuals, often from outside the industry, whose views could unsettle the assumptions of those inside it.

Wack’s microcosm is the jacket, described by a strategist, and his gentle art of reperceiving is the back-and-forth the jacket audit needs: a practice that makes your own unspoken model visible long enough to revise it. His remarkable people are the outsiders whose presence Part Eight identified as the most important factor in the politics of a room — people who don’t share the organisers’ tradition and so can see its walls. Wack’s practice suggests that a tradition can’t do the jacket audit on its own. It needs people who see the tradition from outside, and a form gentle enough to let what they see be heard without setting off the defences every microcosm puts up against disturbance.

§ 8. Set-piece and open play

The essay’s final chapter turns to Chris Rodgers, whom it calls the dissenter inside the tradition — a practitioner close to complex responsive processes who still insists that frameworks aren’t the problem. Rodgers distinguishes set-pieces from open play. Set-pieces are the planned events of organisational life — the workshop, the away-day, the conference, the change programme — where conversation is deliberately convened. Open play is the ordinary, continuous, unremarkable flow of everyday conversation in corridors, meetings, emails and chance encounters, where most of what actually happens in an organisation happens. His point is that practitioners pay far too much attention to set-pieces, because those can be designed and sold, while the real game is played in open play.

That distinction lights up the whole baggy-and-boxed quarrel, which is a quarrel about set-pieces. Snowden’s triopticon is a set-piece; Mowles’s conference is a set-piece; the sticky-dot workshop is a set-piece. All three are convened events, and the argument is about how such events should be structured. But if Rodgers is right, the events matter less than the open play people go back to — and the question that matters most isn’t how a set-piece is structured but how the people it shaped play in the open afterwards. That’s Snowden’s own third charge against complex responsive processes in April, turned against both traditions: the method can’t reach what the ontology describes, because the method is a set-piece and the ontology describes open play. The corpus’s answer, in its essay on the discipline of return, is that formation is the bridge: the set-piece matters because of the practitioner it shapes for the open play she goes back to. Part Nine reached the same answer. Rodgers gives it its most precise vocabulary.

§ 9. Both “ands”

Finally, Rodgers names the paradox at the heart of practice: you need to be immersed in the flow of interaction and to step back from it, to take part and to reflect, to live in open play and to design set-pieces — and to do both at once. The Living Map, following his phrase, calls this holding both “ands”. It refines Stacey’s distinction between abstraction that stays close to lived experience and abstraction that floats off into systemic models, arguing that the problem isn’t abstraction as such but what Whitehead called misplaced concreteness — mistaking the abstraction for the real thing. Frameworks aren’t the problem; forgetting they’re frameworks is. And the mature practitioner, the essay concludes, doesn’t resolve the paradox but holds it open, muddling through in the honourable sense Charles Lindblom gave the phrase: moving forward in careful, revisable steps without pretending to have a complete plan.

Both “ands” is the alternative to the midpoint. “Neither baggy nor boxed” settles the paradox of structure and openness by finding a point between them. Both “ands” refuses to settle it, and asks the practitioner to be fully structured and fully open at once, in tension, without compromise. That’s Part Sixteen’s Maximian third in the language of practice: not a middle point on a line but a different register, where the poles aren’t traded off against each other but held together. The practitioner who holds both “ands” doesn’t ask how much structure to impose. She asks how to be, at the same time, the designer who sees the walls and the participant who lives inside them.

§ 10. The trivium and the crash helmet

The essay proposes the medieval trivium — grammar, dialectic and rhetoric — as its map of maps, and matches its three arts to mapping, process and improvisation: the grammar of the map, the dialectic of the encounter, the rhetoric of the offer. It’s a fitting frame for a book built on scholastic disputation, because the trivium was the curriculum in which disputation itself was learned. The three arts were never meant to be practised separately. A student who knew grammar without dialectic could parse but not argue; one who knew dialectic without rhetoric could argue but not persuade. In the trivium’s terms, the baggy-and-boxed quarrel is between a grammarian and a rhetorician, each convinced his art is the whole of learning.

The Living Map closes with travellers who come back from a voyage with maps that no longer match the territory, and with organisations that, having done this work, are lived in differently — less defended against the new. The practice is never finished; it’s kept up, lost and picked up again, like a musician’s daily practice. And its last sentence is an image of readiness rather than conclusion: the crash helmet is on the table. This book can’t do better than borrow it. The jacket audit isn’t a destination. It’s a readiness — the helmet on the table, for the next encounter with a room whose walls you can’t see yet.

A framework is an offer, and rejecting frameworks is an offer too. The scene dies when each player blocks the other. It lives when each says “yes, and” — not because he agrees, but because he’s seen that the other’s offer was never a void or a cage, just another way of beginning.

Every framework is an offer. So who makes the offers in a room? The conveners — and they have more coats than you might think.

Part NineteenThe convener’s many coats: Open Space, the kenotic convenor, Shaw, Rogers, Kahane, and the servant who claims no power

Where we areEvery room has a convener, and every convener has a style. The imprint has studied many of them.
What this asksPart Nineteen walks through a gallery of conveners, from Open Space to the Quaker clerk’s cousins, and asks how each one claims, sincerely, to hold power lightly.
In brief 15 min read
  • The imprint has written on Open Space Technology and the Art of Hosting, on Harrison Owen and Patricia Shaw, on Carl Rogers, on servant leadership, and on facilitation traditions read through the classical Chinese figure of South Mountain. Each tradition of convening wears a jacket and sees its neighbours’.
  • Open Space is the most declared minimal form: a ritual of circle, wall and marketplace that its practitioners experience as freedom and its critics as technique. Owen’s figure of the convener who empties himself — which the imprint has called the kenotic convenor — is the facilitator’s version of the designer’s claim to stay out of the content.
  • Shaw refuses the container altogether, entering conversations already under way; but a seasoned consultant’s presence is itself a container. Rogers located the structure of the encounter in the facilitator’s way of being — the most incorporated jacket of all.
  • Kahane’s two-axis map of collaboration, which cites Snowden, is a categorisation of the same kind as the baggy-and-boxed axis; Mowles’s tradition holds that complexity is “all the way down”. Their quarrel is the central idea again.

TakeawayEvery convener claims, in some idiom, to hold power lightly. The claim is sincere and is also the convener’s most effective exercise of power, because it is the one that cannot be contested.

Underneath it all, the baggy-and-boxed argument is an argument between conveners — the people who bring others together and decide, openly or not, how the gathering will run. The imprint has written about the craft of convening from many angles. The Architecture of the Held Space covers the Art of Hosting, Harrison Owen’s Open Space Technology and Carl Rogers. The Geometry of the Vanishing Container compares Owen with Patricia Shaw. South Mountain looks at facilitation traditions rooted in classical Chinese poetry. The Sovereign Servant is a critical study of servant leadership drawing on Foucault, Bourdieu, James C. Scott, David Graeber and the Industrial Workers of the World. And the complexity corpus, throughout, discusses Karina Solsø, Adam Kahane, Sharon Varney and others. This Part brings those works together and asks how each tradition of convening looks through this book’s idea.

§ 1. Open Space: the most openly declared minimal form

Harrison Owen developed Open Space Technology in the mid-1980s after noticing, by his own account, that the most valuable part of a conference he’d spent a year organising was the coffee breaks. Open Space tries to make a whole gathering as productive as a coffee break. Participants sit in a circle. The convener explains a handful of principles. Anyone who cares about an issue writes it on a sheet, announces it to the circle and pins it on a wall, which becomes a marketplace of sessions. People go to whichever sessions draw them, and leave when they’re no longer learning or contributing — what Owen called the law of two feet. The principles are famously short: whoever comes are the right people; whatever happens is the only thing that could have; whenever it starts is the right time; when it’s over, it’s over.

To a designer of structured methods, Open Space looks baggy: a bare-bones procedure that leaves the gathering to whoever turns up and whatever they feel like discussing. To its practitioners, it’s a precise ritual — the circle, the opening, the posting, the marketplace, the closing — performed with care, and its minimalism is the result of long experiment with how much can be taken away before the gathering falls apart. In that sense it’s the most openly declared minimal form in the facilitation toolkit. It has few constraints, but every one is said out loud at the opening, and participants know exactly what they are. So it does something the open research conference doesn’t: it’s a minimal structure that’s fully declared. And it does something the triopticon doesn’t: through the marketplace, participants choose what the gathering will be about.

Still, Open Space wears a jacket too. Its principles aren’t neutral descriptions of how groups work. They’re a philosophy — of self-organisation, of trusting whoever comes, of accepting whatever happens — presented as if it were simply true. “Whoever comes are the right people” is a beautiful sentence, and also a way of not asking who didn’t come and why. The law of two feet assumes participants who feel free to walk out, which isn’t true of every participant in every organisation. Open Space’s faith in self-organisation is the jacket its practitioners don’t see. Its critics on the design side see it straight away.

§ 2. The self-emptying convener

Owen often described the convener’s job as holding space and then getting out of the way. In its manuscript Usable Complexity, the imprint named this figure the kenotic convener, after the Greek kenosis — the self-emptying by which, in Paul’s letter to the Philippians, Christ is said to have emptied himself and taken the form of a servant. The kenotic convener empties himself of agenda, authority and control, so the gathering can fill the space he’s left. The imprint linked this figure with what it reads as Stacey’s interest in anti-facilitation, or reverse facilitation, in self-managing groups, and with the Daoist advice, collected in the corpus, to empty yourself so as to wait for what comes.

The kenotic convener is a figure of real beauty, and this book doesn’t want to belittle it. But this book’s idea demands a question. Snowden’s convener stays out of the content; Owen’s convener empties himself and gets out of the way; Mowles’s tradition holds a reflective space where the group’s own dynamics are allowed to unfold. All three are versions of the same claim: the person who convenes doesn’t exercise power over what the gathering produces. Part Three argued that this claim, however sincere, is the convener’s most effective use of power, because it’s the one nobody can challenge. You can resist a convener who openly directs; a convener who has emptied himself gives you nothing to push against. Self-emptying isn’t the absence of a form. It’s a form — disciplined, cultivated, theologically rich — whose power lies in not looking like one.

The theological source carries its own correction. In the Philippians hymn, Christ’s self-emptying isn’t giving up authority but exercising it in a paradoxical way: the one who empties himself is raised up, and every knee bows. Kenosis isn’t powerlessness. It’s a different relationship to power, working through service rather than command. A kenotic convener who understood the tradition he’s drawing on would know his self-emptying is a form of authority, and would say so.

§ 3. The vanishing container

The imprint’s comparison of Owen and Shaw, The Geometry of the Vanishing Container, set side by side two conveners whose relationship to structure mirrors, in miniature, the one between Snowden and Mowles. Owen designs a container — the circle, the wall, the marketplace — and then disappears from it, so the gathering can organise itself inside the space he built. Shaw, a founding figure of complex responsive processes, refuses the container altogether. In Changing Conversations in Organizations (2002) she describes working without pre-designed processes: entering conversations already under way and taking part as they unfold, without knowing in advance what she’ll do. “I cannot know the meaning of my activities before acting,” she writes; the attitude invites her to be as present as possible to whatever improvised possibilities the moment offers. The corpus records the imprint author’s remark that Shaw’s book was turning into a script for Spike Milligan’s Q series — a work that doesn’t settle into prescriptions but opens onto further encounters.

Through this book’s idea, the geometry of the two is revealing. From Shaw’s side, Owen’s vanishing container is still a container: a designed space, however minimal, that decides in advance the shape of what can happen inside it. From Owen’s side, Shaw’s refusal of a container is a container of another sort. The seasoned consultant’s presence, her years of practice, her ability to sense the conversation and move within it, make up a form the participants can’t see because it’s carried in her person rather than drawn on a wall. Owen’s container disappears after it’s built; Shaw’s never appears, because it’s herself. Both have a geometry. The difference is that you can learn Owen’s from a book, and you can only learn Shaw’s from Shaw, or from years of practice like hers. The vanishing container and the invisible container are two answers to the same question, and each sees the other’s answer as a jacket.

§ 4. The chaordic path

The Art of Hosting — a loose international community of practice that grew from the 1990s, bringing together Open Space, the World Café, circle practice and related methods — frames its work through the idea of the chaordic path. The word was coined by Dee Hock, founder of the Visa network, for organisations that combine chaos and order. The chaordic path is the zone between too much order, which stifles, and too much chaos, which falls apart, and the art of hosting is the art of keeping a gathering on it.

In structure, the chaordic path is exactly Snowden’s “neither baggy nor boxed”: a third zone between two poles, claimed as where life happens. So it falls under the same analysis. By the test of Snowden’s own trialectic, it’s a midpoint, not an irreducible third. And as Part Seventeen showed, the science that once seemed to back the romance of a creative edge between order and chaos didn’t hold up in its strong form. What survives of the chaordic path is a rule of thumb — neither total control nor total letting go produces anything — which is true and doesn’t support any method in particular. The Art of Hosting’s practitioners, like Snowden, see their own practice as the living middle, and see designed workshops as too orderly and unfacilitated gatherings as too chaotic. This book’s idea predicts that every tradition of convening will put itself at the chaordic midpoint and its rivals at the poles.

§ 5. Rogers and the structure that lives in a person

Carl Rogers, whom The Architecture of the Held Space places at the root of much modern facilitation, put the structure of a helping encounter not in a procedure but in the helper’s way of being. In his 1957 paper on the necessary and sufficient conditions of therapeutic personality change, he named three core conditions on the therapist’s side: congruence, or genuineness; unconditional positive regard; and accurate empathic understanding, communicated to the client. In his later work with encounter groups he carried these conditions into group facilitation, where the facilitator’s presence, rather than any designed sequence, was what let the group grow. The companion volume Chaos Puzzles gives a movement to his nineteen propositions on personality and behaviour, from Client-Centered Therapy (1951), reading them as a way of interpreting organisations.

Rogers is the extreme case of the jacket that has become part of the person. A facilitator who embodies the core conditions has no procedure to declare; the structure of the encounter is her character, formed over years of practice and supervision. To a designer, the Rogerian group looks completely baggy — no roles, no sequence, just a facilitator being genuine, accepting and empathic. To the Rogerian, the designer’s method looks like a substitute for presence: technique used where genuineness was needed. Each sees the other’s jacket. And Rogers’s own account explains why his is invisible: the core conditions are, by definition, not techniques, because a technique of genuineness would be a contradiction. The Rogerian facilitator can’t declare her form as a form without seeming to betray it. That’s the deepest difficulty the jacket audit faces, and Part Twenty-Six comes back to it as an objection: some forms seem to need to stay invisible.

§ 6. Solsø’s compass

The corpus gives a large section to Karina Solsø, a practitioner and researcher in the complex responsive processes tradition, and treats her account of the practitioner’s attention as the tradition’s most practically precise statement of what a skilled practitioner actually does. Solsø sets out eight dimensions of inner attention — among them sensemaking as it emerges, designing arenas for conversation, and holding what’s upstream and downstream of a moment at the same time. The corpus reads them not as a map of the situation from above but as a compass for someone already inside it: a tool that doesn’t chart the territory but tells you which way you’re facing.

The difference between a map and a compass is exactly the difference this book has been drawing between a framework that claims a view from nowhere and one that declares where it stands. A map pretends to show the whole from above. A compass shows only which way the person holding it is facing, and is useless to anyone else. Solsø’s dimensions are a framework — the first chaos puzzle applies — but an unusual and honest kind: every reading is tied to the reader’s position. Of the tools the complexity traditions have produced, it’s one of the closest to the discipline of declared form, because it asks the practitioner not to locate the situation but to locate herself in it. Its jacket is the jacket of inwardness: it pays attention to the practitioner’s attention, and may pay less to the political structure of the room she’s attending in. But it declares what it attends to, and that’s where honesty starts.

§ 7. Some situations, or all the way down?

The corpus stages a long dialogue between Adam Kahane’s Collaborating with the Enemy (2017) and Mowles’s essay on the practice of complexity, and finds the seam between them is about what reality is like. Kahane’s book rests on a figure with two axes — how much conflict and how much complexity. A quadrant of low conflict and low complexity is where conventional collaboration works; the L-shaped remainder is where what he calls stretch collaboration is needed. His definitions of complexity draw, via an endnote, on Snowden’s framework among others: low complexity is where cause and effect can be known; high complexity is where they can only be worked out looking back. Mowles’s tradition, by contrast, holds that complexity isn’t a feature of some situations, to be diagnosed and matched with an approach, but the texture of social reality itself, present in every conversation, every team and every apparently simple task — complexity all the way down. On this, the corpus notes, the two texts split completely.

Through this book’s idea, that split is the baggy-and-boxed argument at the level of how reality is. Kahane, like Snowden, maps situations onto a space of types and matches methods to types.
