From his side, the claim that complexity goes all the way down looks like refusing to make the distinctions practice needs — a baggy view of reality where everything is complex and nothing can be told apart. Mowles, like the tradition Book One examined, sees every situation as complex. From his side, Kahane’s quadrants look like a box that sorts the living texture of relating into categories it doesn’t fit. Each sees the other’s jacket clearly. And neither can be refuted from inside his own. If complexity goes all the way down, Kahane’s quadrants are an abstraction; if complexity varies by situation, Mowles’s claim is a blanket universal. The corpus’s Stacey Matrix study offers a way through. Its epigraph from Stacey — that life is complex all the time, not just on those occasions far from certainty and agreement — and its reading of the matrix as a threshold object suggest the quadrants can be true as pointers and false as maps. Kahane’s figure is useful at the threshold, where it helps a reader notice that some situations demand stretch. It turns false when it’s taken to mean that other situations don’t.

§ 8. South Mountain

The imprint’s treatise on facilitation traditions rooted in classical Chinese poetry takes its title from the most famous couplet of Tao Yuanming, the fourth- and fifth-century poet who left office to farm, drink and write in retirement. The fifth of his poems on drinking wine reads in part:

結廬在人境，而無車馬喧。問君何能爾？心遠地自偏。采菊東籬下，悠然見南山。山氣日夕佳，飛鳥相與還。此中有真意，欲辨已忘言。I built my hut among the dwellings of men, yet hear no noise of carriage or horse. You ask how this can be? When the heart is far, the place becomes remote of itself. Picking chrysanthemums by the eastern hedge, at ease I see South Mountain. The mountain air is fine at dusk; flying birds return together. In this there is a true meaning; I wished to explain it, and have already forgotten the words.

The poem is often read as the classic image of vision you didn’t go looking for: the poet sees the mountain not because he was looking for it but because, absorbed in his chrysanthemums, he happened to look up. Facilitation traditions have taken it as an emblem of emergence — the insight that arrives as a by-product of paying attention to something else, which no method can command. That reading supports the open room. But the poem carries its own correction. The poet built the hut; he planted the hedge; he grew the chrysanthemums. The unsought sight of South Mountain depends on a tended garden, a chosen home and a daily practice. And the third couplet puts the essential form not in the place but in the heart: when the heart is far, the place becomes remote by itself. The poet lives among people and yet in solitude, because the form of his solitude is inside him. That’s the jacket become body, in one line: the constraint that makes the vision possible has become the poet’s own disposition, so he no longer notices it as a constraint, and just sees the mountain.

§ 9. The sovereign servant

The imprint’s critical study of servant leadership, The Sovereign Servant, looked at Robert Greenleaf’s influential essay “The Servant as Leader” (1970) and the movement it inspired, and asked what forms of power the language of service hides. Its sources — Foucault on pastoral power, Bourdieu on symbolic power, Scott on the arts of resistance, Graeber on bureaucracy and the utopia of rules, the Industrial Workers of the World on direct democracy — all point to one finding: claiming to serve rather than lead can be one of the most effective ways of leading, because it makes the leader’s authority unaccountable.

James C. Scott’s Seeing Like a State (1998) gives the sharpest tool for this argument. Scott showed how modern states, wanting to govern, forced legibility onto the societies they ruled — standard surnames, land-registry maps, grid-planned cities, scientific forestry — and in doing so destroyed what he called mētis: the practical, local, unspoken know-how by which communities had run their own affairs. To the state, the old city’s tangled streets and the peasant’s mixed fields looked like disorder. To the people who lived there, they were intricate orders readable only from inside. That’s this book’s idea written into the history of government. In Scott’s terms, the designer of a structured method is a maker of legibility: his roles, sequences and clustered outputs make a group’s thinking visible to the convener and the sponsor. The club’s unspoken idiom is mētis: an order readable only from inside. The designer sees mētis as bagginess. The inhabitant sees legibility as a box. Scott’s lesson is that legibility always serves somebody — usually those who need to see from above — and that mētis always gets destroyed when a legibility that can’t read it is imposed.

Graeber’s The Utopia of Rules (2015) adds the necessary correction, and it cuts the other way. Graeber, an anarchist and no fan of bureaucracy, still recognised why rules appeal: they promise predictability, protection from arbitrary power, and equal treatment for people without personal influence. Without explicit rules, the informal power of the well-connected wins. That’s Jo Freeman’s tyranny of structurelessness, which Book One drew on, coming from an anarchist. Together, Scott and Graeber state the twin idea as political theory. Legibility destroys mētis; illegibility shelters informal domination. So a convener who wants to serve has to make some things legible — the constraints that bind participants, the power the convener holds — without destroying the mētis of the community he brings together. That’s the discipline of declared form, as a programme for the sovereign servant.

§ 10. Hospitality

One more convener is left, and he gives the most hopeful image this Part has to offer. Henri Nouwen, the Dutch priest whose The Wounded Healer (1972) the imprint drew on in its study of wounded healing in leadership, wrote in Reaching Out (1975) that hospitality means above all creating a free space where the stranger can come in and become a friend instead of an enemy. For Nouwen, hospitality isn’t the absence of a host but a particular way of hosting. The host makes a space, offers it, and doesn’t fill it with his own agenda — but the space is his, he’s responsible for it, and the stranger knows whose house he’s walked into.

Hospitality is the convener’s jacket, declared. It doesn’t pretend the host has vanished, as the kenotic convener sometimes does. It doesn’t pretend the space has no shape, as the open conference sometimes does. It doesn’t pretend the host stands outside what happens, as the designer sometimes does. It says: this is my house, I’ve made room in it for you, and you can change it while you’re here. The stranger — the newcomer, the outsider, the person the tradition would otherwise park — is the one the space is made for. Of all the ways of convening in this Part, hospitality comes closest to the discipline of declared form, and it’s the oldest of them.

The host who says “this is my house, and I’ve made room in it for you” tells the truth that the vanishing convener, the self-emptied facilitator and the designer who stays out of the content all hide: somebody made the room, and has to answer for its walls.

The host who admits it is her house tells the truth the others conceal. But a house is more than walls. It also has a climate.

Part TwentyThe weather of the room: atmosphere, energy, and the jacket felt before it is seen

Where we areEverything so far has been about structure, power and argument. But you feel a room before you understand it.
What this asksPart Twenty asks about the weather of a room — and whether the jacket is first felt, not seen.
In brief 13 min read
  • The complexity corpus holds that perception precedes analysis: we know a room as a mood before we know it as a fact, and atmosphere is the first text. Donald Innes Davies’s atmospheric perception, Gernot Böhme’s aesthetics of atmospheres and Heidegger’s analysis of mood give the claim philosophical weight.
  • Atmosphere is the medium in which jackets are felt before they are seen. The club’s warmth is its constitutive constraint, felt as freedom by members and as chill by newcomers; the workshop’s brisk energy is felt as momentum by its designer and as harassment by Mowles.
  • Neither tradition designs atmosphere: the triopticon designs structure and inherits whatever weather participants bring; the conference inherits a weather made over decades. Böhme shows that atmospheres can be made, but never commanded.
  • The corpus’s own lexicon, which marks each of its terms as analytical, structural figure or atmospheric intensifier, is a model of declared form applied to language — and the language of energy needs exactly that declaration.

TakeawayThe jacket is felt as weather. Whoever enters a room already warmed by his own tradition feels freedom; whoever enters a room warmed by another’s feels the cold of not belonging.

Among the twelve propositions in which the imprint’s complexity corpus sets out its theory formally is one this argument hasn’t touched yet, though it bears on it directly. Perception comes before analysis, it says: we know a room as a mood before we know it as a fact; atmosphere is the first text. The corpus’s main thesis opens with an epigraph from Donald Innes Davies, whose work on the atmospherics of human systems runs through the whole edition: the room had a weather before it had an agenda, and he learned to read the front the moment he crossed the threshold. Book One looked at the structure of rooms — their constraints, who’s in them, their politics. This Part looks at their weather.

§ 1. Atmosphere as the first text

The philosophical basis for that proposition is the phenomenology of atmospheres developed by Hermann Schmitz and, more accessibly, by Gernot Böhme. In Atmosphäre (1995) and later essays on the aesthetics of atmospheres, Böhme argued that atmospheres aren’t private feelings projected onto neutral spaces, nor objective properties of things, but something in between — feelings poured out into space, which we meet as the tone of a place before we’ve picked out anything in it. You walk into a room and feel it as tense or relaxed, solemn or festive, welcoming or cold, before you’ve noticed who’s there or what’s on the table. For Böhme, atmosphere is the first thing we perceive: what we perceive before we perceive objects.

Heidegger had reached a related insight in Being and Time (1927), in his analysis of mood (Stimmung) and attunement (Befindlichkeit). We’re always already in some mood, he argued, and mood isn’t an inner state tinting an otherwise neutral world. It’s the way the world as a whole shows up for us. Mood makes things matter the way they do. Someone afraid meets the world as threatening; someone at ease meets it as welcoming. Mood comes before thinking and shapes it.

If these accounts are right, then seeing a room as baggy or boxed is, at bottom, an atmospheric perception, not an analytical one. Before Snowden has listed the conference’s lack of designed constraints, he’s felt its atmosphere — and an atmosphere that’s warm to its members can feel, to a visitor from another tradition, loose, self-indulgent, unfocused. Before Mowles has analysed the facilitated workshop’s governing constraints, he’s felt its atmosphere — the brisk energy, the applause, the rush — and an atmosphere that feels purposeful to its designer can feel, to a visitor from another tradition, frantic and hollow. The analysis follows the mood. This book’s idea works first at the level of weather.

§ 2. Warm and cold

Think about the club’s atmosphere. Mowles describes his conference as often the highlight of the delegates’ year, and that description is about atmosphere: it names a warmth, a sense of coming home, the ease of being among people who speak your language. That warmth is real, and it’s how the conference’s constitutive constraints feel. Members don’t experience the shared tradition, the key texts, the vocabulary and the habits of conversation as constraints. They experience them as atmosphere — the warmth of a room where you’re understood. Polanyi’s background awareness, which we looked at in Part Six, has an emotional side: what you hold in the background, you feel as comfort.

The same constraints make different weather for the newcomer. The warmth wrapped round the members is, for someone who doesn’t speak their language, the warmth of a party you weren’t quite invited to. Mowles honestly reports the inclusion and exclusion that play out all weekend, and those dynamics are atmospheric before they’re anything else. The newcomer feels the chill of not belonging long before she can name the norms she’s broken. The same atmosphere is warm and cold at once, depending on who walks in. The jacket is felt as weather — and it’s the same weather, felt from inside the garment and outside it.

Now think about the workshop’s atmosphere. Mowles reports that its stated aim was speed and energy, and that he felt facilitated from pillar to post and went along with it like a drone. The atmosphere was one of rush. To the facilitator, that rush was presumably momentum — the energy of a well-run session, the satisfaction of covering ground. To Mowles, trained in a tradition that prizes slowness, reflection and the ability to sit with uncertainty, the same rush was harassment. The workshop’s atmosphere was its governing constraint felt as weather: the timings, the cued applause, the constant reshuffling produced a mood in which reflection was impossible. Mowles felt the mood before he analysed the method. His essay is, to a large extent, a weather report.

§ 3. Neither tradition designs the weather

Here something striking about both traditions comes into view. Snowden’s triopticon is a design of structure: roles, sequences and group sizes. It says nothing, in the essay at least, about atmosphere. The method will be run in whatever weather participants bring with them — the anxieties of a hospital in crisis, the suspicions of a community in conflict, the tiredness of a ministry under pressure — and those moods will live inside its structure. A brilliantly designed sequence run in an atmosphere of fear will produce a fearful deliberation. The designer has designed the room’s architecture and left its climate to chance.

Mowles’s conference doesn’t design its atmosphere either; it inherits it. The warmth of the gathering has been built over decades by the people who’ve come, and it’s renewed every year when they come back. That inheritance is a great asset for members and an obstacle for newcomers, and the tradition has no practice, as far as the essay shows, for deliberately adjusting the weather so a newcomer feels warmth rather than chill. The tradition pays attention to dynamics — inclusion, exclusion, power — but dynamics aren’t the same as atmosphere. You can notice that a newcomer has been left out without changing the climate that left her out.

Böhme’s aesthetics suggests it doesn’t have to be this way. He was very interested in scenography — the work of stage designers, architects and lighting designers — and in what he called making atmospheres: arranging light, sound, space and materials on purpose so as to tune a place to a mood. For Böhme, atmospheres can be made, though never commanded. The designer can set up conditions for a mood but can’t guarantee everyone will feel it. That’s the structure of an enabling constraint, applied to weather. A convener who paid as much attention to atmosphere as to structure would ask not just what roles and sequences the gathering needs but what climate: how the room is lit, how people are greeted, how the newcomer is received in the first five minutes, what tone the opening sets. Nouwen’s hospitality, from Part Nineteen, is a practice of atmosphere: making a free space where the stranger can come in. Neither of Book One’s essays mentions it.

§ 4. The Cobra and the unhurried

The corpus’s most recurring figure is an engine: the Cobra V12, read, its lexicon explains, as desire, as energetic intensity, and as what goes wrong when you accelerate. The lexicon declares the image to be a metaphor, and uses it to talk thermodynamics, psychodynamics and complexity in one grammar. The way it goes wrong bears directly on the workshop Mowles describes. A gathering whose stated aim is speed and energy is a Cobra driven at the limit: it generates intensity, and it risks the failure acceleration always risks — losing control, burning out the engine, wrecking what the speed was meant to serve. Hartmut Rosa’s sociology of acceleration, which Mowles himself draws on, describes the same failure on the scale of a society: a world that speeds up technological change, social change and the pace of life loses its capacity for resonance, because resonance takes time.

At various points the corpus sets against the Cobra an attention to pace that one strand of facilitation practice calls the unhurried. The open conference, with its weekend and its willingness to let conversation go nowhere, has an unhurried atmosphere, and its warmth is partly the warmth of slowness. But Part Sixteen showed that being unhurried is also a privilege: the people who can afford to go nowhere are the ones whose situations don’t require them to arrive. An atmosphere of slowness is a constitutive constraint just as surely as an atmosphere of speed is a governing one. Neither is weatherless. The question for any convener isn’t whether to have a pace but which pace suits which people and purposes — and whether the pace has been chosen, declared and made answerable, or just inherited.

§ 5. Energy, and saying what kind of word it is

The corpus talks about energy throughout. There’s informational energy, in Cliff Kimber’s unpublished work on information as organisational energy. There’s energetic semiosis, Kevin Flinn’s thesis that a sign and a force-field are one event seen at two grains. There’s usable energy, in the imprint’s own manuscript, where its author writes that sensemaking is closely related to empathy, in the same way that empathy picks up emotional states and sensemaking picks up energetic ones. The language of energy gives the corpus much of its life, and it names something real: the felt intensity of a gathering, the difference between a room that’s alive and one that’s dead.

But the language of energy is also open to the very charge Part Two made against the triopticon’s menagerie: a vocabulary borrowed from another field — here physics rather than Indigenous story — may lend glamour rather than insight. The imprint itself has been alert to this. Its critical review of Davies’s book We Are Energy (2026), read through Pettman and Clemens’s Avoiding the Subject, asked how much analytical weight the language of energy can carry in the study of human systems. And the corpus’s lexicon and taxonomy contain a device this book would hold up as a model. It sorts its invented and borrowed ideas into three kinds. Analytical primitives do explanatory work and can be argued for and against (relational emergence, energetic semiosis, the living present, the triad). Structural figures organise the argument as load-bearing images (the Turnstile, the Cobra V12). Atmospheric intensifiers carry tone and momentum and are never asked to prove anything (neon, weather, the afterimage of relation). Every term in the lexicon is marked as metaphor or analysis. Naming the kind, the lexicon explains, prevents the commonest mistake in reading the work: taking a poetic intensifier for an analytical claim.

That’s the discipline of declared form applied to language. It doesn’t give up metaphor, which would make thinking poorer. It declares metaphor as metaphor, so the reader knows what kind of claim is being made. The triopticon’s menagerie would be transformed by the same declaration: Eagle, Raven, Beaver, Coyote and Magpie marked as structural figures, not claims about what people are like, with their origins acknowledged. The language of energy gets stronger, not weaker, when you mark which uses are analytical and which atmospheric. And this book’s own central figure — the jacket — should be declared the same way. It’s a structural figure, a load-bearing image that organises the argument, not an analytical primitive. What it stands for — the unspoken and explicit constraints of a gathering, and the lopsided way they’re seen — can be argued for and against. The jacket itself is just the coat the argument wears.

§ 6. The weather and the audit

What does paying attention to atmosphere add to the jacket audit? First, a seventh question to go with Part Nine’s six: what’s the weather in this room, and who feels it as warm and who as cold? A gathering can declare every constraint, share out every voice and open its frame to challenge, and still feel like a hostile climate to some participants, because atmosphere isn’t used up by structure. The question asks the convener to pay attention to what people feel as they cross the threshold, before they know anything else.

Second, it sharpens this book’s idea. The lopsided seeing the idea describes isn’t just a matter of thinking; it’s a matter of feeling. Each side feels its own jacket as comfort and the other’s as discomfort, before either has thought about it. That makes the idea harder to overcome than a mistake in reasoning would be, because you can’t argue a mood away. Someone who feels a room as cold won’t be talked out of it by being shown its structure. If the jacket audit is going to reach the level where this book’s idea operates, it has to work on atmosphere as well as argument — it has to make rooms that feel different, not just rooms that are understood differently.

And third, it brings a humility that suits the end of a Part. Davies’s epigraph says the room had a weather before it had an agenda. That’s true of every room this argument has played out in — the conference, the workshop, the triopticon, the proposed meeting at Hull — and it’s true of the room of this book as well. A reader coming from Snowden’s tradition will feel its weather one way; a reader from Mowles’s, another; a reader from Jackson’s, a third. This book has tried to keep its own climate temperate. Whether it’s managed is a question of weather, and weather is felt, not argued.

§ 7. The weather on a screen

One kind of room has been missing from this whole book, even though it’s now where a huge number of gatherings happen: the room on a screen. Neither November 2026 essay deals with it. Yet the most vivid episode in Mowles’s essay on gossip happened there — the clinician who, presenting online, sent the whole meeting the contemptuous aside she meant for one colleague. The online room has weather of its own, and that weather bears on every finding in this Part.

First, the online room’s atmosphere is thin. Böhme’s atmospheres are poured into space and felt by bodies that share it. On a screen, nobody shares a space, and the tone of the gathering has to be carried by faces in small rectangles, by squashed and delayed voices, without the small movements — a shift in a chair, a glance across a table — through which a group usually reads itself. Mood still forms, but it forms on less information, and it’s harder for anyone to read. That means the established, who already know each other’s faces and voices, read it far better than the newcomer, who can’t. The online room amplifies the lopsidedness this book describes.

Second, the online room’s furniture acts with unusual force. The platform decides who appears big and who small, who’s muted, whose hand is raised, what order the chat is read in, and whether a private message stays private. These are Latour’s actants in their purest form, and they’re designed — by engineers who never meet the participants, for purposes the participants don’t know. In that sense, every online gathering is a designed room before its convener has designed anything. Its first jacket is cut in a technology company. The traditionalist who thinks his online conversation is open hasn’t noticed the platform; the designer who thinks his online method is his own hasn’t noticed whose method it runs inside.

Third, the online room is where Turkle’s and Mowles’s warnings bite hardest. A meeting held through screens is already partly a meeting with machines, and the frictionlessness Turkle worries about — how easy it is to mute yourself, turn your camera off, do something else while looking present — is built into its form. The online room makes withdrawal as easy as taking part. It’s facilitation without anti-facilitation in its most literal technological form: everything is made easy, including being absent. A convener who wanted to apply the discipline of declared form to an online gathering would have to declare not just her own design and her tradition’s idiom but the platform’s — and tend a weather the medium itself makes thin.

Through this book’s idea, it’s no surprise that both essays leave the online room out. Each tradition’s sense of what a gathering is was formed in physical rooms — the conference hotel, the workshop venue, the seminar room — and each sees the online room as a weaker copy of the rooms it knows, rather than as a form with its own constraints. That way of seeing is itself a jacket: the habit of seeing the new through the categories of the old. The newcomer to online gatherings, who has never known anything else, may see their form more clearly than either tradition.

Eighth Interlude · On Crossing the Threshold

You know before you know. The door opens, and something reaches you first — not a face, not a word, but a temperature. The room is waiting, or the room is closed, or the room has already been talking for a long time about things you weren’t there for. You’ve read the weather before you’ve read a single name badge. And the people inside can’t feel what you feel, because the weather they made is the air they breathe. To them it isn’t weather at all, just the ordinary warmth of being where they belong. That’s why the stranger is always the best weather forecaster. And that’s why nobody ever asks him for the forecast.

The jacket is felt as weather before it is seen as structure. And the people who feel it most keenly are the ones who do not belong. Which brings us back to Mowles.

Part Twenty-OneThe established and the outsiders: gossip, the hidden transcript, and the instrument Mowles did not turn

Where we arePart Thirteen caught Snowden with an instrument he did not use. Fairness demands we check Mowles for the same thing.
What this asksPart Twenty-One asks whether Mowles, in his own writing of the same year, already held the sociology that would have revealed his conference’s hidden boundaries.
In brief 12 min read
  • In a 2026 essay on gossip, “DM me…”, Mowles drew on Elias and Scotson’s The Established and the Outsiders, Scott’s hidden transcripts, Fleck’s thought collectives and Janis’s groupthink to show how groups police their boundaries — and observed thought collectives on LinkedIn policing their members and dismissing rival collectives for bringing nothing new.
  • Elias and Scotson found two neighbourhoods identical in class and differing only in length of residence, in which the established used praise-gossip and blame-gossip to construct their own group charisma and the newcomers’ disgrace. It is the most exact sociological account of the club and the newcomer the dispute could ask for.
  • Mowles possesses, in his own work of the same year, the instruments that would reveal the established–outsider figuration in his own conference. Like Snowden in Part Thirteen, he did not turn them on his own room.
  • The figuration is symmetrical: every tradition, including anthro-complexity and this imprint, has its established core and its outsiders, and polices its boundary by the same means.

TakeawayEach of the two essayists owns the instrument that would have revealed his own jacket, and turned it only on his rival. That symmetry is the central idea’s most exact confirmation.

Part Thirteen found that Snowden has, in his own wider work, the tools that would have shown up the constraints of Mowles’s conference — sense-making before sorting, mapping constraints by energy and time, the “anthro” in anthro-complexity — and that he didn’t point them at the room he criticised. Fairness means asking whether the same is true of Mowles. The imprint’s complexity corpus suggests it is, and that his tools fit the case even more exactly. In 2026, in an essay whose title begins “DM me…”, Mowles took gossip as his subject and gathered a remarkable set of resources for understanding how groups police their boundaries. This Part sets out those resources and applies them where the October essay didn’t.

§ 1. The essay on gossip

The corpus records that Mowles opened the essay with a scene from a group he was facilitating. A senior clinician, presenting online, meant to send a private aside to one colleague dismissing a junior’s question as stupid — and posted it to the whole meeting instead. What had been simmering under the surface was suddenly out in the open, and the team spent weeks living with the fallout. From this small disaster Mowles drew a big claim: gossip isn’t idle noise at the edges of organisational life but one of its central mechanisms, a way of both propping up and undermining power relations, which mostly stays offstage and only surfaces by accident or on purpose. His second story was darker: a department where staff were too frightened to speak, a group circling the known but unspoken fact that two managers bullied their staff, and a woman who finally described her experience with the responsible director sitting beside her. He said nothing, and within weeks she’d left. The lesson, as the corpus sums it up, is bleak and precise: bringing the hidden into the open may change nothing.

Mowles then reached for four bodies of thought. James C. Scott’s Domination and the Arts of Resistance (1990) separates the public transcript — the performance of what’s allowed, where the subordinate fake deference and the powerful perform mastery — from the hidden transcript, the critique of power spoken behind the backs of those in charge. Scott’s sting, the corpus notes, is to puncture the phrase “speaking truth to power”, since power usually already knows the truth and is quite comfortable with the hierarchy it works to keep. Norbert Elias and John Scotson’s The Established and the Outsiders (1965) shows gossip doing structural work at the boundary between groups. Ludwik Fleck’s thought collectives explain how communities of knowledge police their members. And Irving Janis’s groupthink names the drive for consensus at any price that squashes dissent. Read together, the corpus notes, the four traditions light up the same dark room from different doors: each shows a group protecting its cohesion, its knowledge and its hierarchy against disruption and the new.

And Mowles applied them — in a passage both the corpus and the Zen Garden study quote — to the professional life of his own field. If you want to see the politics of power and status claims, he wrote, just visit LinkedIn on any day. Members who see themselves as authority figures in a thought collective spend a lot of time policing fellow members, pointing out where they’ve strayed from orthodoxy and who is or isn’t really a member of the tribe; or they criticise other thought collectives for bringing nothing new.

§ 2. Winston Parva

Elias and Scotson’s study deserves a fuller account, because it’s the most exact sociological description of the club and the newcomer this argument could ask for. In the late 1950s Scotson studied a small community on the edge of an English industrial town, which the book calls Winston Parva. It was made up of two working-class neighbourhoods, alike in jobs, income, housing and education. They differed in just one way: people in one had lived there for two or three generations, while people in the other were fairly new arrivals. That single difference, the study found, organised the whole social life of the place. The established families held the positions of influence in local associations, churches and clubs. They saw themselves as respectable and the newcomers as rough. And they kept the distinction going through a network of gossip — praise-gossip about themselves, blame-gossip about the outsiders — that ran through their dense, long-standing ties. The newcomers, without such ties, couldn’t answer back in kind, and over time many came to accept, at least partly, the low opinion the established had of them.

Elias drew a general theory from the study. In established–outsider relationships, he argued, the decisive resource isn’t wealth or class but cohesion. The established are more tightly knit, share a longer history and a common set of norms, and so can act together, exclude together and speak with one voice. They build for themselves a group charisma — a picture of their own collective virtue, modelled on their best members — and for the outsiders a group disgrace, modelled on their worst. The lopsidedness is the trick: the established judge themselves by their finest and the outsiders by their meanest, and the outsiders, lacking the cohesion to produce a counter-picture, take the judgement in.

Every piece of that pattern appears, in miniature, in a long-standing research community that meets every year around a shared tradition. Its members share a history and a vocabulary that newcomers don’t have. They’re more tightly knit. They can recognise each other’s contributions as good conversation and a newcomer’s as missing the point. And they carry, in their shared memory of past conferences and past arguments, a group charisma — the sense of belonging to a tradition of unusual depth and seriousness — against which the newcomer’s contribution gets measured. None of this needs ill will. Elias’s whole point was that the pattern works through ordinary decent people doing nothing more than belonging to their group. The inclusion and exclusion Mowles honestly reports at his conference are the established–outsider pattern at work, and in his own essay on gossip he has the sociology that names it.

§ 3. The tool he didn’t use

So here’s the symmetry. In the same year he wrote “Staying with the trouble”, Mowles wrote an essay that gathered Elias and Scotson, Scott, Fleck and Janis to show how groups protect their cohesion, knowledge and hierarchy against disruption and the new — and applied them to the policing of thought collectives. In “Staying with the trouble”, writing about a research community bound by a shared tradition, he reported its inclusion and exclusion and treated them as an honest admission of a fertile mess, not as an established–outsider pattern that needed attention. He didn’t point the tool at the room.

This book’s idea explains why. From inside a tradition, the sociology of established and outsiders is a tool for seeing other groups — LinkedIn’s self-appointed authorities, thought collectives that dismiss rivals for bringing nothing new, managers who bully and directors who stay silent. It doesn’t easily present itself as a tool for seeing your own group, because your own group doesn’t feel like a pattern of power. It feels like home. Its cohesion feels like friendship. Its group charisma feels like the plain truth about the tradition’s depth. Its exclusions feel like the newcomer not having caught on yet. Elias’s theory predicts exactly this: the established don’t experience themselves as established. They experience themselves as the normal, decent people of the place.

And Snowden’s essay — which says the open conference is closer to a club, a gathering of people who already share a tradition and its assumptions — is, read through Mowles’s essay on gossip, an example of the very thing that essay describes: a member of one thought collective criticising another for being closed. Mowles could fairly reply that the charge is an outsider’s blame-gossip about a group whose virtues the outsider can’t see. And Snowden could fairly reply that this reply is the established group’s praise-gossip about itself. Both replies would be right. That’s what an established–outsider relationship between two thought collectives looks like from inside each one.

§ 4. The pattern is everywhere

It would be a failure of the audit to apply Elias and Scotson only to Mowles’s conference. The pattern is general, and it’s at work in every tradition with a history and a boundary. Anthro-complexity has its established core — its founder, its company, its accredited practitioners, its body of methods and field guides, its distinctive vocabulary of domains, constraints, probes and estuaries — and its outsiders, who meet the vocabulary as jargon and the methods as proprietary. Its members also build a group charisma — the sense of belonging to a tradition that has moved beyond systems thinking and naïve complexity — and its boundary is policed, like every boundary, by praise and blame. The critical systems tradition at Hull has its established core and its outsiders. And this imprint, whose treatises run to hundreds of thousands of words in a distinctive voice, under rules and lenses of its own, has an established form that readers either learn to live in or experience as an obstacle. Part Eleven named some of its constraints. Elias would add that the imprint, like every group with a style, judges itself by its best pages and its critics by their worst.

The pattern isn’t a vice to be stamped out. It’s a built-in feature of any group with a history, and groups with histories are the only groups that can keep a tradition of thought alive. What the pattern needs isn’t abolition but awareness: the established group recognising that it is established, that its cohesion is a resource the newcomer lacks, and that its judgements of the newcomer are shaped by an uneven measuring stick it doesn’t notice. That recognition is what Elias called detachment — stepping back from involvement far enough to see your own group as one pattern among others. Elias insisted that detachment is never complete, that involvement and detachment are always mixed, and that the balance shifts with circumstances. The jacket audit is an attempt to tip the balance a little towards detachment, at the moments — threshold, breakdown, close — when it matters most.

§ 5. Hidden transcripts in every room

Scott’s distinction between public and hidden transcripts bears on the argument in one more way: it’s about where dissent goes in each kind of room. In the designed room, the public transcript is the method itself — the roles, the sequence, the outputs clustered on the wall. Dissent from the method — the participant who thinks the whole exercise is misconceived — has no place in the public transcript, except through the Coyote’s licensed role, which Part Two compared to Gluckman’s ritual of rebellion. Real dissent goes into the hidden transcript: the chat over coffee, the email afterwards, the shrug in the corridor. Mowles’s essay on the workshop is, in Scott’s terms, his hidden transcript made public after the event — the drone’s critique of the facilitator, spoken not in the room but on Substack.

In the open room, the public transcript is the tradition’s idiom: the reflective, interpretive, psychoanalytically tinged conversation members recognise as good. Dissent from the idiom — the newcomer who finds the whole conversation self-indulgent, or the participant who wants a decision rather than a reflection — has no comfortable place in that public transcript either, since voicing it shows you haven’t understood. It goes into the hidden transcript too: the newcomer’s private decision not to come back, the remark to a friend on the train home. Every room has a hidden transcript, and the room’s form decides what ends up in it. The designed room drives dissent from the method underground. The open room drives dissent from the idiom underground. Neither room hears what it has driven away.

Scott’s bleakest lesson — which Mowles himself drew in his essay on gossip — is that bringing the hidden transcript into the open may change nothing. The woman who described being bullied left within weeks; the director stayed. The jacket audit can’t promise more. A gathering that declares its form and invites challenge may still find the challenge is heard and nothing changes, because the established have the cohesion to absorb it. But the alternative — a gathering that doesn’t declare its form and doesn’t invite challenge — guarantees the hidden transcript stays hidden. The audit offers a chance, not a cure. Scott’s historical record of resistance suggests that chances are what the less powerful usually have, and that they’re worth more than nothing.

§ 6. Groupthink and its mindguards

Janis’s account of groupthink, the most familiar of Mowles’s four resources, listed eight symptoms: the illusion of invulnerability; collective rationalisation; belief in the group’s built-in morality; stereotyped views of outsiders; direct pressure on dissenters; self-censorship; the illusion of unanimity; and self-appointed mindguards who shield the group from awkward information. Janis built the idea from foreign-policy fiascos — above all the Bay of Pigs — where close-knit groups of able people made disastrous decisions because the pressure to agree overrode the ability to doubt.

Two of Janis’s symptoms bear directly on the exchange between the essays. First, stereotyped views of outsiders. Each tradition’s description of the other — baggy, boxed, passive, managerial, a club, a cage — has the simplifying quality Janis described, where the rival is reduced to its worst version. Second, mindguards. In every thought collective there are members who, without any conspiracy, protect the group from information that would unsettle its picture of itself — by not reading the rival’s best work, by summarising it unfairly, by answering the weakest version of its argument. This book has tried to work against mindguarding by reading each tradition at its strongest: Snowden’s trialectic, Mowles’s essay on gossip, Jackson’s declared partiality. Whether it has managed isn’t for it to judge. A group can’t reliably spot its own mindguards; that’s what makes them effective.

§ 7. Who gets to speak in the matrix

The corpus’s bibliography lists Farhad Dalal’s Taking the Group Seriously (1998) with a note that can serve as this Part’s last word: power and the social production of difference at the centre of group life; who may speak in the matrix. Dalal, a group analyst, argued that Foulkes’s group analysis — the tradition the conference’s reflective practice draws on — hadn’t taken power seriously enough. The group matrix, the web of communication every member speaks within, is structured by power relations and by the social categories of race, class, gender and belonging that members bring into it. And some members can speak in the matrix more freely than others — not because of who they are individually, but because of where they stand in the wider patterns the group reproduces.

Dalal’s critique comes from inside the very tradition whose jacket this book has been examining, and that’s what makes it valuable. It shows that the tradition has, in its own literature, what it needs to see what Book One argued it doesn’t: that the open conversation of the matrix is structured by power, and that the question of who gets to speak isn’t answered by the lack of designed procedure. The established–outsider pattern, the hidden transcript, the mindguard, and the unequal right to speak in the matrix are all named in the tradition’s own key texts. The question isn’t whether the tradition can see its jacket. It’s whether, on a given occasion, defending a room it loves, it will.

The established don’t experience themselves as established. They experience themselves as the decent, ordinary people of the place — and the tool that would show them otherwise is sitting in their own library, filed under the study of other people’s groups.

Mowles, too, had the instrument and did not turn it. Both men, then, see clearly outward and dimly inward. What does that tell us about agency itself?

Part Twenty-TwoThe hand that enters the river: agency, structure, and the two strokes of facilitation

Where we areWe have looked at rooms. Now we look at the people acting in them — at agency itself.
What this asksPart Twenty-Two draws on the imprint’s treatise on agency and asks whether facilitation and its opposite are really opposites at all.
In brief 22 min read
  • The imprint’s treatise The Hand That Enters the River (October 2026), seventeen questions on human agency read through Donald Innes Davies, holds that agency is not a possession but a process: the hand that acts is always already in the river of structure, energy and other people.
  • Its account of the structure–agency debate — Giddens’s duality of structure, Sewell’s transposable schemas, Archer’s morphogenetic cycle and stored potential — shows that the baggy and the boxed are two conflations: one sees agency drowned in structure, the other structure dissolved in agency.
  • Its fifteenth quaestio holds that facilitation and anti-facilitation are not opposites but the two strokes of a single respiration. Each, held alone, becomes its own shadow: facilitation without anti-facilitation breeds dependency; anti-facilitation without facilitation breeds Freeman’s tyranny of structurelessness.
  • The boxed room is facilitation without its second stroke; the baggy room is anti-facilitation without its first. Each tradition sees the other’s shadow and mistakes its own single stroke for breathing.

TakeawayAgency lives in the rhythm between making things easier and refusing to make them too easy — and in complex rooms, that rhythm cannot be held by one convener. It must be distributed.

In October 2026, a few days before the Mowles and Snowden essays came out, the imprint finished a scholastic treatise on human agency, The Hand That Enters the River. It’s written as seventeen questions in the same form this book uses, and read through the chapter on agency in Donald Innes Davies’s work. Its title brings together two images. Machiavelli’s river, in the twenty-fifth chapter of The Prince, is fortune: a torrent that floods the plains when it rises, against which a sensible ruler builds dykes and embankments while the weather is calm. Heraclitus’s river is the world itself, which you can’t step into twice because the water is always new. The hand that enters the river is the human agent: never outside the flow it acts on, always already shaped by the currents it hopes to steer. The treatise’s first finding is that the possessive model of agency — agency as something an individual owns — isn’t so much wrong as partial: it sees the hand and forgets the river.

That finding bears directly on the baggy-and-boxed argument, which is at heart an argument about where agency lives in a gathering. The designer puts it partly in the method: the right structure lets participants act well. The traditionalist puts it in the participants’ own relating: the right openness lets their agency emerge. Both, the treatise would say, have seen the hand and forgotten the river. This Part draws out what the treatise adds.

§ 1. Seven words

The treatise is built round a sentence of Davies’s that describes agency as active energy that turns intention into responsive participation, and it reads the sentence word by word. Active, not passive: agency is a doing, not a state. Energy: not a faculty but something you can gain and lose, top up and run down. Turns: a verb of conversion, changing one thing into another. Intention: heir to Aristotle’s prohairesis, to Elizabeth Anscombe’s Intention (1957), to Michael Bratman’s planning theory. Into: a word of entering and direction. Responsive: answerable to others and to circumstances. Participation: Plato’s methexis, having a share in something. Read slowly, the treatise notes, the sentence doesn’t put agency in the will alone. It says intention has to be turned — converted — into participation, and that the turning is the agency.

Through this book’s idea, Davies’s sentence gives a test both rooms can be judged by. A gathering serves agency if it helps participants turn their intentions into responsive participation. The boxed room risks stopping the turning. People arrive with intentions, and the method channels their participation along lines the designer chose, so what gets turned into participation is more the designer’s intention than theirs. The baggy room risks stopping it another way. People arrive with intentions, and with no form to turn them, the intentions of the established, who already know the idiom, get converted into participation while the newcomer’s stay unturned. Neither room abolishes agency. Each converts some intentions and not others, and this book’s idea predicts that each will only see the other’s failures to convert.

§ 2. The three-note chord

The treatise’s second question draws on Mustafa Emirbayer and Ann Mische’s influential article “What Is Agency?” (American Journal of Sociology, 1998), which describes agency as a chord of three notes sounding together. The iterational note faces the past. It works through selective attention, recognising types, filing a situation under a category, keeping expectations steady and settling into habit. It’s how the past is made present. Its vice is rigidity — and without it nothing could get done, because someone who had to think through every gesture from scratch would be paralysed. This, the treatise notes, is the territory of Bourdieu’s habitus, Giddens’s practical consciousness, the submerged continent of the taken-for-granted. The projective note faces the future: anticipating, building narratives, rearranging symbols, hypothesising and experimenting. The practical-evaluative note faces the present: spotting problems, describing situations, deliberating, deciding and carrying out.

The iterational note is the jacket. Emirbayer and Mische’s description of it — selective attention, recognising types, filing a situation under a category — is an exact account of how a practitioner walks into a rival’s room and sees it through the categories her tradition has made habitual. Snowden recognises the conference as a type, files it under “unstructured”, and pays attention selectively to what that category predicts. Mowles recognises the workshop as a type, files it under “over-designed”, and pays attention selectively the same way. The iterational note isn’t a flaw; without it, as the treatise insists, nobody could act. But it works below awareness, so it’s the note through which this book’s idea does its work. The cure isn’t to silence it but to bring in the other two: the projective note, which can imagine the rival’s room differently, and the practical-evaluative note, which can attend to this particular room right now rather than to its type.

§ 3. Two ways of collapsing the problem

The treatise’s third question asks whether structure constrains agency or makes it up, and sets out the three positions Margaret Archer picked out in the long argument of social theory. Downward conflation collapses agency into structure: individuals carry structures, and their actions are fixed by positions they didn’t choose. Upward conflation collapses structure into agency: social structures are nothing but the sum of individual actions and interactions. Central conflation fuses the two. Anthony Giddens’s theory of structuration, in The Constitution of Society (1984), holds that structure — rules and resources — is both the medium and the outcome of the practices it organises, over and over: when I speak English I draw on its grammar, and in doing so I keep it going.

The treatise records two refinements. William Sewell, in a 1992 article, pressed on a weak spot in Giddens: if structure only exists as it’s played out in practice, how do we explain the stubborn, material weight of institutions? His revision held that structures are made of cultural schemas and resources that hold each other up; that schemas can be carried into new situations and resources read more than one way; and that agency arises exactly in that ability to carry schemas across and reinterpret resources — agency lives in the transposability of schemas. Archer went further. If structure and agency are fused in every instant, we can never ask how much of an outcome is due to which, so we can never explain why some societies change fast and others hardly at all. Her morphogenetic cycle separates them in time: structural conditioning at one moment, social interaction at the next, structural elaboration after that — so structures built up at one time lie dormant until conditions release them at another. The treatise links this to Davies’s idea of stored potential: structure built up in the past, waiting to be released.

Through this book’s idea, baggy and boxed show up as two typical kinds of collapse. The traditionalist, looking at the designed room, sees downward conflation: participants turned into carriers of the designer’s structure, their agency drowned in roles and sequences. The designer, looking at the open room, sees upward conflation: structure dissolved into whatever participants happen to do, with no form to shape it. Each sees one of Archer’s collapses in the other’s room, and neither sees the one in his own. The traditionalist’s tradition is a structure that shapes every conversation at the conference — downward conflation, unadmitted. The designer’s method is presented as if its effects were just the participants’ agency set free — a subtle upward conflation, in which the designer’s structure disappears into what the participants achieve.

Sewell’s version offers a way through. If agency lives in carrying schemas across, then a gathering serves agency to the extent that it lets participants carry their schemas — bring the categories of their own experience into new arrangements, and reinterpret the resources the gathering offers. The designed room may help by offering new arrangements, and may hinder by fixing the arrangement in advance. The open room may help by leaving arrangements open, and may hinder by supplying only the established tradition’s schemas, so the newcomer’s can’t be carried in at all. And Archer’s stored potential names what both essays miss: every room carries, from its history, built-up structure that lies dormant and can be released. The club carries decades of stored potential in its tradition; the triopticon carries the stored potential of its designer’s long practice. Neither is a fresh start. Both are rivers already in flood.

§ 4. Breathing in and breathing out

The treatise’s fifteenth question asks whether making things easy serves agency, and its answer is the single most useful thing the imprint’s work offers this argument. Facilitation and anti-facilitation, it argues, aren’t opposites. They’re breathing in and breathing out. Facilitation, from the Latin facilis, “doable”, makes action possible by lowering its cost. Anti-facilitation makes agency possible by refusing to lower the cost too far — by holding back, disrupting or reversing ease, so that people and groups have to build their own agency. And in complex settings, both have to be shared out among everyone present.

The treatise grounds this in six traditions, and each deserves a quick look. In ecology, facilitation means the positive interactions by which one organism makes conditions better for another — the nurse plant whose shade lets seedlings take root in a desert. Mark Bertness and Ragan Callaway’s stress-gradient hypothesis (1994) proposed that facilitation matters more as environmental stress rises. But as the facilitated seedling grows, it finds the nurse plant turning into its competitor for light and water. Facilitation that never ends becomes suppression. In social psychology, Robert Zajonc’s 1965 resolution of seventy years of conflicting findings on social facilitation showed that other people’s presence boosts well-practised responses and hampers new ones: every shared space makes the habitual easier and the new harder, whether anyone means it to or not. In neurophysiology, Charles Sherrington’s The Integrative Action of the Nervous System (1906) showed that coordinated movement depends on the interplay of excitation and inhibition across the whole nervous system: pure excitation is a spasm, pure inhibition is paralysis. In group relations, from Bion’s refusal to lead the groups he chaired to the Tavistock conferences begun at Leicester in 1957, consultants have deliberately declined the role of the one everyone depends on, so that members could take up their own authority — anti-facilitation as a disciplined presence that refuses to rescue. In adaptive leadership, Ronald Heifetz’s Leadership Without Easy Answers (1994) asked leaders facing adaptive challenges to give the work back to the people who have to do it, keeping them in a productive zone of disequilibrium within what Winnicott called a holding environment. Here, the treatise notes, facilitation and anti-facilitation merge in one act: the leader makes the container easy and the content hard. And in complexity, Juarrero’s enabling constraints give the theoretical key. Facilitation, properly understood, is providing enabling constraints; anti-facilitation is removing governing constraints that have outlived their use, so that new constraints suited to the context can organise themselves among participants. Owen’s Open Space, the treatise suggests, is a near-perfect example: a minimal set of enabling constraints and an almost total refusal of governing ones.

§ 5. Each breath alone turns into its own shadow

The fifteenth question’s key finding is that each practice, on its own, turns into its own shadow — and each shadow can only be corrected by the other practice. Facilitation without anti-facilitation breeds dependence: the group that can’t meet without its facilitator, the team that can’t decide without its consultant, the professional who can’t reason without the tool. Anti-facilitation without facilitation breeds Freeman’s tyranny of structurelessness, or Heifetz’s distress with nothing to hold it: people thrown into uncertainty with no container, who then grab the nearest strong voice. Agency, the treatise concludes, lives in the rhythm between them.

That’s this book’s twin idea stated as a theory of practice, and it explains the Snowden–Mowles argument unusually precisely. The boxed room — the sticky-dot workshop Mowles describes, and in a more refined form any method that fixes its sequence in advance — is breathing in without breathing out. It makes action easy, and in doing so risks dependence on the form and the facilitator. The baggy room — the conference where nothing is fixed but start and finish — is breathing out without breathing in. It refuses to make things easy, and in doing so risks leaving the newcomer in a structurelessness where the established win. Each tradition sees the other’s shadow. Mowles sees the workshop’s dependence and calls it turning participants into drones. Snowden sees the conference’s structurelessness and calls it the domination of recognised power. Each is right. And each mistakes his own half-breath for breathing.

That has a corollary for the triopticon, which Snowden presents as the cure for both shadows. Does it breathe? Partly. Its roles and sequences make things easier: they lower the cost of taking part for people who might otherwise be silenced. Keeping the Ravens quiet, refusing early consensus, appointing a Coyote — these make things harder on purpose: they hold back ease where ease would lead to entrainment. But the designer holds both breaths, fixes them in advance and runs them to schedule. The rhythm is composed, not breathed. The fifteenth question’s demand is that in complex settings no single role can hold the rhythm, because no single position can see enough of the field to time it well.

§ 6. Sharing out the rhythm

If making things easier and making them harder have to alternate, who decides when? In a simple setting, the treatise answers, a designated facilitator can judge. In a complex one, no single position can see enough to time the rhythm, so the rhythm has to be shared: everyone present carries some responsibility for when to make things easier and when to stop. At its best, the group breathes. Someone holds the silence instead of filling it. Someone refuses to rescue the colleague looking to them for the answer. Someone names the hidden transcript the meeting has been dodging. Someone else makes it safe enough for that naming to be heard. No one person does all of it.

Sharing has its own shadow, the treatise admits: John Darley and Bibb Latané’s 1968 bystander studies, where the more people present at an emergency, the less likely any one of them was to act. And here again the two breaths correct each other. Shared anti-facilitation interrupts the spreading-thin of responsibility: when someone deliberately refuses to carry the function for the others, it becomes visible that nobody is carrying it — which is what has to happen before everyone can. The treatise points to Liberating Structures, the set of small facilitation formats developed by Henri Lipmanowicz and Keith McCandless. Their formats make things easy in structure and hard in content: everyone gets a slot to speak, and nobody gets the right to answer for the others.

The shared rhythm is the practical form of the discipline of declared form. A gathering whose members know that both breaths are needed, and that any of them can take up either, has made its form visible in the deepest sense — not as a procedure pinned to the wall, but as a shared understanding of what the gathering needs and who can supply it. It answers Snowden’s charge against the process tradition — that it can’t account for the practitioner’s specific contribution — by spreading the practitioner’s contribution across the room. And it answers the process tradition’s charge against Snowden — that the designer takes back the observer’s standpoint — by refusing to let any one person hold the standpoint from which the rhythm is timed.

§ 7. Heron’s three modes

The treatise recalls John Heron’s Complete Facilitator’s Handbook (1999), which distinguishes three modes of facilitation: hierarchical, where the facilitator directs; cooperative, where facilitator and group share direction; and autonomous, where the facilitator respects and protects the group’s own self-direction. Heron argued that good facilitation moves between these over a group’s life, ideally towards autonomy. The autonomous mode, the treatise notes, is the door anti-facilitation comes in through: if the aim of facilitation is a group that no longer needs a facilitator, then at some point the most facilitative thing you can do is stop facilitating.

Heron’s modes reframe the argument in the dimension Part Six found decisive: time. The boxed room is facilitation stuck in the hierarchical mode, never moving towards autonomy, because the method is run again for each new group and no group stays long enough to graduate. The baggy room is facilitation that starts in the autonomous mode — right for a mature group whose members went through the hierarchical and cooperative stages together long ago, and wrong for the newcomer who hasn’t. Each form is right for some moment in a group’s life and wrong for others. This book’s idea predicts that each tradition will treat its own mode as right for every moment, because its members live in that moment and can’t remember the others, or never went through them. The designer, who meets each group once, can’t see what a group becomes over years. The traditionalist, who has belonged for years, can’t remember what it was like to arrive.

§ 8. Designing so you can undo it

The treatise’s twelfth question asks whether agency can be designed, and answers in terms of keeping options open and decisions reversible: institutions serve agency to the extent that they do both, guarding against what Sidney Dekker called drift into failure. It draws on David Collingridge’s dilemma (1980). Early in the life of a technology or institution, change is cheap but information about its effects is scarce. Later, information is plentiful but change has become expensive, because the institution has set hard. The moment you can most easily shape an institution is the moment you least know how it should be shaped.

Applied to gatherings, Collingridge’s dilemma suggests that the triopticon and the open conference sit at different points on that curve. The open conference is old. Its form has set over decades; there’s plenty of information about its effects — Mowles knows its patterns of inclusion and exclusion well — and change is expensive, because the form has become the tradition’s identity. The triopticon is younger and more pliable: its designer could revise it tomorrow, though the evidence about its effects is still thin. Each is caught by the dilemma differently. Read through Collingridge, the discipline of declared form is a way of keeping both kinds of gathering reversible: declaring the form so it can be revised, and recording its effects so revision can be informed. Part Twelve’s double declaration — to participants during, to outsiders after — is a defence against drift.

§ 9. Answering for what you can’t control

The treatise’s thirteenth question asks whether you can answer for what you can’t control, and its tenth introduces Madeleine Clare Elish’s idea of the moral crumple zone: the way responsibility in complex socio-technical systems gets dumped on the human operator nearest the failure, who absorbs the blame for outcomes produced by the design of the whole. The idea has an uncomfortable application to designed gatherings. When a method fails — when the triopticon produces a shallow consensus, or the workshop a list of platitudes — the blame tends to land on the participants (who didn’t engage) or the convener on the day (who didn’t run it well), not on the design. The method itself is protected, with a crumple zone of people absorbing its failures. The open room has its own crumple zone: when the conference shuts a newcomer out, the blame lands on the newcomer, who didn’t understand, rather than on the tradition, whose form shut her out.

For an account of responsibility that fits complexity, the treatise turns to Iris Marion Young’s Responsibility for Justice (2011) and its social connection model. Responsibility for structural injustice is shared by everyone who takes part in the processes that produce it — not because any one of them caused it, but because they’re connected to it through taking part. Applied to gatherings, Young’s model says the designer, the convener, the established members and the newcomers all share responsibility for what the gathering’s form produces — not equally, since power and position differ, but genuinely. Nobody is the crumple zone. That’s the ethical ground for the shared rhythm: if responsibility is shared, so must be the ability to change the form that shares it out.

§ 10. The hand and the river, again

The treatise closes its first question where Davies closes his orientation: agency is ecological; it belongs to patterns of interaction rather than isolated actors. The possessive model sees the hand and forgets the river. In that light, this argument has been a quarrel between two ways of forgetting the river. The designer forgets that his method enters a river already flowing — a stream of participants’ histories, habits and stored potential that his roles and sequences can’t reset. The traditionalist forgets that his tradition is a river too, with banks, a channel and a current, and that the newcomer standing at its edge sees not open water but a torrent she doesn’t know how to get into. Both are hands in rivers. The question is never whether to enter the flow, because every hand is already in it. It’s whether you know the current — and whether you’re willing, with the others in the water, to take turns holding still and swimming.

§ 11. Things that act

The treatise’s eighth question asks whether things act, and answers, with Bruno Latour and Jane Bennett, that agency is spread across humans and non-humans alike. Latour’s actor-network theory, set out in Pandora’s Hope (1999) and elsewhere, treats anything that makes a difference to a course of action as an actant: the speed bump that slows the driver acts just as surely as the police officer it replaces. Bennett’s Vibrant Matter (2010) took the thought into a political ecology of things, arguing that materials, objects and assemblages have powers of their own that human projects have to reckon with. The treatise adds that once beliefs and mental frameworks are understood as physical arrangements of matter and energy, as John Challoner’s account requires, there’s no principled reason to stop at the skull: notebooks, databases, procedures and models do part of the work of thinking.

The rooms in this argument are full of such actants, and neither essay notices them. The flip chart where outputs are written up, the sticky dots used to vote, the wall where the triopticon’s clusters go, the circle of chairs in Open Space, the table at the Friday supper, the online platform through which Mowles’s clinician broadcast the aside she meant to keep private — each makes a difference to what can be said, by whom, and with what result. The sticky dot turns a conversation into arithmetic of preferences. The clustered wall turns a deliberation into a classification. The supper table turns strangers into companions before a word of substance has been said. The platform turns a hidden transcript into a public one at the press of a key. These are jackets made of things. They’re invisible to the people using them for the same reason as every other jacket: they work by being taken for granted. A full audit of a gathering’s form would ask not just what the convener designed and what the tradition supplies, but what the furniture does.

§ 12. The ironies of facilitation

The treatise’s tenth and eleventh questions turn to automation and to software agents acting on our behalf, drawing on Lisanne Bainbridge’s classic paper “Ironies of Automation” (1983). Bainbridge saw that the more a process is automated, the more vital the human operator’s skill becomes at the moments automation fails — and the less chance the operator gets to practise that skill, since the automation does it the rest of the time. The operator is left watching a system she rarely controls, and is expected to take over, expertly, in exactly the situations that are hardest and least rehearsed. The treatise reads these ironies through Davies’s psychological energy: the steady draining of mastery, autonomy and attention in people who supervise what they no longer do.

The ironies of automation have a direct parallel in the ironies of facilitation. The more completely a method structures a group’s deliberation — who speaks when, how contributions are clustered, how conclusions are reached — the less participants practise the judgement deliberation needs, and the worse they’ll be at deliberating when the method isn’t there, which is most of the time. A group that has met for years under a skilled facilitator may find, when she leaves, that it can’t hold a meeting. That’s breathing in without breathing out, seen from the side of skill. The designed room, run for groups that never meet without it, risks producing people who can only deliberate inside a method. The open room, by contrast, practises judgement all the time — but only among people who already have it, leaving the newcomer to fail in exactly the situations she’s never rehearsed. Bainbridge’s cure wasn’t to abandon automation but to design it so operators kept the skills it might call on — to keep them in practice. The parallel is the move from the hierarchical towards the autonomous mode that Heron described, deliberately built into a group’s life, so the method makes itself steadily less necessary.

§ 13. The “we” that acts

The treatise’s fourteenth question asks whether a “we” can act, and surveys the main philosophical accounts of collective agency. In Margaret Gilbert’s On Social Facts (1989), a plural subject is formed by the joint commitment of its members, each obliged to the others to act as a body. In Bratman’s Shared Agency (2014), shared intention is built from interlocking individual intentions and mutual responsiveness. And Raimo Tuomela, in Social Ontology (2013), distinguishes acting in the I-mode, as an individual who happens to be in a group, from acting in the we-mode, as a member of a group whose ends are your own. Its finding, drawing on Saras Sarasvathy’s account of how ventures form, is that in complex situations collective agency is less often a “we” that already exists and then acts, and more often a “we” that assembles itself while acting.

The baggy-and-boxed argument is, among other things, an argument about how a “we” gets made. The open conference assumes a “we” already made — a plural subject formed by years of joint commitment to a tradition — and its conversation is carried on in the we-mode by those who belong. The newcomer arrives in the I-mode and somehow has to be drawn into a “we” whose commitments she hasn’t made yet; the conference’s unspoken form gives her no explicit way in. The triopticon assumes no “we”. It brings strangers together and, through its roles and sequences, builds a temporary plural subject for the length of the event. Its “we” is built by design, and it may dissolve when the event ends. Each room sees the other’s “we” through its own jacket. To the traditionalist, the designed “we” is a simulation — a set of I-modes coordinated by a procedure. To the designer, the traditional “we” is a closed circle — a plural subject that can’t let new members in without falling apart. Gilbert’s account suggests the bridge: you can enter a joint commitment explicitly, by declaring that you’ll act as a body with others. A gathering that wanted to make a “we” out of strangers and members alike would invite that declaration — would say, at the threshold, what the members are committed to, and ask the newcomer whether she’ll commit too.

§ 14. The crazy quilt

The treatise’s sixteenth question takes up Sarasvathy’s theory of effectuation (2001), drawn from studies of expert entrepreneurs. It contrasts causal reasoning — setting a goal and choosing the means to reach it — with effectual reasoning, which starts from the means at hand and lets goals emerge from what can be done with them. Sarasvathy named several principles of effectuation. Start with the bird in hand: the means you already have. Commit only what you can afford to lose. Build a crazy quilt of partnerships, each partner bringing new means and new goals and reshaping the venture. Turn surprises into opportunities — make lemonade from lemons. And be the pilot in the plane, controlling what can be controlled rather than predicting what can’t. The treatise reads effectuation as turning uncertainty into possibility, and sets it beside Weick’s sensemaking and Snowden’s sensing as three ways of acting well without knowing what your action will do.

Effectuation gives the argument a model of form that’s neither designed in advance nor left to chance. The effectual gathering starts with the means at hand — the people who’ve come, what they know, what they’re committed to — rather than with a method picked to hit a goal. It commits only what it can afford to lose, probing rather than plunging. It builds its form like a crazy quilt, each participant who commits bringing something that changes the shape of the whole. It treats surprises — the newcomer’s unexpected question, the Eagle’s unlooked-for disagreement — as material, not disruption. And its convener acts as a pilot, attending to what can be steered now rather than to a plan. A gathering like that isn’t baggy, because it has principles and a discipline. And it isn’t boxed, because its form is put together during the meeting out of what participants bring. The crazy quilt is Part Fourteen’s joiner in cloth: a form made of declared pieces, seams showing, whose pattern nobody designed and everybody made.

Facilitation makes the act doable; anti-facilitation refuses to make it too doable; and agency lives in the breathing between them. The boxed room breathes in forever; the baggy room holds its breath. A room that knows it has to do both, and lets everyone in it take the breath, has started to live.

Agency lives in the breathing between two strokes. And breathing is close to play — which has its own wisdom about rules.

Part Twenty-ThreePlay, cabaret and the drift: ludology, the carnival and the Situationist critique of the room

Where we areThe argument has been serious for a long time. Time to bring in play.
What this asksPart Twenty-Three asks what game theory of the human kind — finite and infinite games, carnival, the Situationist drift — reveals about the rules of a gathering.
In brief 14 min read
  • The imprint’s framework of organisational ludology draws on Carse, Böhme, Gadamer and temporal inversion. James Carse’s distinction between finite games, whose rules may not change, and infinite games, whose rules must change, is the clearest available statement of the contestable frame.
  • Roger Caillois’s axis from paidia (free, turbulent play) to ludus (rule-bound play) is the baggy-and-boxed axis in the theory of games — and Caillois held that ludus disciplines paidia without abolishing it.
  • Bakhtin’s carnival suspends hierarchy, but its critics note that licensed misrule can protect the order it inverts; the imprint’s idea of the organisational cabaret, with its porous boundary between performer and audience, is an attempt to keep the inversion live.
  • The Situationists supply the sharpest critique of both rooms: the facilitated workshop as spectacle, the conference as recuperated refusal — and the dérive, a drift with rules, as a model of structured openness.

TakeawayThe question is not whether a gathering has rules but whether it is a finite game, played to win within fixed rules, or an infinite game, played to keep the play going by changing them.

One of the frameworks the imprint has developed under its own name is what it calls organisational ludology: an account of organisational life as play. It combines James Carse’s finite and infinite games, Gernot Böhme’s atmospheres, Hans-Georg Gadamer’s philosophy of play, and a device borrowed from the film Tenet — time running backwards, so that events are read in reverse from their consequences. The complexity corpus stretches the framework into what it calls the organisational cabaret and the carnivalesque, drawing on Henri Lefebvre, the Lettrists and the Situationists. And the imprint’s earlier essays — “All Coherence Gone”, on Situationist theory, and “Everything Is Incoming / No Original — No Copy”, on sampling and borrowing — are written in the same spirit. This Part reads the baggy-and-boxed argument through play.

§ 1. Finite and infinite games

James Carse’s Finite and Infinite Games (1986) opens with a distinction that has had a long life in management writing, much of it shallow, and that deserves reading in its original sharpness. A finite game, Carse wrote, is played to win; an infinite game is played to keep the play going.
