Finite games have fixed boundaries, agreed players and rules settled before play starts, and they end when someone wins. Infinite games have no fixed boundaries, let new players in, and change their rules as they go — precisely so the play can continue, since a rule that would end the game has to be changed for the game to go on. Carse put the contrast in one sentence: the rules of a finite game may not change; the rules of an infinite game must change.

That sentence is the most compact version going of Part Nine’s sixth question: how can participants challenge and change the form itself? A gathering whose form is fixed before it starts and can’t change during it is, in Carse’s terms, a finite game, however open its content. The triopticon, with its roles and phases settled in advance, is a finite game played to produce outputs. A gathering whose form changes during play so the play can continue is an infinite game. Does the open conference count? In one sense yes: its conversation goes where it goes, and participants can take it anywhere. But Carse would ask whether its rules — the tradition’s unspoken norms, the idiom, the liturgy — can change during play, or whether they’re as fixed as the triopticon’s, just invisible. An open conversation held inside unchangeable unspoken rules is a finite game that doesn’t know it is one. The infinite game isn’t the absence of rules. It’s a game whose rules are always in play.

Carse added a second distinction that bears on this book’s idea: finite players play within boundaries; infinite players play with boundaries. The finite player sees the boundary as a given and plays inside it. The infinite player sees it as one more piece of the game, to be moved when moving it keeps the play alive. Each tradition in this argument plays within its own boundaries — the designer within his method, the traditionalist within his idiom — and sees the other’s boundaries as the other’s prison. An infinite player would see both sets of boundaries as playable. That’s the disposition the jacket audit is trying to grow.

§ 2. Paidia and ludus

Roger Caillois’s Man, Play and Games (1958), a critical extension of Johan Huizinga’s Homo Ludens (1938), sorted games into four kinds — agon, competition; alea, chance; mimicry, make-believe; ilinx, vertigo — and set them all along one line running from paidia to ludus. Paidia is free improvisation, turbulence, spontaneous and uncontrolled fantasy: a child tumbling in the grass. Ludus is play bound by rules, conventions and deliberately cultivated difficulty: chess, or the crossword. Caillois held that ludus disciplines paidia — rules give shape to the raw energy of play and make it a game — and that, broadly, civilisation moves from paidia towards ludus.

The line from paidia to ludus is the baggy-and-boxed line in the theory of games, and Caillois handles it more subtly than either essay. He didn’t set paidia and ludus against each other as the free and the confined. He held that ludus is paidia given shape, and that the pleasure of ludus depends on the paidia energy it channels. A game with rules but no turbulence is dead; turbulence with no rules never becomes a game. Every good game has both. So Caillois anticipated, in the language of play, the breathing Part Twenty-Two described: one breath opens space for turbulence, the other gives it shape, and neither alone is play.

Huizinga, for his part, gave the theory of play its most famous image: the magic circle, the marked-off space — the arena, the card table, the stage, the law court — inside which the rules of play hold and the rules of ordinary life are suspended. Every gathering is a magic circle in Huizinga’s sense: a space set apart from ordinary life, with special rules inside. The open conference is a magic circle whose boundary is its membership; the triopticon is a magic circle whose boundary is its procedure. Neither lacks a circle. The question, again, is whether the circle is drawn where everyone can see it.

§ 3. The game that plays the players

Gadamer, in Truth and Method (1960), made play the key to understanding what art is, and his account bears on this argument in a way neither essay sees coming. Play, Gadamer argued, isn’t mainly something a person does. It’s a movement — a to-and-fro — that players get drawn into, and it has a life of its own. The players don’t so much play the game as get played by it. The game takes priority over the player’s awareness; a player absorbed in play is no longer in charge of what happens, and that absorption is what makes it real play. A player who stood outside the game, deciding moment by moment whether to join in, wouldn’t be playing.

At first glance Gadamer’s account sides with Mowles. The living present of conversation, where participants get drawn into a movement nobody controls, is play in Gadamer’s sense, and the designer who stands outside it arranging its conditions isn’t playing. But Gadamer was just as clear that play has a structure — that the to-and-fro is shaped by the game’s own form, and the player’s absorption is absorption into that form. A child at play is absorbed in the rules of a game she invented a minute ago; a chess player is absorbed in the rules of chess. Play isn’t formless. It’s the experience of form from inside, as movement rather than restriction. That’s the jacket become body, in Gadamer’s terms: the form of the game, fully lived in, feels not like a rule but like the play itself. The designer’s method, fully lived in by participants who’ve practised it, could feel the same way. The conference’s tradition feels that way to its members. The newcomer, not yet absorbed, feels both as rules.

§ 4. Running it backwards

The imprint’s ludology borrows from Christopher Nolan’s Tenet (2020) the device of time inversion: objects and people moving backwards through time, so effects come before causes and a bullet flies back into the gun. The framework uses it as a way of reading organisational events backwards from their consequences, so that what looked like accident going forwards looks like pattern going backwards.

Applied to this argument, running it backwards is a useful exercise. Read forwards, each essay tells the story of a gathering and then judges its form: Mowles describes the workshop and then condemns its structure; Snowden describes the conference and then condemns its openness. Read backwards, the verdict comes first. Each author arrived with a judgement already formed by his tradition, and the story was put together afterwards to support it. Running it backwards exposes the iterational note of agency — the selective attention and recognition of types Part Twenty-Two described — as the hidden cause of the perception it seems to follow. The bullet of the verdict was already in the chamber. That isn’t an accusation of bad faith. It’s a description of how all perception works, and a reminder that the forward story of any gathering is always partly projected backwards from the verdict you brought into the room.

§ 5. Carnival and its licence

Mikhail Bakhtin’s Rabelais and His World, written in the 1930s and 1940s and published in 1965, made the medieval carnival a figure for hierarchy suspended: a time when the official order of church and state was turned upside down, the fool was crowned, the high were brought low, and people laughed together in a second life outside authority’s rule. Bakhtin read carnival as a utopian taste of freedom and equality, and as the source of the grotesque, double-edged, renewing laughter he found in Rabelais.

The complexity corpus takes up the carnivalesque, alongside Lefebvre’s festival, as a deliberate renewal of organisational energy: the crack in the managed order where aliveness gets in. But Bakhtin’s critics have long pointed out what Part Two noted through Max Gluckman’s rituals of rebellion: licensed misrule can protect the very order it turns upside down, by giving discontent a safe channel and then putting the hierarchy back intact when the festival ends. Terry Eagleton put the objection bluntly when he called carnival a licensed affair, a permitted break in the dominant order. The triopticon’s appointed Coyote is a carnival figure in exactly this sense: a licensed fool, crowned for the length of the phase, whose upending of assumptions leaves the method untouched.

The imprint’s idea of the organisational cabaret is, in this light, an attempt to keep the upending live rather than licensed. The cabaret, as the corpus describes it, is a kind of social encounter where the line between performer and audience is deliberately blurred, where the content is satirical and serious at once, and where the emotional charge of the performance is the analysis. The Weimar cabaret the image comes from wasn’t a licensed festival but a nightly provocation, carried on under constant threat of censorship, in which the audience was caught up in the satire it was watching. As the corpus uses them, the difference between carnival and cabaret is the difference between an upending granted by authority for a season and an upending seized by performers every night, with the audience made part of the act. Carried over to gatherings, the cabaret asks whether challenging the frame is a role the designer hands out, or a possibility open to anyone at any moment, with everyone present implicated.

§ 6. The spectacle

Guy Debord’s The Society of the Spectacle (1967) opens by claiming that in societies where modern conditions of production rule, all of life shows up as a vast pile of spectacles, and everything that was once lived directly has retreated into representation. Its fourth thesis states the crux: the spectacle isn’t a collection of images but a social relationship between people, mediated by images.

Read through Debord, Mowles’s account of the facilitated workshop describes a spectacle: flip-chart outputs read out, applause on cue, energy performed, participation represented rather than lived. The participants are spectators of their own participation — a social relationship mediated by the images of participation the method produces. That’s the Situationist critique of the boxed room, and it’s severe: the designed gathering doesn’t just constrain participation; it replaces it with a picture of itself.

But Debord’s critique doesn’t spare the open room. The conference can turn into a spectacle of a different kind: a performance of depth, reflectiveness and critical seriousness, where members show each other how well they can notice dynamics and sit with ambiguity, and where the tradition’s picture of itself — Part Twenty-One’s group charisma — is reproduced as an image. A spectacle of authenticity is still a spectacle. Debord’s point was that the spectacle swallows everything, including the critique of the spectacle — which brings us to the Situationists’ sharpest idea.

§ 7. Recuperation

The Situationists called it récupération: the process by which radical ideas, gestures and practices get absorbed by the order they oppose — neutralised, packaged and sold back. The rebel’s style becomes a fashion; the critique of consumerism becomes a product; refusing work becomes a lifestyle brand. Recuperation isn’t repression. It’s absorption, and it works better than repression because it leaves the radical believing he’s been heard.

Every tradition in this argument is exposed to recuperation, and this book’s idea predicts each will see the other’s and not its own. The process tradition’s refusal of managerial method was a radical gesture, and it has been absorbed into a doctoral programme, a conference, a reading list, a professional identity and a market where its graduates sell their services. The refusal of method has become a method you can buy. The anthro-complexity tradition’s critique of systems thinking and naïve complexity was a radical gesture too, and it has been absorbed into a company, a set of licensed methods, a certification and a brand. The critique of frameworks has become a framework you can buy. Jackson’s critical systems thinking took its name from the Frankfurt School’s critique of instrumental reason, and has been absorbed into a practitioner’s guide. This imprint’s own Situationist streak — its post-punk prophetic voice, its manifestos and broadsides — is published on GitHub and promoted on LinkedIn: the same LinkedIn where, as Mowles noted, thought collectives police their members. Recuperation is the jacket the radical picks up on the way to market, and the wearer can’t see it because it feels like success.

§ 8. The dérive

Debord’s “Theory of the Dérive” (1956, revised 1958) described the Situationist practice of drifting through a city: moving quickly through different atmospheres, letting yourself be drawn by the pulls of the terrain and the encounters you find, and dropping for a while your usual reasons for going anywhere. The dérive is often misremembered as aimless wandering. Debord insisted it wasn’t. It involved playful-constructive behaviour and awareness of psychogeographical effects, and chance played a smaller part than you might think. The drifters were paying disciplined attention to the currents and eddies of the city’s atmospheres — the way some streets pull and others push away — and they wrote down what they found. The dérive had rules: a length of time, a number of people, a terrain, a habit of noting. It was a drift with a method.

The dérive is the Situationist answer to baggy and boxed, and it anticipates Part Twenty-Two’s breathing. It isn’t the commuter’s planned route, moving through the city along lines fixed in advance — the boxed. Nor is it the idle stroll of someone going wherever impulse leads — the baggy. It’s disciplined openness: a practice whose structure exists to make the drifter available to whatever the terrain offers. Psychogeography — the study of how geographical surroundings affect people’s feelings and behaviour — was its science; the dérive was its method. Carried over to gatherings, the dérive suggests a form whose rules exist not to fix where the conversation goes but to make participants available to where it might go: a length of time, a terrain, a habit of noticing, and a willingness to be pulled. That’s close to what the best open conversations achieve, and close to what the best designed methods intend. The Situationists named it, and named what it needs: the drift has to be done with your eyes open to its own currents.

§ 9. All coherence gone

The imprint’s essay on Situationist theory took its title from John Donne’s An Anatomy of the World (1611), written as the new astronomy took hold: “’Tis all in pieces, all coherence gone.” Donne was mourning the collapse of the medieval cosmos under Copernicus and Galileo — the loss of a single frame where everything had its place. The imprint uses the line, in a post-punk and Situationist key, to turn the lament into a condition. The single frame has gone, and with it any chance of a view from nowhere. What’s left is a world of fragments, samples and partial views, which each practitioner has to put together into whatever coherence the moment allows.

The imprint’s manifesto “Everything Is Incoming / No Original — No Copy” carried the same insight into the aesthetics of sampling: every work is put together from earlier works; there’s no original that copies come from, just a constant circulation of material, re-cut and recombined. Applied to the forms of gatherings, that’s freeing and sobering at once. The triopticon samples the Tavistock conference, the expert panel, the small-group exercise and the totem menagerie. The open conference samples the group-analytic session, the academic colloquium and the weekend retreat. Jackson’s grid samples Habermas and operational research. None of these forms is original. Each is a remix, and each tradition’s claim that its form is the one true answer to the problem of structure is a DJ’s claim that his mix is the only music. In a world where all coherence has gone, the honest practitioner does what the post-punk musician did: makes the form himself out of what’s to hand, says where it came from, and accepts that someone else will re-cut it tomorrow.

Ninth Interlude · On the Drift

The commuter knows his route and sees nothing. The idler follows his whims and sees only himself. The drifter sets out with a time, a terrain, a notebook and a companion, and lets the city tell him where to turn — and because he has rules, he can be surprised. That’s the secret the boxed room and the baggy room both forget: openness isn’t the absence of form but a form whose purpose is to make you available. The street that pulls you is only felt by someone who has agreed, for an afternoon, to be pulled.

The infinite game changes its rules to keep the play alive. The Church learned something similar, over two millennia, about order and the Spirit.

Part Twenty-FourTry the spirits: the long Christian argument about order and the wind that blows where it lists

Where we areThe oldest institution in the West to argue about structure and spontaneity is the Church.
What this asksPart Twenty-Four asks what two thousand years of argument about order and Spirit can teach two management theorists — and finds that it taught the lesson long ago.
In brief 14 min read
  • The imprint’s theological works — on the discernment of spirits, on pneumatology and holy fools, on incarnational reality, on soul friendship — enter a quarrel the Church has conducted for two thousand years: between the Spirit that blows where it lists and the order that lets all things be done decently.
  • Paul’s instructions to the Corinthians hold both in a single chapter; the Rule of Benedict calls itself a little rule for beginners; Quaker meetings discern the sense of the meeting in a silence that is rigorously ordered; Ignatius adapts a structured set of exercises to each person. None is baggy or boxed.
  • The holy fool, unlicensed and uninvited, is the true trickster that the appointed Coyote imitates; C. S. Lewis’s “inner ring” names the desire that sustains the established group; and the anam cara shows a constitutive form held in friendship rather than procedure.
  • Discernment — trying the spirits by their fruits — is the audit of jackets in its oldest form: not asking whether a form is structured or open, but testing what spirit it serves.

TakeawayThe Church learned long ago that the Spirit is not opposed to order, and that a form can be tested only by its fruits. The baggy and the boxed are two ways of failing to discern.

The imprint’s work reaches beyond complexity and organisation into theology. Several of its biggest treatises — on the discernment of spirits, under the title Try the Spirits; on the Holy Spirit, three Chinese theologians and the holy fools, under the title Seeing the Invisible; on incarnational reality, drawing on Leanne Payne and C. S. Lewis, under the title Another Is In Me; on soul friendship and the Celtic tradition of the anam cara — take up a question that the baggy-and-boxed argument restates in secular terms. The complexity corpus, too, has a thread about the Spirit, which reads the living present through the breath that organises social life. This Part asks what the oldest institution in the West to have argued about structure and spontaneity can teach the newest.

§ 1. The wind and the order

The Christian tradition carries both poles of this book’s idea in its founding texts. The Gospel of John records Jesus telling Nicodemus that the wind bloweth where it listeth, and thou hearest the sound thereof, but canst not tell whence it cometh, and whither it goeth: so is every one that is born of the Spirit (John 3:8, in the Authorised Version). The Spirit is the baggy principle above all others: unpredictable, uncontrollable, refusing every container. And the same tradition built, over two thousand years, the most elaborate institutional order the West has known — offices, councils, canons, liturgies, rules of life — and justified it as the work of the same Spirit.

The tension shows up in the earliest Christian writings. Paul’s first letter to the church at Corinth, written around the middle of the first century, is addressed to a community whose worship had got chaotic, with members speaking in tongues all at once and prophets talking over each other. His answer holds both poles in a single chapter. On one side: “How is it then, brethren? when ye come together, every one of you hath a psalm, hath a doctrine, hath a tongue, hath a revelation, hath an interpretation” (1 Corinthians 14:26). The gathering is open; every member brings something; nobody is the only speaker. On the other: “Let all things be done decently and in order” (14:40), “for God is not the author of confusion, but of peace” (14:33). Prophets are to speak two or three at a time, with the others weighing what’s said; if a revelation comes to someone sitting by, the first speaker is to hold his peace. Paul doesn’t choose between openness and order. He gives openness an order: a sequence, a limit on numbers, a practice of weighing what’s said, a rule of giving way to the person with something new. The gathering at Corinth is neither baggy nor boxed. It’s ordered openness, two thousand years before anyone used the phrase.

§ 2. Prophecy and office

In the second century the tension turned into a split. The movement later called Montanism, which arose in Phrygia around the 160s, held that the Spirit was speaking directly through new prophets — Montanus and the prophetesses Priscilla and Maximilla — whose ecstatic words carried an authority that could correct the bishops. The emerging order of bishops rejected the claim, and that rejection helped settle an understanding of authority as resting in office, tradition and the canon of Scripture rather than in charismatic speech. Historians have long argued about the episode, and sympathetic readers have seen in Montanism a silenced voice of the Spirit against a hardening institution. This book doesn’t need to settle that. What matters is that the Church faced, right at its foundation, exactly the quarrel this book has been following: between people who saw the institution as a box smothering the Spirit and people who saw the prophets as a disorder endangering the community. Each saw the other’s jacket. And the Church’s eventual settlement didn’t abolish either. It kept prophecy within an order, and kept the order answerable, at least in principle, to the Spirit it claimed to serve.

§ 3. A little rule for beginners

The monastic tradition faced the same tension in the desert. The earliest Christian monks of Egypt and Syria, in the third and fourth centuries, were hermits — the Desert Fathers and Mothers, whose sayings are among the great documents of Christian spirituality — and in this book’s terms their life was the baggy extreme: each alone with God, guided by an elder, under no shared rule. In the early fourth century Pachomius founded communities living under a common rule, and the communal tradition he started culminated in the West in the Rule of Benedict, written in the sixth century for the monastery at Monte Cassino.

Benedict’s Rule is the most successful constitution in the history of Western institutions — it has governed communities without a break for fifteen centuries — and the way it describes itself is revealing. In the prologue Benedict says he hopes to set up a school for the Lord’s service, in which nothing harsh or burdensome is laid down. In the last chapter he calls it a little rule for beginners, and points anyone who wants to go further to the Fathers and the Scriptures. The Rule is detailed — it lays down the hours of prayer, the psalms to be sung, the shape of the day, how the abbot should behave, how guests should be received — and yet it presents itself as a minimum, a beginning, a trellis rather than a cage. And it’s full of provisions for adapting itself. The abbot is to adjust it to each monk’s strength, to the season, to the needs of the sick and the old. The community is to be consulted on important matters, and the abbot is to listen even to the youngest, since the Lord often shows what’s better to the younger.

So the Rule contains, fifteen centuries early, almost every element of the discipline this book has proposed. Its form is declared: it’s written down and read aloud to the community regularly, so nobody can claim not to know it. It’s fitted to people: not applied uniformly but adjusted for each. It can be challenged within limits: the abbot has to consult and listen. It understands itself as a beginning, a scaffold for formation rather than a replacement for it. And it’s lived over a lifetime, so that its forms become, for those who stick with it, the shape of their days — the jacket become body in the most literal sense, since the monk’s habit is the garment he wears until he dies. Benedict didn’t ask whether his community should be structured or open. He asked how a structure could serve the freedom of the people living inside it, and he answered by writing a rule that knew it was a rule.

§ 4. The sense of the meeting

The Religious Society of Friends, the Quakers, offers the most striking modern Christian example of ordered openness, and one that bears directly on this argument because it’s about how gatherings are run. In a traditional Quaker meeting for worship, Friends gather in silence, with no minister, no liturgy and no programme. Anyone can stand and speak if they’re moved to, and the meeting may pass entirely in silence. To an outsider nothing could look baggier. Yet the silence is strictly ordered by long-standing practice. Friends are to speak only when genuinely moved, not to reply to each other, to leave time between contributions, and to test whether what they feel prompted to say is for the meeting or just for themselves. Long-standing members carry the form without saying it, and newcomers are taught it, gently.

The Quaker meeting for worship for business, where decisions get made, is more remarkable still. There’s no voting. A clerk listens to what’s said and tries to discern what Friends call the sense of the meeting — not a majority, not a compromise, but the direction the gathered community is being led in — and proposes a minute recording it, which the meeting can accept, change or reject. A single Friend who can’t unite with the sense can stand aside or, in serious cases, hold the meeting back. The clerk isn’t a chair who directs or a facilitator who designs. The clerk serves the meeting’s discernment, and her authority depends entirely on the meeting recognising that the minute is true. The practice has its own failures — meetings can be dominated by weighty Friends whose view gets mistaken for the meeting’s, an established–outsider pattern in plain dress — but its form is fully declared, its clerk is answerable, and its way of discerning is open to the whole community.

The Quaker practice is neither the triopticon nor the open conference. It has no designed roles beyond the clerk, and its silence is as open as anything Mowles describes. But its openness sits inside a declared discipline newcomers can learn, and its decisions are reached through a discernment answerable to everyone present. It may be the closest thing in existing practice to the formative encounter Snowden named in May: a process whose outcome belongs not to the clerk or any member but to the meeting, and which changes the people who take part.

§ 5. The Exercises, adapted

Ignatius of Loyola’s Spiritual Exercises, published in 1548, are among the most structured tools of formation in the Christian tradition: a sequence of meditations, contemplations and examinations, laid out over four “weeks” and carried out under a director’s guidance. Yet the introductory notes Ignatius put at the start insist again and again on adapting them. The exercises are to be fitted to the age, education and ability of the person making them; someone who can’t benefit from the full sequence is to be given less. The director isn’t to lean towards one choice or another but to let the Creator deal directly with the creature, standing like the needle of a balance, tilting neither way. The structure is elaborate, and its purpose is to make the person free — free, in Ignatius’s word, to be indifferent to everything except what serves the end for which human beings were made.

So Ignatius resolves this book’s idea in his own terms: the Exercises are a box that exists to produce freedom, and a director who imposed them uniformly, or steered the person towards his own preferences, would betray them. The director’s self-restraint — tilting neither way — is the Ignatian version of the convener who stays out of the content, and it carries the same risk this book has pointed out: claiming not to lean can hide leaning. Ignatius knew this, which is why his rules for discerning spirits pay such close attention to deception.

§ 6. The angel of light

Ignatius’s rules for discernment, attached to the Exercises, distinguish consolation — movements of the soul that draw it towards God, with peace, joy and growing faith — from desolation — movements that draw it away, with turmoil, darkness and a pull towards low things. In the rules for the second week he adds a warning drawn from Paul’s remark that Satan himself is transformed into an angel of light (2 Corinthians 11:14). With a soul that’s making progress, the enemy doesn’t tempt it with obvious evils. He starts by suggesting good and holy thoughts that fit its devotion, and then little by little leads it towards his own ends. The test, Ignatius says, is to watch the whole course of the thoughts — beginning, middle and end — and notice whether they end in something less good, or in disquiet and lost peace.

The warning applies uncomfortably well to ways of gathering. A designed method that presents itself as freeing — sharing out voice, protecting the unexpected, cutting across hierarchy — may be exactly what it says. Or it may begin with good thoughts and end by bringing back the designer’s authority under a new name. An open conversation that presents itself as the place of authentic relating may be exactly that. Or it may begin with good thoughts and end by shutting out everyone who doesn’t share its idiom. The test isn’t how the form describes itself, which will always sound attractive, but the whole course of what it produces. The angel of light is the jacket that looks like freedom. Discernment is the practice of watching where it leads.

§ 7. Try the spirits

The first letter of John gives the imprint’s treatise on discernment its title: “Beloved, believe not every spirit, but try the spirits whether they are of God” (1 John 4:1). And the Gospels give the test: “Ye shall know them by their fruits” (Matthew 7:16). Discernment doesn’t ask whether a teacher is eloquent or a movement impressive. It asks what it produces in the lives of the people it touches. Paul’s list of the fruit of the Spirit — love, joy, peace, longsuffering, gentleness, goodness, faith, meekness, temperance (Galatians 5:22–23) — is a set of tests any spirit, and any form, can be tried by.

Discernment is the jacket audit in its oldest form, and it corrects the baggy-and-boxed argument in a way the argument badly needs. Snowden and Mowles argue about forms — structured versus open — and each judges the other’s form by how it looks: the conference looks baggy, the workshop looks boxed. Discernment refuses to judge by appearances. It asks what each form produces. In whom does it grow patience, gentleness, faithfulness, self-control? Whom does it leave in turmoil and darkness? Whom does it draw in, and whom does it drive away? A structured gathering that leaves the least powerful heard and the newcomer welcomed has borne good fruit, whatever its structure. An open gathering that leaves its members renewed and its newcomers shut out has borne mixed fruit, whatever its openness. This book’s idea makes the test hard, because each tradition’s jacket shapes what it sees as fruit. But the test itself is clear, and it’s older than any of the traditions in the room.

§ 8. The holy fool

The imprint’s treatise Seeing the Invisible gives sustained attention to the holy fools — the saloi of Byzantium and the yurodivye of Russia — who pretended to be mad for Christ’s sake, mocked the pieties of the respectable, disrupted the liturgy, rebuked tsars and lived outside every institution. Symeon of Emesa in the sixth century, who came into the city dragging a dead dog and threw nuts at the candles in church, and Basil the Blessed of Moscow in the sixteenth, who went naked through the Russian winter and is said to have rebuked Ivan the Terrible, are among the best known. The holy fool stands for the Spirit’s freedom against the institution’s order: unlicensed, uninvited, disruptive, and recognised as holy precisely because his disruption can’t be contained.

Part Two argued that an appointed trickster is a contradiction in terms: the triopticon’s Coyote, licensed to challenge in a designated phase, is a court jester rather than a trickster, and his challenge protects the frame it seems to question. The holy fool is the real figure the appointed Coyote imitates. Nobody appointed Symeon. No method gave Basil his phase. Their challenge reached the frame itself — the liturgy, the throne — and it was dangerous, which is why it was holy. You can’t design a holy fool into a gathering. You can only put up with him, recognise him and, sometimes, listen. A gathering that wanted to make room for him wouldn’t appoint a Coyote. It would make its frame open enough that someone moved to challenge it could do so, and humble enough to ask whether the challenger was mad or sent.

§ 9. The inner ring

C. S. Lewis, whose work the imprint’s treatise on incarnational reality draws on, gave a memorial lecture at King’s College London in 1944 called “The Inner Ring”. Lewis noticed that in every institution, alongside the official hierarchy, there are unofficial circles — inner rings — whose membership is never formally announced but always keenly felt, and whose edges shift as people are drawn in and left out. He didn’t condemn such rings, which he thought inevitable and sometimes harmless. What he warned against was wanting to be inside them: the passion for the inner ring, which he thought one of the most powerful and most corrupting human motives. It leads good people to do bad things for the sake of belonging, and it can never be satisfied, because every ring you get into reveals another one beyond it. The cure, Lewis suggested, was to seek the company of people you genuinely love and the work you genuinely value, and let any ring that forms around those things be a by-product rather than a goal.

Lewis’s inner ring is Elias’s established group described from inside, by a moralist rather than a sociologist. The open conference, the anthro-complexity community, the critical systems school and this imprint all have inner rings, and wanting to belong to them shapes what their members see as good work, good conversation and good thinking. The newcomer feels the ring before she can see it; the member doesn’t feel it, because he’s inside. Lewis’s cure is the one Part Twelve reached through the master and the manual: let belonging follow from love of the work rather than come before it. A gathering ordered by love of the work it exists to do, rather than by its members’ wish to stay inside, will have rings — every human gathering does — but they’ll be porous.

§ 10. The soul friend

The imprint’s treatise on soul friendship takes up the Celtic tradition of the anam cara — the soul friend, the person you share the deepest things with — made popular by John O’Donohue’s Anam Ċara (1997), and examines Stephen Cope’s Soul Friends (2017), which reads friendship as a path of development through Winnicott and attachment theory. In the early Irish Church, the anam cara was a spiritual companion and confessor, and the saying credited to Brigid — that a person without a soul friend is like a body without a head — expresses the conviction that nobody is formed alone.

Soul friendship is a constitutive form held in a relationship rather than a procedure. It has no method, no sequence, no designed roles; to an outsider it might look completely baggy. Yet it’s one of the most demanding human forms, with its own disciplines of honesty, confidentiality, faithfulness and patience, learned over years and lived in until they become the shape of the friendship itself. It’s the jacket become body at the scale of two people. And it suggests, in the end, where formation actually happens: not mainly in gatherings, baggy or boxed, but in the long-term relationships that gatherings are only occasions for. The open conference forms its members because they’ve known each other for years; the triopticon, met once, can’t. Part Sixteen’s formative encounter, Part Twelve’s masters, Part Nine’s forming people rather than designing events — all point to the same conclusion, which the Celtic Church reached in a proverb. Nobody is formed by a form. People are formed by people, inside forms that serve their friendship.

The wind blows where it likes, and the Church learned to let it blow inside a rule that knew it was a rule. Try the spirits by their fruits, not their clothes: the spirit dressed in structure and the spirit dressed in openness are known alike by whom they leave at peace and whom they drive away.

Try the spirits by their fruits. Strategy, too, has learned that forms must keep moving to stay true.

Part Twenty-FiveStability through movement: water without constant form, the wayfarer, and the nuts in the Zone

Where we areFrom the cloister to the battlefield: strategy has its own version of this quarrel.
What this asksPart Twenty-Five asks how forms stay stable in a moving world — and ends in Tarkovsky’s Zone, with a guide throwing nuts into the grass.
In brief 13 min read
  • The imprint’s large treatise on strategy, The Complex Advantage of Stability Through Movement, holds that in dynamic environments stability is achieved by motion, as a bicycle is stable only while it moves. Applied to gatherings, it suggests that a form remains true only while it keeps changing.
  • Sunzi’s maxim that an army has no constant propensity, as water has no constant form, and Jullien’s reading of Chinese efficacy, set the dispute in the strategic register: neither the fixed plan nor the absence of plan, but the reading of the situation’s lean.
  • Tim Ingold’s distinction between transport and wayfaring, and the imprint’s study of Sonja Blignaut’s wayfinding, describe a mode of movement that is neither the planned route nor the aimless wander.
  • In Tarkovsky’s Stalker, the guide throws nuts tied with strips of cloth to find a path through a Zone whose traps keep moving; the sceptical writer mocks the ritual as superstition. The scene is the central idea on film.

TakeawayA form that stands still becomes a box; a form that refuses to stand at all becomes a drift without direction. The forms that last are those that stay stable by moving.

The biggest of the imprint’s works on strategy has a title that could have been written for this argument: The Complex Advantage of Stability Through Movement in Dynamic and Kinetic Styles of Strategy. It argues, across many fields of thought and practice, that where uncertainty is high and change is fast, you don’t get stability by standing still but by moving. The systems that last are the ones that keep themselves going through constant adjustment — like a bicycle, which is only stable while its wheels turn and falls over when it stops. Two of the imprint’s essays carry the same idea into navigation and film. Knowing As We Go is a scholastic reading of Sonja Blignaut’s work on waysfinding and waycraft. Crossing the Event Horizon reads complex responsive processes through Tarkovsky’s Stalker, and burns the Stacey Matrix to see what’s underneath. This Part reads the baggy-and-boxed argument through movement.

§ 1. The bicycle and the statue

The image at the heart of the strategy treatise is simple and exact. A statue is stable because it doesn’t move; push it hard enough and it falls, and it can’t recover. A bicycle is unstable at rest — it falls the moment it stops — and stable in motion, because the rider’s constant small corrections, and the dynamics of the turning wheels, keep it upright. These are two different kinds of stability. Static stability resists change; dynamic stability is made of it.

Seen through that image, baggy and boxed are both failures of dynamic stability. The boxed room aims for a statue’s stability: a form settled in advance and carried out as written, stable as long as nothing pushes too hard, and brittle when something does. The workshop Mowles describes, constantly reshuffled by its facilitator, looks like movement — but it’s the movement of a statue being shifted from plinth to plinth, not the movement of a rider. The participants are carried, not balancing. The baggy room aims for no stability at all: a form that refuses to settle, where conversation goes wherever it goes. But a bicycle with no rider doesn’t ride; it falls. The open conference, as Book One argued, isn’t actually unsettled — a tradition holds it upright — and its stability is the dynamic kind only for members who know how to ride it. For the newcomer, it’s a bike she’s never learned to balance.

A form with dynamic stability would keep itself going through constant adjustment by the people riding it — a form that changes in order to stay true. That’s Carse’s infinite game, whose rules have to change so the play can continue. It’s Part Fourteen’s dissipative structure, kept going by flow. It’s Part Nine’s back-and-forth, where the form is brought into view from time to time and revised. The strategy treatise names the principle in the language of motion: stability through movement. Applied to gatherings, it means no form, however well designed or deeply traditional, stays true if it stops moving. Run the triopticon the same way for a decade and it will harden into a box. Run the conference the same way for a decade and it will harden into a club. Each has to keep moving to stay what it is.

§ 2. Water has no constant form

The sixth chapter of the Sunzi, the classical Chinese treatise on war, ends with a comparison the imprint has come back to throughout its work on strategy:

夫兵形象水，水之形，避高而趨下；兵之形，避實而擊虛。水因地而制流，兵因敵而制勝。故兵無常勢，水無常形。能因敵變化而取勝者，謂之神。The form of an army is like water. Water in its form avoids the high and hastens to the low; an army in its form avoids the solid and strikes the empty. Water shapes its flow according to the ground; an army shapes its victory according to the enemy. Thus an army has no constant propensity, as water has no constant form. One who can win by changing in response to the enemy is called divine.

The passage is often read as advice to be formless, and the baggy tradition can press it into service: the best army, like the best gathering, has no fixed form. But the text says something more precise. Water has no constant form. It has a form at every moment, and the ground decides it. It avoids the high and seeks the low; it shapes its flow to the terrain. Its formlessness isn’t the absence of form but form responding to circumstance. And the army that’s like water doesn’t abandon form. It changes form according to the enemy, reading where the enemy is solid and where it’s empty and moving accordingly. The 勢 of the passage — propensity, the potential lodged in a configuration — isn’t constant because the configuration isn’t constant. What’s constant is the discipline of reading it.

François Jullien, whose The Propensity of Things (1992) and Treatise on Efficacy (1996) the imprint draws on throughout, contrasted two models of getting things done. In the Western model, an agent imagines an ideal form, sets it as a goal and imposes it on the world through a plan. In the Chinese model, the agent reads the propensity of the situation and lets the situation carry the effect, stepping in at the moment when the slightest pressure will tip it. The Western model is the boxed: the plan worked out in advance and imposed. But the Chinese model isn’t the baggy. It’s a discipline of perception more demanding than any plan. The general who masters 勢 has to read the terrain, the weather, his troops’ morale and the enemy’s dispositions more closely than the planner does, precisely because he won’t impose a form on them. Jullien’s Chinese efficacy is the reading of the grain from Part One’s closing word, and the cook carving along the hollows of the ox in Part Seven. It’s neither design nor its absence. It’s attention.

§ 3. Friction

Carl von Clausewitz, the Prussian theorist of war whose On War was published after his death in 1832, gave the Western strategic tradition its sobering counterpart to the Chinese advice. Everything in war, he wrote, is very simple, but the simplest thing is difficult; the difficulties pile up and produce a friction nobody who hasn’t seen war can imagine. Friction is the sum of countless small resistances — weather, tiredness, misunderstanding, chance, fear — that make any plan come out differently from how it was conceived. Clausewitz didn’t conclude that plans were useless. He concluded that the commander needs the judgement — he called it genius, and the French called it coup d’œil (the trained glance) — to see in the friction of the moment what the plan didn’t foresee, and to act on it.

Friction is Part Twenty-Two’s river, met in battle. It’s what every designed method runs into the moment it’s used: participants who misunderstand the instructions, Eagles who run over time, trios that collapse into agreement, a Coyote who’s just rude. A designer who hasn’t reckoned with friction will be surprised by it. A designer who has will end up relying on the convener’s trained glance — on formation rather than form, as Part Twelve argued. And friction is just as present in the open room, where it takes the form of the unspoken resistances by which the tradition’s idiom pushes back against the newcomer. Clausewitz’s lesson is that no form escapes friction, and the only resource against it is judgement formed by experience. It’s the same lesson the Connoisseur drew from Jackson’s years as dean: the grid didn’t deliver the strategy; the man did.

§ 4. Transport and wayfaring

The anthropologist Tim Ingold, in Lines: A Brief History (2007) and The Perception of the Environment (2000), distinguished two ways of moving. Transport is moving from point to point along a route fixed in advance. The traveller is carried across a surface without engaging with it, and the journey is a gap between destinations to be crossed as fast as possible. Wayfaring is moving along a path made as you go. The traveller pays constant attention to the terrain, the weather and the signs of the way, and knowledge is gathered along the line of travel rather than held in a map checked before setting off. The transporter knows where he’s going and doesn’t see where he is. The wayfarer knows where she is and discovers where she’s going.

The imprint’s study of Sonja Blignaut’s work, Knowing As We Go, develops a related distinction between waymaking and wayfinding, and the complexity corpus puts it in one of its recurring images: the Cobra V12, which finds the way, set against what the corpus calls Brontosaurus Broadway, which needs a road. The V12 reads the territory; the Broadway needs the territory paved first. So Ingold and Blignaut give the argument a third way of moving that’s neither the boxed room’s planned route nor the baggy room’s aimless wander. The wayfarer isn’t without direction; she has purposes, and she reads the terrain in their light. Nor is she tied to a route; she adjusts at every step to what the terrain shows her. Her form is her attention.

The corpus’s farewell to its reading of the Stacey Matrix sums the thought up in an image: the map was burned, and nothing was lost, because the map had only ever measured certainty and agreement, while the field had been running on something else all along. Through this book’s idea, burning the map isn’t abolishing form. It’s recognising that the form you need isn’t drawn on paper but carried in the traveller’s trained perception — and that a traveller without that perception, handed no map, isn’t free but lost.

§ 5. Etak

The corpus comes back several times to the navigators of the Pacific, whose voyages across thousands of miles of open ocean without instruments have become, in complexity writing, a symbol of knowing without a map. The Carolinian navigation system described by Thomas Gladwin and others uses an idea called etak. The navigator imagines the canoe as standing still and the islands as moving past it along the horizon, reading star paths, swell patterns, birds in flight and cloud shapes as a constant read-out of position. The navigator doesn’t consult a chart. He holds a moving model of the ocean in his trained perception, built over years of apprenticeship.

The Pacific navigator is often held up in praise of improvisation, and that supports the open room against the designed one. But the navigator’s knowledge is one of the most structured forms of human expertise ever developed: a body of star compasses, swell lore, chants and techniques passed down through long apprenticeship within lineages, and practised in conditions where a mistake means death. The navigator isn’t formless. He’s so thoroughly formed that the form has become perception. To someone untrained, the navigator seems to be guessing; to the navigator, he’s reading. That’s this book’s idea at sea, and its answer too: the freedom of the open ocean is only available to people whose formation has made the ocean readable.

§ 6. The nuts in the Zone

The imprint’s essay Crossing the Event Horizon takes its central images from Andrei Tarkovsky’s Stalker (1979). In the film, three men — the Stalker, a guide; the Writer, a famous author turned cynical; and the Professor, a scientist — travel into the Zone, a forbidden area where the ordinary laws of nature have been suspended. Somewhere at its centre, a Room is said to grant the deepest wishes of whoever goes in. The Zone is dangerous: its traps are invisible and they move, so a path that was safe yesterday may kill you today. The Stalker finds his way by throwing metal nuts tied with strips of white cloth ahead of the group, watching where they land, then walking to them. He insists on roundabout routes, warns that you must never go back the way you came, and talks about the Zone as something that responds to the people who enter it.

The Writer mocks the ritual. To him, the nut-throwing looks like superstition — a pointless procedure performed by a man who has made himself priest of a place he doesn’t understand. At one point the Writer refuses the roundabout route and sets off straight for the Room, and is stopped by a voice, or the sense of a voice, that he can’t explain. The film never tells us whether the Zone’s dangers are real. It only shows us the Stalker’s discipline and the Writer’s contempt for it.

The scene is this book’s idea on film, in its most unsettling form. To the Writer — urbane, sceptical, raised in a culture of irony — the Stalker’s procedure is baggy mysticism with no rational basis. To the Stalker, it’s the only rigour possible in a terrain whose rules can’t be known in advance: a practice of probing, watching and responding, carried out with total seriousness because a mistake means death. Each sees the other through his own jacket. The Writer sees superstition where the Stalker sees method; the Stalker sees recklessness where the Writer sees freedom. And the film refuses to tell us who’s right — which may be the most honest thing any work of art has said about the baggy-and-boxed argument.

There’s more. The Stalker’s method — throw a nut, see where it lands, walk there, throw again — is a probe in the strictest sense: a small, safe-to-fail action whose result informs the next one. It’s almost exactly the probe-sense-respond method Snowden’s framework prescribes for the complex domain. And the Writer’s mockery is the mockery a certain kind of intellectual has always aimed at practices whose rigour outsiders can’t see. The Stalker is a sense-maker in the Zone. The Writer is a sorter who has already decided what kind of place it is. That reversal of the usual line-up — the probe looking like superstition, the sceptic looking like the man without method — is this book’s idea’s final irony: the most disciplined practitioner can look, to someone who doesn’t share his discipline, like the baggiest of all.

§ 7. The Room nobody enters

At the end of their journey the three men reach the threshold of the Room, and none of them goes in. The Writer, afraid of what his deepest wish might turn out to be, holds back. The Professor, who has brought a bomb to destroy the Room in case it falls into the wrong hands, takes it apart and throws the pieces away. The Stalker, who has led many people to the threshold, has never gone in himself. They sit in silence at the edge of the place they came to find, while rain falls into the chamber.

The Room is the destination every tradition of convening promises: the gathering where participants’ deepest purposes come true — the new thing Mowles hopes for, the fair deliberation Snowden designs for, the emancipation Jackson seeks. And Tarkovsky’s ending suggests what this book has slowly concluded: no method gets you into the Room, and the journey to its threshold — the discipline of the way, the attention to the terrain, the willingness to be changed — is the whole of what can be achieved. The traditions argue about the route. None of them has been inside. The honest practitioner is the Stalker at the threshold, who has brought others there many times, has never presumed to go in, and knows that the value of the journey was in the walking.

§ 8. Stable by moving

Taken together, the imprint’s strategic works give one verdict on the baggy-and-boxed argument. The boxed room seeks a statue’s stability and is brittle in the face of friction. The baggy room refuses stability and, for those not formed to ride it, falls over. Water has no constant form, but it always has a form, given by the ground. The wayfarer has no route, but she has purposes and trained attention. The navigator has no chart, but he carries the ocean in his perception. The Stalker has no map, but he has a discipline of probing the sceptic can’t see. And the forms that last — Benedict’s Rule, the Quaker meeting, the infinite game — are the ones that stay stable by continuing to move: declared, revised, fitted to people and seasons, kept true by being kept alive.

The strategy treatise’s title has the last word. Stability through movement is the condition of every form that wants to be neither a box nor a void. A form that stands still becomes a box. A form that refuses to stand at all becomes drift without direction. The forms that last ride like a bicycle: upright because they’re moving, moving because someone’s riding, and ridden well only by people who have learned, through many falls, to balance.

The Writer laughed at the nuts and cloth and called the Stalker’s method superstition; the Stalker watched the Writer stride towards the Room and called his freedom recklessness. The Zone said nothing. It’s the oldest scene in the argument, and nobody yet knows who was right — only that the man who threw the nuts came home.

The man who threw the nuts came home. And now, after all these examinations of other people’s jackets, the mirror has to turn.

Part Twenty-SixThe mirror turned: an audit of the imprint’s own jackets

Where we areFor twenty-five Parts this treatise has examined everyone else’s jacket. That cannot be the end.
What this asksPart Twenty-Six asks the hardest question of all: what jacket is the author wearing?
In brief 10 min read
  • The audit of jackets, applied to Principia Humanitatis itself, finds an imprint with strong constitutive constraints: a lineage sympathetic to complex responsive processes, the 道理勢 triad, the vatic-scholastic register, the Additive Covenant, the single-file treatise of great length, and a formation in post-punk and Situationist culture.
  • Its author is not an outside observer of the dispute: he is credited by Snowden as an interlocutor, has answered Mowles’s posts in public, and writes for an audience on the same platform where, as Mowles noted, thought collectives police one another.
  • The imprint has also shown, more than once, that it can see and change its own form — removing its Chinese spine from a foreword at a collaborator’s request, marking its metaphors as metaphors, and adopting a plainer register for this treatise.
  • Its works are composed with the help of an AI system — a fact that bears directly on Mowles’s and Turkle’s warnings about machine intimacy, and that the discipline of declared form requires to be stated.

TakeawayThe disputant is wearing a jacket too. To say so is not to disqualify the verdict but to let the reader judge it from where it was given.

A book that has found three traditions guilty of seeing every jacket but their own, and has put the imprint’s own earlier tribunal on trial in Part Eleven, owes its readers one more examination. It has asked every party the six questions from Part Nine and the seventh from Part Twenty. Now it has to ask them of the imprint that publishes it. This is the back-and-forth the discipline calls for — turning attention from the background to the spotlight for a while, so the form you wear can be seen — and it’s attempted knowing it can only ever be partial. Nobody can see the whole of their own back.

§ 1. What’s fixed, and on whose authority

The imprint’s works are commissions in a particular sense. The author takes them on at his own direction, often with a target length set in advance — sixty thousand words, a hundred thousand, three hundred thousand, and in one case five hundred thousand — and pursues them over many passes until the target is met or passed. This book was extended at its author’s instruction from about thirty thousand words to more than a hundred thousand. A target length is a governing constraint: it fixes the shape of the outcome before the inquiry is finished. It has real virtues. It drives depth, it guards against wrapping up too early, and it forces the inquiry to keep looking. And it has the cost Pascal named when he apologised for a long letter: something long because it had to be long isn’t always long because it needed to be. A reader trained on the essay will feel the governing constraint as length; the author feels it as thoroughness. Both are right.

§ 2. What’s been shaped, and to prevent what

The imprint’s enabling constraints are many and well developed. There’s the scholastic question, with its objections, contrary authorities, answer and replies. There’s the reference apparatus of glossaries, indexes of interlocutors, concordances and tables. There are the reading aids added to this treatise. There are the build scripts with their built-in checks. And there’s the convention of a single self-contained HTML file, put up on GitHub Pages and promoted through LinkedIn. Each was designed to prevent something: the question format, assertion without argument; the apparatus, a long work becoming impossible to find your way round; the single file, a work splintering across platforms. These are honest forms, declared in the works themselves, and this book has tried to keep them declared. The imprint is their author. Their effects — on who reads, how far, and how easily — are part of what the audit has to notice.

§ 3. What the imprint takes for granted that some readers lack

The imprint’s constitutive constraints are the hardest for it to see, and this book has already named several. Part Eleven named the Additive Covenant and the triad 道理勢 as lenses that shape what the imprint sees. The Connoisseur and much of the complexity corpus were written from a position sympathetic to complex responsive processes. The Stacey canon, Shaw, Griffin, Mowles, Flinn and Rodgers supply a lot of the imprint’s vocabulary, and this book’s criticism of Mowles is criticism of a tradition the imprint has long lived inside. The imprint’s voice — scholastic in method, prophetic in tone, drawing on post-punk and Situationist culture, on a formation that goes back to the author’s late-1980s writing on cyberpunk as a Situationist map of the near future — is a style. Like every style, it welcomes some readers and puts others off. A reader without classical Chinese, or without the Stacey canon, or without the patience for forty-thousand-word studies of a single diagram, meets the imprint the way the newcomer meets the club: a warm room for those who know its idiom, a cold one for those who don’t.

The imprint has its own established–outsider pattern, too. Its works are read, discussed and commented on by a network of practitioners — collaborators such as Kevin Flinn, Cliff Kimber and Donald Innes Davies, people it argues with in the complexity community, readers on LinkedIn — and that network, like every network, has a core and an edge, a shared vocabulary and a group charisma. Elias’s analysis applies to it as much as to the research conference. The imprint judges itself, as Part Twenty-One noted, by its best pages.

§ 4. Who’s here, and who isn’t

The author of this book isn’t an outside observer of the argument it examines. Part Sixteen recorded that Snowden’s April 2026 post, “Stacey Unresolved”, credited a conversation with this book’s author for reframing its central argument. The complexity corpus records that the author has replied to Mowles’s posts in public, including a response to a post on the practice of complexity in which he admitted, against himself, a tendency towards radical individualism — towards walking around, as he put it, like a self-determining independent free state looking for revolution and feeling like Kevin Coyne. The corpus reads that admission as acting out what it concedes. This book reads it as the kind of jacket declaration it has asked of every party: the author names the disposition he sees from.

That position has consequences. Someone credited by one side and in conversation with the other can’t claim a referee’s neutrality, and this book hasn’t claimed it. Its verdicts on Snowden and Mowles are verdicts from inside the field, by someone who has argued with both. A reader from either tradition is entitled to ask whether that history shapes the verdicts — whether Snowden’s credit softens the treatment of his trialectic, or whether long sympathy with Mowles’s tradition sharpens the criticism of his October essay, the way family quarrels do. The audit can’t answer those questions from inside. It can only declare the position, so readers can weigh the verdicts knowing where they were given.

And who isn’t here? Jackson, again, whose voice this book has passed on but not heard. The people who took part in the gatherings being argued about, whose experience comes only through the two essayists. The newcomer whose exclusion this book has called on again and again, and who hasn’t been asked. Practitioners in hospitals, ministries and communities in conflict, whose need for structure Snowden invokes and whose actual experience of structured methods nobody in this exchange has recorded. By its own test of who’s in the room, this book is a room with a lot of empty chairs.

§ 5. Which ways of speaking the imprint welcomes

The imprint welcomes the scholastic and the prophetic: the argued and the proclaimed, the objection weighed and the aphorism set in capitals. It’s less welcoming to the plain report of practice — what happened in the room, who said what, how it felt — which Young’s critique of deliberative norms, in Part Eight, identified as the register of story, so often marked down by more formal ways of speaking. In its later versions this book has moved some way towards plainness. At its author’s direction it dropped a playful register, swapped its Latin headings for English, and put its capitals aside for flowing prose. But it’s still a work of argument rather than testimony, and the testimony it lacks is exactly the kind that would test its verdicts.

§ 6. Whether the form can be changed

Here the audit finds something to the imprint’s credit. More than once, the imprint has shown it can see its own form and change it. When it wrote a foreword for Donald Innes Davies’s anthology on governance, it took out the Chinese philosophical elements at Davies’s request, and in a later review of his work it deliberately left the triad out, respecting that steer — a constitutive constraint set aside when a collaborator said it didn’t help. Its complexity corpus marks every invented term as analytical, a structural figure, or an atmospheric intensifier — the discipline of declared form applied to language, as Part Twenty noted. And this book has been reshaped again and again in response to its author’s sense of how it read: from staccato to flowing, from playful to serious, from Latin to English, from a single column to a layout with reading aids. Those aren’t the actions of a tradition that can’t see its jacket. They’re the actions of one that sometimes can — when someone outside it points.

The lesson is the general one Part Eighteen drew from Wack’s remarkable people: a tradition can’t do the jacket audit on its own. The imprint changed its form when Davies, or a reader, or its own author reading as a stranger, pointed at the seam. It will only see its jacket again the same way.

§ 7. The machine at the desk

One more piece of the imprint’s form has to be declared, because it bears directly on the essay whose argument this book has examined most closely. Mowles’s “Staying with the trouble” opened with Sherry Turkle’s warning that artificial intelligence trains us as well as being trained — making us more passive, more dependent and more averse to the friction of real encounter — and it described the chatbot as a thin respondent with no hinterland, offering a probability-based reworking of scraped text. The imprint’s recent works, this one included, have been written in collaboration with an AI system: drafted, extended and revised in conversation with a language model, under the author’s direction and judgement.

The audit requires saying so, and facing what follows rather than dodging it. Turkle and Mowles would ask whether a treatise written this way carries the marks of the frictionlessness they fear. Has the machine’s fluency smoothed over what should have stayed rough? Has its readiness to keep going swapped volume for thought? Can a respondent with no stake in the game supply the resistance real thinking needs? Those are fair questions, and this book can’t answer them from inside. It can say that the collaboration hasn’t been frictionless — the author has repeatedly rejected the machine’s registers, redirected its arguments, supplied sources it couldn’t have found, and insisted on depth where it might have settled — and that the judgement on every page is the author’s. It can also say that the machine has no stake in the quarrel between Snowden and Mowles, which may make it a poor partisan and an unusually patient reader of both. But none of that makes the question go away. It puts it, declared, in front of the reader — who is entitled to weigh a treatise about the jackets of gatherings knowing that it was itself made inside a new and unfamiliar kind of gathering, between a person and a machine, whose own form is still being worked out.

This may be the deepest application of this book’s idea so far. To a reader formed in the tradition of solitary authorship, a treatise written with a machine may look like the boxed extreme — writing handed over to a procedure. To a reader formed in the complexity tradition, it may look like the baggy extreme — authorship dissolved into an unaccountable process. The author working inside it experiences it as neither: as a conversation with a strange partner, which demands its own discipline of directing, correcting and refusing. Each reader will see the form through their own jacket. The audit only asks that the form be visible.

§ 8. What the mirror shows

The mirror shows an imprint with a strong, distinctive jacket: a governing ambition of scale; a set of well-declared enabling forms; a powerful constitutive formation that welcomes some readers and shuts out others; a position inside the field it examines; a preference for argument over testimony; an ability — not always used — to change its form when shown it; and a new, still-unsettled way of making its works. None of this disqualifies its verdicts. It locates them. The verdicts of Books One and Two are adequate partial truths, in Jackson’s phrase, given from a bench whose position has now been declared.

The Connoisseur closed its tribunal by noting that the connoisseur of partial truths had called his truths partial and called them adequate. This Part closes by doing the same for the imprint. Its truths about Snowden, Mowles and Jackson are partial. They’re seen from a particular formation, by a participant in the quarrel, through lenses the imprint has worn for years, in a register not everyone can get into, and with help from a tool whose effects aren’t yet fully understood. They’re offered as adequate — adequate to the questions they address, backed by the evidence they cite, and open to correction by anyone who can see the seams from outside.

Tenth Interlude · On the Back of Your Own Coat

Every coat has a spot its wearer can’t see: between the shoulder blades, where the seam runs down the spine. Other people see it at once — whether it’s straight, whether it’s fraying, whether a label is sticking out. The wearer can only find out by asking, or by catching it in a mirror someone else holds at an angle. A man who has spent his life describing other people’s coats, and has got good at it, is no better placed than anyone else to see his own back. He’s just better placed to know it’s there.

The disputant wears a jacket and has said so. That gives every reader the right to object — and the treatise owes them answers.

Part Twenty-SevenSeven objections to this book, answered in the same form

Where we areA book that tests others has to accept being tested. Here it does.
What this asksPart Twenty-Seven puts seven of the strongest objections to the treatise itself, and answers them in the same form it used on everyone else.
In brief 14 min read
  • A book that tests others has to face the same test. Seven objections are put to this book: that its central idea can’t be disproved; that its symmetry is false balance; that its discipline is managerialism in disguise; that cultivation is elitist; that it repeats the appropriation it condemns; that declaring tacit forms destroys them; and that it claims the view from nowhere it denies to others.
  • The central idea is supported by empirical work on the actor–observer asymmetry, naïve realism and the bias blind spot, and is falsifiable in principle.
  • The symmetry is of perception, not of value; the treatise judges forms by their fruits.
  • The last objection is the strongest and is partly conceded: the treatise wears the curator’s jacket. It answers by declaring it, and by handing the last word to the reader.

TakeawayThe treatise survives its objections only as an adequate partial truth — which is all it has claimed for anyone else.

The scholastic disputation was never a monologue. Its method demanded that every claim face the strongest objections anyone could raise, and that the master answer them one by one. Book One turned that method on Snowden and Mowles; Book Two has turned it on Jackson, the Connoisseur, the wider field and the imprint itself. What’s left is to turn it on this book as a whole. The seven questions that follow carry on the numbering from Book One’s eighteen, and use the same four steps.

Question XIXCan the main claim of this book ever be proved wrong?

Is this book’s central idea a real finding, or a claim so stretchy that nothing could ever disprove it?

Someone might say that the idea explains too much. If every party sees other people’s constraints and not its own, then every perception of bagginess or boxedness confirms it, and any sign of self-awareness can be waved away as partial. A claim that every possible observation confirms isn’t a finding but a dogma; Karl Popper’s test for what counts as science would put it outside.

On the other hand there’s a substantial body of research in social psychology that has documented exactly the lopsidedness the idea describes — using methods that could have found otherwise.

My answer is that the idea generalises three well-established findings. Edward Jones and Richard Nisbett’s account of the actor–observer asymmetry (1971) proposed that people tend to put their own behaviour down to the demands of the situation, while observers put the same behaviour down to the person’s character: I did it because of the circumstances; you did it because of who you are. Lee Ross and Andrew Ward’s account of naïve realism (1996) described the conviction that you see the world as it is, so anyone who disagrees must be uninformed, irrational or biased. And Emily Pronin, Daniel Lin and Lee Ross’s studies of the bias blind spot (2002) found that people readily see thinking and motivation biases in others while denying them in themselves. This book’s idea is those findings applied to traditions rather than individuals. Each tradition sees its own form as what the situation demands and the other’s as a matter of character; each believes it sees the room as it is; each sees the other’s bias and not its own.

So the idea can be proved wrong in the same way those findings can. It would be refuted by a tradition that saw its own constraints as clearly and readily as it saw its rivals’. Book Two has found partial counter-examples — Stacey giving up his matrix, Jackson declaring his truths partial, the imprint dropping its triad at a collaborator’s request — and they qualify the idea the way the bias literature qualifies itself. The lopsidedness is a strong tendency, reduced by deliberate effort and by outsiders being present, not an iron law. That’s the status this book has claimed for it: a threshold object, true when it points beyond itself to a practice of attention, false when it’s used as a key that opens every door.

Back to that first objection — the idea doesn’t say every perception confirms it. It says perceptions of other traditions’ forms will tend to be more vivid, more structural and more critical than perceptions of your own. That tendency can be measured, as the bias research has measured it — and it could have turned out not to be there.

Question XXIs this book guilty of false balance?

By blaming every side equally, is this book guilty of false balance?

Someone might say that by finding every tradition guilty of the same blindness, this book practises the false balance journalism has learned to distrust: presenting two sides as equal when one has better support. Some ways of gathering really are worse than others. A method that silences the weak isn’t redeemed by pointing out that its critics have jackets too.

On the other hand this book hasn’t held back from judging. It has condemned the sticky-dot workshop, found Snowden’s “neither” guilty of sitting in the middle, found Mowles’s essay guilty of leaving Jackson’s question unanswered, and proposed specific changes to every tradition.

My answer is that the symmetry this book asserts is a symmetry of perception, not of value. It holds that every tradition is subject to the same lopsidedness in how it sees its own form and others’. It doesn’t hold that every form is equally good. On value, it has offered tests — the five conditions of Part Eight, the six questions of Part Nine, the seventh of Part Twenty, the fruits of Part Twenty-Four — for judging forms, and it has applied them, finding different forms lacking in different ways. The triopticon does better than the open conference at declaring its form and worse at letting its frame be challenged. The open conference does better at keeping power reversible for its members and worse for newcomers. The sticky-dot workshop does badly on almost everything. Those aren’t equivalences.

What the symmetry of perception adds is a warning about how such judgements get made. If every tradition sees others’ forms more harshly than its own, then any tradition’s verdict on a rival has to be discounted for that tendency, and the most reliable verdicts will come from people who belong to neither, or to both. The symmetry doesn’t abolish judging. It tells us whose judgements to trust least: those a tradition makes about its rival, from inside its own jacket, without testimony from anyone outside.

Back to that first objection — false balance means refusing to judge between positions with unequal support. This book judges. What it refuses is the assumption that the judge stands outside the argument.

Question XXIIs the “discipline of declared form” just management jargon in disguise?

Isn’t the “discipline of declared form” just another management checklist waiting to be sold?

Someone might say that the discipline of declared form — three kinds of constraint, six questions, three moments, a seventh question on atmosphere — is one more framework of exactly the kind Mowles’s tradition distrusts: a checklist that will be turned into a workshop module, sold by consultants and administered to groups, reproducing the managerialism it claims to rise above.

On the other hand this book saw the objection coming in Part Nine, where it named the ways the discipline can go wrong, and in Part Seventeen, where it called the discipline a threshold object.

My answer is that the objection is right about the risk and wrong about it being inevitable. Every framework, as Part Twenty-Three’s account of recuperation argued, can be absorbed by the market it criticises, and the discipline of declared form is no exception. If it’s administered as a checklist that certifies a gathering as honest, it will turn false in exactly the way the Stacey Matrix turned false when it was used as a map. This book has three defences against that, and none is a guarantee. First, it insists the discipline is a quality of attention, not a procedure — its questions are prompts for seeing, not boxes to tick. Second, it requires the discipline to be shared out, as Part Twenty-Two’s breathing requires, so no convener or consultant owns it. Third, it requires outsiders to be present, because a gathering can’t manufacture an outsider’s view of its own jacket. A consultant who sold the discipline as a module, ran it alone and did without outsiders would be selling something else under its name.

Beyond those defences there’s only what Part Twenty-Six admitted about the imprint itself: recuperation is the jacket the radical picks up on the way to market, and its wearer can’t see it. If the discipline gets recuperated, its first victims won’t notice. That’s a reason for vigilance, not silence.

Back to that first objection — the charge of managerialism applies to every spelled-out practice, including the refusal of spelled-out practices, as the first chaos puzzle pointed out.
