The question isn’t whether a framework can be misused but whether it carries inside it the means of noticing its own misuse. This one asks to be walked through, not stood on.

Question XXIIIs valuing long formation a kind of snobbery?

Isn’t a preference for long formation really a preference for people with time on their hands?

Someone might say that this book, having found Mowles guilty of treating the luxury of going nowhere as a method, commits a bigger version of the same fault. Its conclusion — that form and spontaneity are reconciled in people formed over decades, like the seventy-year-old who follows his heart without stepping outside the square — prizes a formation only open to people with the time, security and institutional backing to go through it. The ward sister who has to agree a rota by Monday doesn’t have decades to spare.

On the other hand formation isn’t a luxury for scholars. It’s the ordinary condition of every skilled practice — nurses, electricians, teachers, carers — all formed over years through apprenticeship and experience.

My answer is that the objection partly lands and partly misses. It misses because formation isn’t the same as leisure. Dreyfus and Benner, whose work Part Twelve drew on, found expert judgement in nurses and pilots, not philosophers; Part Twenty-Five’s Pacific navigator was formed by apprenticeship at sea, not in a seminar. The ward sister is herself a formed practitioner, and her judgement in agreeing a rota comes from years of practice. This book isn’t claiming that only people with leisure can be formed. It’s claiming that formation, wherever it happens, is the real source of competence, and that methods and openness both depend on it.

But the objection does land in one respect. The formations this book has praised most warmly — the Benedictine monk, the Quaker elder, the group-analytic practitioner, the classical scholar — happen inside traditions that take decades and have historically been open to few. And this book’s cure for exclusion, bringing newcomers into a tradition’s form, assumes the newcomer wants to be formed into that tradition, which she may not. The honest concession is that forming people rather than designing events suits communities with time and continuity, and that gatherings of strangers under pressure — the hospital in crisis, the community in conflict — may need design precisely because there’s no time for formation. That’s Snowden’s case, and this book grants it. The two aren’t rivals. They suit different timescales, as Heron’s modes and Collingridge’s dilemma both suggested.

Back to that first objection — the ward sister needs a well-designed meeting on Monday and a well-formed team over the year. This book has argued that the first should be designed with the second in mind, and that the second isn’t a luxury but what makes the first work at all.

Question XXIIIDoes this book borrow symbols the way it accuses others of doing?

This book criticises borrowed symbols. Doesn’t it borrow plenty of its own?

Someone might say that this book, having condemned the triopticon for borrowing Raven and Coyote from Indigenous storytelling traditions, borrows freely from other people’s sacred and philosophical traditions itself: the Analects and the Daodejing, the Zhuangzi and Xunzi, the Two-Row Wampum and two-eyed seeing, the Pacific navigators’ etak, the holy fools of Orthodoxy. Its characters in gold on a deep blue page lend an aura of ancient wisdom to an argument about facilitation. The menagerie’s fault is the book’s own.

On the other hand this book has cited its sources, translated them, placed them in their debates, and used them to think with rather than handing them to people as roles.

My answer is that the objection is serious, and the difference this book claims is real but not absolute. Part Two distinguished between using another tradition’s figures as role-names that make an authored division of labour look natural, and using its texts as partners in an argument. This book has tried to do the second. It quotes the classical Chinese texts in their own words with its own translations; it sets out the debates they came from — Mencius against Xunzi, the Zhuangzi against the Confucians — and it uses them to test its claims rather than decorate them. It has handled the Two-Row Wampum and two-eyed seeing with explicit acknowledgement that they belong to living peoples and aren’t the imprint’s to adopt as names for its devices. That’s a different kind of thing from appointing a participant to be a Coyote.

But the complexity corpus records, in its tribunal on Chinese thought, Roger Ames and David Hall’s warning against the missionary’s error — reading another tradition through your own lens, so that it confirms what you already believed. This book has found support for its thesis in every classical source it consulted: the square at seventy, the rustic and the clerk, the farmer of Song, Cook Ding, the empty room. That consistency is either evidence that the classical tradition grasped what this book argues, or evidence that this book read the classical tradition through its jacket. Part Eleven raised the same doubt about the triad as a lens that always finds what it’s looking for. The honest answer is that both may be true, and that a reader formed in the Chinese tradition is better placed than this book to judge. The aura of the characters on the page is real, and this book can’t wholly disown it. It can only say that the characters are there because they were read, and ask the reader to read them too.

Back to that first objection — this book doesn’t claim innocence. It claims a better practice — citing, translating, placing in context, and using in argument — and accepts that a better practice isn’t a pure one.

Question XXIVDoes naming an unspoken form destroy it?

If unspoken forms only work while they stay unspoken, won’t declaring them destroy them?

Someone might say that the discipline of declared form defeats itself. Polanyi showed that background details only work when you’re not attending to them; drag them into the spotlight and they stop working, the way a pianist who watches her fingers can no longer play. Rogers’s core conditions can’t be declared as techniques without ceasing to be genuine. The holy fool’s disruption can’t be scheduled. A community asked to name its unspoken assumptions will lose the ease that made its conversation fruitful. Some forms only live in the dark.

On the other hand Polanyi himself held that tacit knowledge made explicit can be taken back in at a higher level, so the pianist who analyses her fingering plays better afterwards.

My answer is that the objection names a real cost, which this book acknowledged in Part Six and builds into its discipline through the back-and-forth. Declaring isn’t constant exposure. It happens at the threshold, where a newcomer needs to know what form she’s walking into; at a breakdown, where the form has already become conspicuous by failing; and at the close, where the form can be reviewed without disturbing the work. In between, the form is allowed to fade back into use. A community that named its unspoken assumptions at every turn would indeed lose its ease. A community that named them once a year, to newcomers and in review, would lose little and gain a lot.

Some forms really do need to stay invisible in the moment. A Rogerian facilitator can’t be genuine while announcing her genuineness, and a holy fool can’t be holy on schedule. But even these forms can be declared at another level. The facilitator can tell a group at the start that she won’t direct it, and why. The community can say of its holy fools that it will put up with disruption and ask whether the disrupter has been sent. What’s declared is the frame the invisible form works within, not its every movement. That’s what Benedict did when he wrote a Rule that left room for the abbot’s discernment, and what the Quakers do when they explain to a newcomer how the silence works without saying what anyone will say in it.

Back to that first objection — some forms only live in the dark, but they live in rooms whose doors can be marked. The discipline asks for the door to be marked, not for the room to be floodlit.

Question XXVHas this book claimed the view from nowhere it denies everyone else?

In the end, hasn’t this book claimed exactly the view from nowhere it denies everyone else?

Someone might say that this book’s whole architecture is a view from nowhere. It stands above Snowden, Mowles and Jackson, diagnoses each tradition’s jacket, maps eight complexities, surveys three thousand years of thinking about structure, and proposes a discipline for every gathering. It does exactly what it found Jackson’s grid guilty of: it builds a coat cut for describing coats. Its “jacket audit” is a cathedral where every tradition gets a side chapel and none is allowed to stay outside. Applied to this book, its own idea finds it guilty.

On the other hand this book has declared its position at every stage — in Part Eleven, where it named the imprint’s loyalties; in Part Sixteen, where it named its author’s place in the argument; in Part Twenty-Six, where it audited the imprint’s jackets; and in this question, where it puts the objection to itself.

My answer is that this is the strongest of the seven objections, and it has to be partly conceded. This book does wear a jacket, and Part Five gives it a name: the jacket of choosing methods, the curator’s jacket, cut for spotting partiality in every tradition and arranging them all in one field. That’s the jacket the Connoisseur found Jackson guilty of wearing, and this book has worn it throughout, with the curator’s usual difficulty seeing his own seams. Declaring its position doesn’t take the jacket off. It makes it visible, which is the most any jacket can be asked to do.

What separates this book from a claim to the view from nowhere, if anything does, isn’t that it stands outside the argument. It’s that it has tried to keep the argument’s outcome open. It hasn’t proposed a method that would settle the quarrel between the traditions, or a grid that would give each its territory. It has proposed a discipline whose content is supplied by the gatherings that practise it, whose questions have no fixed answers, and which needs people present that this book can’t speak for. Its final recommendation for the room at Hull isn’t a design but a set of conditions under which the three traditions might see themselves as the others see them — conditions whose outcome, in the language of Snowden’s trialectic, belongs to the encounter and not to this book. If that’s a view from nowhere, it’s one that points to somewhere it can’t see.

And there’s one party whose position this book can’t occupy, and so has to hand the last word to: the reader. Every reader brings a jacket — the designer’s, the traditionalist’s, the curator’s, or one this book hasn’t named — and will see its argument through it. A reader from Snowden’s tradition will find it unfair to the triopticon; a reader from Mowles’s will find it hard on the conference; a reader from Jackson’s will find it repeats the Connoisseur’s charges in gentler words. Each will be partly right. This book can’t see those readings from inside. It can only be read.

Back to that first objection — this book is a jacket that has tried to describe jackets, itself included. It doesn’t claim to have succeeded. It claims to have shown its seams, and it asks its readers to show it theirs.

The treatise survives its objections only as an adequate partial truth. That is enough. What remains is to gather it all together.

Part Twenty-EightFifty theses on jackets, and the Grand Ruling

Where we areYou have come a long way. It is time to gather everything into something you can carry.
What this asksPart Twenty-Eight distils both Books into fifty theses and a final ruling.
In brief 6 min read
  • The findings of both Books are distilled into fifty theses, grouped under eight heads: the central idea; design; tradition; curation; the field; formation; the room; and the imprint.
  • The Grand Ruling gathers the verdicts on Snowden, Mowles, Jackson, the Connoisseur and the imprint, and states what the room at Hull — or any room — would need.

TakeawayNo room is without walls. The work is to see them, to name them, to ask who built them, and to move them together when they bind those least able to move them alone.

The imprint’s earlier tribunals ended with lists of findings — a hundred in the Connoisseur, a book of a hundred aphorisms in the study of the Malayan Emergency — where the arguments of many Books were squeezed into sentences you could carry away. This book ends the same way: fifty theses under eight headings, each resting on an argument made earlier, followed by a ruling that pulls the verdicts together. They’re offered in the spirit the whole book has asked of everyone: as adequate partial truths, declared from a known position, and open to the reader’s correction.

I. On the central idea

  1. To the strait-jacketed, the other always seems baggy; to the baggy, the other always seems boxed.
  2. Bagginess is rarely a property of a room. It’s the name a jacket gives to someone else’s skin.
  3. The constraints you wear sit in the background and feel like freedom; the constraints someone else wears hit you head-on and feel like obstacles, or like nothing at all.
  4. Form mostly becomes visible when it fails or when it’s foreign; the people a structure fails will always describe it most vividly.
  5. The idea is the actor–observer asymmetry, naïve realism and the bias blind spot applied to traditions. It’s a strong tendency, reduced by effort and by outsiders — not an iron law.
  6. The idea works first as weather: each side feels its own jacket as warmth and the other’s as cold, before either has thought about it.
  7. The go-between sees in others the deficit his role needs.

II. On design

  1. Snowden is right that the question isn’t how much structure but what kind.
  2. Snowden is right that choosing no constraints is itself a choice — and that sentence undoes his title.
  3. A third term placed between two poles is a midpoint, not a trialectic. By Snowden’s own May 2026 test, “neither baggy nor boxed” is Cassian, not Maximian.
  4. The designer who stays out of the content is in every turn of the form; the convener’s claim to hold no power is the use of power hardest to challenge.
  5. An appointed trickster is a licensed jester: the Coyote may challenge every assumption except that there should be a Coyote.
  6. Animal role-names aren’t neutral. They make an authored division of labour feel like a natural classification of people, and they borrow from peoples whose figures they are.
  7. A method that treats all disagreement as a matter of thinking has quietly renamed the political axis.
  8. Putting confident experts first builds in the anchoring it was meant to prevent.
  9. In sense-making, constraint-mapping and the “anthro” of anthro-complexity, Snowden has the tools that would have shown up the club’s jacket. The maker of a tool is the last to point it at a rival’s room.

III. On tradition

  1. Mowles is right that over-designed process can reduce participants to going along, that knowledge isn’t wisdom, and that uncertainty is fertile.
  2. The open conference is a liturgy, not a void; its freedom rests on a group-analytic frame it doesn’t declare.
  3. Therapy, Mowles’s best example, is the most framed encounter in human practice: the crack the light gets in through belongs to a vessel.
  4. Rosa, Han and Jaeggi, read in full, support enabling conditions, not the refusal of design.
  5. Going nowhere is a method for the secure and an abandonment for the hard-pressed.
  6. Jackson asked who is protected; the essay answered how long the meeting should take.
  7. The established don’t experience themselves as established. In Elias and Scotson, Scott, Fleck and Janis, Mowles has the tools that would have shown up his own room’s pattern of power — and pointed them only at others.
  8. Staying with the trouble means staying with the other — the alien method, the strange kin, the drone in the next chair — not staying among your own.

IV. On curating methods

  1. The curator’s jacket is the subtlest of the three: it’s cut for describing jackets, so it’s the last to see its own loom.
  2. Every grid is a confession: it tells you what its maker feared to leave unsorted.
  3. Of the three traditions, Jackson’s has most often tried to describe its own jacket from inside. Calling your truths partial and calling them adequate is the discipline of declared form at the scale of a whole system.
  4. The grid rightly insists that every form has a proper range; it struggles to admit that its own map of ranges is a form too.
  5. The court that tries the mapmaker has to sit somewhere; its most honest verdict names the bench.

V. On the field

  1. One fault line runs across the eight complexities — where the practitioner stands, and whether method is legitimate — and from it come two mirror-image charges, each accurate about the other’s jacket and unanswerable from inside your own.
  2. The quarrels inside complexity thinking are family quarrels among branches of a common ancestor, bitter in proportion to how alike they are.
  3. Every map of the field is drawn by someone, and where that someone stands is the one spot the map can’t show.
  4. Rejecting frameworks is itself a framework.
  5. Every form in the argument is a threshold object: true when it points beyond itself, false when used to do the job it seems to offer.
  6. No order is free. Whoever calls a room baggy is seeing order whose cost he can’t see; whoever calls it boxed is seeing order whose cost he can.
  7. A room that produces new things is far from equilibrium — and so, by the physics of open systems, constrained.

VI. On formation

  1. Every tradition sells its manual and lives by its masters.
  2. At seventy, the square became desire: freedom is form fully absorbed, not form removed.
  3. Neither neglect nor forcing: the farmer who doesn’t weed and the farmer who pulls at sprouts are the baggy and the boxed, and cultivating is weeding.
  4. Minimal structure is only minimal to people who’ve absorbed it; to the unformed it’s a maze or a void.
  5. Facilitation and anti-facilitation are breathing in and breathing out. Each alone becomes its own shadow, and in complex rooms the rhythm has to be shared.
  6. Nobody is formed by a form; people are formed by people, inside forms that serve their friendship.

VII. On the room

  1. Who’s in the room matters more than how they’re arranged.
  2. Declare the form to the people it binds while it binds them, and record it for the people who’ll judge it afterwards.
  3. Ask of every gathering: what’s fixed, and on whose authority; what’s been shaped, by whom, and to prevent what; what it takes for granted that some people lack; who’s here and who isn’t; which ways of speaking it welcomes and which it marks down; how its form can be changed; and what its weather is, and for whom.
  4. The finite game plays within its rules; the infinite game changes its rules so the play can go on.
  5. Try the spirits by their fruits, not their clothes.
  6. The host who says “this is my house, and I’ve made room in it for you” tells the truth the vanishing convener hides.

VIII. On the imprint

  1. The one doing the judging wears a jacket too: a lineage, a lens, a rule of writing, a register, a place inside the quarrel, and a new way of making its works. Saying so locates the verdict without disqualifying it.
  2. No room is without walls. The work is to see them, name them, ask who built them, and move them together when they hem in the people least able to move them alone.

Fifty theses. Now, the verdicts on everyone — and on every room.

The Grand RulingUpon both Books, all parties, and every room

Where we areThe theses are set down. Now the verdicts.
What this asksThe Grand Ruling asks what each party, and every room, owes to the argument you have just followed.

Book One gave rulings on two essays. Book Two has widened the court to the whole field they came from, and to the imprint that convened it. The Grand Ruling brings together what both Books have found.

On Snowden

Snowden’s essay is right to move the question from how much structure to what kind; right that unstructured rooms don’t abolish power; right that mixed, pressured settings need design; and right — gloriously right, in a sentence that undoes its own title — that choosing no constraints is still a choice. It’s wrong to put its method between the poles it sets up — wrong by the standard of Snowden’s own trialectic, which rules out midpoints. It’s wrong to see the club’s constitutive form as an absence, and wrong to propose settling the matter inside its own form. The failing isn’t one of intelligence but of application: the author of sense-making, constraint-mapping and the formative encounter didn’t bring those tools to the room he criticised. The best thing he wrote in 2026 was the May trialectic. The ruling commends it to him as the third term his November essay was missing.

On Mowles

Mowles’s essay is right about the frictionless machine, right about wisdom and knowledge, right about how hollow over-facilitated process can be, and honest about the exclusions in his own room and his own going-along in the workshop. It’s wrong to treat structure as such as the enemy of real encounter, when every one of its best examples depends on form. It’s wrong to leave Jackson’s question unanswered. And it’s wrong to repeat, after the charge had been made in public, the jump of Jaeggi from history to an afternoon. This too is a failing of application: the author of the essay on gossip — who gathered Elias and Scotson, Scott, Fleck and Janis to show how groups police their boundaries — and of the practical-wisdom turn that asked what should be done, didn’t bring those tools to the room he was defending. The ruling commends to him the question his own practical-wisdom turn would ask: what should the conference do about the newcomer it shuts out?

On Jackson

Jackson, who hasn’t spoken in his own voice in either essay, comes out better under this book’s idea than either essayist or the Connoisseur allowed. His grid claims too much when it presents its map of situations as neutral, and the Connoisseur’s two-sided verdict stands. But his insistence that every form has a proper range is half the jacket audit; his late adoption of recoverability is the discipline of declared form reached by another road; and his calling his own truths adequate and partial is the closest any of the three traditions has come to seeing its own jacket. The ruling records that the court owes him a hearing in his own words, and hasn’t yet given it.

On the Connoisseur

The Connoisseur’s verdict on Jackson stands, with a third clause: its own tribunal was a room — one that left out the tradition it wouldn’t name, whose witnesses were picked by one hand, and whose lenses and rules weren’t declared to its readers. Its convergence of a hundred rivers is evidence of a widely shared process-minded sensibility, not independent proof that the sensibility is right. Its best finding — that each tradition credits the manual for what only training passes on — turned out, when examined, to apply to every tradition, including the one it came from.

On the imprint

The imprint wears a jacket and has now declared it: a lineage sympathetic to complex responsive processes; a triad that cuts and may also always find what it’s looking for; a rule that adds and never subtracts; a register some readers can’t get into; a place inside the quarrel it examines; and a way of writing its works with a machine. It has shown it can see and change its form when someone outside points at the seam. Its verdicts are partial, and offered as adequate.

On every room

There’s no room without walls. The baggy-and-boxed quarrel is a quarrel between people who see the walls of other rooms and not their own. It isn’t settled by finding the right amount of structure, or by finding a midpoint between too little and too much, or by a grid that gives each form its territory. It’s settled, as far as it can be, by a practice of attention. Declare the form to the people it binds while it binds them. Ask who’s here and who isn’t. Make room for every way of speaking. Let the frame itself be challenged. Keep power reversible. Tend the weather so the stranger feels welcome. Breathe in and breathe out, and let everyone take the breath. Record what was done so it can be judged. And try every form by its fruits. Above all, it’s settled by formation: by people shaped, over time and in friendship, to see their own jackets, to step into other people’s without contempt, and to move the walls together when they hem in the weakest.

On the room at Hull

If the three traditions ever do meet as Snowden proposed, the Grand Ruling repeats and extends Book One’s conditions. Let the room include people from none of the three traditions, chosen in a way that doesn’t just reproduce existing networks. Let each tradition start by describing itself as the others see it. Let the gathering’s form be a treaty rather than a uniform — each tradition running part of it in its own way, with agreed points of exchange, like the vessels travelling side by side in the Two-Row Wampum. Let its form be declared at the threshold, reconsidered at every breakdown, and reviewed at the close. Let there be room for stories as well as argument, and let the frame itself be open to challenge from the first hour. Let its weather be tended. Let everyone breathe both breaths. And let everyone who comes understand that the point isn’t to decide which tradition is right, but to help some of the people there see their own jacket for the first time — which is the only outcome any of the three traditions has ever really wanted for anyone else.

Every gathering is wearing clothes. The serious question about any room isn’t whether it has walls, but whether the people inside can see them, name them, ask who built them, and — when the walls hem in those least able to move them alone — move them together.

GlossaryKey terms, in brief

Central idea (of this book; also called the predicate)
To the strait-jacketed, the other always seems baggy. Its twin: to the baggy, the other always seems boxed. Each position perceives the other’s constraints and not its own.
Jacket
The book’s figure for the form, explicit or tacit, that any gathering or tradition wears. A jacket “becomes a body” when it is so fully incorporated that it is felt as freedom.
Governing constraint
A constraint that fixes outcomes in advance (Juarrero, as borrowed by Snowden).
Enabling constraint
A constraint that shapes the conditions of interaction without fixing what emerges.
Constitutive constraint
A constraint that makes a community or whole what it is: shared tradition, vocabulary, canon, habit. Snowden’s pairing omits it.
Triopticon
Snowden’s structured method for gatherings, with expert Eagles, listening Ravens, building Beavers, challenging Coyotes and carrying Magpies.
Anthro-complexity
Snowden’s tradition: complexity science applied to human systems through designed constraints and safe-to-fail probes.
Complex responsive processes (CRP)
The Stacey–Griffin–Shaw–Mowles tradition: organisations as patterns of conversation and power relations among people, drawing on Elias and Mead.
Critical systems thinking (CST)
Jackson’s tradition: choosing among systems methods according to context and emancipatory purpose.
Entrainment
The rapid falling of a group into familiar patterns, often set by the first confident voice.
Habitus / doxa
Bourdieu’s terms. Habitus: durable dispositions that structure conduct without conscious rule-following. Doxa: what goes without saying because it comes without saying.
Subsidiary awareness
Polanyi’s term for the awareness we attend from (the hammer in the palm) as opposed to what we attend to (the nail).
Ready-to-hand
Heidegger’s term for equipment in use that withdraws from notice and becomes visible only in breakdown.
Thought collective / thought style
Fleck’s terms for a community of inquiry and the directed perception it cultivates in its members.
Sittlichkeit
Hegel’s “ethical life”: freedom realised in customs and institutions that people recognise as their own.
禮 lǐ (ritual)
Confucian ritual propriety: form as the cultivation of character.
理 lǐ (pattern)
The inherent grain or pattern in things, originally the veins in jade. Discovered in the material, not imposed on it.
偽 wěi (artifice)
Xunzi’s term for deliberate human effort and design, such as ritual, which straightens nature like a press-frame.
勢 shì (propensity)
The potential inherent in a configuration of circumstances, as read by Jullien.
Out-group homogeneity effect
The tendency to see other groups as more uniform than one’s own.
Third face of power
Lukes’s term for power exercised by shaping what people want and perceive, so that they do not recognise it as power.
Agonism
Mouffe’s term for conflict between adversaries within shared institutions, as opposed to antagonism between enemies.
Ritual of rebellion
Gluckman’s term for licensed, bounded inversion of an order, which tends to reinforce that order.
Discipline of declared form
The book’s proposal: declaring constraints in three registers, asking six questions, at three moments (threshold, breakdown, close).
Anti-facilitation
The deliberate withholding, disruption or reversal of ease, so that a group must constitute its own agency; with facilitation, one of two strokes of a single respiration (The Hand That Enters the River, Quaestio XV).
Assumed deficit / innate capacity / formative encounter
Snowden’s trialectic of May 2026, named for Augustine, Pelagius and Maximus the Confessor. The practitioner supplies what is missing; or enables what is present; or co-creates, with the system, something neither possessed beforehand.
Beholder-relativity
The corpus’s term for the way every map of a field reflects the position of the one who draws it.
Chordal triad
Emirbayer and Mische’s account of agency as iterational (oriented to the past), projective (to the future) and practical-evaluative (to the present).
Constitutive constraint
See Book One. In Book Two, the tradition, idiom and liturgy that make a community what it is, felt by members as freedom and by newcomers as an invisible wall.
Dérive
The Situationist drift: a disciplined openness, with a duration, a terrain and a practice of noticing, that makes the drifter available to what the city offers.
Established and outsiders
Elias and Scotson’s figuration, in which a more cohesive group uses praise-gossip and blame-gossip to build its own charisma and an outsider group’s disgrace.
Finite and infinite games
Carse’s distinction. The rules of a finite game may not change; the rules of an infinite game must change so that the play can continue.
Hidden transcript
Scott’s term for the critique of power spoken offstage, behind the backs of the dominant.
Kenotic convenor
The imprint’s term for the convener who empties himself of agenda so that the gathering can fill the space. Self-emptying is a form of authority, not its absence.
Paidia and ludus
Caillois’s axis running from free, turbulent play to play bound by rules; ludus disciplines paidia without abolishing it.
Recoverability
Checkland and Holwell’s criterion of rigour: declare your framework and method in advance, so that outsiders can recover and scrutinise the process.
Recuperation
The Situationist term for the absorption of radical gestures by the order they oppose.
Set-piece and open play
Rodgers’s distinction between convened events and the everyday flow of conversation in which most organisational life happens.
Social entropy
The Zen Garden study’s reading of disagreement as the high-probability social state, and of agreement as a low-entropy order paid for by the continuous work of power.
Stability through movement
The imprint’s thesis that, in dynamic environments, forms stay stable only by moving, as a bicycle stays upright only while it is ridden.
Threshold object
A form that is true only while it points beyond itself, and false the moment it is used to do the work it appears to offer.
Twin charges
The symmetrical accusations between anthro-complexity and the process schools: quietism on one side, a reoccupied observer’s standpoint on the other. Each accurately describes the other’s jacket.

All characterisations of Snowden’s essay are paraphrase from the published text, with one short quotation. Parts One to Three take Mowles’s and Jackson’s positions as Snowden reports them; Part Four reads Mowles’s essay directly, by paraphrase with one short quotation. Neither essay quotes Jackson at length. Book Two draws on the imprint’s own works, cited above, and on Snowden’s and Mowles’s 2026 writings as those works record them.
